Feedback Report: Draft on the Exxon Valdez Oil Spill

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and emotionally resonant topic, and your personal outrage at Exxon's response comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more as a passionate reflection than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, support your claims with verifiable evidence, and revise for structure and clarity. Below are specific areas to address.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Your opening claim—"money is the root of all evil" and this is "personally true"—is a broad, clichéd generalization that doesn't set up a clear, arguable thesis. What exactly are you arguing? Possible options:

Exxon's negligence, not just the spill itself, caused unnecessary harm and Exxon should be held more accountable than it was.
Corporate profit motives systematically undermine environmental safety, and stronger regulation is needed.
The Exxon Valdez case demonstrates a need for specific policy changes (eg, double-hull requirements, higher penalties).

Choose one specific, defensible claim and state it explicitly in your introduction. Right now, the essay drifts between personal anger, historical narrative, and policy recommendations without a throughline connecting them into a coherent argument.

Fill in the Gaps—Literally and Evidentially

You have an unresolved placeholder () where a quotation from Exxon spokespersons should go. This needs to be filled in with an actual, cited quote—this is a significant gap that undermines the essay's credibility as it stands.

More broadly, your essay relies heavily on assertions without citation:
"11 million gallons of raw crude oil" — cite your source.
"After close to two years, Exxon finally made monetary amends" — what was the amount? What court case or settlement? (This likely refers to the $5 billion punitive damages verdict, later reduced by the Supreme Court to about $507 million—details matter here and citing them will strengthen your credibility.)
"Exxon's notes and court transcriptions prove..." — which documents? Quote or summarize specific findings.
The claim that double-hulling "is common practice now" should be linked to the Oil Pollution Act of 1990, which mandated this change—this is actually a strong piece of evidence for your argument and deserves more development.

Academic argumentative writing requires that claims be traceable to sources. Add citations (check with your instructor on the required format—MLA, APA, etc) for all factual claims, statistics, and quotations.

Balance Emotional Appeal with Logical Argument

Your personal reaction—watching the spill on television, feeling anger and loathing—is a legitimate rhetorical strategy (pathos), but it currently dominates the essay at the expense of logical argument (logos) and credible evidence (ethos). Consider:

Keep a brief personal anecdote to establish your stake in the issue, but move quickly to substantive analysis.
Replace phrases like "beyond belief," "unbelievable," and "mind boggling" with concrete details that let the facts speak for themselves. Precise details (specific numbers, specific consequences for specific communities or species) are more persuasive than intensifiers.

Strengthen Structure and Paragraph Unity

The essay currently moves somewhat associatively: personal reaction → background on Exxon → narrative of the spill → the unfilled quote → response → conclusion with policy demands. Consider reorganizing along these lines:

Introduction: State your specific thesis about corporate accountability.
Background: Briefly summarize the spill (with citations).
Evidence of negligence: Detail the specific failures (understaffing, lack of double hulls, delayed response) with documented support.
Consequences: Environmental, economic, and human impact—again with specific, cited data.
Accountability and outcome: What Exxon paid, what changed (legally/industry-wide), and whether this was sufficient.
Your argument for further action: Your policy proposals (research funding, land acquisition, corporate accountability laws) should follow logically from the evidence you've just presented, rather than appearing as a new set of demands in the conclusion.

Editing and Mechanics

Watch for repeated words ("prior to the grounding the Exxon" should be "grounding of the Exxon Valdez").
Vary sentence openings—many paragraphs begin with "I."
Proofread for missing words and awkward phrasing (eg, "the Captain in charge the vessel").

Next Steps

For your revision, I'd recommend:
Draft a one-sentence thesis statement and share it with me before rewriting.
Locate 3–4 credible sources (news archives, the Oil Pollution Act text, court records) to support your key claims.
Reorganize your paragraphs around the structure above.
Cut emotional intensifiers by half and replace with specific evidence.

You clearly care about this topic—channel that passion into a tightly argued, well-supported essay, and it will be far more persuasive to your reader.

Feedback Report: "Crime Does Not Pay"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and relatable topic, and your personal anecdote about the businessman who scammed elderly investors is a powerful illustration of your thesis. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: clarifying your central argument, strengthening your essay's structure, expanding your evidence beyond a single story, and correcting grammar and mechanics. Below are specific suggestions for improvement.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction never actually states your thesis clearly—there's a placeholder () where your title should be referenced, which suggests this section was left unfinished. Since this is an argumentative essay, your first paragraph should end with a clear, debatable claim: something like, *"Although illegal activity may bring quick wealth, crime ultimately destroys the lives of those who pursue it."* Right now, readers must wait until paragraph two to learn your actual position.

Additionally, consider sharpening your argument. "Crime does not pay" is a common saying, but as a thesis it's somewhat vague. What does "pay" mean here—financially, emotionally, in terms of family relationships, freedom? Your anecdote actually touches on several of these (loss of money, loss of family trust, imprisonment), so your thesis could preview these specific consequences to give the essay more direction.

Structure and Organization

Your essay currently reads like two loosely connected parts: a general reflection on why people turn to crime, followed by an extended narrative. To strengthen the organization:

Trim the introduction. The first paragraph currently offers several explanations for why people commit crimes (impatience, environment, rejection of societal norms). This is interesting, but it distracts from your main argument. Consider condensing this into two or three sentences that set up *why* people are tempted by "quick riches," then move quickly to your thesis.

Give the anecdote its own clear purpose. Right now, the story of the businessman takes up most of your essay. This is fine, but you should explicitly connect details of the story back to your thesis as you go. For example, after describing his arrest, add a sentence like: *"This moment illustrates how quickly illegally-gained wealth can be lost."*

Expand your conclusion. Your final paragraph functions more as a wrap-up of the story than a conclusion to the essay. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis, briefly synthesize your argument, and perhaps offer a broader insight about crime and its consequences—not just restate "crime does not pay" once more.

Development and Evidence

A single anecdote, however compelling, is not enough to fully support an argumentative essay. Ask yourself: what would make a skeptical reader believe your claim? Consider:

Adding a second example or counterexample. What about people who commit crimes and are never caught? Addressing this complicates your argument, but also makes it stronger if you can explain why crime still "doesn't pay" even for them (eg, loss of trust, living in fear, moral or psychological toll).
Incorporating outside evidence. Even a brief reference to statistics on recidivism, incarceration rates, or white-collar crime penalties would add credibility and move the essay beyond personal experience.
Explaining "why." You describe *what* happened to the businessman, but you could analyze more deeply *why* his crime failed to "pay off" in the long run. Was it the risk of discovery? The betrayal of trust? The psychological burden of guilt? Digging into causation, rather than just narrating events, will deepen your analysis.

Style and Mechanics

There are frequent grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors that need editing:

"societies rules" → *society's rules*
"expectable" → *acceptable*
"catigory" → *category*
"not being will to go about" → *not being willing to go about*
"His family way very proud" → *His family was very proud*
"beleive" → *believe*
"The one day" → *One day*

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool, then manually reviewing each flagged issue. Careful proofreading will make your argument much more persuasive, since grammatical errors can distract readers from your point.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, specific thesis statement in your introduction.
Tighten the connection between your general claims and your anecdote.
Add at least one more form of evidence (example, counterexample, or data).
Expand your conclusion to synthesize your argument, not just restate the story's outcome.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

You have a strong foundation with a vivid, relevant example—now it's a matter of building a more complete argumentative structure around it. Good luck with your revision!

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You're grappling with a genuinely interesting and important distinction: the difference between "equal work" (defined by task or difficulty) and "equal contribution" (defined by cultural and economic value). This is a sophisticated move, and your choice of child care work as an illustrative case is well-suited to make the abstract concrete. However, the essay's argument becomes tangled by the end, and several structural and stylistic issues weaken its persuasive power. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Clarify Your Thesis

The placeholder  in your opening sentence suggests you may still be finalizing your central claim—but as a reader, I need to know precisely what proposition you are examining or refuting from the very first sentence. Right now, I can infer you're pushing back against a simplistic "equal work, equal pay" formula, but the essay would be much stronger if you stated explicitly, early on, what you ultimately want the reader to conclude. Is your thesis that child care work is undervalued because of cultural bias? That economic and status-based valuation systems are circular and self-reinforcing? That government or employer intervention is necessary despite being imperfect? Right now, elements of all three appear, but none is fully committed to.

Resolve the Circularity in Your Argument

Your concluding paragraph identifies what you call an "irony": child care workers won't be valued until they're paid more, but they won't be paid more until they're valued. This is a genuinely important insight—but as it stands, it reads as if the essay is conceding defeat rather than offering a resolution or a path forward. If circularity *is* your point, make that argument explicit and use it to justify a specific conclusion (eg, "this circularity is precisely why external intervention, imperfect as it is, becomes necessary"). If you don't intend to end on such an ambivalent note, consider revising the conclusion to state clearly what should be done, or what your analysis reveals about how value is assigned in a market economy.

Strengthen the Logical Connections

Some of your key claims move quickly past the reasoning that would make them convincing. For example, you assert that a diverse, motivated workforce is impossible without properly valuing child care—and therefore there is an "undeniable economic connection" between child care and national economic strength. This is a compelling claim, but you state it rather than build it. Walk the reader through the logic step by step: without affordable, valued child care, which workers are excluded from the labor force? What does that cost the economy? Are there statistics on labor force participation rates for parents (especially mothers) tied to child care access or cost? Concrete evidence here would transform an assertion into an argument.

Add Evidence

Your essay currently relies entirely on general claims ("Americans profess to value children," "it is not difficult to conclude that child care workers are underpaid") without any supporting data. Even brief, specific evidence—median wages for child care workers compared to other occupations, statistics on workforce participation, or examples of media debates about child care policy—would substantially strengthen your credibility and give the reader something concrete to evaluate.

Tighten Style and Correct Mechanical Errors

A few sentence-level issues need attention:
"pereniel" should be "perennial"
"man with family responsibilities" should be "men"
The long opening sentence is difficult to parse; consider breaking it into two sentences for clarity.
Phrases like "It is difficult to imagine," "it is not difficult to conclude," and "it is tempting to conclude" are hedging constructions that weaken your authorial voice. Where you have evidence and reasoning to support a claim, state it directly rather than framing it as merely "not difficult to conclude."

Develop the Policy Dimension

You raise the question of employer or government involvement in child care but immediately dismiss it as "may not be the ideal solution" without explanation. If this is a live issue in your argument, it deserves development: what specifically is imperfect about such intervention? What alternatives exist? If it's tangential to your main point, consider cutting it to maintain focus.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis explicitly and early.
Decide whether your ending is meant to resolve the argument or complicate it—and make that choice clear to the reader.
Build out the logical steps connecting child care value to economic strength.
Incorporate at least some concrete evidence or data.
Edit for grammar, and reduce hedging language.

You have the seeds of a compelling essay about how cultural values are inseparable from (and sometimes circularly dependent on) economic valuation. Sharpening the thesis and reasoning will make that insight land with much greater force.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback on "The Value of Home-Making Service"

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a genuinely interesting and timely question—how society assigns value to labor—and it does so with an accessible, conversational voice. You raise a compelling problem (the disconnect between social contribution and financial compensation) but the essay would benefit from tightening the logical connection between your thesis and your conclusion, and from developing your proposed solution more fully.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your introduction promises that "some sort of compensation should be made for 'home-making service.'" This is a strong, debatable claim. However, your conclusion doesn't quite deliver on it. Instead of proposing actual compensation for homemaking, you suggest issuing the *existing* paycheck to the couple rather than the individual. This is a meaningful idea, but it's a different argument than the one you set up—it's about *symbolic recognition of partnership*, not about *creating new financial value for unpaid labor*. The family's total income doesn't change; only the name on the check does.

I'd encourage you to decide which argument you actually want to make, and revise your thesis to match:
If you want to argue for genuine compensation, you need to grapple with *who pays* (the state? the working spouse? employers?) and how much—your $50,000 figure for a neurosurgeon-equivalent salary raises this question but doesn't resolve it.
If you want to argue for the joint-paycheck solution, your thesis should be reframed around symbolic recognition rather than economic compensation, and you should explain more precisely how this symbolic gesture translates into real social change.

Right now, the essay's final paragraph reads as somewhat tentative ("Perhaps this is a solution worth its salt")—hedging language that undercuts your argument's authority. Once you've decided what you're claiming, commit to it more firmly.

Evidence and Support

Your claim that "many of the ills of society in the U.S. today have been traced by study after study to the lack of firm guidance and support from the family" is doing a lot of work in your argument, but it's unsupported. Since this claim underpins your assertion that homemaking has immense societal value, it needs at least one specific citation or example. Without it, a skeptical reader may see this as an assumption rather than an argued point.

Similarly, you could strengthen your case by addressing counterarguments directly. For instance:
Some might argue that homemaking is a personal choice, not a service to "society" in the way a doctor's labor is—how would you respond?
Others might point out practical difficulties in valuing or compensating homemaking (how do you measure "quality" of parenting? Would compensation create perverse incentives?).

Engaging with these objections would make your argument more persuasive and show that you've tested your position against opposing views.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay moves logically from defining the problem (paragraph 1) to establishing what "equal work" means in the marketplace (paragraph 2) to complicating that idea with the homemaking comparison (paragraph 3) to describing the stigma against homemakers (paragraph 4) to proposing a solution (paragraph 5). This is a sound structure. However, paragraph 5 feels rushed relative to the buildup in paragraphs 1–4. Since your solution is the payoff of the whole essay, it deserves at least as much space as your problem statement—consider expanding it into two paragraphs: one exploring the mechanics/practicality of the joint-paycheck idea, and one addressing objections or alternative solutions (eg, direct government subsidies for at-home parents, tax credits, Social Security credit for homemaking years).

Style and Word Choice

"Doctors, lawyers and Indian chiefs" is a dated idiomatic phrase that may read as flippant or even mildly offensive to some readers; consider replacing it with a more neutral list of professions.
There are a few small grammatical slips to proofread for: "an neurosurgeon" should be "a neurosurgeon"; "in so immense" should be "is so immense."
Your use of rhetorical questions ("But what is 'equal work'?" "How about making the paycheck out to the couple...?") works well to engage the reader and mark transitions—keep this technique, but be sure each question is followed by a clear, direct answer rather than another question or a hedge.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Align your thesis and conclusion so they're arguing the same claim.
Support the "ills of society" claim with a specific source.
Expand your proposed solution and address at least one counterargument.
Revise the "Indian chiefs" phrase and proofread for small grammatical errors.

With these adjustments, this essay could move from a thoughtful reflection to a tightly argued, persuasive piece.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: "Money as the Root of All Evil"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious philosophical question with genuine intellectual energy. The opening line is a strong hook, and your instinct to interrogate the *logic* of the maxim—rather than simply agreeing or disagreeing with it—shows sophisticated thinking. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of philosophical musings in progress than a fully realized argument. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Clarify and Stabilize Your Thesis

Your central claim seems to be that the saying "money is the root of all evil" is meaningless or impractical because desire (and evil) cannot be measured objectively against wealth. This is a good, arguable thesis—but it gets lost. By the second paragraph, you seem to shift toward examining whether wealth correlates with *happiness* and *virtue*, and by the third paragraph you're discussing whether desire for money and desire for virtue are "compatible." These are related but distinct questions. Right now, the essay tries to hold all of them simultaneously, and the reader loses track of which claim you are actually defending.

Revision suggestion: State your thesis explicitly at the end of the first paragraph, then make sure each subsequent paragraph visibly serves *that* claim rather than drifting into adjacent territory.

Interrogate Your Own Definitions

You introduce two significant, load-bearing definitions almost in passing:

Happiness = "a lack of desire for further money, a contentedness with all that one has"
Virtue = "the counterpart of happiness"

These are provocative claims, but you assert them rather than argue for them. A skeptical reader will immediately ask: *Why should we accept that happiness is defined this way? Why should virtue be equated with happiness at all?* These are exactly the kind of assumptions your essay's method (skeptical of unexamined equivalences) should be most suspicious of. Right now, you're demanding rigor from the maxim you're critiquing while allowing yourself looser definitions.

Revision suggestion: Either defend these definitions more carefully (why is contentment the same as an absence of desire for money? why must virtue mirror happiness?) or flag them explicitly as working hypotheses you intend to test and possibly overturn—which seems to be what happens in paragraph 3.

Resolve or Acknowledge the Self-Contradiction

Paragraph 2 tentatively suggests wealth may correlate with virtue (via contentment). Paragraph 3 then argues that only "serendipitously rich" people could be virtuous, implying the two desires are incompatible for anyone who actively pursues wealth. This is an interesting philosophical tension—but the essay doesn't tell the reader that it's a tension. It reads as if you changed your mind mid-argument without noticing.

Revision suggestion: Make this pivot explicit. Something like: "This account, however, immediately runs into a problem…" would signal to the reader that you're deliberately complicating your own claim, which is a legitimate and even sophisticated move—if it's *framed* as one.

Fill the Gap Marked ""

There is a placeholder left in your draft where a key transitional idea should be. This is presumably an oversight, but it leaves a structural hole exactly where your essay needs to pivot toward its concluding claim about history. Right now, "A truer statement" has no antecedent—we don't know what statement it's being compared to. This needs to be filled in with actual content, likely a restatement of the "money is the root of all evil" maxim you're implicitly replacing.

Supply Concrete Evidence

Your strongest claim—"it is not subtle argument, but the unfolding of history that makes desire such an elusive... measuring stick"—is asserted but never demonstrated. An argumentative essay invoking "history" needs at least one or two actual historical examples (of wealthy oppressors, wealthy saviors, or both) to make this claim persuasive rather than merely gestural. Right now the essay's most important evidentiary move is missing its evidence.

Proofread Carefully

There are several typos and grammatical slips that undercut the essay's credibility: "privelaged," "acruement," "wheighed," "serrendipitously," "It is not subtle argument" (missing "a"), and inconsistent verb tenses. Given how abstract and demanding your prose already is, clean mechanics will help readers follow your reasoning rather than getting distracted by errors.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write one clear thesis sentence and test every paragraph against it.
Defend or reframe your definitions of happiness and virtue.
Make the pivot in paragraph 3 explicit rather than implicit.
Fill the placeholder with real content connecting to your conclusion.
Add at least one concrete historical example.
Proofread thoroughly.

You have real philosophical instincts here—the next draft should focus on making your argument's *structure* as rigorous as the skepticism you're applying to the maxim itself.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: Marx's Principle and Western Egalitarianism

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious philosophical question—whether Marx's egalitarian principle can function in a Western, individualistic society—and you raise some genuinely interesting tensions (family loyalty vs. broader social obligation, individual freedom vs. collective good, relativism vs. shared values). However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected reflections than a tightly argued piece. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Fill in the Missing Quotations

This is the most urgent issue: your essay opens with a Marx quotation marked "" that never appears, and a New Testament reference and a definition of "giving back" are also left blank. Since your entire argument depends on responding to *specific* claims, the reader cannot evaluate your reasoning without seeing the actual text you're analyzing. Before revising anything else, insert the actual quotations (likely "from each according to his ability, to each according to his need") and cite them properly.

Sharpen Your Thesis

Your final line—"The concept provided is an admirable goal but one whose ends will unlikely be achieved in any western culture"—functions as your thesis, but it appears only at the very end. Readers need to know your argument's destination much earlier. Consider revising your introduction to state clearly: *Marx's egalitarian principle, though embedded in Western institutions like family and religion, cannot be fully realized in modern Western society because of its commitment to individualism and moral relativism.* This gives your essay a spine that each paragraph can visibly support.

Reconsider the Logical Structure

Right now the essay moves: family → religion → problems of defining contribution → individual freedom → relativism → conclusion. The connections between these moves are implicit rather than argued. Ask yourself:

Family paragraph: You claim the family is inherently egalitarian because parents nurture children. But is parental care actually an instance of Marx's principle ("to each according to need"), or is it a different phenomenon (kin altruism, biological instinct)? You need to explicitly justify why family nurturing counts as evidence for your broader claim, rather than asserting it by example alone.

Religion paragraph: You assert Christianity reinforces egalitarian norms, but then in the next paragraph you pivot to critiquing "giving back" as monetary generosity. The transition is abrupt—are you saying religion supports Marx's principle, or that the *distortion* of religious values into monetary terms undermines it? Clarify this pivot explicitly.

The "who defines contribution" paragraph: This is your strongest, most original section—it raises a real philosophical problem. But it needs to connect back to your thesis more directly: does this problem show that egalitarian principles are *unenforceable*, *unjust*, or merely *ambiguous*? Right now you list good questions but don't draw a conclusion from them.

Individualism and relativism paragraphs: These feel like separate essays. Consider merging them, since both ultimately argue that Western individualism resists imposed collective norms. Show explicitly how relativism follows from individualism, rather than treating them as two independent "problems."

Strengthen Evidence and Development

Several claims are asserted without support:
"This norm is deeply entrenched in western ideology" — Which norm? Support this historically or textually.
"This tension has been recognized by philosophers, poets & politicians throughout the history of western civilization" — Name one or two. Even a single concrete reference (eg, Rousseau, de Tocqueville, or a specific policy debate) would strengthen your credibility considerably.
The claim that religious and non-religious people alike find "comfort" in either God's reward or "secular, intrinsic values" needs unpacking—what are these secular values, specifically?

Editing and Clarity

There are several typos and unclear phrasings to fix:
"on e would understand" → "one would understand"
"who it to define" → "who is to define"
"learned primarily form the family" → "from the family"
"Absent a deviation from our general nature to propagate..." is a very dense sentence—consider breaking it into two for clarity.
"Society also has social institutions & practices in order which preserve" is grammatically tangled; try: "Society also maintains institutions and practices that preserve the egalitarian concept."

Avoid ampersands (&) in formal academic prose—write out "and."

Suggested Next Steps

Insert and properly cite all quotations.
Write a clear thesis statement placed in your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one explicitly builds toward that thesis, with transitions that state *why* one point follows another.
Add at least two or three concrete examples or citations (historical, textual, or philosophical) to support your broader claims.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice.

You have real philosophical instincts here—particularly in questioning how "contribution" and "reward" should be measured. With a clearer structure and stronger evidence, this could become a compelling essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an engaging question with a clever central metaphor—treating crime as a business with costs and benefits. This economic framing is a strong organizing device, and your breakdown of different "costs" (capital, time, health, legal) is logical and easy to follow. However, the essay would benefit from a sharper thesis, more substantive evidence, and a more decisive conclusion.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your central claim is somewhat buried. You open with a definitional question ("what is meant by 'pays'") and spend the whole essay elaborating on that definition, but you never clearly state what you conclude. Is your argument that crime *rarely* pays once all costs are factored in? That it *depends* on circumstances? Your final paragraph gestures toward "in some cases it may," but this is vague and arrives too late to feel like a genuine thesis rather than an afterthought.

Suggestion: State your position explicitly in the introduction. For example: "This essay argues that when all forms of cost—financial, physical, and psychological—are accounted for, crime rarely pays in any sustainable sense, though the calculus varies based on a criminal's alternative economic opportunities." This gives your reader a claim to test against your evidence.

Structure and Organization

Your paragraph-by-paragraph breakdown of loss types (capital/time, equipment, health, legal) is a reasonable structure, but the essay reads more like a taxonomy than an argument building toward a conclusion. Each paragraph introduces a new category without clearly connecting back to your thesis or building cumulatively toward it.

Suggestion: After introducing each type of cost, explicitly tie it back to your central claim. For instance, after discussing health costs, you might add a sentence like: "This cost is often invisible in popular narratives about crime, which is why superficial calculations of 'pay' are misleading."

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's weakest area. Your only concrete example is the hypothetical jewelry store theft in the introduction, and after that, your claims become increasingly abstract and unsupported ("Many times violence is a part of crime," "Very few criminals have worker's compensation"). These are plausible assertions, but they read as speculation rather than argument.

Suggestion: Bring in real evidence. Criminology has produced substantial research on this exact question—Steven Levitt's famous study of gang finances (used in *Freakonomics*) found that street-level drug dealers often earn close to minimum wage once risk is factored in. Citing studies like this, or statistics on recidivism, incarceration costs, or violence rates among criminal populations, would transform your essay from a thought experiment into a persuasive academic argument. Even one or two well-chosen sources would substantially strengthen your credibility.

The Business Analogy

The comparison between criminals and small businessmen is creative and worth developing further, but it currently remains at the level of assertion. You mention that "larger criminal corporations will always seek to drive him out of business" without explaining what this means or providing an example (organized crime syndicates undercutting independent operators, for instance). If you want to use this analogy as a structural backbone, follow through on it more rigorously—perhaps by systematically comparing categories of business risk (startup costs, competition, liability, taxation) to their criminal equivalents throughout the essay, rather than introducing it late.

Style and Mechanics

"Equiptment" should be "equipment"—proofread carefully for spelling.
Watch for informal phrasing suited to spoken argument but not academic prose: "in the hole," "hideout hotel room." These are vivid, but consider whether they fit the register you're aiming for.
The comparative "does better the legitimate businessman" is grammatically incomplete; it needs "than" ("does better than the legitimate businessman").

The Conclusion

The final line—"no taxes"—is witty, but as the last note of an argumentative essay, it undercuts the seriousness of your analysis. Humor can work in academic writing, but placing your punchiest, most memorable line at the very end risks making readers remember the joke rather than your argument. Consider either developing this point more seriously (eg, discussing how criminal income evades taxation but also lacks legal protections, contributing to your overall cost-benefit picture) or moving the humor earlier and ending with a firmer restatement of your thesis.

Priority Revisions

Write a clear, specific thesis statement in your introduction.
Incorporate at least two or three concrete pieces of evidence (studies, statistics, or documented cases) to support your claims about violence, health costs, or financial outcomes.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs so each cost-category explicitly advances your argument.
Revise the conclusion to end on your strongest analytical point rather than a punchline.

With these changes, this essay could move from an interesting speculative exercise to a well-supported piece of argumentative writing.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0008.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: The Love of Money vs. Money Itself

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting philosophical distinction—between money as a neutral tool and the *love* of money as a corrupting force. This is a promising thesis with real intellectual substance. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: establishing context and thesis clarity, strengthening evidence, and polishing style and mechanics.

Thesis and Argument Development

The essay opens with "This idea is completely erroneous," but readers have no idea what "idea" you're referring to. You seem to be responding to a specific claim (likely "money is the root of all evil"), but you never state this claim explicitly before refuting it. Begin by clearly articulating the position you're arguing against, ideally in your first sentence, so readers understand the debate before you take a side.

Your core argument—that money is a neutral instrument while the *love* of money is destructive—is a nuanced and defensible claim. However, you assert this distinction several times without ever fully exploring *why* it matters philosophically or practically. Consider adding a paragraph that examines what makes love/desire different from the object itself. Is this a broader pattern (eg, the same could be said of power, fame, or other desirable things)? Exploring this would deepen your analysis beyond restating the claim.

Structure and Organization

The essay lacks clear organizational signposts. Ideas repeat rather than build:
You state money is useful (paragraphs 2-3)
You state love of money is different (paragraph 4)
You give examples (paragraphs 5-7)
You return to distinguishing money from love of money (paragraph 8)
You give more examples (paragraph 9)
You restate the thesis (paragraph 10)

This circular structure weakens the sense of progression. Consider reorganizing using a clear structure: (1) State the claim you're refuting, (2) Define money's legitimate uses with specific support, (3) Define "love of money" and how it differs, (4) Provide developed examples/evidence, (5) Address a counterargument, (6) Conclude with synthesis rather than repetition.

Evidence and Development

Your examples remain abstract and unsupported: "How many times have we seen on the evening news a family being broken apart..." This is a rhetorical question, not evidence. Argumentative essays are strengthened by *specific* examples—a named case, a statistic, a historical event, or a citation from economics, psychology, or philosophy (Aristotle, or even the biblical source of this phrase, offer rich material).

Similarly, your child development example ("a 4-5 year-old realizes the power that money brings") is asserted without support. Is this based on developmental psychology research? An anecdote? Without grounding, these claims feel like unsupported generalizations rather than persuasive evidence.

Revision suggestion: Choose two or three concrete examples (a specific corporate scandal, a documented case of family conflict over inheritance, or a psychological study on materialism) and develop them with detail rather than listing multiple vague scenarios.

Counterargument

The essay would be stronger with an explicit counterargument. For instance, some might argue that money *itself* creates systemic inequality regardless of individual "love" for it—that the love/money distinction lets economic systems off the hook. Address this challenge directly to make your argument more robust and show you've considered opposing views.

Style and Mechanics

Several sentence-level issues need attention:

Word choice/typos: "capitivates" → captivates; "It can be sued for good" → used; "it's needs" → its needs. Proofread carefully for these errors.

Repetition: The phrase "love of money" appears close to a dozen times. Vary your language—consider synonyms like "greed," "avarice," "excessive desire for wealth," or "materialism."

Sentence fragments/run-ons: "is money the root of all evil? No." This should be integrated into a complete sentence or properly punctuated as a rhetorical question within your prose, not left as an isolated fragment.

Tone consistency: Phrases like "wreaks havoc" and "captivates their minds" are vivid, but the overall register shifts between formal argument and informal, sermon-like language ("desperate desire," "love affair with money"). Decide on a consistent academic tone.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph restates the thesis effectively but doesn't move the argument forward with new insight or a broader implication. Consider ending with a reflection on what your argument implies for how individuals or societies *should* relate to wealth—this gives your essay a forward-looking conclusion rather than mere repetition.

Priority Revisions
Clearly state the opposing claim at the outset.
Add specific, well-developed evidence (not rhetorical questions).
Address a counterargument.
Proofread for typos and reduce repetitive phrasing.
Strengthen the conclusion with a forward-looking insight.

Feedback on "Crime Does Not Pay" Essay

Overall Impression

This is a promising essay with an engaging premise: testing a common cultural saying against evidence. Your instinct to interrogate a cliché that people repeat without examining is exactly the kind of critical thinking academic writing rewards. The essay has real strengths—an accessible voice, a provocative organizing question, and several genuinely interesting examples. However, the argument needs tightening in several areas: thesis clarity, logical consistency, evidence quality, and the handling of a key conceptual shift midway through.

Specific Issues to Address

The bracketed placeholder. Your submission includes "" instead of the actual phrase you're analyzing. Make sure the final draft states the phrase explicitly and consistently—likely "crime does not pay"—since this is your entire subject.

Sharpen your thesis and forecast your argument. Currently the thesis appears only in the final line ("the phrase is better considered as wishful thinking than as a truism"). Consider moving a version of this claim earlier, perhaps at the end of your second paragraph, and briefly forecasting the two or three lines of evidence you'll use (individual criminals, and the industry that profits from crime). This will help readers see the essay's structure and stakes from the outset.

Distinguish two different senses of "pays." This is the essay's most significant conceptual issue. Your first two paragraphs and your discussion of murder, Milken, and Nixon are about whether *the criminal* profits. Your final substantive paragraph, however, shifts to whether *the incarceration industry* profits from crime—a different and equally interesting claim, but a different one. Right now the transition ("In addition to having crime pay for some well-known criminals...") blurs these two arguments together as if they were the same point. I'd suggest explicitly naming this shift: something like, "Even when crime does not pay the criminal, it may pay handsomely for the industries built around punishing crime." This turns a potential weakness into a deliberate, sophisticated move—showing that "who profits" is more complicated than the platitude suggests.

Strengthen and verify your evidence.
The reference to "a working class man" and "a former politician" who was incarcerated for "$23.00 per month" needs a source or should be framed more clearly as illustrative anecdote rather than fact.
"I recently read that the average amount of time a convicted murderer spends in prison is 15 years" is vague sourcing. For an academic essay, find and cite the actual study or statistic (eg, Bureau of Justice Statistics). This claim is central to your argument about murder being "cost-effective," so its credibility matters.
Check the spelling: it's Michael Milken, not "Milliken." Also verify the details of his case (restitution amount, sentence, and current occupation) before publishing, since factual errors undermine reader trust.
Your Nixon paragraph is compelling but could be more precise. You call him "certainly a criminal at least in the sense of having violated public trust," which hedges in an interesting way—but you might say more about *why* his case counts as crime "paying off" (a pardon, restored public status) versus simply being a case of political rehabilitation. Spell out the connection to your reinforcement argument explicitly.

Develop the "reinforcement" concept. Your claim that "behavior that is not reinforced in some way will not persist" is a nice pivot—it introduces a psychological/behaviorist framework that could organize the whole essay. But you state it and then move directly into examples without unpacking it. Consider a sentence or two explaining what reinforcement means in this context (financial gain, social status, reduced punishment relative to reward) so readers understand you're using a specific analytic lens, not just asserting an opinion.

The CHINS/Indiana paragraph needs a clearer connection back to your thesis. This paragraph shifts from individual criminals to the institutional economics of incarceration, which is valuable, but the final sentences (about local jobs and economic stimulus) trail off without connecting explicitly to "crime paying." Add a sentence making clear that this is evidence for your *industry* argument specifically.

Conclusion. Your ending is brief and could do more work. Consider returning to the opening image (the working-class man reciting the phrase with satisfaction) and noting the irony: he may be reassured by the phrase precisely because the systems and institutions around him profit from its being repeated, even while quietly benefiting from crime themselves.

Suggested Revision Priorities
Restate the actual phrase and clarify your thesis early.
Verify and properly cite factual claims (Milken's name, the 15-year statistic).
Explicitly separate and signal the shift from "criminal profits" to "industry profits."
Briefly unpack the reinforcement concept.
Strengthen your conclusion by returning to the opening scenario.

This essay has a strong analytical core—keep pushing on the tension between individual and institutional profit, as that's your most original contribution.

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this thoughtful reflection on effort, reward, and motivation. You're clearly engaging with an interesting philosophical question, and your writing shows genuine conviction. Below are some observations to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your central claim—that financial reward for individual effort is essential to a functioning society—doesn't fully emerge until the end of your first paragraph, and even there it's somewhat buried in a longer sentence about historical context. Consider stating your thesis more directly and placing it where readers can locate it easily. Ask yourself: what is the *one* claim I want readers to accept by the end of this essay? Right now, the essay drifts between several related but distinct ideas: (1) reward has always been tied to effort, (2) technology has changed how we work but not this basic principle, and (3) equal outcomes regardless of effort would be harmful. Each of these could be a supporting point, but they need to be explicitly connected back to your thesis rather than left to imply the connection.

Structure and Paragraph Unity

Your paragraphs would benefit from clearer topic sentences that signal how each one supports your argument. For example, the second paragraph describes subsistence living in "earlier times," but it isn't clear what specific point about *today's* reward systems this history is meant to prove. Try adding a sentence that bridges the historical example to your present-day argument—something like: "This principle—that reward follows effort—has not disappeared with modern convenience; it has simply changed form."

The fourth paragraph, which imagines a society of complete equality, is your most provocative section, but it moves very quickly from a hypothetical premise to dramatic consequences ("the world would become divided or even destroyed"). This is a significant logical leap. Even if you intend this as a thought experiment rather than a literal prediction, you should walk the reader through the reasoning more carefully: *why* would equal distribution regardless of effort lead to jealousy, laziness, and stagnation? Consider drawing on a specific historical or economic example (there is substantial writing on this from economists, sociologists, or even historical case studies of planned economies) rather than relying on speculation alone. This would give your argument much more credibility.

Evidence and Counterargument

Right now, the essay relies entirely on general assertions and hypothetical scenarios. A stronger version would include at least one concrete example—historical, economic, or personal—to ground your claims. You might also strengthen your argument by acknowledging a counterargument: some readers will point out that not all effort is equally rewarded (due to luck, discrimination, unequal starting points, or the undervaluing of certain kinds of labor, like caregiving). Addressing this doesn't weaken your argument—it shows you've considered complexity, and lets you refine your claim (for example, arguing that effort *should* be rewarded even if the current system does this imperfectly).

Conclusion

Your final paragraph raises an intriguing idea—that reward isn't really "about money" but about meeting needs and wants—but this feels like a new argument rather than a conclusion. If this point is important to your overall thesis, it deserves development earlier in the essay, not introduced in the final lines. A strong conclusion should return to your central claim and show how the evidence you've presented supports it, rather than opening a new line of thought.

Style and Mechanics

There are several grammatical issues to address in revision:
"should be financially reward" → "should be financially rewarded"
"We have many machines to held us meet" → "help us meet"
"Competitiveness and jealously" → "jealousy"
Watch for comma splices, eg, "with little or no effort on our part, it would appear."

Additionally, some phrasing is quite informal for an academic essay ("Let's imagine," "I would venture to guess," multiple exclamation points). While a personal voice can be effective, consider whether a more measured, evidence-based tone would make your argument more persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify and foreground your thesis early in the essay.
Strengthen the logical connections between your historical examples and your present-day argument.
Support your central hypothetical (paragraph 4) with concrete evidence rather than speculation.
Consider addressing a counterargument to add nuance and credibility.
Revise the conclusion so it reinforces your thesis rather than introducing new ideas.
Proofread carefully for grammar and tone consistency.

You have a clear point of view and real conviction here—the next step is to build an argumentative scaffold that lets your reasoning, not just your assertions, carry the reader to your conclusion. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: Compensation and Contribution to Society

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and difficult question—whether pay should (or even can) correspond to one's "contribution to society." You raise several thought-provoking examples, particularly the contrast between the migrant worker and the wealthy tipper, and the observation that people simultaneously believe they are underpaid while accepting others' high prices as "fair." These are the seeds of a strong argument. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a unified argument. Below are some suggestions for revision.

Clarify and Sharpen Your Thesis

Your first paragraph gestures toward a thesis ("contribution to society is subjective and therefore impossible to compensate equally"), but this claim is stated abruptly and never fully unpacked. What exactly does "compensate equally" mean here—equal pay for equal contribution, or equal fairness in how contribution is judged? Consider rewriting your opening paragraph so that it clearly previews the reasoning you will use to support this claim (eg, "because judgments of value differ by employer, by culture, and by ideology, no objective standard of 'fair' compensation can exist"). Right now, a reader has to work hard to reconstruct your argument after finishing the essay; the thesis should orient the reader from the start.

Strengthen the Historical Example

The reference to 1920s America and "great authors" who criticized the wealthy is promising but underdeveloped. Who are these authors? (F. Scott Fitzgerald seems implied, but you should name him and perhaps cite a specific text like *The Great Gatsby*.) Without specific evidence—a quotation, a summary of an author's argument, a critic's interpretation—this paragraph functions as an assertion rather than support. Academic essays need concrete, citable evidence, especially when invoking historical or literary context. Consider also explaining *why* these authors viewed idle wealth as parasitic, and connect that reasoning explicitly back to your thesis about subjective contribution.

Improve Paragraph Coherence and Transitions

Each paragraph introduces a new angle—1920s investors, inherited wealth, migrant workers, "Democracy," doctors—but the connections between them are implicit rather than argued. For example, the transition from investors "living as parasites" to migrant workers is abrupt; the link seems to be "unfairness in compensation," but you should state this explicitly. Similarly, the paragraph invoking "feelings about Democracy" needs more explanation: what do democratic values have to do with judgments about who deserves money? Right now this feels like a claim dropped into the essay without support. Consider adding a topic sentence to each paragraph that explicitly ties it back to your central claim, and use transitional phrases ("This raises the question of...", "Yet this view assumes...") to guide the reader through your logic.

Develop Your Strongest Example More Fully

The image of a migrant worker earning less in a day than a wealthy person spends on a lunchtime tip is vivid and effective—arguably the strongest moment in the essay. But you move past it too quickly. Spend more time analyzing what this example reveals about the *incommensurability* of contribution and compensation. Does it suggest that markets, not moral judgments about contribution, actually determine pay? This point deserves its own well-developed paragraph rather than a passing mention.

Reconsider the Ending

Your concluding paragraph introduces a new example (doctors' fees) that could be powerful, but it arrives too late and isn't fully connected to the rest of the essay. A conclusion should synthesize your argument, not introduce fresh evidence. Consider moving the doctors' example earlier, perhaps alongside your discussion of subjective valuation, and using your final paragraph purely to restate and extend your thesis—perhaps by suggesting what implications follow from the impossibility of equal compensation (should we abandon fairness as a goal? redefine it?).

Proofread Carefully

There are several distracting errors that a careful proofreading pass would catch:
"in American" → "in America"
"Democracry" → "Democracy"
"The answer this question lies" → "The answer to this question lies"
"A migrant work" → "A migrant worker"
"too money" → "too much money"
Inconsistent verb tense throughout (mixing past and present when discussing the 1920s and general claims)

Summary

Your essay has real potential: you're grappling with a genuinely complex ethical and economic question, and your examples show good instincts. The main task in revision is to make your argument's structure visible—state your thesis clearly, give each paragraph a clear job in relation to that thesis, support your historical/literary claims with actual citations, and build toward a conclusion that deepens rather than merely repeats your opening claim. Focus especially on paragraphs 2 and 4, which currently feel the most disconnected from your central argument.

Feedback on "Crime Pays" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a provocative, counterintuitive thesis, and you support it with lively, memorable examples. Your writing has personality and confidence. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: argumentative logic, organization/clarity, and mechanics. Let me address each.

Logical Structure

Your central problem is that you conflate several different phenomena under the umbrella of "crime pays":

Historical reputation vs. financial profit: The Boston Tea Party and Benedict Arnold examples show that history's *moral judgment* of an act can shift, not that the perpetrators *profited financially*. This is a different claim than your thesis suggests. Are you arguing that crime pays financially, or that crime can be socially rehabilitated over time? These need separate arguments.

Celebrity/notoriety vs. crime itself: Divine Brown, Buttafuco, and O.J. Simpson didn't profit from *committing* crimes—they profited from *media attention* to their crimes. This is worth exploring, but you should acknowledge the distinction: is it crime that pays, or is it America's media culture that monetizes scandal? This complicates your thesis in an interesting way you should address directly rather than gloss over.

Prison benefits vs. profit: Free room, board, and medical care are not "profit" in the way readers will expect—they're subsistence, and most Americans would not consider prison a desirable trade-off. This example actually undercuts your argument; consider removing it or reframing your claim more carefully.

Revision suggestion: Consider narrowing your thesis. Rather than "crime pays" in a blanket sense, you might argue something like: "American culture's fascination with notoriety often converts criminal infamy into financial opportunity, revealing an uncomfortable truth about our justice and media systems." This is a more defensible, sophisticated claim.

Organization

Your three body paragraphs are organized by category (history, material gain, consequences/justice system), which is a reasonable structure. However:

The transition sentences are weak ("Finally, is the issue of capture and punishment" has a grammatical error and doesn't clearly connect to your argument).
Each paragraph would benefit from a clear topic sentence stating the specific claim, followed by evidence, followed by *analysis* explaining how the evidence proves the claim. Right now you have claims and evidence but limited analysis. For example, after mentioning autobiographies and movies-of-the-week, explain *why* this constitutes "crime paying"—walk your reader through the logic rather than assuming it's self-evident.
Your conclusion doesn't synthesize your three arguments—it restates the thesis without pulling together the distinct threads (history, money, justice system) into a unified insight.

Counterarguments

A strong essay on this topic needs to address the obvious objection: most criminals are *not* celebrities, do *not* get book deals, and *do* suffer real consequences (poverty, violence, death, family destruction). Your essay cherry-picks famous exceptions. Acknowledging this and explaining why your examples still matter (eg, "while most criminals face genuine hardship, the exceptions reveal something troubling about incentive structures in American media and justice") would make your argument far more credible and sophisticated.

Style and Tone

Your voice is engaging and sometimes witty ("if one has the right agent"), which serves you well. However:

The opening sentence ("Any upstanding citizen will avow...") sets up a straw man. Consider a more nuanced opening that doesn't put words in your opponent's mouth.
Some phrasing is imprecise: "crime is a recurrent theme" is vague—recurrent theme of *what*?
"National Enquirer brand of fame" is a nice phrase—more moments like this would strengthen your voice.

Mechanics (Please Proofread Carefully)

You have several errors that need correction:
"vents" → "events"
"Los Vegas" → "Las Vegas"
"speak-easies" (missing hyphen placement/spelling)
"tes." — stray text at the end, likely a copy-paste error
"No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money, if it were not why would people commit them" — this sentence has a subject-verb agreement issue and awkward logic (this is a comma splice and "them" doesn't have a clear antecedent)
"convicted killers autobiographies" needs an apostrophe: "convicted killers' autobiographies"
"Finally, is the issue of capture and punishment" is a sentence fragment/awkward construction—revise to "Finally, there is the issue of capture and punishment" or similar.

Priority Revisions

Narrow and clarify your thesis to something more precise and defensible.
Add analysis after each piece of evidence—don't just present examples, explain their significance.
Address counterarguments to strengthen credibility.
Proofread thoroughly for the errors listed above.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here—with tighter logic and cleaner prose, this could be a compelling essay.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for tackling a complex and often controversial topic. Your essay shows genuine engagement with the subject of feminism and women's roles, and you're clearly trying to work through a nuanced position. However, the essay currently struggles with several fundamental issues that need attention before this could be considered strong academic writing. Let me walk through the main areas for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your essay lacks a clear, consistent thesis. You seem to be arguing multiple things simultaneously: that feminism has helped women, that feminism has hurt women by imposing a single standard, that gender equality is impossible because of "nature," and that modernization has caused social harms (divorce, stress, neglected children). These claims are not woven together into a coherent argument—they often contradict one another. For instance, you say "women should be created equal" in your final line, but spend the rest of the essay arguing that biological difference makes equality impossible. Before revising further, write a single sentence that captures your central claim, and make sure every paragraph supports it.

Unsupported and Overgeneralized Claims

Many of your strongest claims have no evidence behind them. For example:
"Women just can't fight in a war very well" and "they are not strong enough" — this is a sweeping generalization presented as fact. Academic writing requires you to support claims like this with data, studies, or expert testimony—especially when the claim is contested.
"Divorce is at fifty percent and rising, women are having more heart attacks, being more stressful, and kids are being more neglected" — these statistics need citations, and more importantly, you haven't demonstrated that these trends are *caused* by feminism or modernization rather than correlated with them. This is a classic correlation/causation error.
"God would have created us all the same if this was not true" — this is a theological claim, not an argumentative one, and it won't be persuasive to readers who don't share your religious framework. In a secular academic essay, you'll need reasoning that doesn't depend on a specific faith position.

Revision strategy: For every generalization, ask yourself "How do I know this?" If you can't answer with evidence, either cut the claim or research it.

Tone and Word Choice

Phrases like "I felt that these people were crazy" undermine your credibility and shift the essay from analysis to personal reaction. Academic argument requires you to represent opposing views fairly and respond to them with reasoning, not dismissal. Consider revising to something like: "I disagree with this position because..." followed by actual reasons.

Addressing Counterarguments Fairly

You raise an interesting point about feminism not representing *all* women's preferences—this could be a genuinely compelling thesis if developed carefully. But you undercut it by immediately pivoting to claims about women's physical inferiority rather than exploring the more interesting tension: the idea that a movement advocating for choice might inadvertently pressure women toward one "correct" choice. This is a much stronger, more defensible argument than claims about combat readiness. I'd encourage you to build the essay around this idea instead.

Organization

The essay moves somewhat randomly between topics: workplace equality, combat roles, sexual harassment, promotions, divorce rates, and religion. Each paragraph should have one clear focus that connects back to your thesis. Consider an outline like:
Introduction with clear thesis
Historical context (briefly)
Your central argument (eg, feminism's blind spot regarding women who prefer traditional roles)
A counterargument and your response
Conclusion that reinforces—not contradicts—your thesis

Mechanics and Precision

There are several errors worth fixing:
"piers" should be "peers"
"verses" should be "versus"
"The are not strong enough" should be "They are not strong enough"
Watch comma splices and run-on sentences throughout (eg, "This makes it hard, because if they don't...")

Moving Forward

Your instinct that feminism is not monolithic and doesn't represent every woman's desires is worth developing—this is actually a point historians and scholars of feminism themselves debate (see discussions of "choice feminism" or intersectionality). I'd recommend doing some outside reading to find scholarly sources that engage this specific tension, then rebuilding your essay around a single, well-supported claim. Focus less on asserting what men and women "naturally" can or cannot do, and more on the specific, documented tension between feminist advocacy and individual women's autonomy to choose their own paths.

Revise with an eye toward: one thesis, evidence for every major claim, and a tone that persuades through reasoning rather than assertion.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Crime Does Not Pay"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting question—whether crime "pays" and how punishment escalates with severity—but it currently reads more like a personal reflection or story than an academic argument. The core idea (that criminal behavior escalates and consequences worsen accordingly) has potential, but it needs a clearer thesis, more structured reasoning, and academic evidence to support your claims. Below are specific areas to revise.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your opening line states a belief ("crime does not pay"), but the essay's actual argument seems to be something more specific: that criminal behavior often escalates through a psychological cycle, and that legal consequences scale with the severity/risk of the crime. This is a more precise and arguable claim than the general saying you start with. Consider revising your thesis to state this directly, for example: "Because criminal behavior tends to escalate un-checked, and because the justice system imposes harsher penalties as crimes become more dangerous, minor offenses can quickly snowball into life-altering consequences." Right now, readers have to infer your argument; state it explicitly in your introduction.

Structure and Development

The essay's structure is uneven. Paragraph 1 introduces the "cycle of escalation" idea using a personal anecdote and a childhood analogy. Paragraph 2 briefly asserts that punishment varies by severity, but doesn't develop this claim before pivoting to your own moral character ("having a 'weakness' for children"). Paragraph 3—by far the longest—tells an extended hypothetical story that only loosely connects to your original claims.

To improve structure:
Separate your two claims (crime escalates; punishment scales with harm) into distinct, well-developed paragraphs, each with its own topic sentence, explanation, and evidence.
Shorten the hypothetical story. Right now it consumes most of the essay and reads like narrative fiction rather than argumentative support. Trim it to 3–4 sentences that illustrate your point (robbery escalating to attempted murder/assault charges), and spend the space you save explaining *why* this illustrates your thesis.
Add a conclusion. The essay ends abruptly with "Something that was supposed to be so simple ended up to be a big mess." You need a paragraph that returns to your thesis and explains the broader significance of your argument.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's biggest weakness. Your support currently comes from:
Personal belief ("I was always told...")
An analogy to children testing boundaries
A hypothetical, invented story

Academic argumentative writing requires external evidence: criminology research on recidivism, sentencing guidelines, or case studies of real crimes that escalated in the way you describe. For instance, you could cite actual sentencing statutes showing how charges stack (robbery + weapons charge + assault), or research on why first-time offenders reoffend. This would transform your essay from an opinion piece into a persuasive, evidence-based argument. Even one or two credible sources would strengthen your credibility significantly.

Style and Tone

The essay's tone is very conversational—appropriate for a personal essay, but not for academic argumentation. Phrases like "a cool million," "run like hell," "guess what happens," and "You know what happens" create an informal, almost storytelling voice. Similarly, "I am a true believer" and "having a 'weakness' for children" insert personal identity into what should be an objective argument. For revision, consider:
Removing first-person anecdotes not directly tied to evidence.
Replacing casual phrases with precise, formal language.
Avoiding rhetorical asides that address the reader directly ("guess what happens... You know what happens").

Grammar and Mechanics

There are numerous errors that need correction in revision:
Spelling: "committs," "individuals," "convenient store" (should be "convenience store," used throughout), "whrn," "strats," "hurridly," "persistant," "deterent," "Its's"
Run-on/comma-splice sentences, especially in paragraph 1 ("With this attitude someone could definitely headed for destruction" — also a verb agreement error; should be "could definitely be headed").
Inconsistent verb tense, especially in the hypothetical story (shifts between present and past tense).

I'd recommend reading the essay aloud slowly, or using grammar-checking software, to catch these errors systematically.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, specific thesis statement.
Restructure into logically organized paragraphs, each supporting one claim.
Shorten the hypothetical narrative drastically; use it as illustration, not the essay's centerpiece.
Add academic/credible evidence (statistics, legal sources, research).
Revise tone to be more formal and objective.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

You have a genuinely interesting argument buried in here—now it needs a scholarly foundation and tighter organization to support it. Good luck with your revision!

Here is feedback on your essay:

Overall Impression

You've chosen a thought-provoking topic, and your central idea—that money itself is neutral but human attitudes toward it determine its moral valence—is a solid thesis worth developing. However, the essay currently reads more like a first draft of ideas than a fully developed argument. It needs a clearer structure, more specific evidence, and careful editing for mechanical errors. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Structure

Your strongest and most interesting claim appears in the second paragraph: "Money of itself is neither good nor bad. One's attitude about money is the deciding factor." This is actually a more sophisticated and defensible thesis than your opening line ("money is the root of all evil"), which is a cliché you then complicate. I'd suggest leading with your nuanced claim rather than burying it. Right now, the essay opens with an absolute claim, walks it back, and then arrives at your real argument mid-essay. Consider restructuring so your introduction previews the attitude-dependent thesis from the start, and each paragraph then builds evidence toward it.

Additionally, the essay lacks clear topic sentences that signal each paragraph's function. Paragraph 1 defines money and its power; paragraph 2 argues attitude determines money's moral effect; paragraph 3 discusses greed. These are good building blocks, but they need explicit transitions showing how they connect logically (eg, "Because attitude determines money's effect, one particularly destructive attitude is greed...").

Development and Evidence

This is the area needing the most work. Your claims are largely asserted rather than demonstrated. For example:

You list addictions (drugs, gambling, prostitution) but don't explain *how* money-seeking behavior in these areas illustrates your thesis. A brief example or scenario would help—even a hypothetical one ("Consider someone who...") or a real-world reference (a historical figure, a news story, a psychological study on materialism).
The claim that "social prestige" can drive addictive money-seeking is asserted but not illustrated. What does this look like? Conspicuous consumption? Status competition? Give the reader a concrete picture.
Your final paragraph on greed makes strong claims ("greedy people usually have one objective," "values and ethics play very little importance") but these are broad generalizations. Consider qualifying them ("can" rather than "usually," or acknowledging exceptions) and supporting them with an example—a corporate scandal, a literary character (Scrooge, Gatsby), or a philosophical/religious perspective on greed.

Academic essays are strengthened by engaging with a source, quotation, or concrete case rather than resting on assertions. Even one well-chosen example per paragraph would substantially improve credibility.

Logical Gaps

There's a slight tension between paragraph 1 and paragraph 2 that needs addressing. Paragraph 1 suggests money's power to grant status/acceptance "sound like positive benefits" but hints at a "darker side." Paragraph 2 then pivots to say money itself is neutral. You should clarify: is the danger in what money *represents* (status-seeking), or in the *attitude* toward acquiring it? Right now these feel like two separate arguments loosely connected. Tightening this connection—perhaps by explicitly stating that the desire for status/acceptance *becomes* dangerous only when it turns into an unhealthy attitude (obsession, addiction)—would strengthen your logic.

Conclusion

The essay ends abruptly with "Money does good or evil—depending on who has the money," which is a decent summary line but functions as a conclusion in one sentence. You need a proper concluding paragraph that: (a) restates your refined thesis, (b) briefly synthesizes your key points (definition of money as power, attitude as determinant, greed as case study), and (c) perhaps gestures toward broader implications—why does this distinction matter? What should readers take away in terms of how they view their own relationship to money?

"Ones's" should be "One's"
"in structure of a greedy persons life" needs rewording: "in the structure of a greedy person's life" (missing article, missing apostrophe)
The line "when the need for money becomes..." starts with a lowercase "when" after a semicolon-like pause—check punctuation and capitalization throughout.

The final paragraph ends without a period after "money," and lacks a strong concluding sentence.
