Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing such a personal and passionate piece of writing. You clearly have strong opinions grounded in lived experience, and that authenticity is a real strength. However, to make this into a more persuasive *academic* argument, there are several areas that need attention.

Clarify Your Thesis and Central Claim

Your essay makes two claims that sit in tension with each other: (1) feminism has produced many "noble" and necessary changes (equal pay, breaking the glass ceiling, respecting women's career choices), and (2) feminism is responsible for killing chivalry. Right now, the essay reads as if you're arguing both "feminism is good" and "feminism is bad," without clearly explaining *why* it's good in some respects and harmful in this specific one. Consider sharpening your thesis to something like: "While the feminist movement achieved important social and economic gains, it also produced an unintended consequence—confusion and anxiety around dating etiquette that has eroded traditional courtesies between men and women." This gives your reader a single, focused claim to track throughout the essay.

Distinguish Correlation from Causation

You attribute the decline of chivalry directly to feminism, but the essay doesn't establish that connection—it only asserts it. Between the 1960s and 1990s, many other social changes occurred (rising divorce rates, changing sexual norms, economic pressures requiring dual incomes, the sexual revolution, litigation culture around harassment). Feminism may be *one* factor, but presenting it as *the* cause is an oversimplification. Consider acknowledging these other contributing factors, or more carefully limiting your claim to something you can actually support—for instance, that certain interpretations or rhetoric within feminist discourse created ambiguity about traditional gestures.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Your primary evidence is personal experience and anecdotes from "several male friends and a significant other." This is valuable as illustrative material, but it cannot carry the full weight of your argument in an academic essay. Ask yourself: what would make this more persuasive to a skeptical reader? You might incorporate:
Sociological or psychological research on dating norms and gender expectations over time
Survey data on attitudes toward chivalry (many exist—Pew Research, YouGov, etc)
Scholarly or journalistic commentary on the "confusion" men report, to show this isn't just your personal circle's experience

Right now, phrases like "I say and conclude that this is bunk" replace argument with assertion. Persuasion requires showing *why* the reader should agree, not just stating your conclusion emphatically.

Address the Counterargument More Substantively

You gesture toward the feminist perspective (that gestures like opening doors can feel patronizing or reinforce inequality) but dismiss it quickly ("this is bunk") without genuinely engaging it. A stronger essay would take this counterargument seriously: Why might some women feel that traditional chivalry implies weakness or unequal status? Engaging honestly with this view—and then explaining why you still find value in traditional courtesies despite it—would make your position more credible and nuanced, rather than dismissive.

Watch Your Organization

The essay moves somewhat associatively: personal history → chivalry's decline → feminist gains → division of household labor → return to chivalry → direct address to "you men out there." Consider a clearer structure:
Introduce the tension (feminist gains vs. loss of chivalry)
Acknowledge and detail the legitimate achievements of feminism
Focus specifically on the dating/courtship issue with evidence
Address the counterargument
Conclude with your nuanced position

The paragraph on household division of labor, while interesting, is a tangent from your central argument about dating and chivalry. Either cut it or explicitly connect it back to your main claim.

Reconsider Tone and Voice

The shift into direct address at the end ("I'm not asking for the world from you men out there") breaks the essay's mode—it moves from analysis to something like a personal appeal or op-ed voice. This isn't necessarily wrong, but it should be an intentional rhetorical choice signaled earlier in the essay, not a sudden shift in the final lines. Also, informal phrases ("bunk," "hanging around this continent") suit a personal essay or blog post but undercut academic credibility. Consider whether your assignment calls for that register, and adjust accordingly.

Proofread for Consistency

Small issues—like "stigmatism" (should be "stigma") and inconsistent verb tense when moving between past and present—should be cleaned up in revision.

Overall: You have a genuine, debatable claim with real-world resonance. Sharpening the thesis, strengthening evidence beyond anecdote, and engaging more seriously with opposing views will transform this from a passionate personal reflection into a compelling academic argument.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting angle on a well-known saying, and you've identified several genuinely distinct costs of crime (financial, emotional, and social). This is a promising foundation, but the essay needs revision in several areas to strengthen its argument and prose. Below are the main issues, organized by priority.

Missing Placeholder Text

The most immediate issue is that your opening line contains an unfilled placeholder: "The phrase  has been repeated many times..." This suggests the essay was drafted from a template and the actual quotation (presumably "crime doesn't pay") was never inserted. Before doing anything else, fix this—your reader needs to know exactly what phrase you're examining from the very first sentence.

Thesis Statement

Your introduction gestures toward an idea—that crime "costs money"—but this claim is too narrow to encompass everything you go on to discuss (remorse, stigma, real estate values are not strictly about money). Consider crafting a thesis that unifies all four of your body paragraphs. For example: "While the phrase 'crime doesn't pay' is often used casually, it captures a deeper truth: crime imposes financial, emotional, and social costs not only on offenders but on their families, victims, and communities." This kind of explicit roadmap will help readers see how your paragraphs connect.

Paragraph Unity and Transitions

Right now, each paragraph reads as a separate mini-essay on a different cost of crime (incarceration costs, criminal remorse, real estate values, social stigma), but they aren't explicitly connected to one another or to your central claim. Two suggestions:

Add topic sentences that link back to your thesis. For instance, instead of starting paragraph 3 with "The moral aspect of crime for the criminal is also something to take into account," you might write something like, "Beyond the financial burden on taxpayers, crime exacts an emotional price on the criminal that no verdict can erase."
Use transitions between paragraphs. Phrases like "In addition to this financial toll..." or "Beyond its cost to the public purse..." would help readers move smoothly from one idea to the next.

Logical Clarity Within Paragraphs

A few claims need more careful reasoning:

In paragraph 2, you assert that incarceration costs are passed on to "the individual's family, friends and acquaintances in the form of tax dollars." This logic needs unpacking—why would a criminal's specific family and friends bear this cost more than any other taxpayer? If your point is that *all* taxpayers share this burden, say so directly.
In paragraph 3, the connection between "remorse" and the economic argument "crime doesn't pay" is unclear. Remorse is an emotional cost, not strictly a financial one. If you want to include this point, you may need to broaden your thesis (as suggested above) to explicitly cover non-monetary costs.
In paragraph 4, you argue that crime lowers real estate values, but the sentence structure obscures your point: "so even though an individual can try to add value to his or her real estate possessions in these areas the effect of crime will cause a direct loss of money for the law abiding citizen." Consider simplifying: "Even if a homeowner improves their property, a high local crime rate can still drive down its value."

Evidence and Support

Currently, every claim in the essay is asserted without support—no statistics, studies, examples, or even hypothetical scenarios are used to substantiate points like "the number of criminals increases so too will the tax money needed to house these individuals" or the claim about real estate values. Even in a short argumentative essay, some concrete evidence (a statistic about incarceration costs, a specific example of a neighborhood's property values declining, etc) would make your argument far more persuasive.

Conclusion

The essay ends somewhat abruptly after discussing the "label" of criminal, with no concluding paragraph that ties your points together or restates your thesis in light of the evidence presented. Add a conclusion that synthesizes your four costs (financial, emotional, social/real estate, and reputational) and returns to the meaning of the phrase you began with.

Proofreading

There are several typos and grammatical slips to correct:
"freinds" → friends
"acquantances" → acquaintances
"so to will" → so too will
"oppurtunity" → opportunity
"their is no escape" → there is no escape
"so even though" (paragraph 4) should begin with a capital letter, as it starts a new sentence.

A careful proofreading pass—ideally reading the essay aloud—will catch these.

Summary

Your core idea has real potential: crime carries costs that go well beyond the criminal justice system. To strengthen the essay, fill in the missing phrase, sharpen your thesis to unify all four costs you discuss, add transitions and evidence, and proofread carefully. With these revisions, this could be a much more persuasive and polished essay.

Feedback Report: "Crime Doesn't Pay" Essay

Thomas, thank you for sharing this draft. Your topic—comparing organized and unorganized crime through the lens of the "crime doesn't pay" adage—has real potential, and your coursework clearly gives you some useful background knowledge. However, the essay currently reads more like an informal reflection than a structured argumentative essay. Below are the key areas to strengthen in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your central claim is buried and inconsistent. You open by questioning whether "crime doesn't pay" is a statement or a question, but you never explicitly state what you believe. By the end, you seem to argue that unorganized crime doesn't pay while organized crime does—but this needs to be stated clearly and early, ideally at the end of your introduction. Right now, a reader has to infer your thesis rather than see it. Add a clear, arguable thesis sentence such as: "While unorganized crime rarely pays because it lacks planning and resources, organized crime pays handsomely because it is built on money, patience, and corruption." This gives your essay a spine that the rest of your paragraphs can support.

Structure and Organization

The essay's organization is logical in broad strokes (unorganized crime, then organized crime, then a brief comparison), but the execution has problems:

The personal introduction is misplaced. Telling readers your name, age, and major at the very end feels like a non-sequitur and undercuts your conclusion. If this information is required by the assignment, place it in your introduction, or better, cut it entirely if it doesn't serve the argument.
Paragraph transitions are weak. Phrases like "This leads me to my next part" are functional but generic. Use transitions that signal the *logical* relationship between ideas—contrast, cause-effect, comparison—rather than just sequence.
The essay lacks a strong concluding paragraph. Your final paragraph introduces a new quotation ("Money Is Power") and makes a broad claim about money's power, but it doesn't synthesize your argument or return to your thesis. A conclusion should remind readers of your claim and its significance, not introduce new territory.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's weakest area. You make strong factual claims—such as the average American spending "$30 per person" on illegal gambling, or officials receiving "two or three fold" their salary from protecting organized crime—without any citation. Since you mention learning this in your Organized Crime & American Politics class, use that as an opportunity: cite your course readings, lectures, or assigned texts. Academic essays require you to show *where* your evidence comes from, especially when making bold financial and political claims. Without sources, these assertions weaken your credibility rather than strengthen it.

Similarly, your claims about unorganized criminals being "somewhat less educated" or committing crime out of "survival" need evidentiary support or at least more careful, qualified phrasing. As written, these read as unexamined generalizations rather than argued points.

Style and Tone

The essay's tone is casual—which suits a personal reflection but not academic argumentation. Consider revising the following:

Avoid rhetorical filler like "save the best for last" and "Now that you have read this and may be trying to figure me out." These phrases distract from your argument.
Avoid using all caps for emphasis (MONEY!, BIG, ANYTHING). Academic writing conveys emphasis through sentence structure and precise word choice, not typography.
Phrases like "both sides of the track" and "on a need-to-know basis" are clichés; replace them with more precise language.

Clarity and Precision

Some sentences need tightening for clarity:
"An unorganized criminal must be very good and have considered all aspects of what they are doing in order not to get caught" contradicts your later claim that unorganized crime "very rarely pays" because criminals "just are not that good." Resolve this inconsistency—are these criminals skilled or not?
The claim that organized crime "involves two or more individuals working together" needs a clearer, more formal definition, ideally drawn from a criminological source, since you're explicitly correcting a popular misconception.

Priority Revisions

Write a clear, arguable thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Cite sources for all statistics and factual claims about organized crime.
Remove or relocate the personal biography section.
Strengthen your conclusion so it returns to and reinforces your thesis.
Revise tone—eliminate all-caps emphasis and casual asides.

You clearly have the knowledge base to write a compelling essay on this topic. The next draft should focus on turning your personal interest into a disciplined, well-supported argument. I'd be glad to discuss any of these points further before your revision.

Feedback Report

Thank you for sharing this deeply personal narrative. Your essay recounts a traumatic experience with honesty and emotional clarity, and the story itself is compelling. However, as an argumentative essay, this piece needs significant revision to strengthen its persuasive structure. Below is feedback organized by key areas.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Right now, your essay reads primarily as a personal narrative rather than an argument. You need a clear, explicit thesis statement—likely in your introduction—that states the claim you want readers to accept. Are you arguing that:
The justice system *can* work when victims advocate for themselves?
Plea bargains often fail victims and should be reconsidered?
Crime doesn't pay, and the system ultimately delivers justice?

Your concluding lines suggest the "crime doesn't pay" thesis, but this needs to be stated early and then supported deliberately throughout, not just arrived at by narrative accident. Consider adding a thesis sentence such as: *"My experience as a burglary victim taught me that while the justice system can initially seem to fail victims, active engagement and advocacy can ensure that justice is truly served."*

Fix the Incomplete Sentence

Your second sentence has a placeholder error: "...they saying that ." This needs to be completed—what did they say? This sentence is important because it sets up your family's belief system, which the rest of the essay tests and ultimately confirms. Fix this early so your framing device works.

Strengthen the Argumentative Elements

Currently, your essay is almost entirely narrative (what happened) with minimal analysis (why it matters, what it proves). To make this argumentative, add:

Explicit connections between events and your claim. After describing the plea bargain threat, explicitly state what this reveals about systemic problems with victim advocacy.
A discussion of counterarguments. Why do prosecutors offer plea bargains? Acknowledging their perspective (caseloads, resource constraints, uncertain trial outcomes) and then explaining why victim testimony should still matter would strengthen your argument considerably.
A broader claim beyond your personal case. Does your experience suggest something about how victims should be treated in general? Should victim impact statements carry more weight in plea negotiations? Push your conclusion beyond "it worked out for me" toward a claim with wider application.

Reconsider the "Crime Doesn't Pay" Frame

This adage is used as your essay's emotional and structural anchor, but examine it critically: the essay actually shows crime *almost* paying—the system nearly let the offenders off with no jail time, and it was your personal intervention that changed the outcome. This is actually a more interesting and complex argument than "crime doesn't pay." Consider reframing your thesis around the idea that justice is not automatic—it requires victim advocacy, which is a stronger, more specific, and more defensible claim than the simpler moral lesson your father taught you.

Address Structure and Pacing

The narrative moves at an uneven pace:
The break-in and immediate aftermath are described in rich detail (helpful for establishing stakes).
The two-month wait and court process are compressed and vague ("we kept going over things," "constantly talking to attorneys").

Consider trimming some of the domestic details (the doctor's appointment, listing every stolen item) to make room for more development of the *court process and your advocacy meeting with the prosecutor*—this is actually the climax of your argument and deserves more space, dialogue, and detail. What did you say in that meeting? What was their response? This is where your argumentative power lies.

Proofread for Grammar and Mechanics

There are several grammatical issues to address:
"the stealing was wrong" → "that stealing was wrong"
"my husbands firearms" → "my husband's firearms"
"prosecutors office told us" → "the prosecutor's office told us"
"kept remembering what my father told be" → "told me"
"these people did not have permission to be in our house" and similar sentences should be reviewed for verb tense consistency (you shift between past and past perfect inconsistently)
"they were going to go to jail" —consider varying sentence structure for more sophistication

A careful proofreading pass will elevate the professionalism of the essay significantly.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Add an explicit, arguable thesis statement in your introduction.
Complete the incomplete sentence early in the essay.
Expand the prosecutor meeting scene—this is your strongest evidence.
Add analytical commentary connecting events to your argument.
Consider a counterargument (why plea bargains exist) and rebut it.
Proofread thoroughly for grammar and punctuation.

Your personal story has real power—the challenge now is shaping it into an argument that uses this experience as *evidence* for a claim, rather than letting the narrative stand alone. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting question—how a movement for equality became associated with negative stereotypes—and you clearly have personal investment in the topic. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: the clarity of your central argument, the quality of your evidence, and some concerning generalizations that undermine your credibility.

Argument and Structure

Missing context. You're responding to "the statement," but you never quote or clearly restate what that statement is. As a reader, I have to infer that you're addressing a claim like "feminism has become harmful" or "feminists hate men." Begin by quoting the statement directly so your argument has a clear target.

Thesis needs sharpening. Your actual position is nuanced and worth developing: feminism's original goals were legitimate, but the movement has been distorted by a vocal minority, which has damaged its public perception. State this explicitly in your introduction rather than making readers piece it together across five paragraphs.

Circular structure. The essay moves from history → stereotypes → your personal definition → back to history → back to stereotypes. Consider a linear structure: (1) historical roots of feminism, (2) how public perception shifted, (3) your assessment of whether that shift is justified, (4) what should change going forward.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's biggest weakness: every claim is asserted, not demonstrated.

Who are these "radical feminists" who want dominance rather than equality? Name specific groups, individuals, or actual quotes/policies. Right now, this reads as a strawman.
"Many people see the feminist movement moving in the wrong direction"—who? Cite a source, survey, or specific commentator.
The Susan B. Anthony reference is historically thin. Anthony was a suffragist in the *first wave* of feminism (roughly 1848–1920). Feminism has since moved through second, third, and fourth waves, each with different goals (workplace equality, reproductive rights, intersectionality, etc). Presenting Anthony as feminism's sole "original" leader oversimplifies a complex history and weakens your credibility with informed readers.

Revision suggestion: Find 2-3 concrete examples—op-eds, statements from feminist organizations, statistics on wage gaps, or documented backlash against feminism—to ground your claims.

A Significant Concern: The "Chunky Lesbian" Stereotype

You write: "The concept many people today have of a 'feminist' is that of a big chunky lesbian who hates all men." Even though you say "this is hardly the case," introducing this stereotype without necessity does two things:

It reproduces a homophobic and body-shaming trope that has nothing to do with your actual argument.
It's unnecessary—you can describe the negative stereotype (angry, man-hating) without the specific physical and sexual characterization.

Consider revising to something like: "The popular caricature of a feminist is an angry woman who resents men rather than seeking equality with them." This preserves your point without the offensive baggage.

A Logical Tension

You criticize "radical" feminists for painting all men as evil, but you also generalize broadly about "radical women" and "feminist groups" without evidence, doing to them what you accuse them of doing to men. If your argument is that overgeneralization is the problem, be careful not to overgeneralize about the people you're critiquing. Precision and specific examples will strengthen your credibility here.

Language and Mechanics

A few issues to address in your next draft:

Capitalization after a period: "by this I do not mean" should be "By this I do not mean."
Subject-verb agreement: "A women is just as much a citizen" should be "A woman is..."
Comma splices: "Men had the exclusive rights to the vote, employment opportunities and other societal features that empower a human being to be innately whole" is a strong sentence, but check surrounding sentences for run-ons.
Word choice: "chunky" is informal and, as noted, unnecessary; "home free" is casual for academic writing.
Repetition: The phrase "call it feminism" is repeated for rhetorical effect, which works, but check that this technique doesn't read as sloppy repetition elsewhere.

Priority Revisions

State the original claim/statement you're responding to.
Sharpen your thesis into one clear sentence in the introduction.
Remove the "chunky lesbian" stereotype and replace with a more precise description of the negative caricature.
Add specific evidence—quotes, statistics, named examples—for every generalization about "radical feminists."
Correct historical oversimplification regarding Susan B. Anthony and the "waves" of feminism.
Proofread for agreement and capitalization errors.

You have a real argument here about the gap between feminism's stated goals and its public perception—now it needs the scaffolding of evidence and precision to make it persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Women's Liberation Movement and Public Perception

Overall Impression

You've identified a genuinely interesting and debatable claim: that the failures of feminist movements stem not from the actions of "radical" feminists themselves, but from how outside observers selectively remember and stereotype them. This is a promising thesis with real analytical potential. However, the essay currently struggles to fully develop and support this argument, and several structural and factual issues undermine your credibility. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your central claim doesn't fully emerge until the third paragraph ("I contend that the fault does not lie with the radical members of the movement, but rather, in the perception of those observing the movement"). Consider moving a clear statement of this thesis to your introduction, so readers know from the start what argument you're making and why it matters.

More importantly, your argument contains an internal tension you don't resolve. In paragraph two, you write that Anthony and Sand were "ridiculed, thus defeating their cause, because of their aggressive action"—which suggests their *behavior* caused the movement's setbacks. But your thesis argues the opposite: that perception, not action, is at fault. Which is it? If you want to argue that public perception is the primary problem, you need to explain why the "aggressive action" itself shouldn't be blamed—perhaps by arguing that such action was justified, strategically necessary, or unfairly characterized as "radical" in the first place. Right now the essay wants to have it both ways.

Evidence and Historical Accuracy

This is the area needing the most attention. Several historical claims are inaccurate or confusing:

Emily Dickinson was not a public feminist figure "ignored" by contemporaries because of her gender politics—she was a poet who lived in near-total seclusion for personal and artistic reasons unrelated to activism. Presenting her as a quiet counterpart to Anthony misrepresents her actual historical role.
George Sand was a 19th-century French novelist; her inclusion in a discussion of the *American* Women's Liberation Movement needs justification. You acknowledge she was French but don't explain why she belongs in this argument at all.
"Stein" (presumably Gertrude Stein) died in 1946 and cannot be meaningfully described as a radical figure of the *late 1960s* resurgence of feminism. This appears to be a factual error that will seriously undermine your credibility with an informed reader.

Revision should either correct these figures or replace them with accurate, well-researched examples (eg, Betty Friedan, Gloria Steinem, or specific media coverage of 1960s-70s feminist protests). Vague, uncited historical claims weaken an argumentative essay considerably. Aim to include specific evidence—quotations from news coverage, statistics on public opinion, or scholarly sources on media framing of feminism—to substantiate your claim about *perception*.

Structure and Transitions

The essay moves chronologically but the connections between paragraphs are often abrupt. Phrases like "This is still true twenty-five years later" assume the reader can track the shift in decade without more explicit signposting. Consider adding topic sentences that explicitly link each historical period back to your central claim about perception versus reality.

Tone and Voice

The line "So what am I getting at?" is too informal for academic argumentative writing. While direct address to the reader can be effective, this phrasing reads as filler rather than a deliberate rhetorical strategy. Replace it with a transitional sentence that restates your thesis more precisely, such as: "This pattern suggests that feminist activism itself has not damaged the movement's reputation; rather, selective public memory has."

Grammar and Mechanics

Please proofread carefully for the following:
"Whenever a movement is began" → "begins"
"oppostion," "readiliy," "therfore" — spelling errors
Comma splices, eg, "This ignorance of other less aggressive feminists, made it seem..." (remove comma)
The final sentence's pronoun "it" lacks a clear antecedent ("the public it was being aimed was not so jaded")

Suggestions for Revision

State your thesis clearly and early.
Resolve the tension between blaming "aggressive action" and blaming "perception."
Correct or replace inaccurate historical examples; add specific, cited evidence.
Strengthen transitions between time periods.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize your argument rather than trail off—consider ending with a reflection on why this perception problem matters for how we understand social movements today.

With these revisions, this essay could offer a compelling analysis of media framing and collective memory in social movements.

"Suffragates" should be "suffragists" or "suffragettes."
"Gloria Steinam" should be "Steinem."
Watch for repetitive phrasing (multiple uses of "equal" in close proximity in paragraph two could be varied for style).

Suggested Next Steps

Decide whether this is a U.S.-focused essay or a global comparative essay, and revise your introduction accordingly.

This is a serious issue because your entire essay hinges on a distinction between two similar-sounding claims:

"The love of money is the root of all evil" (the actual biblical quotation, 1 Timothy 6:10)
"Money is the root of all evil" (a common but inaccurate paraphrase)

Your essay implicitly argues that the *first* statement is true but the *second* is false — but this needs to be stated explicitly and early. Consider rewriting your introduction to say something like: "The commonly repeated phrase 'money is the root of all evil' is a misquotation of 1 Timothy 6:10, which actually states that the *love* of money is the root of evil. This distinction matters because..." This gives your reader a roadmap and immediately signals the sophistication of your argument.

Strengthen Logical Organization

Your paragraphs each make reasonable points, but they don't build on one another in a clear progression. Right now the essay reads as a list of related-but-separate observations:
Money isn't inherently evil (paragraph 2)
Money *can* lead to evil (paragraph 3)
Other things also cause evil (paragraph 4)
Charitable institutions rely on money (paragraph 5)
Evil is a matter of individual choice (conclusion)

The final point—that evil stems from individual choice—is actually your most interesting and original claim, but it appears only in the conclusion, unsupported by the body of the essay. This feels like a new argument rather than a natural conclusion. Consider restructuring so that the "individual choice" idea appears early (perhaps as part of your thesis) and is then supported throughout the body paragraphs. For instance, your paragraph on honest money-making and your paragraph on money-driven crimes could both explicitly tie back to the idea that *the person*, not the money itself, determines the moral outcome.

Deepen Your Evidence and Analysis

Several claims are asserted without support:

"Most money is attained in honest ways" — this is a significant claim that needs evidence or at least acknowledgment that it's your assumption, not a demonstrated fact.
"I am sure that most if not all funds donated are used for the purposes intended" — again, this needs some justification, or you should qualify it as your belief rather than presenting it as settled fact.

Consider adding a concrete example (a historical case, a news story, or even a hypothetical scenario) to illustrate your claim that greed—not money—drives evil action. Real examples of both charitable and greedy uses of money would make your argument more persuasive and less abstract.

Address Counterarguments More Fully

You gesture toward a counterargument (charities being exploited for personal greed) but resolve it too quickly with "I am sure." A stronger essay would spend more time exploring *why* someone might think money itself is corrupting—perhaps citing the scale of wealth inequality or the psychological effects of financial power—before explaining why you still believe the deeper cause is human choice rather than money itself.

Proofread for Clarity and Precision

A few line-level issues need attention:
"wold" should be "would" (first sentence).
"how can honestly be associated with evil?" should likely be "how can *honesty* be associated with evil?"
The repeated use of "worth-while causes are sometimes being used" is grammatically awkward; consider "are sometimes exploited to satisfy..."
Vary your sentence openings—several paragraphs begin with "If" or "It is true," which creates a repetitive rhythm.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fix the introduction and state your thesis explicitly and early.
Reorganize so the "individual choice" argument frames the whole essay, not just the conclusion.
Support key claims with examples or evidence rather than assertions.
Engage more deeply with the counterargument before dismissing it.
Proofread carefully for typos and awkward phrasing.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a series of loosely connected observations to a tightly argued, original take on a well-worn philosophical question.

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay explores an interesting philosophical question about money and power, and you clearly have genuine convictions about this topic. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a unified argument. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to clarify your central claim, tighten your organization, and address some technical issues.

Identifying Your Argument

This is the most pressing issue: What saying are you responding to, and what is your thesis about it?

Your opening line references "this saying" without stating what it is. Presumably you're responding to "money is power" or a similar quote, but readers need this stated explicitly. Additionally, your essay seems to pursue *two* different arguments:

Money is necessary and powerful in modern society
Power (not money specifically) corrupts, and money's value depends on how it's earned and used

These are related but distinct claims. Right now, the essay shifts between them without transition, making it hard to follow your reasoning. Decide which claim is your primary thesis, and make the other point subordinate to it, or find the thread that ties them together explicitly (eg, "Money is not inherently corrupting; how we obtain and use it determines whether it becomes our master or our tool").

Structural Suggestions

Consider reorganizing around a clear thesis statement in your introduction, followed by body paragraphs that build toward it:

Paragraph 1: Introduce the saying, state your thesis (eg, money itself isn't corrupting, but our relationship to it can be)
Paragraph 2: Address the necessity of money in modern life (your current opening)
Paragraph 3: Discuss how earned vs. unearned money affects our values
Paragraph 4: Develop the "master vs. slave" metaphor—this is actually your strongest idea
Conclusion: Return to the original saying with your revised understanding

Strengthen Your Evidence

Right now, your claims are asserted rather than demonstrated. For example:
You claim hard-earned money is spent more wisely—can you cite an example, statistic, or even a hypothetical scenario?
You reference "another theory" about how people use money—whose theory? If this is your own idea, say so and develop it further.
The corruption-of-power paragraph would benefit from a real-world example (historical, political, or personal) to ground the abstraction.

The "Master vs. Slave" Idea

This is your most original and compelling idea—money as either "slave" or "master." I'd encourage you to make this the *heart* of your essay rather than an aside. What determines which role money plays in a person's life? You gesture toward an answer (values, self-satisfaction vs. competition) but don't fully develop it. Consider adding a concrete example: someone who represents each type.

Please proofread carefully for:
Spelling: "currupts" (corrupts), "cicle" (cycle), "sence" (sense), "sucess" (success), "indespencable" (indispensable)
Word confusion: "begats" should be "begets"; "heir" should likely be "or"; "where to sleep" should be "somewhere to sleep"
Unclear phrasing: "Absolute power currupts absolute" is missing a word (absolutely?)
Grammar: "ones" should be "one's" (possessive apostrophe needed throughout)

I recommend reading your essay aloud, which often catches these errors more effectively than silent reading.

Revision Priorities

If you only have time to address a few things, focus on:

State the original saying explicitly and clarify your thesis in relation to it
Choose one central argument and make everything else serve it
Develop the master/slave metaphor with concrete examples
Proofread thoroughly for spelling and grammar

You have the beginnings of a thoughtful argument here. With sharper focus and more concrete support, this essay can move from a series of interesting thoughts to a persuasive, unified piece of writing. Feedback Report: "Is Money the Root of All Evil?"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and time-honored question, and your final position—that money itself is neutral while human choices determine good or evil—is a thoughtful and defensible claim. However, the essay needs significant revision in thesis clarity, logical consistency, structural organization, and use of evidence to make that argument persuasive and academically rigorous.

Thesis and Argument

The biggest issue is that your thesis doesn't appear until the final paragraph. Readers should know within the first paragraph what you intend to argue and why it matters. Right now, the essay opens with a string of questions, which is a reasonable rhetorical strategy, but you never explicitly state which one of these questions you are answering or how. Consider revising your introduction to state clearly: "While the popular saying suggests money is inherently evil, I will argue that money is merely a neutral tool, and evil arises from how individuals choose to use it."

There's also a significant logical tension you need to resolve. In your third and fourth paragraphs, you argue that pursuing money "leads you away from the christian way of life and into a life of evil," and that the desire for money can make people abandon their families or turn to gambling. This makes it sound like money—or at least the desire for it—*does* cause evil. Then, abruptly, your fifth paragraph asserts "Money is not evil" and blames only human judgment. These two positions aren't necessarily incompatible (you could argue that it's the *love of money*, not money itself, that corrupts), but as written, the essay doesn't acknowledge or resolve this tension. I suspect you are gesturing toward the biblical distinction between money and "the love of money" (1 Timothy 6:10, which the popular saying often misquotes). Making this distinction explicit early on would resolve the contradiction and give your argument a much clearer throughline: it's not money that's evil, but the human obsession *with* money.

Structure and Organization

The essay currently reads as a list of loosely connected observations rather than a unified argument. Each paragraph raises a good point, but the transitions between them are abrupt, and the organization doesn't build toward your conclusion. I recommend restructuring along these lines:

Introduction: State the popular saying, briefly explain the misconception, and present your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: Explain why people believe money is evil (the psychological/moral pull toward greed, materialism, neglect of relationships).
Body Paragraph 2: Introduce a counterpoint or complication—money is an object, not a moral agent, and therefore cannot be "evil" in any meaningful sense.
Body Paragraph 3: Provide concrete examples of money used for good (education, poverty relief, medical care) to demonstrate its moral neutrality.
Conclusion: Synthesize by returning to your central claim—it is human character, not currency, that determines whether money's effects are harmful or beneficial.

Your closing line—"it is all in how it is used and how you allow it to use you"—is genuinely strong and memorable. Build your entire essay toward that insight rather than saving it as an afterthought.

Evidence and Development

The essay relies entirely on assertion without any supporting evidence, examples, or outside sources. Academic essays are strengthened by concrete evidence: historical examples (philanthropists like Andrew Carnegie), religious texts more explicitly (since you invoke Christian values, quote or paraphrase relevant scripture), or even hypothetical scenarios developed in more detail. Right now, claims like "you can be lead into gambling" or "money can provide opportunities to educate the uneducated" are stated as facts without illustration. Adding even one specific, developed example per paragraph would make your argument far more convincing.

Additionally, your essay assumes a Christian audience and worldview without acknowledging this. If your assignment allows for a faith-based argument, that's fine, but you should explicitly frame it as such ("From a Christian perspective...") rather than presenting religious values as universally shared assumptions.

Style and Mechanics

There are several proofreading issues to address:
"subservant" → *subservient*
"mear" → *mere*
"educated the uneducated" is awkward; consider "provide education to those who lack it"
"christian" should be capitalized (Christian) throughout
Watch for comma splices and sentence fragments, particularly in paragraph two.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis clearly in the introduction.
Resolve the logical tension between "desire for money leads to evil" and "money is not evil."
Reorganize paragraphs to build toward your conclusion rather than listing separate ideas.
Add concrete evidence and examples throughout.
Proofread carefully for spelling and mechanical errors.

With these revisions, this essay has real potential—the core insight is sound and the final line shows genuine rhetorical skill. Now the surrounding argument needs to catch up to that insight.

Feedback on "Crime Does Pay"

Your essay takes a bold, provocative stance, which can be an effective rhetorical choice—but as it stands, the argument needs stronger reasoning, more credible evidence, and attention to counterarguments to be persuasive to a skeptical academic reader. Below is feedback organized by key areas for revision.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your thesis—"crime does pay"—is clear and attention-grabbing, but it's also a sweeping generalization that ignores the vast complexity of criminal life. You compare crime only to legitimate work in terms of *hourly wage* and *flexibility*, but this is a very narrow lens. A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections and refines its claim. Consider whether your thesis should be qualified: perhaps "certain types of crime can be more financially efficient than legitimate work, when risk is ignored" is more defensible than an absolute claim.

Logical Gaps and Unsupported Claims

Several assertions need support or reconsideration:

"A criminal will always be employed" — This is not demonstrated; it's simply asserted. Crime rates, arrest rates, and incarceration statistics would suggest otherwise. Many criminals cycle in and out of "employment" due to arrest, injury, or death.
Risk is entirely absent from your analysis. This is the biggest gap in your argument. Legitimate workers, once paid, keep their earnings (barring theft or emergency). Criminals face incarceration, violent retaliation, asset forfeiture, and death—none of which are factored into your $1,170,000 projection. A convincing essay on this topic must address risk-adjusted return, not just gross earnings.
The math is inconsistent. In paragraph 3, you say a criminal works one hour a day to earn $300 per week. In paragraph 4, your retirement calculation uses $300 *per hour*. These are very different numbers, and the discrepancy undermines your credibility. Recalculate and make sure your figures are internally consistent, and cite a source for these numbers rather than presenting them as though self-evident.

Evidence and Sources

Right now, your evidence is entirely hypothetical (dollar amounts you've invented) rather than grounded in real data. To strengthen your argument, consider:
Citing statistics on recidivism, incarceration rates, or the average "career" length of criminals.
Referencing actual case studies (eg, white-collar criminals who profited significantly) versus street-level crime, since these have very different risk/reward profiles.
Comparing median criminal "earnings" (which are typically low and unstable) to your rosy hypothetical—many criminологists note that most crime is not lucrative at all.

Counterargument

You don't acknowledge the opposing view at all. Even a brief paragraph addressing the obvious rebuttal—loss of freedom, danger, lack of legal protections, family and psychological costs, difficulty in "spending" illegally obtained money without detection—would make your argument feel more credible and intellectually honest. Right now, the essay reads as one-sided in a way that invites easy dismissal.

Organization

Your paragraph structure follows a reasonable comparison structure (job security, wages, retirement), but transitions are abrupt. The opening line, "I beg to differ," implies a response to a specific claim or opponent that we never see stated. Consider adding a sentence or two establishing what argument you're responding to. Also, your conclusion is very short and simply restates the thesis without synthesizing your evidence or leaving the reader with a final, thoughtful takeaway.

Grammar and Style

There are several errors to address in revision:
"it's advantages" → should be "its advantages" (possessive, not contraction).
"rather or not" → should be "whether or not."
"There is never a lack of work are a means for continuing employment" → this sentence is garbled; likely you meant "or" instead of "are," but the sentence needs to be rewritten for clarity.
"Then if we think about the work day of most working people" is a sentence fragment; revise to a complete sentence with a clear subject and verb.
Watch inconsistent verb tense (shifting between present and conditional "could," "would," "will").
Avoid casual phrasing like "Let's take time to compare" in favor of more formal academic transitions.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fix the mathematical inconsistency and clarify your figures with cited sources.
Add a counterargument section addressing risk, danger, and loss of freedom.
Qualify your thesis to make it more defensible.
Proofread carefully for grammar and sentence structure.
Strengthen the conclusion by synthesizing your argument rather than merely restating it.

With these revisions, your essay can move from an entertaining thought experiment to a more rigorous, persuasive academic argument.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a bold and provocative claim—that crime is a rational economic choice for underprivileged youth—and you write with confidence and energy. However, the essay currently relies almost entirely on anecdotal observation and assertion rather than evidence, which weakens its persuasive power. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your central claim ("crime pays") is stated clearly by the end, but it would strengthen the essay to state this thesis explicitly in the introduction, along with a preview of your supporting reasons (eg, low incarceration rates, insufficient policing, lack of economic alternatives). As it stands, the reader has to piece the argument together across paragraphs.

More importantly, the essay needs evidence beyond casual observation. Statements like "Drive down any street in urban America and you will see..." function as anecdotal generalization, not proof. A skeptical reader will immediately ask: How do you know these are the *same* youths driving luxury cars and wearing brand-name clothing who are also committing crimes? Could there be other explanations (family wealth, credit, secondhand goods, borrowed items)? To make this argument persuasive, you need data: statistics on juvenile crime rates, incarceration and recidivism rates, police funding trends, or the actual profitability of specific criminal enterprises versus minimum-wage work. Even a few concrete statistics would transform this from opinion to argument.

Logical Structure and Reasoning

Several claims need stronger logical support:

"The consequences are few... considering the shockingly low percentage of criminals who actually end up in jail" — This is a strong claim that needs a citation. Without one, it reads as assumption.
The McDonald's comparison sets up a false dichotomy: the only alternatives to crime are either wealth through crime or minimum-wage drudgery. Are there no other paths—education, trade skills, entrepreneurship, mentorship programs? Acknowledging these (even to argue they are inaccessible) would make your argument more sophisticated and harder to dismiss.
Consider addressing counterarguments directly: What about the risk of violence you mention only briefly ("the only danger... comes from other criminals")? What about the long-term consequences of a criminal record on future opportunities? A paragraph that raises and then responds to an objection would strengthen your credibility.

Tone and Framing

This is the most important area to revise. The opening lines—invoking "young males and females, both black and white" driving luxury cars—risk reading as a stereotype-driven scene-setting rather than an analytical observation. Since your argument is about systemic economic conditions, you don't need to racially code your opening at all; the economic argument stands on its own without it. Consider revising to focus on economic conditions and opportunity structures rather than visual profiling of individuals.

Additionally, phrases like "as it should be" (referring to jail space priorities) insert a value judgment without argument or support. Since your essay's stance is analytical (crime is *rational*, not necessarily *right*), be careful to maintain critical distance—analyze the incentive structure without appearing to endorse or romanticize criminal activity ("living larger," "wealth and comfortable living"). Language that glamorizes crime undercuts the seriousness of your economic argument and may alienate readers who could otherwise be persuaded by the underlying logic.

Organization

Each paragraph makes a reasonable point (policing shortages, lenient sentencing for non-violent crime, public misperception of criminals, economic comparison to minimum-wage jobs), but the transitions between them feel abrupt. Consider using topic sentences that explicitly link back to your thesis—eg, "A second reason crime pays is the inadequacy of law enforcement resources..." This will help readers track your reasoning as a structured argument rather than a series of separate observations.

Style and Mechanics

A few technical issues to fix in revision:
"wether" → "whether"
"en in sight" → "end in sight"
Stray period after "hire them."
"come from" → "comes from" (subject-verb agreement)
Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences, especially in paragraph 2.

Summary of Priority Revisions

State your thesis explicitly and early.
Replace anecdotal claims with actual data/statistics wherever possible.
Remove or reframe the racially coded opening imagery.
Address at least one counterargument.
Tighten transitions between paragraphs using clear topic sentences.
Proofread for grammar and typos.

With these revisions, your essay's provocative thesis will be much more persuasive and academically rigorous. You have an interesting argument—now it needs the evidentiary and structural support to back it up.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious and genuinely interesting question—how has the computer simultaneously helped and harmed us? However, the essay's central argument needs sharpening, and several claims require better support or reconsideration. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Clarify and Refine Your Thesis

Your thesis—that computers make life "easier from a physical standpoint and harder from a mental standpoint"—is a promising organizing principle, but the body paragraphs don't consistently support this framework. For instance, job loss due to automation appears in your "physical" paragraph, but job loss seems more like a *social* or *economic* consequence than a physical one. Later, you discuss job loss again under "mental effects" (worker anxiety). This creates confusion: is job loss a physical effect, a mental effect, or both? Consider explicitly defining what you mean by "physical" and "mental" effects early on, and then sort your evidence accordingly. Alternatively, you might restructure around clearer categories, such as economic effects, cognitive effects, and emotional/psychological effects.

Also, "catch-22" is a colorful phrase, but it specifically means a paradoxical, no-win situation. Your argument is more that computers have *mixed* effects, not necessarily a paradox where every choice leads to failure. A more precise term or explanation would strengthen your framing.

Strengthen Logical Consistency

Several claims work against each other:

You state that "computers make errors all the time," but then immediately clarify that these are actually *human* errors, not computer errors. This is an important distinction—computers execute instructions with high fidelity; the "errors" you describe are really human error, miscalculation, or flawed assumptions in models. Revise this section so the causal chain is clear: it's not that computers are unreliable, but that human overreliance on computers can mask human limitations. This is actually a stronger and more interesting point than "computers make errors," so lean into it.

You also claim that computers, if "programmed 100% correctly," "never make errors," which seems to contradict your earlier point. Reconcile these: are computers reliable or not? Your real argument seems to be that computers *are* highly reliable, and this reliability is precisely what displaces workers—so foreground that argument and cut the more confusing claims about computer error.

Support Your Claims with Evidence

Right now, the essay relies heavily on assertions rather than evidence. Claims like "mathematical models, theories, etc are absolutely riddled with errors" or "everyone already knows how abundant computers are" need support—specific examples, statistics, or references to actual cases (eg, a well-known instance of algorithmic or modeling error, data on job displacement due to automation, etc). Even one or two concrete examples per major claim would significantly strengthen your credibility and give readers something to evaluate rather than simply take on faith.

Reconsider Problematic Phrasing

The parenthetical "(except for people in 2nd & 3rd world countries)" reads as dismissive and could be seen as insensitive to global inequality in technology access—a topic that actually deserves serious treatment, not a passing aside. If discussing the digital divide is relevant to your argument (and it could be, given your thesis about impact on society), consider developing it as its own point with real discussion, rather than a bracketed comment.

Tighten Structure and Paragraphing

The "mental effects" section tries to do two things at once (errors/overreliance on calculation, and job displacement/reliability), which makes it feel like two loosely connected paragraphs rather than one focused argument. Consider splitting this into two clearly separate points, each with its own topic sentence, evidence, and analysis.

Revisit the Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces new ideas—better math education, curtailing "theoretical programming," and even a call for interdisciplinary learning (philosophy, psychology, math, science)—that aren't foreshadowed in your thesis or developed in the body. If these solutions matter to your argument, introduce them earlier and develop them with the same rigor as your other points. Otherwise, consider a conclusion that returns more directly to your thesis and synthesizes your findings rather than pivoting to new claims.

Editing for Precision

Watch for run-on sentences and comma splices (eg, "This has created a surplus of 'needless' computations, figures, etc People can easily do calculus..."). Reading your essay aloud can help you catch places where sentences need to be split, combined, or restructured for clarity.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify your organizing categories (physical/mental or otherwise) and apply them consistently.
Resolve the contradiction about whether computers themselves are error-prone or reliable.
Add concrete evidence for major claims.
Revise or remove the dismissive aside about developing countries—or develop it seriously.
Align your conclusion's solutions with points established in the body.

You have the seeds of a compelling argument here about human dependency on technology outpacing human understanding of it. Sharpening the logic and adding evidence will make this essay much more persuasive.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Computer Literacy

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a topic with real contemporary relevance—the necessity of computer literacy in modern life. The auto-parts manager anecdote is a strong instinct; concrete stories are often more persuasive than abstract claims. However, the essay currently reads more like a rough outline than a fully developed argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen as you revise.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction states that "having knowledge of how the computer operates is considered a necessary component of leading a successful life." This is a reasonable thesis, but it needs sharpening. Right now, it's descriptive rather than argumentative—it tells us computers are important, but doesn't stake out a debatable claim. Consider revising toward something like: "Because computer literacy has become essential to employment and financial security, individuals who fail to acquire basic technological skills risk serious professional and personal consequences." This gives you a clear claim to defend with evidence and lets readers know exactly what you intend to prove.

Paragraph Development

This is the essay's biggest weakness: each paragraph introduces an idea but abandons it before it's fully explored.

The manager anecdote is your best material, but it's underdeveloped. What kind of company was this? What specifically happened when he couldn't keep up? Did you interview someone, read an article, or is this a composite example? Naming your source (or clarifying that it's illustrative) will build credibility. Also explore the emotional and financial fallout—how did losing his job affect him? This is where you can appeal to pathos and drive your argument home.

The banking paragraph introduces two distinct ideas—hacking and personal account monitoring—but treats neither in depth, and neither connects clearly back to your thesis about *individual* computer literacy. Ask yourself: how does knowing about hacking help make your argument that people need computer skills to succeed? Right now this paragraph feels like a list of unrelated facts about banking technology rather than a supporting argument. Consider narrowing your focus: perhaps argue that people who don't understand basic computer security are more vulnerable to financial harm, and give a specific example (a scam, a data breach, a personal account error) to illustrate this.

The conclusion is only one sentence and restates the thesis without synthesizing your evidence or leaving the reader with a strong final impression. A conclusion should remind readers of your key points and explain their broader significance—why should we care that computer literacy matters?

Structure and Transitions

The essay needs clearer signposting between paragraphs. "The banking field is another area computers have come to dominate" is a good topic sentence, but the connection between paragraphs should explicitly serve your argument, not just move to a new topic. Ask: how does this example prove my thesis? Make that connection explicit for the reader rather than assuming they'll draw it themselves.

You might also consider adding one more paragraph with a different kind of example—perhaps education, healthcare, or communication—to show the breadth of your claim before returning to your central argument in the conclusion.

Evidence and Credibility

Right now, your claims rely on assertion rather than evidence. Where possible, integrate outside sources: statistics on job losses linked to automation, studies on digital literacy and employment outcomes, or reporting on banking cybersecurity. Even a few well-chosen facts or expert quotations will substantially strengthen your persuasive power and show that you've engaged with the topic beyond personal opinion.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are several errors that need correction in revision:

"twenthieth" → twentieth
"succesful" → successful
"auto-pasts company" → auto-parts company
"internet working" → likely meant networking
"defecit" → deficit (though "liability" might be the more precise word choice here)

Beyond spelling, watch sentence clarity. For example, "One of the most persuasive discoveries of the twentieth century has been that of the computer" is awkward—"persuasive" isn't quite the right word for a technological discovery; you might mean "significant" or "transformative." Read your sentences aloud during revision to catch phrasing that doesn't quite convey your intended meaning.

Priority Revisions

If you focus on three things before your next draft, make them:

Sharpen your thesis into a debatable, specific claim.
Deepen the manager example with more detail and explicit connection to your argument.
Revise the banking paragraph to support your thesis directly, with one clear example rather than several undeveloped points.

You have a solid foundation and a genuinely interesting angle—now it needs fuller development, tighter organization, and more precise language to make its case convincingly.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Computers on Society

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and relevant topic, and your essay shows genuine engagement with the subject. You have a clear point of view and offer some concrete examples (ATMs, cars with sensors) that help ground your argument. However, the essay would benefit significantly from revision in several key areas: thesis clarity, organization, argument development, and proofreading. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your introduction states that computers have brought both "bad and good" changes, but this framing is quite broad and doesn't give readers a strong sense of your specific argument. Ask yourself: What is the *main point* you want readers to take away? Is it that computers are a net positive despite some drawbacks? That we need to be cautious about over-reliance on technology? Your conclusion hints at a nuanced position (we should determine which improvements are worthwhile and what should "remain slow"), but this insight should be foreshadowed in your introduction and developed more fully throughout.

Suggestion: Revise your thesis to be more specific. For example: "While computers have increased efficiency in many areas of daily life, society must remain cautious about the loss of human interaction and the risks of over-dependence on technology." This gives your essay a clearer direction.

Paragraph Organization

Currently, your essay follows a simple "good vs. bad" structure, which is a reasonable start, but each paragraph tries to cover too much ground without fully developing individual points. For instance, in your second paragraph, you move quickly from writing/typewriters to personal computers to ATMs to a general claim about "every facet of life." Each of these ideas deserves more space to breathe.

Suggestion: Consider dedicating each paragraph to a single, well-developed example rather than listing several. For instance, one paragraph could focus solely on how computers have transformed the workplace (with specific examples), while another explores communication or education. This will allow you to explore causes, effects, and implications more thoroughly rather than simply listing changes.

Depth of Analysis

Your essay identifies effects of computer technology but doesn't always explain *why* these effects matter or explore them in depth. For example, you mention that computers have "impersonalized" the world and that people "miss human interaction," but you don't unpack what is specifically lost in this transition. Similarly, your point about people "relying too much" on computers and "not thinking" is compelling but needs development—what evidence or examples support this claim?

Suggestion: For each point, try adding a layer of *why* or *so what*. For example: Why does human interaction matter in banking transactions? What are the psychological or social consequences of reduced human contact? Adding this deeper analysis will strengthen your argument considerably.

Use of Evidence

Right now, your essay relies primarily on personal opinion and general observation ("I feel," "in my opinion"). While your voice is clear, academic argumentative writing benefits from external evidence—statistics, expert opinions, historical examples, or documented case studies—to support your claims. For instance, you could cite data on job displacement due to automation, or discuss specific historical moments when computer reliance led to failures.

Suggestion: Consider researching a few concrete examples or statistics to support your key claims. This will make your argument more persuasive and academically rigorous.

Tone and Diction

You use some informal language and expressions that would benefit from more academic phrasing:
"EVERYTHING" (all caps) should be replaced with formal emphasis, such as "nearly every aspect of daily life."
Phrases like "I feel," "in my opinion," and "I fear" are used repeatedly. While personal voice is valuable in argumentative writing, over-reliance on these phrases can weaken your authority. Try stating claims more directly: instead of "I feel afraid when our society relies so heavily," try "Society's heavy reliance on technology raises serious concerns."

Proofreading and Mechanics

There are several grammatical and spelling issues to address:
"Interraction" should be "interaction" (multiple instances)
"obstical" should be "obstacle"
"live" should be "life" ("computers have also made live transpire")
"proceed with the same transactions" should likely be "process the same transactions"
Watch subject-verb agreement and sentence fragments (eg, "Not to mention how it will change people's life styles in the future" is a fragment)

Suggestion: After revising content, do a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch awkward phrasing and errors.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis to reflect a more specific, nuanced argument.
Reorganize paragraphs to focus on one idea at a time, with deeper development.
Add outside evidence to support your claims.
Reduce reliance on personal-opinion phrasing; assert claims with more authority.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling errors.

You have a solid foundation here—your ideas are relevant and thoughtful. With more structural organization and deeper development of your points, this essay can become a much stronger piece of argumentative writing. Keep working on it!

Feedback on Your Essay: Cultural Awareness and Prejudice/Discrimination

Thank you for sharing this essay on such an important and timely topic. You clearly care deeply about this subject, and that passion comes through in your writing. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of personal reflections than a developed academic argument. Below are some specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Clarify and Strengthen Your Thesis

Your opening paragraph tells the reader *that* significance is subjective, but it doesn't tell us *what you will argue* about cultural awareness as a "discovery." Right now, the thesis is buried and vague. Consider stating explicitly: What is the "discovery" you're claiming happened in the 20th century? Is it the recognition that prejudice is learned (and can be unlearned)? Is it the rise of movements demanding equality? Be precise, and put this claim in one clear sentence near the end of your introduction so the reader knows exactly what you intend to prove.

Also, reconsider whether framing "unlearning prejudice" as a *discovery* is the strongest angle. You gesture at this issue in your final paragraph ("Unlearning prejudice may not seem like a traditional discovery...") — this concern should be addressed head-on in your introduction, not tacked onto the conclusion. Anticipating this objection early will make your argument feel more rigorous.

Develop Your Evidence

This is the essay's biggest weakness: you make broad, sweeping claims without supporting them with specific evidence. For example:

"Racism is almost inherent in today's society" — inherent in what sense? Provide an example (a historical event, a documented case, a statistic, a scholarly source).
"Organizations and forms are presently being held to discover new ways of dealing with discrimination" — which organizations? What forms? This sentence is so vague it doesn't actually tell the reader anything.
"Numerous hidden talents... has been buried under the ignorance of our society" — this is a powerful image, but it needs a concrete illustration (a historical figure, a case study, a specific example of suppressed talent) to be persuasive rather than merely poetic.

As an academic essay, this piece needs at least 2-3 concrete examples — historical (eg, civil rights movement, decolonization, women's suffrage/second-wave feminism), sociological (studies on implicit bias), or media-based (specific ad campaigns or films) — with proper citations. Right now the essay operates entirely at the level of generalization, which weakens its persuasive power.

Improve Structure and Organization

The essay is very short for the scope of its claims (world history, race, gender, media, education, organizational change — all in two body paragraphs). Consider breaking your ideas into distinct paragraphs, each with a clear focus:

Paragraph 1: Historical context — how prejudice functioned as an accepted "way of life" before the 20th century, with examples.
Paragraph 2: The turning point — specific movements, thinkers, or events that challenged this normalization.
Paragraph 3: The current shift — how education, media, and institutions are actively addressing bias now (with examples).
Conclusion: Tie back to your thesis and explain the broader significance.

Right now, ideas about racism, women's inequality, media language, and organizational reform are all compressed into one paragraph, which makes it hard for the reader to follow your line of reasoning.

Address Grammar and Mechanics

There are numerous errors that need correction before this essay is ready for submission. A few examples:
"peoples lives" → "people's lives"
"excepted" → "accepted"
"sterotypes" → "stereotypes"
"enought" → "enough"
"avalibility" → "availability"
"existance" → "existence"
"than" not "then" ("more than blacks")
"deeply breed" should likely be "deeply bred" or "deeply ingrained"

I strongly recommend running the essay through a spelling/grammar checker and then reading it aloud slowly — many of these errors are the kind you'll catch by ear. These mistakes currently distract from your argument and undermine your credibility as a writer.

Refine Your Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new and interesting idea — that unlearning prejudice is itself a "discovery" — but doesn't fully develop it. Conclusions should synthesize your argument, not introduce new complications. Consider moving this idea earlier (perhaps into your introduction, as noted above) and using your conclusion instead to reflect on the broader stakes: what happens if humanity does *not* continue moving in this direction? What does the "peaceful existence" you mention actually look like?

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, specific thesis statement.
Add concrete historical or sociological evidence with citations.
Reorganize into distinct, focused paragraphs.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You're grappling with an important and timely topic—the gap between America's founding ideals (equal opportunity, meritocracy) and lived reality in a fast-paced, media-saturated, individualistic society. This is a rich subject with real argumentative potential. However, the essay in its current form reads more like a collection of related observations than a developed argument. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening sentence tries to do too much at once—it names an "ideology," critiques it, and asserts historical change, all in one breath. As a reader, I finish the paragraph unsure what your essay will actually argue. Is your thesis:
that the American ideology of meritocracy is a myth that obscures structural inequality?
that modern technology and pace of life have made this myth more dangerous or harder to see?
both, and if so, how are they connected?

Right now these feel like two separate essays glued together. I'd suggest writing a single, specific thesis sentence that names the *causal or logical relationship* between the ideology of equal opportunity and the effects of technology/individualism you describe in paragraph two. For example, something like: "The myth of equal opportunity, always false, has become even more difficult to challenge in a media-driven, hyper-individualistic society where success is performed on screen and struggle is rendered invisible." (This is just a model—craft your own in your voice.) Put this thesis at the end of your introduction so readers know exactly what claim you'll defend.

Structure and Organization

The essay currently has two paragraphs that gesture at two different arguments (the ideology of meritocracy; the effects of technology/pace of life) without a clear transition or logical bridge between them. Consider:

Introduction: State the ideology you're critiquing, and end with a clear thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Develop the claim that opportunity has never truly been equal—give a specific historical or contemporary example (redlining, unequal school funding, wage gaps, etc) rather than the general assertion "nothing was ever equal."
Body paragraph 2: Show *how* media and technology reinforce this myth—perhaps by analyzing a specific commercial or show rather than referring to "T.V. programs" in the abstract.
Body paragraph 3: Connect the two—explain how the fast pace of modern life and individualism make it easier to blame individuals ("lack of effort") rather than see structural causes.
Conclusion: Return to your thesis and suggest implications—what should change, or what should the reader understand differently now?

Evidence and Development

This is the essay's biggest weakness right now. Every claim is asserted but not demonstrated:
"One can see these ideas in popular media everyday" — *which* commercials? *Which* programs? Analyze one specific example in detail (a Nike ad, a reality show, a political speech) to show the ideology at work.
"nothing was ever equal" — This is a huge historical claim stated in five words. You need at least one concrete example (slavery, Jim Crow, gender exclusion from property rights, etc) to earn this claim.
"the unlucky, the unsuccessful" — Who specifically? Consider citing a statistic, a news story, or a scholarly source on inequality to ground this in something beyond your own impression.

Academic argument depends on specific, checkable evidence. Right now the essay relies on generalizations that a skeptical reader could easily dismiss.

Style and the Closing Metaphor

Your final image—"The parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated"—is intriguing but arrives as a fragment, disconnected from a clear metaphor developed earlier in the essay. If you want to build a machine metaphor (America as a machine, people as parts, the unsuccessful as broken/neglected parts), you need to introduce and sustain it earlier so this line lands as a payoff rather than a confusing afterthought. Right now it feels like the beginning of a new thought rather than a conclusion.

Also watch for sentence fragments used unintentionally (not for stylistic effect) and long run-on sentences that bundle multiple ideas without clear connectors. Reading your essay aloud will help you hear where sentences break down grammatically.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are frequent errors that need cleanup: run-on sentences, comma splices, missing articles, verb tense inconsistency ("comercials," "substitued," "unsuccessfull," "thier," "nolonger," "realitive"). I recommend running a careful proofreading pass *after* you've revised for content and structure—there's no point polishing sentences you may end up cutting.

Priority for Revision

Write one clear, arguable thesis sentence.
Add concrete, specific evidence for every generalization.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one develops a single, clear idea connected to your thesis.
Proofread last.

You have a genuinely interesting argument buried here—the next draft should focus on excavating and supporting it.
