Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious and genuinely interesting question—the double-edged nature of technology—and you write with real conviction, especially when drawing on personal experience. However, the essay's central argument gets lost somewhere between paragraphs two and three. What begins as a meditation on technology's dual nature (friend and "Nemesis") shifts into a critique of American education and geographic illiteracy, and the connection between these two ideas is never fully explained. Revising this essay means deciding what your actual claim is and then making sure every paragraph serves it.

Thesis and Focus

Your opening paragraph promises an essay about technology as simultaneously beneficial and dangerous. Your second paragraph supports this nicely: technology helps us, but becomes dangerous "when welded with certain aspects of and certain people in our societies." This is a strong, specific claim—I'd like to see it developed further. Instead, paragraph three pivots abruptly to a claim about American isolationism and educational failures around geography and foreign languages. This is a compelling topic in its own right, but as written, it reads as a *different* essay grafted onto the first one.

Ask yourself: Is your real argument "technology is dangerous when combined with human flaws" or is it "technology has made Americans complacent and insular"? These are related but distinct claims requiring different evidence and structure. Right now, the reader isn't sure which one you're defending, because you never explicitly connect technology to the geography/language problem. Is technology causing this insularity? Enabling it? Failing to fix it? You need a sentence that makes this causal (or correlational) link explicit—something like: "Ironically, the very technology that could shrink the world to nothing has instead allowed Americans to close themselves off from it, believing information and comfort are always at their fingertips."

Structure and Development

Consider reorganizing around a single throughline. One option: keep the "technology as friend and enemy" frame, but make the American insularity example one *instance* of technology's harm—specifically, the idea that technological self-sufficiency breeds complacency and reduces incentive to learn about the wider world. This would let paragraph two's abstract claim flow logically into paragraph three's concrete example.

Right now, paragraph two ends with a strong, provocative idea (technology's danger lies in its combination with human weakness) that is simply dropped. Pick it back up. Show us *how* American self-sufficiency and technology combine to produce the geographic/linguistic ignorance you describe.

Evidence and Support

Your anecdotes (the children asking if Spain is in Michigan, your own inability to study Spanish until age 20) are vivid and effective—keep them. But they currently do more work than the essay's logic supports. You move from a personal experience to a sweeping claim about "a great majority of Americans" and "our minds." Be cautious about this kind of overgeneralization; a stronger essay would either qualify the claim ("in my experience, many...") or supplement personal anecdote with at least one external reference—a statistic on foreign language requirements in U.S. schools, for instance, or a study on geographic literacy. Even one outside source would substantially strengthen your credibility.

Sentence-Level and Clarity Issues

The final paragraph needs revision for clarity. The sentence "The effort of the United States has caused our minds to be heard, and a world that is growing while my country lags behind in its selfish self-satisfaction" is grammatically unclear—I'm not sure what "minds to be heard" means in this context, and the sentence structure breaks down (it's not clear what connects to "a world that is growing"). Since this is your concluding statement, it needs to be your clearest, most forceful sentence, not your most confusing one. Rewrite it directly: what do you want the reader's final thought to be?

Similarly, watch for comma splices and fragments (eg, "This thing, technology has been..."), and consider varying sentence openings—several paragraphs lean on personal anecdote as their entry point, which becomes repetitive.

Priorities for Revision

Clarify your central claim and make sure paragraph three explicitly connects to paragraph two's argument about technology and human weakness.
Add at least one external source to support your claims about American education and technology's societal effects.
Rewrite the concluding paragraph for grammatical clarity and a stronger final statement.
Reconsider the essay's scope—you may have two essays here; choose the one you're most invested in and develop it fully.

You have strong instincts for storytelling and a genuine stake in this topic—the revision work now is about discipline and connective tissue between ideas.

Feedback on "How Could People Live Without the Invention of the Computer?"

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses a topic with clear relevance—the pervasiveness of computers in modern life—but as it stands, the piece reads more like a descriptive report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this into a persuasive piece, you'll need to sharpen your central claim, provide concrete evidence, and develop a more sophisticated line of reasoning. Below are specific areas to focus on for revision.

Clarify Your Argumentative Claim

Right now, your thesis is essentially "computers have significantly changed people's lives"—a statement almost no reader would dispute. An argumentative essay needs a claim that is debatable and specific. Ask yourself: what is the *arguable* position here? Some possibilities:

Computers have made people *dangerously* dependent, to the point of vulnerability.
The benefits of computers outweigh the risks of that dependency (or vice versa).
Computer literacy is now a *necessity*, not a convenience, for economic survival.

Your final paragraph actually gestures toward an interesting angle—dependency and vulnerability when computers fail—but this idea appears only in the conclusion. Consider making *this* your thesis and building the entire essay around it, rather than introducing it as an afterthought.

Address the Repetition and Circular Structure

The essay opens and closes with nearly the same rhetorical question ("How could people live without computers?"). While a bit of echo between introduction and conclusion is a strong technique, here it feels less like a deliberate call-back and more like the essay hasn't progressed. Each paragraph also tends to restate the same basic point ("computers are used a lot," "people rely on computers") rather than building toward a deeper insight. Revise so that each paragraph adds something new: perhaps one paragraph on efficiency, one on education/employment, one on the risks of dependency, and a conclusion that weighs these against each other.

Add Specific Evidence and Examples

This is the most important area for improvement. Currently, your claims are supported only by vague generalizations: "computers can be found everywhere," "people often rely greatly on computers," "many people find knowledge of computer skills very helpful." These statements *tell* the reader something is true without *showing* it. Strengthen your argument with concrete, verifiable evidence:

Statistics (eg, percentage of households with computers, percentage of jobs requiring computer literacy)
Specific examples (eg, how hospitals use computers for patient records, how banks rely on computers for transactions)
A real or hypothetical scenario illustrating what happens when computer systems fail (a bank outage, a school database crash)

Concrete evidence will do more to persuade your reader than repeated assertions.

Avoid Vague Subjects like "People"

The word "people" appears dozens of times, often as the subject of a sentence ("People use computers," "People rely on computers," "People have come to depend on computers"). This makes the essay feel abstract and repetitive. Try substituting more specific subjects: "students," "office workers," "small business owners," "surgeons," "airline dispatchers." Specific actors make your claims more vivid and credible.

Develop the Counterargument More Fully

Your final paragraph hints at a counterargument—that computer dependency has drawbacks—but it appears too late and too briefly to carry weight. If this is genuinely part of your argument, it deserves its own well-developed paragraph, ideally placed *before* the conclusion, with specific examples of what goes wrong when computers fail (lost productivity, inability to complete basic tasks, security breaches, etc). Right now, this powerful point is compressed into a few sentences at the very end, when it could be one of the most persuasive parts of your essay.

Revisit the Historical Claim

You state that the calculator was "the first computer." This claim needs support or qualification—computer history is more complex (mechanical calculators, Babbage's Analytical Engine, ENIAC, etc). If you want to include a historical point, research it briefly and integrate it accurately rather than presenting it as a settled fact in passing.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

"Laboreous" should be spelled "laborious."
Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences (eg, "This is when it becomes evident that people are very dependent on computers when computers do break down.")
Vary sentence openings—many paragraphs begin with "People" or "Computers," which creates monotony.

Suggested Revision Plan

Rewrite your thesis to stake out a specific, debatable claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each develops a distinct point (efficiency, education/employment, dependency risks).
Replace generalizations with concrete examples and evidence.
Expand the counterargument about computer failure into its own paragraph.
Write a conclusion that synthesizes your argument rather than simply repeating the opening question.

With more specific evidence and a sharper, more debatable thesis, this essay has real potential—the raw material for an interesting argument about dependency is already here; it just needs to be foregrounded and supported.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious topic—the philosophical and social implications of Einstein's theory of relativity—and shows genuine intellectual curiosity. You're reaching for big ideas: how scientific paradigms shift human consciousness, how abstract theories translate into concrete historical outcomes (like nuclear weapons), and how "fear" versus "peace" might function as organizing principles for global society. These are worthwhile threads. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of associative reflections than a structured argument. Below are areas to focus on for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Right now, it's unclear what your central claim actually is. You gesture toward several possible theses:
Relativity hasn't had its "full impact" on human philosophy
Einstein's ideas were misused (atomic bombs)
Global society should shift from "fear" to "peace" as its foundational principle

Each of these could be a compelling essay on its own, but combined without clear transitions, they compete for attention. Choose one central claim and let the others become supporting evidence. For instance, if your thesis is "Einstein's theory of relativity fundamentally challenged Western assumptions about knowledge and certainty, and society has not yet reckoned with this shift," then the atomic bomb material becomes a *symptom* of humanity misapplying a tool it didn't philosophically absorb.

Clarity and Logical Development

Several claims need more support or clearer reasoning:

"Relativity hasn't had its full impact" — What would "full impact" look like? You gesture at this with the Socratic reference, but the connection between relativity (a physical theory about spacetime) and epistemological closure (a philosophical claim about knowledge) needs to be argued, not asserted. Why does rejecting a fixed reference point in physics imply we can't achieve certainty in knowledge generally? This is a significant philosophical leap—walk your reader through it.

The shift from relativity to atomic bombs to Cold War geopolitics happens very quickly. Each transition needs a bridging sentence explaining the logical (or causal) connection. Right now, it feels like free association rather than argument.

"Fear has been the main point of departure for global community construction" — this is an intriguing metaphorical extension of "relativity" (using "point of departure" to echo your earlier physics discussion), but it needs unpacking. Is this a rhetorical device or a literal historical claim? Either is fine, but be explicit about which you intend.

Use of Sources and Evidence

Your essay relies heavily on personal reflection ("Personally, I find..." / "my notion of time has changed") and unexplained references (Socrates, "sidphantha and his rock," Stephen Hawking). For an argumentative essay, you need to:
Support claims with evidence, not just personal impression
Explain allusions rather than assuming reader familiarity (the "sidphantha" reference, for example, is unclear—likely a name/reference that needs correct spelling and context)
Consider whether personal, meditative reflection is the right mode for this essay, or whether you want to shift toward a more evidence-based, analytical register

Style and Mechanics

There are numerous editing issues to address before resubmission:
Spelling: "Einstien" (Einstein), "nostolgic" (nostalgic), "sidphantha" (please verify this name)
Punctuation: Several sentence fragments and comma splices (eg, "And that it was only a matter of time...") should be integrated into complete sentences
Word choice: "histories" should be "history's"; "a literature" should likely be "of literature"
Bracketed asides — the bracketed insertions (eg, "[his and his wife's]," "[24 hours = day]") read as marginal notes-to-self rather than integrated prose. Either incorporate this information smoothly into sentences or cut it.
Punctuation before "But" — starting a sentence with "But" after a semicolon-ended fragment ("too utopic; / But if...") needs revision for proper sentence structure.

Suggestions for Revision

Narrow your thesis to one clear, arguable claim.
Add a roadmap paragraph early on that tells the reader what you'll argue and how (this will also help you organize your own thoughts).
Slow down your reasoning at key transitions—especially the leap from physics to epistemology, and from philosophy to geopolitics.
Verify and explain all references (Socrates, Hawking, and especially the unclear allusion to "sidphantha").
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar; consider reading the essay aloud to catch fragments and awkward phrasing.

Final Note

The passion and intellectual ambition here are real strengths—don't lose them in revision. Your instinct to connect abstract physics to lived human experience and geopolitics is exactly the kind of interdisciplinary thinking that makes for a compelling essay. The next draft should channel that energy into a tighter, more clearly argued structure.

Feedback on "The Impact of Shopping Malls on Kalamazoo"

Strengths to Build On

You've chosen a topic with real intellectual substance—the relationship between retail geography, transportation, and economic inequality is a legitimate area of urban studies. Your local knowledge of Kalamazoo gives the essay authenticity, and the causal chain you're tracing (transportation shifts → suburban migration → downtown decline → harm to those left behind) is a genuinely interesting argument. The rhetorical question ("Why should we... go downtown where the more poorer people are...") is an effective device that voices the counter-perspective in a vivid way.

Areas for Revision

Sharpen and Foreground Your Thesis

Your thesis—that malls are "more of a detriment than a blessing"—appears in your opening paragraph but then gets buried under several paragraphs of historical narrative before your actual argument (harm to the poor) resurfaces. Consider revising your thesis to preview your *specific* claim and reasoning: something like, "While shopping malls offer convenience to suburban consumers, their proliferation has drained resources from downtown Kalamazoo, disproportionately harming the low-income residents who cannot follow businesses to the suburbs." This gives readers a roadmap for what's coming.

Reorganize for Logical Flow

Right now the essay reads as a chronological narrative (how cities developed, then how malls arose, then who's affected) rather than an argument organized around claims and support. Try restructuring around your reasoning:
Paragraph 1: Thesis + roadmap
Paragraph 2: Historical context (why downtowns were once accessible to all)
Paragraph 3: The mechanism of decline (suburbanization, capital flight)
Paragraph 4: The specific harm to poor/working-class residents (this is your real point—it deserves more space, not less)
Paragraph 5: Counterarguments/complications (the university and festival examples) and your response to them
Conclusion: Restate thesis with a call to action or a broader implication

Currently, your strongest point—harm to the poor—is compressed into a short final paragraph. Expand it. What specifically happens to these communities? Fewer jobs? Higher prices? Loss of services? Give concrete examples from Kalamazoo.

Strengthen Evidence and Development

Right now your argument relies entirely on general historical claims ("throughout history, the rich get richer") and personal impression. To make this persuasive:
Add *specific* evidence about Kalamazoo: names of stores that closed, dates, statistics on downtown vacancy rates or population shifts, or a specific mall (eg, Crossroads Mall) and its opening date relative to downtown's decline.
The university/festival paragraph currently reads as a complication to your thesis (evidence that downtown *can* thrive) without being explicitly addressed as such. Tell your reader directly: "Some might argue that colleges and festivals prove downtown Kalamazoo can still succeed—however, these are temporary or narrow solutions because..." This turns a stray example into deliberate counterargument-and-rebuttal, which strengthens your credibility.

Revise Style and Mechanics

There are several issues to clean up:
Fragments as paragraph breaks: The isolated lines "and still could be!" and "and money is not spent in the stores near them" appear to be leftover fragments, possibly from editing. Integrate these ideas into full sentences within your paragraphs.
Word choice/spelling: "clientel" should be "clientele"; "flu" should likely be "flight" (a significant meaning change—check this carefully, as it currently reads as a typo that confuses your point).
Apostrophe/possessive errors: "There neighborhoods" should be "Their neighborhoods"; "the could keep a distance" should be "they could keep a distance."
Run-on and comma-spliced sentences: Several sentences string together independent clauses with commas alone (eg, "Businesses that could move out of the downtown district did, and still do, and relocate..."). Read your paragraphs aloud to catch these; break long sentences into two.
Inconsistent capitalization and formatting: "%" symbols or stray characters (the "�" character) suggest encoding issues—proofread the final document carefully before submission.
Tone shifts: The essay moves between formal analysis and casual rhetorical questions ("Why should we... go to a nice indoor mall"). This can be effective if controlled, but make sure the shift is intentional (eg, clearly marked as representing an opposing viewpoint) rather than accidental.

Summary

Your instincts about structure and cause-effect reasoning are good, but the essay needs a clearer thesis stated up front, a reorganization that puts your strongest argument (harm to the poor) at the center rather than the end, more concrete local evidence, and a careful proofreading pass. Revise with an eye toward: "What is my main claim, and does every paragraph work to support it?"

Feedback Report

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic—the dual nature of television's impact on society. You've identified a genuine tension (television's potential vs. its actual use) that could form the basis of a strong argumentative essay. However, the essay currently reads more as a list of assertions than a developed argument. Below are suggestions for revision organized by category.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis is somewhat buried and inconsistent. In the introduction, you claim television "has made the most impact on world views and lifestyle," but by the conclusion, your actual argument seems to be that television has made society "more informed but too lazy to think about this new information." This is a more interesting and specific claim—consider leading with it. A stronger thesis might look like: "While television has the potential to educate and connect society to the wider world, it has instead become a tool for escapism that has made Americans passive consumers of information rather than active thinkers." State this clearly at the end of your introduction so readers know exactly what you will prove.

Evidence and Development

This is the essay's greatest weakness. Every paragraph relies on generalized claims ("millions of Americans," "many families," "society has become") without any concrete evidence. You need:

Specific examples or statistics (eg, Nielsen ratings, studies on TV viewing hours, or named programs)
Named sources or research to support claims like "TV is a major cause of lack of togetherness in American families"—this is a strong claim that requires more than assertion
Concrete illustrations of television's educational potential (name specific documentaries, news programs, or children's shows) versus its "cheap entertainment" (name specific sit-coms or genres you're critiquing)

Without evidence, your argument sounds like opinion rather than a supported academic claim. Ask yourself: how do I *know* this? Then show your reader that source.

Structure and Paragraph Organization

Your essay follows a reasonable pattern (potential vs. reality), but the paragraphs could be more tightly organized:

Paragraph 2 sets up the contrast well but ends abruptly without fully developing *why* television became an "escape route" rather than an educational tool. What caused this shift?
Paragraph 3 conflates several distinct claims (television as diversion, loss of family togetherness, increased laziness) without clearly connecting them or dedicating enough space to each. Consider separating these into distinct paragraphs, each with its own topic sentence, evidence, and analysis.
The conclusion essentially repeats the introduction's structure without adding new insight. A strong conclusion should synthesize your argument and perhaps gesture toward implications (What should we do about this? What does this mean for future technology?) rather than simply restating points.

Logical Gaps

Some causal claims need more support or qualification:
You assert TV "is a major cause" of family disconnection—this is a strong causal claim. Consider whether correlation is being mistaken for causation, or acknowledge other contributing factors (work schedules, other technologies).
The leap from "watching TV" to "increase in laziness" and lack of "motivation" needs a logical bridge. Why does watching television specifically cause reduced motivation, as opposed to being a symptom of other factors?

Style and Clarity

A few smaller issues to address in revision:

Redundancy: Your introduction and conclusion essentially repeat the same points (television's potential vs. its actual use) without progression. Make sure your conclusion adds something new—synthesis, implications, or a call to action.
Word choice: Phrases like "staring at a box" are effective but should be balanced with more formal academic language elsewhere.
Proofreading: There are several errors to fix in your next draft: "integrety" (integrity), "dificulties" (difficulties), "becomed" (became), and "00 years ago" (likely meant to be a specific number—fill this in).
Pronoun consistency: You use "he" as a generic pronoun ("the less motivated he becomes")—consider using "one" or restructuring for gender-neutral language.

Suggestions for Revision

Narrow your thesis to the specific claim about information vs. critical thinking, and make it explicit early on.
Add concrete evidence—specific shows, studies, statistics—to support every major claim.
Restructure body paragraphs so each focuses on one clear idea (educational potential, escapism, family impact, laziness) with full development.
Strengthen causal reasoning by acknowledging complexity rather than asserting direct causation.
Revise the conclusion to move beyond summary and offer synthesis or implications.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammatical errors.

With more specific evidence and a tighter argumentative structure, this essay could become a compelling exploration of television's complicated legacy. The core idea is promising—now it needs support and precision.

While a personal connection to the topic (comparing polio to AIDS in your generation) can be effective, it should be developed more purposefully rather than inserted briefly and dropped. Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious topic—the wide-reaching effects of air travel—and you've organized it logically around four distinct categories: personal travel, business, entertainment, and international relations. This is a solid structural foundation. However, the essay would benefit from deeper analysis, more varied evidence, and careful proofreading. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your thesis states that the airplane has "drastically changed the lives of many people," but the essay reads more like a list of examples than an *argument*. Ask yourself: what is the deeper claim you're making? Is it that the airplane is humanity's most transformative invention? That its benefits outweigh its costs? That it has fundamentally altered how humans conceive of distance and time?

Consider revising your thesis to make a more specific, arguable claim. For instance: "While the airplane has undeniably accelerated global connectivity in business, entertainment, and diplomacy, this same technology has introduced new vulnerabilities—particularly in warfare—that complicate any simple narrative of progress." This gives your essay a sharper analytical edge.

Paragraph-Level Development

Each of your body paragraphs introduces a good topic but doesn't develop it fully:

Travel paragraph: Your Michigan-to-California comparison is effective and concrete—keep this! But consider expanding: *why* does this matter beyond convenience? What did extended travel time cost previous generations (missed opportunities, family separation, etc)?

Business paragraph: You assert that business "has definitely flourished" but only support this with a vague personal observation ("I have seen this"). This needs concrete evidence—statistics, specific companies, or scholarly sources about the growth of international trade or globalization.

Entertainment paragraph: The Michael Jackson example is memorable, but you don't explain *how* this changes entertainment culture. Does it create global celebrity in ways that weren't previously possible? Does it homogenize culture, or spread it more widely? Push your analysis further.

Intercontinental relations paragraph: This is your most balanced section since you acknowledge both positive and negative effects—this is good critical thinking! However, the Pearl Harbor and atomic bomb examples deserve more than one sentence each. These are complex historical events; even brief context about *how* airplanes made this destruction possible (range, speed, payload) would strengthen your point.

Use of Evidence

Right now, most of your evidence is anecdotal (your own trip to Spain, "I have seen this"). While personal experience can be a valid starting point, an argumentative essay benefits from:
Historical data or statistics (eg, growth in international trade post-WWII)
Expert testimony or scholarly sources
Specific historical events with more context

Consider researching a few concrete facts—for example, actual figures on the growth of global tourism or international business travel—to substantiate your claims.

Organization and Transitions

Your paragraphs are organized by topic, which is helpful, but the transitions between them are abrupt. Phrases like "As I've already said" and "The last area I'm going to touch on" feel mechanical. Try using transitions that connect ideas causally or comparatively—for example, showing how faster business travel *led to* the growth described in your entertainment paragraph.

Additionally, your concluding sentence feels rushed. Consider expanding your conclusion to synthesize your points and return to your (revised) thesis, rather than simply restating that the airplane has "positive" and "negative" effects.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several errors worth correcting:
"efficeintly" → efficiently
"effected" → affected (this mix-up appears multiple times; remember "affect" is usually the verb, "effect" the noun)
"choosen" → chosen
"musicans" → musicians
"Habor" → Harbor
"perspective countries" → respective countries
"than previously possibly" → than previously possible
Watch your use of the ampersand (&)—spell out "and" in formal academic writing
"This example" fragments and comma splices appear in a few places (eg, "Travel times have been significantly shorten; for example...")

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or having a peer review it, to catch these mechanical issues.

Next Steps

For your revision, I encourage you to:
Sharpen your thesis into a more specific, arguable claim
Add at least one piece of researched evidence (not just personal anecdote) per paragraph
Deepen your analysis by explaining *why* each example matters, not just describing *that* it happened
Proofread carefully for the grammar issues noted above

You have good instincts for organization and a genuinely interesting topic—now it's time to add depth and precision to match that structure.

Overall Impression

For instance: "While AIDS initially fueled discrimination and paranoia, increased public education has gradually shifted societal attitudes toward greater caution and empathy." This gives your essay a clear trajectory from problem to (partial) resolution.

Factual Accuracy—A Critical Concern

Several claims in this essay need serious revision:

"It always leads to death and there is no cure" — This was not entirely accurate even by the time treatments emerged, and antiretroviral therapies have dramatically extended life expectancy. Overgeneralizations like this undermine your credibility as a writer.

Ryan White's story — This is a strong, specific example, but you should verify the details (his diagnosis, the exact nature of the school's response) and perhaps cite a source. Right now it functions as anecdote rather than evidence.

Evidence and Development

Right now, you have one specific example (Ryan White) and several broad, unsupported assertions ("this has really scared people," "I feel this has really made people think"). Argumentative essays are strengthened by:

Concrete evidence: statistics, expert testimony, historical documentation
Multiple examples: Consider discussing other notable cases, policy changes (like the ADA's application to AIDS patients), or shifts in public health messaging
Counterarguments: Did everyone react with fear? Were there compassionate responses too? Acknowledging complexity strengthens your credibility

Tone and Word Choice

Phrases like "ignorant thinking" and casual assertions ("I'm sure more changes will come about") shift the essay's register from analytical to conversational. While personal conviction can be powerful, academic argumentation benefits from measured, evidence-based language rather than personal feelings stated as fact ("I feel this has really made people think about their actions").

Try this shift: Instead of "I feel this has really made people think about their actions," try: "This uncertainty likely contributed to more cautious sexual behavior, as evidenced by [specific source/data]."

Your paragraphs jump between distinct ideas (general impact → prejudice/Ryan White → undetectable strains → behavioral change) without clear transitions explaining *why* one point leads to the next. Consider using topic sentences that explicitly link back to your thesis.

Grammar and Mechanics

You'll want to proofread carefully for:
Spelling: "peoples" → "people's"; "untill" → "until"; "devistating" → "devastating"; "definately" → "definitely"
Word confusion: "there is more of stigma" → "there is more stigma" or "there is a greater stigma"
Comma splices and run-on sentences in several places

A careful proofreading pass (reading aloud often helps) will catch most of these.

Revision Priorities

Narrow and sharpen your thesis into a specific, arguable claim
Verify and correct factual claims, especially about AIDS treatment and behavioral statistics
Add 2-3 more pieces of concrete evidence (data, additional examples, expert sources)
Revise emotionally-loaded language into more analytical prose

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an ambitious and genuinely significant topic—the atomic age as a turning point in human history. Your prose has moments of real power (the image of "frigid planet and cold neighbors" thawing is memorable), and you clearly have strong personal investment in this subject. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: sharpening your central argument, supporting your claims with evidence, and tightening your prose. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis is somewhat buried and, more importantly, unclear in its stance. You claim nuclear weapons are "the most significant factors of change" in modern history—but significant *how*? Destructive? Transformative? Both positive and negative? By the final paragraph, you're celebrating the "thawing" of Cold War tensions, which suggests nuclear weapons were primarily a negative force. But you never explicitly argue this. 

Revision suggestion: State your thesis clearly and specifically in the first paragraph. For example: "The development of nuclear weapons fundamentally reshaped global politics, culture, and psychology for nearly fifty years—and understanding this legacy is essential as the world now moves toward disarmament." This gives your reader a clear claim to test against your evidence.

Organization

Your three paragraphs follow a loose chronological arc (rise of nuclear fear → costs of the Cold War → recent dismantling), which is a reasonable structure. However, each paragraph tries to do too much at once, mixing personal anecdote, sweeping historical claims, and emotional appeals without clear topic sentences to guide the reader. 

Revision suggestion: Consider organizing around distinct *effects* of nuclear weapons—psychological (the culture of fear), economic (military spending vs. humanitarian needs), and political (the recent thaw)—with one paragraph devoted to each. Begin each paragraph with a topic sentence that states its main claim.

Evidence

This is the essay's biggest weakness for an argumentative piece. You make several large factual claims—"uncountable trillions of dollars," "nine presidential administrations," "reduced by several thousand troops"—without any citations or sources. Personal memory (the section about growing up associating communism with nuclear threat) is compelling, but it should *supplement* factual evidence, not replace it.

Revision suggestion: Find at least two or three concrete statistics or historical facts (defense spending figures, specific treaties like INF or START, dates of Berlin Wall's fall) and cite them. This will transform your impressionistic claims into a persuasive, evidence-based argument.

Style and Mechanics

A few issues to address in your next draft:

Grammar and typos: "Though advances" should be "Through advances." "arrived a standstill" should be "come to a standstill" or "reached a standstill." "cheif" should be "chief." Proofread carefully for these errors.

Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences string together independent clauses with commas alone (eg, the long sentence beginning "As a commonplace goal..."). Break these into shorter sentences or use semicolons/conjunctions.

Vague phrasing: Terms like "Americana," "our small world," and "family of nations" are abstract and clichéd. Replace them with more precise language. What specifically do you mean by "Americana"—suburban life? Political culture? Popular media?

Shifting pronouns and voice: You move between "our," "my country," and more formal third-person description. Choose a consistent voice—since this is a personal, reflective argument, first person ("I," "we," "my country") may work well, but use it deliberately and consistently.

Mixed metaphors: The closing image of "cold neighbors" thawing is effective, but be careful not to overload a single paragraph with too many extended metaphors (walls, barriers, ice, thaw)—choose one central image and develop it fully rather than layering several.

Final Suggestions

For your revision, I'd recommend:

Write a one-sentence thesis statement and put it at the end of your first paragraph.
Outline three body paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence tied to that thesis.
Find 3–5 concrete facts or statistics to replace vague claims.
Read your essay aloud to catch run-on sentences and typos.
Reconsider your conclusion—right now it ends on a hopeful but vague note ("warm and friendly people"). Consider ending instead with a specific reflection on what this history means for the future, or a call to action.

This essay has real potential—your instincts about the significance of this topic are sound. With clearer argumentation and stronger evidence, it will be much more persuasive. I look forward to seeing your revision.

Feedback on "The Discovery of Penicillin" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely interesting topic, and your writing shows an understanding of how the antibiotic industry developed from a single discovery into a global enterprise. However, as it stands, this essay reads as a historical summary rather than an argument. Since this assignment calls for argumentative writing, the most important revision you can make is to identify a specific, debatable claim and structure the entire essay around defending it.

Identify a Real Thesis

Right now, your essay's implicit claim is something like "penicillin's discovery was important and led to a big industry" — but this is a statement of fact, not an argument. Nobody would reasonably disagree with it, so there's nothing to prove.

Consider narrowing your focus to a claim that requires evidence and could be contested. For example:
Was the synthetic modification of antibiotics *more* consequential than the original discovery of penicillin?
Did the commercialization of antibiotics (via companies like Upjohn) accelerate medical progress, or did it introduce problems (eg, overuse, resistance) that outweighed the benefits?
Should the discovery of penicillin be credited primarily to Fleming, or was the collaborative industrial/academic effort that followed equally responsible for its impact?

Any of these would give you a thesis you could state clearly in your introduction and then support point-by-point.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Currently, the essay makes broad claims ("caused a great need," "the industry increased tremendously," "huge enterprises... were formed and grew") without any specific evidence — no dates, no named individuals besides Upjohn, no statistics, no citations. For an argumentative essay, you need concrete support:
Dates and figures: When was penicillin discovered? When did mass production begin? How many new antibiotics were discovered in a given decade?
Named examples: Which "new drugs" were "medically useful"? Which were not, and why?
Sources: Cite historians of medicine or primary documents to back up claims about the industry's growth.

Without this kind of specificity, your claims feel asserted rather than proven.

Address a Factual Problem

One claim needs correction: you state that antibiotics made it "much easier to obtain... medicine for simple illnesses, such as the common cold." The common cold is caused by a virus, and antibiotics are ineffective against viral infections — this is a well-known point of concern in public health (antibiotic overprescription for colds is often cited as a *problem*, not a benefit). This error undermines your credibility as a "medicinal chemistry" writer. Consider replacing this example with a bacterial illness (eg, strep throat, pneumonia, bacterial ear infections) or use the cold example instead as evidence of *misuse* of antibiotics — which could actually become part of a more nuanced argument about the downsides of easy antibiotic access.

Improve Structure and Transitions

Your three paragraphs move roughly chronologically (discovery → synthesis boom → conclusion), which is a reasonable skeleton, but the essay would benefit from:
A clear topic sentence for each paragraph that ties back to your thesis.
Explicit transitions explaining *why* one development led to the next, rather than simply listing events in sequence.
A conclusion that does more than restate the body — it should return to your thesis and explain its broader significance (eg, what does this history suggest about how scientific discoveries scale into industries? What lesson does it offer for today's antibiotic resistance crisis?).

Vary Sentence Structure and Fix Mechanics

You rely heavily on passive constructions and the phrase "It was..." to open sentences (eg, "It was discovered that," "It was also attempted to," "It was now much easier"). This creates a repetitive rhythm and obscures who is doing the discovering or attempting. Try recasting these in active voice: "Chemists discovered that older drugs could be synthesized efficiently" is more direct and readable than the passive equivalent.

Also proofread carefully — "attemped" should be "attempted." A few sentences also shift awkwardly between plural and singular subjects (eg, "a small bacteria" — bacteria is already plural; the singular is "bacterium"). A careful proofreading pass will catch these small errors.

Summary of Priority Revisions
Craft an arguable thesis and revise every paragraph to support it directly.
Add specific evidence: dates, names, statistics, and citations.
Correct the antibiotic/cold factual error, or repurpose it to support a more critical argument.
Tighten transitions and strengthen the conclusion's connection to your thesis.
Vary sentence openings and proofread for grammar.

With a clearer argumentative spine and more specific evidence, this essay has real potential — the topic is rich, and you clearly have foundational knowledge to build on.

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've chosen a rich topic with genuine cultural significance, and your essay shows a clear structural instinct—entertainment, communication, and family life are logical categories to explore. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: thesis clarity, argumentative focus, development of ideas, and proofreading. Let me address each.

Transformative in complex ways? Right now, you present both sides of each issue (entertainment, communication, family) without staking a position. This is a common structure for a *summary* or *survey* essay, but the prompt asks for an *argumentative* essay.

Then use each body paragraph to build toward that claim rather than simply listing "some say this, others say that."

Your three body topics (entertainment, communication, family) are reasonable, but each paragraph tries to do too much by presenting competing views without synthesis or evaluation. For instance, in the family paragraph, you note that TV both "brings families together" and "diminishes communication"—but you never tell the reader which effect you believe is more significant, or why.

Evidence and Specificity

The essay relies entirely on generalizations ("many believe," "some argue," "others say") without any concrete evidence—no statistics, historical examples, studies, or even specific anecdotes. Academic argumentative writing needs support beyond assertion.

When discussing entertainment, you might reference the shift in average hours watched per household historically.

Grammar and Mechanics (Important—Proofread Carefully)

There are numerous errors that need correction before this essay is submission-ready:

"Of course their have been" → "there have been"
"20 century" → "20th century" (appears twice)
"come in to our homes" → "come into our homes"
"way of live" → "way of life"
"programing" → "programming"
"As television programing improves their is" → "there is"
"any time" is fine, but check consistency in phrasing
"and diminished communication" → "and diminishes communication" (verb tense agreement with "disrupts")
"The children grow up watching violence and sex and the become desensitized" → "they become desensitized"
"news forms" → "new forms"
Redundant "and and" should be corrected to a single "and"

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, sentence by sentence, or using a grammar-checking tool as a first pass—but always follow up with your own careful review, since automated tools miss context-specific errors.

Once you sharpen your argument, revise the conclusion to reflect a genuine synthesis—what do you want readers to conclude about television's role in shaping modern life, beyond simply "it's controversial"?

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting question—whether labor-saving devices like washing machines have hidden costs despite their obvious benefits. This is a promising angle, but the essay needs significant revision to develop this idea clearly and persuasively. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

The central problem is that your essay lacks a clear, stated thesis. You begin by praising the washing machine as a life-improving invention, then pivot to critiquing it (and modern gadgets generally) for making us lazy and out of shape. This shift happens so gradually that readers may finish the essay unsure of what you're actually arguing. 

Ask yourself: what is my main claim? If it's "modern conveniences like the washing machine have contributed to reduced physical activity and rising health concerns," state this explicitly in your introduction. Right now, your intro discusses inventions generally (wheel, fire) without signaling this specific argument, so the essay feels aimless until the final paragraph, where your real point finally emerges.

I'd also suggest resolving the internal contradiction: you write "I would have to agree" that washing machines are beneficial, then immediately pivot to criticizing them. This isn't necessarily a flaw—nuanced arguments often acknowledge both sides—but you need to make the concession-and-rebuttal structure explicit. Are you arguing that convenience *comes at a cost we should be more aware of*? Say so directly, and use the rest of the essay to support that specific claim.

Structure and Organization

Currently, the essay reads as a loose collection of related but disconnected observations. Consider this structure:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly (something like: "While inventions like the washing machine save time, they may also be contributing to a decline in physical activity with real health consequences.")
Body paragraph 1: Acknowledge the genuine benefits of the washing machine (time saved, labor reduced).
Body paragraph 2: Pivot to the cost—loss of physical exercise from daily tasks.
Body paragraph 3: Connect this to broader patterns (weight management, health trends) with actual support.
Conclusion: Return to your thesis and suggest an implication or call to reflection.

Your current second and third paragraphs (about people panicking at the thought of hand-washing) are entertaining but don't build the argument—they're variations on the same joke. Consider cutting one and using the space to develop your stronger point about exercise and health.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's weakest area. Right now, your claims rest entirely on hypothetical scenarios (people "running about in the streets" clutching washing machines) and rhetorical questions rather than evidence. These exaggerations are amusing but undermine your credibility—they read as jokes rather than argument.

Your strongest claim—that reduced manual labor correlates with increased reliance on "diet programs & other such systems"—is asserted without support. This is exactly the kind of claim that needs data: Is there research linking domestic technology adoption to declining caloric expenditure? Historical data on obesity rates before/after widespread appliance adoption? Even one or two concrete statistics would transform this from a series of assertions into an actual argument.

Style and Tone

The essay's tone is very casual—closer to a spoken riff than academic writing. This may be intentional (perhaps you're going for a humorous, informal essay), but for an argumentative paper, dial back:

Avoid ampersands (&)—spell out "and" in formal writing.
Cut rhetorical questions used as filler ("Where would we find the time to hand wash our clothes?"). Rhetorical questions can be effective, but you're using several in a row without answering them, which reads as evasive rather than persuasive.
Avoid ellipses ("But is that all that bad?....")—this is a spoken-language habit, not an academic writing convention.
Trim exaggerated hypotheticals (the person clutching the washing machine while fleeing a fire). One vivid, brief example can work well; overused, it feels like padding.

Grammar and Mechanics

Please proofread carefully for:
Spelling: "continously" → continuously; "necesity" → necessity
"with out" should be one word: "without"
"handclothes washing" is unclear—did you mean "hand-washing clothes"?
Watch comma usage, especially with introductory phrases and in your list in paragraph 4.

Summary

You have the seed of an interesting, specific argument about the tradeoffs of modern convenience. Focus your revision on: (1) stating a clear thesis early, (2) cutting redundant humor to make room for real evidence, and (3) adopting a more consistently formal tone. With these changes, this could become a much stronger essay.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay has a warm, engaging voice, and your enthusiasm for the zip-lock bag comes through clearly. The personal anecdotes (the smelly locker, the shampoo disaster) make the essay memorable and relatable. However, as an *argumentative* essay, the piece currently reads more like an informal, descriptive list of "reasons I love zip-lock bags" rather than a structured argument that makes a claim and defends it with evidence and reasoning. Below are some suggestions to help you strengthen the argumentative core of the essay.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Claim

Right now, your thesis is essentially "the zip-lock bag was a good invention," which is stated almost in passing in your opening and closing paragraphs. For an argumentative essay, you need a clearer, more assertive claim—ideally one that could be debated. For example: *"The zip-lock bag deserves recognition as one of the most transformative everyday inventions because it solves practical problems related to freshness, hygiene, and portability that no comparable product addresses as effectively."* This gives your reader a specific claim to test against your evidence, and it gives you a roadmap for organizing your paragraphs.

Establish Criteria for "Good Invention"

An argument about whether something is a "good invention" is much stronger if you explicitly state *why* an invention counts as good—criteria like affordability, versatility, convenience, or problem-solving power. Right now, you list examples (freshness, odor control, travel uses) without ever stepping back to say, "This matters because..." Adding this evaluative framework early in the essay will help transform your list of anecdotes into a genuine argument.

Address Counterarguments

A hallmark of academic argumentative writing is acknowledging opposing views. Consider: Are there downsides to zip-lock bags (eg, environmental impact, plastic waste, cost over time)? Are there other inventions that do similar jobs better (reusable silicone bags, Tupperware, vacuum sealers)? Even briefly addressing and then countering these points would make your argument far more persuasive and show critical thinking rather than one-sided enthusiasm.

Improve Paragraph Organization

Your paragraphs are organized by *use case* (freshness, odor, travel), which is a reasonable structure, but the transitions are weak, and one paragraph undercuts itself. For instance, in the paragraph about odors, you offer three "solutions" but then joke that the third is to "bag everything!"—this humor is charming in conversation but weakens the logical force of your argument. Each paragraph should build toward your thesis, not trail off into a joke. Consider giving each paragraph a clear topic sentence that ties back to your criteria (eg, "The zip-lock bag also excels in *convenience*, particularly during travel.").

Reconsider Tone and Register

The conversational tone ("It is funny that we are writing on this subject," "(onlies???)") works well for a personal essay but may be too informal for an academic argumentative piece. You don't need to eliminate your voice entirely, but consider tightening the register: reduce asides, minimize rhetorical questions in the conclusion, and replace some casual phrasing with more precise, analytical language. This will help the essay feel authoritative rather than chatty.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates examples rather than synthesizing your argument or explaining its broader significance. Ending with "What more could you ask for?" is engaging but doesn't drive home *why* this invention matters in a larger sense. Consider closing with a statement about how small, unglamorous inventions like the zip-lock bag quietly improve daily life in ways more celebrated technologies don't—this elevates your argument beyond a personal endorsement.

Proofreading and Mechanics

There are a few punctuation and clarity issues to address:
Watch for comma splices and unnecessary commas (eg, "Gallon size zip-lock bags, solve this problem.")
"Earing" should be "earring."
Double periods and stray spacing errors should be cleaned up.
Consider varying sentence openings; several sentences begin with "And" or "But," which is fine occasionally for style but overused here.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Write a clear, arguable thesis with explicit criteria for "good invention."
Add at least one counterargument and rebuttal.
Tighten paragraph structure with strong topic sentences tied to your thesis.
Adjust tone to be more analytical while keeping some personal voice.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize rather than just recap.
Proofread carefully for mechanical errors.

You have great raw material here—personal, vivid, and enthusiastic. With a clearer argumentative structure and a bit more analytical depth, this essay could move from a charming anecdote collection to a genuinely persuasive piece.

Feedback on "The Computer as the Most Influential Invention"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging and defensible topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. The essay has a discernible structure (introduction, historical development, impact, and future implications), which is a good foundation. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of assertions than an *argument*. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your claims, support them with evidence, and address the historical and logical gaps that currently undermine your credibility.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim—that the computer is the most influential invention of the 20th century—is a strong, arguable thesis. However, it needs to be stated more precisely and defended more rigorously. Right now, the thesis simply asserts influence without explaining *why* the computer surpasses other transformative inventions of the century (the airplane, antibiotics, television, the internet as distinct from the PC, nuclear technology, etc). A stronger essay would briefly acknowledge one or two competing candidates and explain why the computer's influence is more far-reaching or foundational. This comparative move is what turns an assertion into an argument.

Evidence and Support

This is the area that most needs development. Currently, your claims are broad and unsupported:

"The productivity of people has increased ten-fold" — Where does this statistic come from? Even an approximate figure needs a source or at least a specific, illustrative example (eg, a comparison of typing/publishing speed before and after word processors, or the automation of specific business functions).
"Every day new and exciting improvements are being made" — This is vague. Which improvements? Give one or two concrete examples (eg, increases in processing speed, storage capacity, or specific applications like spreadsheets or databases).
The prediction about universal PC ownership linked by "cellular modem" to global databases is speculative and undefended. Predictions are fine in an argumentative essay, but they carry more weight when grounded in trends you've already established (eg, declining costs, increasing internet infrastructure) rather than asserted as inevitable fact.

As a revision strategy, try adding at least one concrete example, statistic, or brief case study to each paragraph. Even a single well-chosen fact (eg, the shift from mainframes costing millions to PCs costing a few hundred dollars) does far more persuasive work than a general claim.

Historical Accuracy

A few factual issues need correction, as they affect your credibility:

You state the computer is "an invention of the late 20th century," but early computers (ENIAC, UNIVAC) date to the 1940s, and the PC revolution began in the mid-to-late 1970s/1980s. You may want to clarify that you're discussing the computer's *evolution* across the century, or narrow your claim to the personal computer specifically.
Consider being more specific about "the first computers"—naming one (eg, ENIAC) would ground your historical narrative and show research.

Structure and Organization

Your paragraphing follows a logical chronological-to-thematic progression (past → present → future), which works well. However, each paragraph tries to cover too much ground too quickly. Consider:

Introduction: Sharpen your thesis and preview your main supporting points.
Historical development: Add specific dates, names, and figures.
Present impact: Focus this paragraph on *one* domain (business) with concrete evidence, rather than gesturing broadly at "business" and "data."
Future implications: Distinguish between reasonable extrapolation and speculation; cite a trend or expert prediction if possible.
Conclusion: Rather than ending on a stylistic flourish ("the future looks 'bright' indeed"), consider restating the significance of your argument and perhaps gesturing toward what's at stake if we don't understand this influence.

Style and Mechanics

A few technical issues to address in revision:

"Occured" should be "occurred."
"2st century" should be "21st century."
The scare quotes around "bright" at the end feel like an attempt at cleverness but come across as unclear—do you mean this literally, ironically, or metaphorically? Choose a clearer closing image.
Watch for comma splices and run-on constructions (eg, the long sentence about cellular modems and digital assistants). Breaking this into two sentences would improve clarity.
The essay would benefit from more varied sentence openings; several paragraphs begin with "The" or "This," which creates a slightly repetitive rhythm.

Suggestions for Revision

Narrow and sharpen your thesis to specify *why* the computer outranks other inventions.
Add at least 3–4 concrete pieces of evidence (statistics, examples, or named technologies) throughout the essay.
Correct historical inaccuracies and typos.
Consider briefly addressing a counterargument (eg, "Some might argue the internet or antibiotics had greater impact, but...") to strengthen your persuasive stance.
Revise the conclusion to end on a clearer, more substantive note rather than a stylistic flourish.

With these revisions, you'll transform this from a well-intentioned overview into a persuasive, evidence-based argument. You clearly have enthusiasm for the topic—now let's back that enthusiasm with the specificity and rigor it deserves.

Feedback on "AIDS and Society" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and emotionally resonant topic—how the AIDS epidemic reshaped public consciousness about mortality, discrimination, and sexual behavior. You clearly have real engagement with this history, and the Ryan White example is a strong, concrete anchor for your argument. However, the essay would benefit significantly from a clearer central argument, more rigorous organization, and more careful, precise language. Below are specific areas to focus on for revision.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Argument

Right now the essay moves through several related but distinct claims: AIDS changed how people think about mortality; society only cared once "innocent" victims (children) were affected; public health education followed; and finally, acceptance matters more than a cure. This is a lot of ground to cover, and the connections between these ideas aren't always explicit. Ask yourself: what is the *one* claim you want a reader to walk away with? 

For example, you might build the whole essay around this argument: *"Society's response to AIDS reveals a pattern of selective compassion—one that only shifted toward education and (partial) acceptance once the disease could no longer be dismissed as affecting only marginalized groups."* This would give you a spine to hang all your examples on, including Ryan White, IV drug users, and Magic Johnson, and would set up your conclusion about acceptance more logically.

Also reconsider the opening claim that AIDS was "discovered." A disease being recognized/named is different from a virus being scientifically discovered (HIV was identified in 1983-84). Being precise here strengthens your credibility as a writer engaging with medical/social history.

Strengthen Organization and Paragraph Unity

Each paragraph currently does several jobs at once. The second paragraph, for instance, jumps from Ryan White to public panic to the sex-education/condom debate to a claim about promiscuity—all in one paragraph. Consider breaking this into two or three paragraphs, each with a single clear function:
One paragraph solely on Ryan White and the "innocent victim" narrative.
One paragraph on the resulting public health education campaigns.
One paragraph on the ongoing controversy over abstinence-only vs. comprehensive sex education.

This will make your logic easier for readers to follow and will let you develop each point more fully rather than skimming past it.

Watch for Logical Gaps

A few claims need more support or reconsideration:
"Most people are more careful about who they sleep with, and AIDS has become less of a taboo subject" — this sentence conflates two separate claims (behavioral change and social attitude change) that don't obviously follow from each other. Consider separating and supporting each with evidence.
The claim that children are "becoming sexually active at age eleven" needs a source; without one, it reads as an assumption rather than evidence.
The transition from discussing children and Ryan White to discussing sex education in schools could be made more explicit—right now the reader has to infer the connection.

Add Evidence and Specificity

This essay would be much stronger with concrete evidence: dates, statistics, or references (even brief ones) to news coverage, CDC data, or scholarship on the AIDS epidemic's social history. For an argumentative essay, especially one making historical claims (eg, about early 1980s attitudes, or Ryan White's case), citing sources will lend authority to your argument and protect you from generalizations that could be challenged. Even one or two well-chosen statistics (eg, infection rates by 1990, timeline of federal funding for AIDS research) would considerably strengthen your credibility.

Revise for Sentence-Level Clarity

A few technical issues to address in your next draft:
Sentence fragments: "Even if a cure is never found." is a fragment—combine it with the surrounding sentence.
Ampersands: Avoid "&" in formal academic writing; spell out "and."
Wordy/awkward phrasing: "no one really cared that gays would die" is a strong claim—consider whether you want to soften this into something more analytical, like "public sympathy remained limited," unless you intend the bluntness for rhetorical effect (which can work, but use it deliberately, not by default).
Tone consistency: Some phrases ("How wrong they were") work well rhetorically but should be used sparingly so they retain their punch.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your final paragraph gestures toward an important idea—that acceptance matters more than a cure—but it arrives somewhat abruptly and could be developed into a more powerful closing argument. Consider explicitly returning to your thesis and explaining *why* acceptance matters more, and what specific steps toward acceptance you believe society still needs to take.

Summary

You have a compelling topic and some strong instincts (especially the Ryan White paragraph). Focus your revision on: (1) establishing one clear thesis, (2) tightening paragraph structure around single ideas, (3) adding evidence/citations, and (4) polishing sentence-level clarity. These changes will transform a passionate response into a rigorous argumentative essay.

Feedback on Your Essay: Twentieth-Century Inventions and Human Progress

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious and genuinely interesting question, and your personal investment in the topic comes through clearly. The core claim—that medical inventions are uniquely beneficial because they don't seek to dominate nature or other people—is a provocative and debatable thesis, which is exactly what a strong argumentative essay needs. However, the essay in its current form reads more like a sketch of an argument than a fully developed one. Below are suggestions for strengthening it.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your thesis is clear: medical inventions are the sole exception to an otherwise destructive pattern of twentieth-century innovation. This is a strong, specific claim—but it needs much more support. Right now, each paragraph asserts a broad generalization (technology destroys nature; technology is used to dominate others; medicine is uniquely kind) without offering concrete evidence. Ask yourself: what specific twentieth-century inventions am I thinking of? The essay would be far more persuasive if you selected 2-3 concrete examples per category:

For "dominating nature": automobiles and fossil fuel combustion, industrial agriculture, plastics, nuclear power
For "dominating other people": nuclear weapons, surveillance technology, assembly-line manufacturing and labor exploitation, propaganda/mass media
For medical inventions: antibiotics (penicillin), vaccines (polio), insulin, organ transplantation, X-rays

Concrete examples will transform your assertions into an argument readers can actually evaluate.

The Psychological Premise Needs Support

Your opening paragraph makes a bold psychological claim: that all invention stems from "fear of death" and "human insecurities." This is an interesting Freudian-sounding premise, but you present it as self-evident when it's actually quite contestable. Some questions to consider:

Could inventions also stem from curiosity, creativity, or desire for connection rather than fear?
Is medicine's origin in "fear of death" truly different from, say, weapons technology's origin in fear of death (self-defense)?

If this premise is central to your argument, it needs either a citation to a thinker who makes this claim (existentialist philosophy, perhaps Ernest Becker's *The Denial of Death*, would be a natural fit) or a more careful, argued defense in your own words. As written, it risks feeling asserted rather than argued.

A Key Logical Tension to Address

There's a tension in your reasoning that a careful reader will notice: you claim medical inventions stem from the same fear of death as other technologies, yet somehow escape the domination impulse that (in your account) characterizes all other invention. Why? You gesture at an answer ("they do not attempt to dominate nature nor other human beings"), but this needs to be argued, not just stated. For instance:

Could medical inventions be used to dominate? (Consider eugenics, unequal access to healthcare, medical experimentation, pharmaceutical industry profit motives)
If medicine can be misused, does your absolute claim ("cannot be used to dominate") hold up?

Engaging with this complication—even if you ultimately conclude medicine is still net positive—would make your argument more sophisticated and harder to refute.

Counterarguments

The essay would benefit significantly from acknowledging opposing views. A skeptical reader might argue that other twentieth-century inventions (sanitation systems, birth control, communication technologies that connect isolated people, agricultural advances that reduced famine) have also improved lives without primarily serving domination. Addressing at least one strong counterexample and explaining why it doesn't undermine your thesis would strengthen your credibility.

Organization

Currently, the essay has four short paragraphs, each stating a claim rather than developing it. Consider restructuring around your evidence:
Introduction with thesis
Nature-domination argument, with 2-3 examples
Human-domination argument, with 2-3 examples
Why medicine is different, addressing the potential counterargument that medicine can also dominate
Conclusion that returns to your opening reflection on human nature

Style and Mechanics

Watch for typos: "occured" → occurred; "affect" → effect (as a noun, "effect" is almost always correct); "0the century" → 20th century; "truely" → truly
"Interestingly enough" is a weak opening phrase—consider starting with your thesis or a provocative claim instead
Some sentences repeat ideas (the domination-of-nature and domination-of-people points echo the introduction almost verbatim in body paragraphs)—use body paragraphs to *develop*, not just restate

Summary of Priorities

Add concrete historical examples throughout
Support or complicate your psychological premise about fear and invention
Address whether medicine itself can be used to dominate
Incorporate at least one counterargument
Proofread for typos and word choice

Your instinct to argue a bold, specific thesis is a real strength—now it's time to give that thesis the evidence and complexity it deserves.

Feedback Report on Your Essay: The Satellite Dish and Global Communication

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and ambitious topic—the impact of satellite technology on global communication—but it needs significant revision to meet the standards of academic argumentative writing. The core issue is that this piece reads more like a collection of loosely related observations than a focused, evidence-based argument. Below, I outline the main areas for improvement.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

An argumentative essay requires a clear, debatable thesis that you defend throughout. Currently, your essay doesn't take a clear stance. The opening paragraph says the satellite dish "has changed the meaning of telecommunication," but your conclusion retreats into vague relativism: "The pro's and con's...are numerous. It is up to the average person to decide." This is not an argument—it's an abdication of one.

Revision suggestion: Choose a specific, arguable claim. For example: "While satellite technology has democratized access to information, its concentration in the hands of a few media conglomerates has created new forms of global inequality" or "Satellite technology has fundamentally altered the balance of power between nation-states and multinational media corporations." Whatever you choose, every paragraph should build toward proving that claim.

Structure and Coherence

The essay jumps between topics—CNN, corporate executives, MTV, cable TV's effect on intelligence, naval programs, and "STARWARS"—without clear transitions or logical connections. Each paragraph reads as a separate mini-topic rather than a building block of a larger argument.

Revision suggestion: Organize body paragraphs around 2-3 major supporting points for your thesis, not around every possible application of satellite technology. Cutting material is often as important as adding it. The paragraph on naval programs and "STARWARS," for instance, is only two sentences and feels like an afterthought—either develop it fully and connect it to your thesis, or remove it.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's weakest area. Claims are asserted but not substantiated:

"40% of U.S. millionaires are entertainers" — What is the source? How does this connect to satellite technology specifically, rather than television or media generally?
"The intelligence of an average teenager is lowering itself due to cable viewing" — This is a strong claim requiring rigorous evidence (studies, statistics, expert testimony), not assertion.
"Decisions of governmental autonomy are being made based on that night's news CNN commentator" — This is a fascinating claim but needs a specific example (a named crisis, decision, or quotation from a political figure or scholar).

Revision suggestion: For every claim, ask yourself "How do I know this?" and "Would a skeptical reader accept this without a citation?" Academic writing requires you to show your sources, whether through direct citation, paraphrase with attribution, or reference to specific, verifiable events.

Tone and Register

The opening image—comparing a satellite dish to "1/2 egg...as large as a small FIAT"—is vivid but too informal and imprecise for academic writing. Similarly, phrases like "subtitling the Information Age, with the MTV age" are unclear and colloquial.

Revision suggestion: Aim for precise, formal language. Save your creativity for how you frame arguments and interpret evidence, not for informal analogies that distract from your point.

Mechanics and Proofreading

There are numerous errors that undermine your credibility:

"it's center" should be "its center"
"recieve" should be "receive"
"millionares" should be "millionaires"
"panasonic" and "CORE" should be capitalized (Panasonic; also, what is CORE? This needs clarification)
"pro's and con's" should be "pros and cons"
"people lives" should be "people's lives"
"created in the last 0 years" appears to be a typo—did you mean a specific number?

Revision suggestion: Proofread carefully, ideally reading the essay aloud or having a peer review it. Many of these errors are easily fixed and will significantly improve the professionalism of your writing.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Develop a clear, specific, arguable thesis and ensure every paragraph supports it.
Cut unrelated tangents (naval programs, space programs) unless you can connect them meaningfully to your argument.
Support every claim with evidence—statistics need sources, and generalizations need specific examples.
Formalize your tone and eliminate colloquialisms.
Proofread thoroughly for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

This essay has an interesting core idea. With a sharper thesis and stronger evidence, you can transform these scattered observations into a compelling argument. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to discuss your thesis with me before your next draft.
