Feedback Report: Draft Essay on Television's Cultural Impact

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious and interesting topic—the transformative social impact of television—and you've organized it around a clear three-part thesis (mass culturization, commercialization, and information acceleration). This structure is a real strength: each body paragraph corresponds logically to a claim in your introduction. However, the essay would benefit from more rigorous argumentation, stronger evidence, and careful proofreading before it's ready for submission. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis is promising but could be sharpened. Right now it reads as a list of three effects without an overarching claim about *what these effects mean*. Consider adding a unifying argument: Are these three trends evidence that television is fundamentally a homogenizing force? A commercializing one? Your conclusion gestures toward an evaluative claim ("television is a powerful and dangerous tool... we must watch what we watch"), but this insight arrives too late. Introducing this critical stance in your thesis would give the essay a sharper analytical edge rather than reading as a descriptive survey.

Evidence and Development

This is the area needing the most attention. Your claims are frequently asserted but not substantiated:

Paragraph 2: You claim television created "mass culturalization" through shows like *Leave It to Beaver* and *Green Acres*, but you don't cite any scholarship on cultivation theory or audience studies, nor do you provide viewership statistics or historical context beyond your own generalizations. Consider consulting media historians (eg, Lynn Spigel's work on television and domestic culture) to ground this claim.
Paragraph 3 is quite thin—only three sentences. The claim that advertising "developed into an art form" and that "15 second blocks... are sold for millions" needs a source and more analysis. What is the *effect* of this commercialization on consumer behavior, political discourse, or children's development? Right now this paragraph asserts a trend without examining its consequences.
Paragraph 4 (on Vietnam) is your strongest, because it uses a specific, well-known historical example. Expand this kind of specificity elsewhere. You could also address counterarguments here—some historians argue television's role in shifting Vietnam-era opinion is overstated compared to other factors (mounting casualties, the draft, elite dissent). Engaging this complexity would strengthen your credibility.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay reads well at the macro level, but individual paragraphs sometimes drift. In paragraph 2, you move from 1950s sitcoms to contemporary portrayals of "Ghetto life" without a clear transition or critical framing—this comparison needs unpacking, since it touches on issues of racial representation that deserve careful, non-generalized treatment. Be cautious with phrases like "to white people, Ghetto life is slightly less of a mystery," which risks oversimplifying a complex issue of media representation and racial perception. Consider revising this to explicitly discuss stereotyping, tokenism, or selective representation rather than implying television offers unmediated access to "reality."

Style and Clarity

Your prose has energy, but several stylistic issues need fixing:

Title/Opening: "The 0th Century" is a typo—presumably you mean "20th Century." Fix this before anything else, as it's the very first thing a reader sees.
Abbreviations: You use "&," "w/," and "w/o" throughout, which is appropriate for notes but not for formal academic prose. Spell these out.
Spelling/typos: "grupes" (groups), "breath" (breadth), "inhance" (enhance), "livingrooms" (living rooms), "the united States" (capitalize United States), "vietnam" (capitalize Vietnam). A careful proofreading pass is essential.
Repetition: "Populuxe" and "must-have ideology" are evocative terms, but they appear without explanation—define this term or cite where it comes from (it appears to reference Thomas Hine's *Populuxe*).

Citations

Currently, the essay contains no citations at all, despite making historical and sociological claims (eg, about the 1950s, about Vietnam-era media coverage, about advertising economics). Even in an argumentative essay, empirical claims need support. Adding 4–6 scholarly or journalistic sources would substantially raise the essay's credibility.

Conclusion

Your closing paragraph contains your most interesting idea—television as simultaneously beneficial and manipulative—but it's underdeveloped. This tension deserves to be the organizing principle of the whole essay, not just a final flourish. Consider revising your introduction to preview this idea, then use each body paragraph to show both the benefit and the risk of each trend you discuss.

Priority Revisions

Fix the title typo and proofread throughout.
Expand paragraph 3 significantly.
Add scholarly sources to support key claims.
Revise the "Ghetto life" passage for more careful, nuanced language.
Integrate your concluding insight (manipulation vs. stimulation) into your thesis.

You have a solid conceptual foundation here—the revision work is mostly about deepening evidence and tightening language.

Feedback Report: The Fall of the Berlin Wall Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay is emotionally resonant and clearly written from a place of genuine personal investment. The opening scene-setting is vivid, and the anecdote about your classmate's father adds a powerful human dimension to a historical event. However, as an argumentative essay, this draft needs significant revision to make its central claim clear, support that claim with evidence, and organize the piece around argumentation rather than narrative recollection.

Thesis and Argument

Your actual argument—that the fall of the Berlin Wall was "the catalyst force that led to the end of the Cold War"—doesn't appear until the final paragraph. In an argumentative essay, readers should know your thesis early, ideally by the end of the introduction. Right now, the personal narrative dominates, and the argument feels tacked on at the end almost as an afterthought.

Revision suggestion: Consider restructuring so your introduction establishes the claim you intend to defend (eg, "The fall of the Berlin Wall was not merely a symbolic moment but the decisive catalyst that triggered the collapse of communism across Eastern Europe"), and then use your personal experience and historical evidence to support that claim throughout.

Clarity of Reasoning

There is a confusing sentence that undermines your argument: "Without the 'events of Berlin Wall' history probably would not have taken a very different course. Eventually, I think that things would have turned out as they did." This seems to contradict itself—first suggesting the Wall's fall *did* change history's course, then suggesting history would have unfolded the same way regardless. If this is a typo (perhaps you meant "would *not* have taken the same course" or "would have taken a *very different* course"), please clarify, because this is actually the crux of your argument. You need to explicitly explain *why* the Wall's fall was causally significant—did it inspire other nations through demonstrated success? Did it remove a key psychological or strategic barrier? Right now this causal mechanism is asserted but not argued.

Evidence and Support

Your personal anecdotes are compelling, but an argumentative essay about a historical/political claim needs more than personal testimony. Consider incorporating:
Specific dates and sequence of events in Hungary, Czechoslovakia, Poland, and Romania to show the causal chain you're claiming
Historical context about *why* the Wall's fall had ripple effects (eg, Gorbachev's policies, Soviet unwillingness to intervene militarily, media coverage inspiring other populations)
A counterargument you can address—was the Wall's fall truly the catalyst, or was it itself a symptom of forces already underway (Gorbachev's reforms, economic collapse, Solidarity in Poland predating 1989)? Acknowledging and responding to this alternative view would strengthen your argument considerably.

Structure and Organization

The essay reads as three loosely connected movements: (1) a narrative of the day itself, (2) reflections on the classmate's father and the human cost of communism, (3) a claim about historical causation. Consider organizing around your argument instead:

Introduction with clear thesis
Body paragraph(s) establishing the human stakes (your personal anecdotes fit well here as emotional evidence)
Body paragraph(s) tracing the specific causal chain from Berlin to other Eastern Bloc uprisings
Counterargument and rebuttal
Conclusion returning to the thesis with its broader significance

Style and Mechanics

Punctuation: You use dashes (--) extensively where periods, semicolons, or commas would be more appropriate. This makes some sentences feel run-on and reduces clarity. Reserve dashes for genuine interruptions or emphasis.
Spelling: Please correct "mesmirized" (mesmerized), "Tchekoslovaquia" (Czechoslovakia), and "Roumania" (Romania).
Tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense somewhat abruptly (eg, "our now as-President Bush" is awkward phrasing—clarify whether Bush was president at the time of writing or the event).
Word choice: "as-President" should likely be "President" or "then-President," depending on when you're writing relative to his term.

Final Thoughts

You have real material here—a vivid personal connection to a major historical turning point. The revision task is to let your argument lead and your narrative serve that argument, rather than the reverse. Tighten your thesis, clarify your causal reasoning (especially that confusing sentence near the end), add historical specificity, and address a counterargument. With these changes, this could become a genuinely persuasive piece rather than simply a moving personal reflection.

Feedback on "The Television" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an engaging topic—television as a transformative twentieth-century invention—and you clearly have some interesting ideas about its power to connect the world. However, the essay would benefit significantly from a clearer thesis, more developed argumentation, and careful proofreading. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim seems to be that television is *the* most significant invention of the twentieth century, but this thesis needs sharpening. In your opening paragraph, you say television "stands out a little more among its competition"—this is vague and hedges too much for an argumentative essay. State your claim with more conviction: *why* is television more significant than, say, the A-bomb or antibiotics? Right now, the essay doesn't directly engage with or refute other contenders, which weakens the comparative claim you're making.

Additionally, your final paragraph introduces a *different* argument—that television is a power requiring control and responsibility—that doesn't fully connect back to your original thesis about television's significance. Is your essay arguing that television is important, or that it is dangerous and must be regulated? These are related but distinct claims, and you need to decide which one you're making (or how to unify them) before revising further.

Structure and Development

The essay's structure is logical (historical context → television's advantages → its global reach → its power/risks), but each paragraph needs deeper development:

Paragraph 2 (radio/newspaper era) is mostly descriptive scene-setting. Consider tightening this and moving more quickly to your actual argument.
Paragraph 3 (television's addition of images) makes a reasonable point but relies on assertion rather than evidence. *How* does adding images change absorption or understanding? Can you point to a specific historical example (eg, the Kennedy-Nixon debates, the moon landing, Vietnam War coverage) that demonstrates this?
Paragraph 4 (global connection) is promising but underdeveloped. The Olympic and presidential examples are good instincts, but you assert "the examples could be endless" instead of actually providing more examples or explaining these two in depth. Show your reasoning rather than gesturing at it.
Paragraph 5 shifts tone toward caution/warning, which feels like a new essay. If you want to end on the theme of responsibility, you need a paragraph earlier that sets up this concern, so the conclusion doesn't feel abrupt.

Suggestion: Consider adding one paragraph with concrete historical evidence (a specific broadcast, a statistic about television ownership growth, or a scholarly/journalistic source on television's cultural impact) to ground your claims. Right now, the essay relies entirely on general assertions and hypothetical examples (a "small town store owner" and her son) rather than researched evidence, which weakens its persuasive power.

Style and Clarity

Avoid absolute language you can't support: "the examples could be endless" is a shortcut around actual argumentation.
The play on "peak"/"peek" and "vision in television" (as wordplay on "television") are clever but risk feeling gimmicky in a formal argumentative essay. Use such devices sparingly and make sure they land clearly.
Some sentences run together or lack clear subject-verb agreement (eg, "The television strength must be realized" should be "The television's strength" or "Television's strength").
Watch for comma splices and fragments, such as "A system that seemed to work, many households throughout the nation owned..." — this needs restructuring into a complete sentence.

Mechanics: Proofreading Needed

There are several spelling and typographical errors that need correction before this essay is submission-ready:
"vertually" → virtually
"boarders" → borders
"absorbtion" → absorption
"spening" → spending
"necesary" → necessary
"olympic" → Olympic (capitalize)
"one lives easier" → "our lives easier" (or "one's life")

I recommend reading the essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these errors systematically.

Revision Priorities

For your next draft, focus on these three things:

Sharpen your thesis so it clearly states one claim (either television's *significance* or its *need for control*) and carry that claim through every paragraph.
Add concrete evidence—historical examples, statistics, or expert commentary—to support your assertions rather than relying on hypothetical scenarios and vague claims.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and sentence structure issues.

You have a genuinely interesting angle here—television's role in "bringing worlds together"—and with sharper focus and stronger evidence, this essay could make a compelling case. I look forward to seeing your revision.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Computer's Impact on Society

Overall Impression

Your essay demonstrates enthusiasm for your topic and traces an interesting history of computing technology. However, in its current form, this reads more like an informative/descriptive essay than an *argumentative* one. To meet the assignment's requirements, you'll need to sharpen your central claim and support it with evidence rather than general assertions.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis—that the computer "has had a great impact on how society and especially business gathers, creates, responds to, and understands everyday information"—is too broad and not really debatable. Few readers would disagree with this claim, which means there's no real argument to make. Ask yourself: what is the *contestable* claim you want to defend? For example:

Has the computer's impact been primarily beneficial, or has it created new problems (dependency, inequality of access, job displacement)?
Is the personal computer *the* decisive turning point compared to other candidates (the internet, the smartphone)?
Has increased access to information genuinely improved understanding, or merely increased volume without improving comprehension?

Choosing a narrower, arguable thesis will give your essay direction and make your evidence purposeful rather than descriptive.

Structure and Organization

The essay currently reads as chronological narrative: early computers → the 1980 PC → later advances → conclusion. This structure works for a history paper but not for argumentation. Consider organizing by *reasons* that support your thesis rather than by decade. Each paragraph should open with a topic sentence stating a specific claim (a sub-argument), followed by evidence and analysis of *why* that evidence supports your point.

Paragraph 3, in particular, tries to cover too much ground—the 1980 PC, increased speed, modems, portability, faxes—without pausing to analyze any single development in depth. Consider breaking this into two or three focused paragraphs, each with its own clear point.

Evidence and Development

This is the area needing the most attention. The essay currently relies entirely on general assertions ("It has made our lives easier," "It is a very powerful tool indeed") without concrete evidence: no statistics, no historical dates verified against sources, no expert testimony, no specific examples of how businesses or individuals used these tools. Academic argumentative writing requires you to *support* claims, not just state them. Some suggestions:

Cite specific sources on the history of computing (verify the 1980/1981 IBM PC release date, for instance).
Use concrete examples: a specific company, industry, or individual whose work was transformed.
Address a counterargument. For instance, some might argue that increased access to information has led to information overload or misinformation rather than better understanding. Engaging with this complicates and strengthens your argument.

Style and Language

You rely heavily on repeated phrasing—"receive information," "process information," "much more"—without variation. Read your paragraphs aloud to identify redundancy, and consult a thesaurus or simply rephrase to avoid repeating the same verbs and structures.

Watch for informal, conversational phrasing ("It is a very powerful tool indeed," "just about any task you need it for," "just about anything that you had to do"). Academic writing calls for more precise, formal diction. Replace vague quantifiers like "many," "much," "any" with specific claims and examples.

Some sentences drift into second person ("you can sit in your home," "you had to do")—this is common in conversational writing but should generally be avoided in formal academic prose. Consider third person ("users could," "individuals were able to") for consistency and formality.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few issues to correct in your next draft:

Subject-verb agreement: "There has been many inventions" should be "There have been many inventions."
Spelling: "recieve" should be "receive" (appears twice).
Capitalization: "Modem" should not be capitalized mid-sentence.
Comma splices/run-ons: Several sentences string together multiple clauses with commas where periods or semicolons would improve clarity (eg, the long sentence beginning "What the PC...").
Date accuracy: Double-check the IBM PC release date (1981, not 1980) and cite your source.

Priorities for Revision

Narrow your thesis into a specific, debatable claim.
Reorganize paragraphs around reasons/evidence rather than chronology.
Add concrete evidence—sources, statistics, examples—to support each claim.
Address a counterargument to strengthen your position.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and repetitive phrasing.

With a sharper argumentative focus and stronger evidence, this essay has good potential. Focus your revision on *what specific claim you want a reader to be persuaded of*, and build every paragraph around supporting that claim.

Feedback on "The Century of Democracy" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay has an engaging, personal voice and a clear emotional investment in your topic. However, as an *argumentative* essay, it currently reads more like a personal reflection or journal entry than a piece that builds and defends a claim. Below are suggestions organized by area of concern.

Thesis and Scope

Your central claim—that the 20th century should be called "The Century of Democracy"—is ambitious and interesting, but it needs to be earned through evidence, not simply asserted. Right now, your argument rests almost entirely on events from 1989–1992 (the fall of the Berlin Wall, German reunification, the collapse of the Soviet Union). This is a narrow evidentiary base for a claim about an entire century.

Suggestion: Either narrow your thesis to focus specifically on the late 20th century's democratic transitions (which is what you actually discuss), or broaden your evidence to address democratic developments across the whole century—the expansion of suffrage, decolonization movements, the fall of fascism after WWII, the end of apartheid, etc As written, there's a mismatch between the scope of your claim and the scope of your evidence.

Balancing Personal Narrative with Argument

Your personal anecdotes (buying a piece of the Berlin Wall, feeling patriotic on election day) are vivid and humanizing, but they function as *illustrations* of your feelings rather than *evidence* for your argument. An argumentative essay needs to do more analytical work: Why do these events matter historically? What makes democracy specifically responsible for increased peace or prosperity? You assert connections (eg, "with the end of the Cold War, there has been more peaceful relations") without explaining or supporting the causal link.

Suggestion: After each anecdote, add a sentence or two of analysis connecting the specific event to your broader thesis. Ask yourself "so what?" after each example.

Addressing Counterarguments

A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections. Right now, your essay presents a fairly one-sided, celebratory narrative. Consider:
Not all democratic transitions in this period were peaceful or complete (ethnic conflicts emerged in the former Yugoslavia and elsewhere after the Cold War ended).
The 20th century also contained two world wars, genocides, and authoritarian regimes that could complicate a purely triumphant "Century of Democracy" narrative.

Suggestion: Include at least one paragraph acknowledging a counterpoint or complication, then explain why your thesis still holds despite it. This will make your argument more persuasive and sophisticated.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your introduction is long and somewhat meandering—it takes several sentences before we reach your thesis. The list of decades ("Roaring 20s," "Great Depression," etc) is a nice rhetorical device, but consider tightening it so it doesn't delay your main point.

Additionally, several paragraphs (on Germany, the Soviet Union, and the end of the Cold War) are quite short and could be combined or developed further with more historical detail and analysis.

Suggestion: Consider a structure like this:
Brief hook + thesis (tightened)
Body: Democratic transitions in Europe (Germany, USSR) with analysis
Body: Broader global consequences (reduced arms spending, decreased nuclear threat) with more concrete evidence/statistics
Body: Acknowledge complications or counterarguments
Conclusion: Synthesize argument, avoid repeating the introduction

Evidence and Specificity

Claims like "arms sales have gone down" would benefit from specific data or a citation. Since this is an argumentative essay, concrete facts (statistics, dates, named events) will strengthen your credibility far more than general impressions.

Style and Tone

The essay uses informal, conversational language ("How could we ever forget...", "I'm feeling very patriotic these days") that may not suit an academic argumentative essay. Consider whether this tone matches your assignment's expectations.
Watch sentence fragments, such as "For change is inevitable, and as difficult as it may be..." This reads as a fragment or run-on; revise for clarity.
The repeated "Perhaps..." structure in paragraph two is a nice stylistic touch, but make sure each "perhaps" clearly supports your argument rather than just building atmosphere.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates your feelings rather than synthesizing your argument or leaving the reader with a strengthened sense of your thesis. Consider ending with a forward-looking claim: what does calling this the "Century of Democracy" mean for how we should think about the future, or how should this framing change our understanding of the century's other events?

Summary of Priority Revisions
Clarify and possibly narrow your thesis to match your evidence.
Add analytical sentences connecting anecdotes to your argument.
Incorporate a counterargument for balance.
Tighten the introduction and strengthen the conclusion.
Add concrete data where possible.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a heartfelt personal reflection toward a more rigorous, persuasive argumentative essay.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and timely topic, and your passion for environmental protection comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more as an emotional appeal than a structured academic argument. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your central claim, support your assertions with evidence, and tighten your organization.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your essay lacks a clear, arguable thesis statement. Are you arguing that nuclear energy should be banned entirely? That we should transition to alternative energy sources? That world leaders are failing in their environmental responsibilities? Right now, the essay touches on all of these but commits to none. Choose one specific claim and make it the backbone of your essay. For example: "Despite its practical benefits, nuclear energy poses risks that outweigh its advantages, and world leaders must prioritize transitioning to safer alternatives."

Additionally, your essay conflates two distinct issues: (1) nuclear weapons (the atomic bomb) and (2) nuclear energy production (power plants). These have very different risk profiles, historical contexts, and policy implications. Decide which one is your primary focus, or clearly explain how they relate to your argument.

Factual Accuracy

There's a significant historical error here that needs correction: Oppenheimer did not "invent" the atomic bomb in 1945, nor did he single-handedly "unveil" it to the world. He directed the Manhattan Project's scientific team, but the bomb was a collaborative effort involving thousands of scientists. The bombs were deployed by the U.S. military on Hiroshima and Nagasaki in August 1945. Precision with historical facts is essential for credibility in academic writing.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's greatest weakness. You make several serious claims without supporting evidence:
"Killing our species as well as all species on earth" — this is a significant claim requiring scientific support
"Holes in our ozone layer" caused by nuclear energy — the connection between nuclear power and ozone depletion needs clarification, as this is not a primary environmental concern associated with nuclear plants (radioactive waste and contamination risks are more accurate concerns)
"Very few efforts" by world leaders — what efforts *have* been made? (Consider researching the Paris Agreement, IAEA safety protocols, or specific countries' nuclear policies)

Revision suggestion: For each major claim, find at least one credible source (scientific journal, reputable news outlet, or government report) to substantiate your point. This will transform your essay from opinion into argument.

Addressing Counterarguments

A strong argumentative essay acknowledges opposing viewpoints. You mention that nuclear energy "supplies and energizes the world," but you don't seriously engage with this benefit. Consider addressing:
Nuclear energy's low carbon emissions compared to fossil fuels
Its reliability compared to some renewable sources
Why, despite these benefits, the risks still outweigh them (if that's your position)

Engaging fairly with counterarguments will make your own position more persuasive, not weaker.

Tone and Language

Your emotional investment is evident, but phrases like "the saddest part of this tragedy," "appalling," and "what is to become of our children" tip into melodrama that can undermine credibility in academic writing. Aim for measured, precise language that lets your evidence and logic carry the emotional weight. Compare:

*Original:* "The lack of sight from our leaders is almost appalling."
*Revised:* "Despite mounting evidence of environmental risk, international policy on nuclear energy regulation remains fragmented and insufficiently enforced."

Structural and Mechanical Issues

Title/opening: "Atomic energy" doesn't need capitalization; watch for repeated words ("the the")
Paragraph transitions: Each paragraph currently stands somewhat independently; use transitional phrases to show logical connections
Conclusion: "Write your local congressman" is a call to action appropriate for a persuasive speech or op-ed, but feels abrupt here. Consider ending with a stronger synthesis of your argument instead, or frame the whole essay as building toward this call to action from the start
Proofreading: Watch for typos ("routed" should be "rooted," "irreversable" should be "irreversible")

Next Steps

Draft a clear, one-sentence thesis statement
Decide whether your focus is nuclear weapons, nuclear energy, or both (and if both, clarify their relationship)
Research 3-4 credible sources to support your key claims
Add a paragraph addressing the strongest counterargument
Revise emotional language into more analytical prose
Proofread carefully for typos and word repetition

This topic has real potential for a compelling essay—the raw material of concern and conviction is there. Now it needs the scaffolding of evidence, precision, and structure to become a persuasive academic argument.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic—the influence of television as a 20th-century invention—and you clearly have valuable insights to offer. However, the essay would benefit from significant revision in several areas: thesis clarity, argumentative structure, development of evidence, and treatment of counterarguments. Below is detailed feedback organized by area.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction promises an argument about television's downsides ("There are, however, several downsides to the television, and, like all inventions can be used in a useless way"), but the body paragraphs almost entirely celebrate television's positive effects. This creates a mismatch between what you promise and what you deliver. Ask yourself: is your actual argument that television is overwhelmingly beneficial *despite* some drawbacks? Or are you trying to present a balanced view? Right now, the essay reads as one-sided despite gesturing toward complexity.

Revision suggestion: State your thesis explicitly in one clear sentence, such as: "While television has been criticized for encouraging passive viewership and desensitization, its power to compress global distances and inform public consciousness makes it the most significant communicative invention of the 20th century." This gives your reader a clear argumentative claim to track through the essay.

Structural Issues

The essay's structure doesn't fully support argumentative writing. Currently, it moves from a general claim about communication, to television's role in war coverage, to television's "shrinking" effect on the globe, with counterarguments mentioned only in passing, almost as an afterthought in the final paragraph ("There are many who feel that it can be a harmful thing, and this is true"). This sentence introduces a counterargument but doesn't develop it—you concede the point and move on without explaining *why* people feel this way or engaging with their reasoning.

Revision suggestion: Consider dedicating a full paragraph to counterarguments (eg, desensitization, passive consumption, misinformation, or television's role in trivializing serious events). Explain the counterargument fully, then respond to it directly. This will strengthen your credibility and demonstrate critical engagement rather than surface acknowledgment.

Development of Evidence

Your examples—the Vietnam War and Somalia—are promising but underdeveloped. You assert that Vietnam coverage "led to much of the protesting" and that Somalia coverage led to "public concern and effort," but you don't explain *how* or provide specific details (dates, images, events, statistics, or scholarly claims) that would make these claims persuasive. Right now, these examples function more as gestures toward evidence than actual evidence.

Revision suggestion: Choose one example and develop it fully with specific details. For instance, you might discuss the 1968 Tet Offensive coverage and cite scholarship on the "living room war" phenomenon, or reference specific footage from Somalia that shaped U.S. intervention policy (this connects to what is sometimes called the "CNN effect" in media studies—research on this concept could strengthen your argument significantly).

Logical Gaps

Some claims need clearer reasoning. For example, you state that television "changed how we live" and "shrinks the planet," but the connection between these ideas and your specific examples isn't always explicit. How exactly does showing war footage "shrink the planet"? Does it create empathy, awareness, political pressure, or something else? Spelling out these causal links will make your argument more rigorous.

Additionally, the claim that television "desensitizes" viewers because "the picture itself is not real" needs clarification—what do you mean by "not real"? Do you mean it's mediated, selective, or edited? This is a substantive point that deserves more precise language and explanation.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Capitalize "Vietnam" (proper noun).
Revise awkward phrasing: "Not even truly dealing with the amount of television that is watched by the average person" is unclear—consider revising to something like "Setting aside questions about how much television people watch..."
Watch for wordiness: "the most frequently used and influential invention of the field" could be simplified to "the most influential invention in this field."
Vary sentence openings—several paragraphs begin with generic transitions ("One interesting development," "The greatest effect").

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify your thesis so it accurately reflects the argument you actually make.
Develop a genuine counterargument section, engaging fully with television's downsides rather than mentioning them briefly.
Expand your evidence with specific historical details and, if possible, scholarly support.
Strengthen causal reasoning—explain *how* and *why* television produces the effects you claim.
Proofread carefully for mechanical errors and wordy constructions.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a series of interesting observations toward a more focused, well-supported argument. You have a strong topic—now it needs a sharper argumentative backbone.

Overall Impression

The personal anecdote about visiting Spain is a nice touch that shows you're trying to connect abstract historical claims to lived experience. However, the essay currently reads more as a list of observations than a developed argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Argument

Right now, your introduction makes three separate claims—travel, perception of the world, and the concept of flying itself—but doesn't unify them into a single, arguable thesis. An argumentative essay needs a claim that could reasonably be contested, which you then defend with evidence and reasoning. Consider narrowing your focus: Are you arguing that the airplane's *primary* impact was cultural (broadening worldviews), economic (international trade), or something else? Choose one central claim and let every paragraph serve it.

Also, you introduce the idea of "flying" as a formerly impossible concept in your final introductory sentence, but you never return to this idea in the body of the essay. Either develop it as a paragraph of its own or remove it from the introduction—right now it creates an expectation the essay doesn't fulfill.

Strengthen Paragraph Development

Your second paragraph is your strongest because it includes a personal example. However, the example needs tighter integration. You transition from discussing pre-airplane travel dangers to discussing what you learned about your own culture in Spain—but you never explicitly connect this back to air travel. Did you fly to Spain? How did the *speed and accessibility* of air travel (versus the boat travel you describe) make this personal, cultural realization possible? Make that causal link explicit.

Your third paragraph on international relations is underdeveloped. You assert that the airplane improved diplomacy and trade, but you offer no specific example, historical moment, or data point. Consider adding something concrete: a reference to how leaders now conduct summits in person, or how just-in-time global supply chains depend on air freight, or a historical contrast (eg, how long negotiations took before air travel versus after). Even one or two specific, researched examples would greatly strengthen your credibility here.

Add a Conclusion

The essay currently ends abruptly with "Life with airplanes is more rapid paced." This reads like a stray thought rather than a conclusion. Add a final paragraph that synthesizes your argument, restates your thesis in light of the evidence you've presented, and perhaps gestures toward broader significance (eg, how this connects to globalization today, or what might be lost if air travel were suddenly unavailable).

There are several sentence-level issues worth addressing:

"The extend to which" should be "The extent to which."
"a machine: the concept of flying" — this sentence is grammatically tangled. Consider simplifying: "It also introduced a concept once thought impossible for humans without mechanical assistance: flight."
"The frequency that which foreign countries..." should be "The frequency with which foreign countries..."
Several sentences are overloaded with semicolons and clauses (eg, "One could travel by boat but this was inconvenient and often dangerous because of various things like disease, hunger, and threatening weather; also as noted above, it was extremely time consuming"). Break these into two clearer sentences.
Avoid vague intensifiers like "astronomical" and "various things." Replace with specific, concrete language.
Watch for repetition of the "doors" metaphor (openings doors to travel, opened many doors...). It's fine once, but repeated use starts to feel like a filler phrase rather than precise language.

Consider Using Outside Evidence

For a stronger argumentative essay, you'll want at least some historical or statistical evidence beyond personal experience—for example, statistics on how travel time between continents changed, historical context about the first commercial flights, or examples of specific international agreements/summits made possible by air travel. This will elevate the essay from personal reflection to persuasive argument.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a single, focused thesis statement.
Either develop or remove the "flying" concept introduced early on.
Add concrete evidence (historical or statistical) to the international relations paragraph.
Explicitly connect your Spain anecdote back to air travel.
Write a conclusion paragraph.
Proofread for grammar and sentence clarity, especially around extended clauses.

You have a solid foundation and a genuine, personal investment in this topic—now it's a matter of tightening the argument and supporting it with more concrete evidence. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: AIDS and Education

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and complex topic—the role of education in combating AIDS when medical treatment falls short. This is a promising angle, but the essay currently reads more like a collection of related thoughts than a unified argument. With revision focused on thesis clarity, organization, and evidence, this could become a much stronger piece.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim seems to be that education, rather than medicine alone, is essential to preventing the spread of AIDS. This is a solid, arguable thesis, but it isn't stated clearly or early enough. The introduction currently focuses on medical failure rather than previewing your actual argument about education. Consider revising your opening to explicitly state: "Because medical science has not yet found a cure for AIDS, public education about transmission and prevention has become society's most powerful tool against the disease." This gives your reader a clear roadmap.

Additionally, some of your supporting points don't fully connect to this thesis. For instance, the paragraph about dentists forgetting gloves, drug users, and casual sexual partners is really about *sources of transmission*, not directly about *education* as a solution. You need to explicitly link this information back to your thesis: how does knowing these transmission routes lead to prevention through education?

Organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer structure. Right now, paragraphs feel loosely connected rather than building toward a cumulative argument. Consider this structure:

Introduction: State the problem (no cure exists) and your thesis (education is the answer).
Body Paragraph 1: Describe educational efforts already underway (support centers, school assemblies).
Body Paragraph 2: Explain who is at risk and why—this shows *why* education matters and *what* it should cover.
Body Paragraph 3: Address the psychological barrier of fear/denial and how education overcomes it.
Conclusion: Reinforce that education, paired with continued medical research, is our best current defense.

The line "Obviously, our culture has begun to educate its population or I would be unable to write about this particular topic" is a clever moment of self-awareness, but it functions more as a throwaway observation than a developed point. Consider either cutting it or expanding it into a real point about how public discourse (including your own writing) is itself a form of AIDS education.

Evidence and Development

This essay relies entirely on general assertions without specific evidence. Academic writing on a topic like AIDS calls for:

Statistics (infection rates, demographic data)
Named programs or examples (Which schools? Which hospitals? Are there specific organizations like the Red Cross or CDC campaigns you could cite?)
Quotations or paraphrases from experts in public health

Right now, phrases like "many schools" and "hospitals have begun" are vague. Naming specific programs or statistics will make your argument far more persuasive and credible.

Logic and Nuance

Some claims need more careful reasoning:

The line about drug use as "a possible suicide" is an interesting idea but underdeveloped—you introduce it and then move on without explaining what you mean.
The claim that people who have casual sex "have actually been selling their lives short" is a strong moral judgment presented without support. Consider whether this rhetorical move helps or undermines your credibility as a persuasive writer. A more analytical tone (discussing risk rather than moralizing) may serve your argument better.
Be careful not to imply that only certain groups (homosexuals, drug users) are "carriers"—your own text corrects this misconception, which is good, but make sure your language throughout doesn't reinforce stereotypes even while trying to dispel them.

Style and Mechanics

This draft needs significant polishing for a formal academic essay:

Avoid texting abbreviations: "w/", "&", and similar shorthand are inappropriate for academic writing. Spell out "with" and "and."
Subject-verb agreement: "The AIDS viruses was discovered" should be "The AIDS virus was discovered" (or if referring to multiple viral strains, "were discovered").
Comma splices and fragments: Several sentences run together clauses that need clearer punctuation or conjunctions (eg, the long sentence beginning "Upon realizing that homosexuals...").
Consistent tense: You shift between present and past tense when discussing ongoing educational efforts—stay consistent unless you have a clear reason to shift.

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your introduction to state a clear, specific thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each one supports that thesis directly.
Add at least 2–3 concrete pieces of evidence (statistics, named programs, or expert testimony).
Proofread carefully for abbreviations, agreement errors, and sentence fragments.
Reconsider moralizing language in favor of an analytical, evidence-based tone.

You have a genuine argument here—now it needs sharper focus and stronger support to make it fully persuasive.

Feedback on "Contact Lenses" Essay

Overall Impression

Your enthusiasm for this topic is clear, and your personal experience gives the essay an authentic, relatable voice. However, as it stands, this piece reads more like a personal narrative or reflection than an argumentative essay. To meet the expectations of academic argumentation, you'll need to shift your focus from *telling your story* to *building a case*. Below are specific areas to address in your revision.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening claim—that contact lenses are "one of the most important inventions of the 20th century"—is a strong, debatable thesis. This is exactly the kind of claim an argumentative essay needs. The problem is that the rest of the essay doesn't actually argue this claim; it illustrates it through your personal life.

To strengthen your argument, ask yourself: *What criteria make an invention "important"?* Is it because it improves quality of life for millions of people? Because it advanced medical technology? Because it changed how society thinks about vision correction or appearance? Once you establish these criteria explicitly, you can organize your evidence around them rather than around your own biography.

Right now, the essay uses you as the primary piece of evidence. While personal anecdote can be a powerful illustrative tool, an argumentative essay needs broader support: historical context, statistics on contact lens usage, expert opinions, or comparisons to other candidate inventions (television, antibiotics, the internet, etc). Even a brief acknowledgment of why contact lenses might beat out other "amazing inventions" would help justify your superlative claim.

Addressing Counterarguments

A hallmark of strong argumentative writing is engaging with the opposing view. Right now, you mention almost dismissively that hard lenses are "uncomfortable" and that colored lenses seem "ridiculous," but you never seriously engage with objections to your thesis. For example:
Contact lenses can cause eye infections or complications if not used properly.
They are costly compared to glasses.
Some people simply cannot wear them due to eye conditions.

Bringing in these counterpoints—and then explaining why the benefits of contact lenses still outweigh them—would make your argument far more persuasive and sophisticated.

Structure

Currently, your essay is organized chronologically (hard lenses → soft lenses → disposables → colored lenses), which makes sense for a personal narrative but not for an argument. Consider reorganizing around *reasons* rather than *history*. For example:
Paragraph 1: Contact lenses improve quality of life (introduce your criteria)
Paragraph 2: Contact lenses offer practical advantages over glasses (sports, swimming, convenience)
Paragraph 3: Innovations like disposables show continued improvement and accessibility
Paragraph 4: Acknowledge counterarguments/limitations
Conclusion: Reaffirm why contacts deserve to be called one of the century's great inventions

This reorganization would allow your personal experience to serve as *supporting evidence* within each point, rather than being the entire structure of the essay.

Tone and Style

Because this is meant to be an academic argumentative essay, the tone needs to be more formal. Right now, exclamation points, casual phrases ("lense craze," "who am I to say!"), and rhetorical questions ("How would I be able to see...?") create a conversational, diary-like tone. Save this style for personal essays; for argumentative writing, aim for measured, confident assertions supported by evidence.

Also, be cautious with hedging phrases like "I must admit" or "which I must admit is a bit ridiculous"—these undercut your authority as a writer making a claim.

Mechanics

A few technical issues to clean up in revision:
"Lense" should be "lens" (lenses is already plural).
Watch comma splices and inconsistent punctuation with quotation marks (eg, "hard lenses" doesn't always need quotes once you've defined the term).
Avoid the ampersand (&) in formal writing—spell out "and."
Vary sentence openings; several paragraphs begin with "I" or "Also," which becomes repetitive.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to include *specific criteria* for why contact lenses are important.
Add at least one paragraph of researched context (a brief history of contact lens technology, statistics on usage, etc).
Include a counterargument paragraph with a rebuttal.
Reorganize by argument/reason rather than chronology.
Revise tone to be more formal; reduce exclamation points and rhetorical questions.
Proofread for casual abbreviations and punctuation errors.

Your personal experience is a real strength—it's vivid and specific. Keep it, but reframe it as supporting evidence within a larger, more structured argument. With these revisions, this essay has strong potential to move from a personal reflection to a compelling piece of argumentative writing.

This is a creative angle on a well-worn topic, and I'd encourage you to keep it. However, the essay in its current form is too brief and underdeveloped to fully realize this promising thesis. Your thesis appears in the final paragraph rather than being clearly stated upfront: feminism is a "discovery in progress." This is a strong, debatable claim—exactly what an argumentative essay needs—but you introduce it too late. Readers need to know from the start what argument you're making and why the "discovery" metaphor matters. Structure and Paragraph Development

The essay's four short paragraphs feel more like sketched notes than developed arguments. Each body paragraph raises an important point—historical progress, economic inequality, cultural/media representation—but drops it almost as soon as it's introduced. For an argumentative essay, aim to develop each point with:

Specific evidence (dates, statistics, named events, sources)
Analysis explaining why that evidence supports your thesis
Transitions connecting each paragraph back to the "discovery" framework

For example, your second paragraph mentions the Equal Rights Amendment and the wage gap, but doesn't explain how these facts support the idea of feminism as an ongoing "discovery." Are you suggesting the ERA's failure shows the discovery is incomplete? Make that connection explicit.

Accuracy and Precision

A few factual points need correction or clarification:

Women won the right to vote in the U.S. in 1920 (19th Amendment), not "the 1800s." This is an important date to get right, especially since you're building a historical timeline.
The wage gap statistic ("60 cents for every dollar") should be cited to a source and given a timeframe—labor statistics change, and unsupported numbers weaken your credibility.
"The first significant equal rights movement... was in the 70s" overlooks earlier feminist activism (eg, first-wave feminism, the suffrage movement itself, 1960s civil rights-era organizing like NOW's founding in 1966). You might mean the ERA was the first *major legislative* push, but the phrasing overgeneralizes.

Word Choice and Style

Your prose is generally clear, but a few phrases could be tightened:

"0th century" is likely a typo for "20th century"—proofread carefully.
Scare quotes around "discovery" appear often; while the ambiguity is intentional, make sure each use is doing distinct work rather than just hedging.
Phrases like "as recently as the 1950s, it was still the most typical as well as the ideal" are wordy; consider: "As recently as the 1950s, the ideal woman was still expected to remain at home."

Suggestions for Revision

Expand significantly. This essay needs at least 2–3 times its current length to adequately develop your argument. Each body paragraph should be its own well-supported unit.
Strengthen the "discovery" metaphor. Consider addressing a possible counterargument: is it problematic to call feminism a "discovery," as though women's equality is something men or society "found," rather than something women fought for and built? Engaging with this tension could deepen your argument considerably.
Add scholarly or historical sources. Citing specific feminist theorists, historical documents, or sociological data will strengthen your credibility and give the essay more intellectual weight.
Revise the conclusion into a fuller synthesis. Right now it restates the thesis but doesn't add new insight. Consider ending with reflection on what remains to be "discovered."

Your idea is compelling—now give it room to breathe.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Environmental Awareness in the 20th Century"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a substantial and relevant topic, and your essay shows genuine enthusiasm for environmental issues. However, the essay currently reads more like a list of environmental concerns than a developed argument. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, deepen your analysis, and revise for clarity and precision. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening claim—that "caring for the environment" is the idea that "most significantly changed people's lives"—is broad and asserted rather than argued. An argumentative essay needs a claim that could be contested, and then evidence/reasoning that persuades a skeptical reader.

Revision suggestion: Narrow your thesis. Instead of trying to cover nuclear weapons, deforestation, recycling, energy conservation, and animal rights all in one essay, consider focusing on *one specific way* environmental awareness changed 20th-century life, and argue that point in depth. For example: "Environmental awareness fundamentally changed the relationship between individuals and their daily consumption habits" would let you go deeper into recycling and energy use with real analysis.

Structure and Development

Right now, each paragraph introduces a new topic (nuclear waste, deforestation, recycling, energy, animal rights) but doesn't develop it. Each point is stated, briefly illustrated, and then abandoned. This makes the essay feel like a survey rather than an argument.

What's missing:
Evidence: You make claims like "many groups have formed to plant more trees" or "companies are no longer able to freely dump their wastes"—but you don't name any groups, laws, or specific historical moments (eg, the Clean Water Act, Earth Day 1970, the EPA's founding). Concrete, specific evidence will make your argument far more convincing.
Analysis: You tell us *what* changed, but not *why* it matters or *how* it changed. For instance, why did people's assumption that resources were "never ending" change? What historical events or scientific discoveries prompted this shift? This is where real argumentative depth lives.
Causal reasoning: The essay would benefit from explaining the *mechanism* of change—was it activism, legislation, scientific discovery, disasters (eg, Chernobyl, Love Canal, the Cuyahoga River fire)? Naming causes will strengthen your argument considerably.

Suggestion: Choose two or three of your six sub-topics and develop them fully with specific examples, rather than covering all six briefly.

Coherence and Transitions

The paragraphs currently function as a checklist ("Another important topic..." "We have also become..."). Try to build logical connections between paragraphs—show how one idea leads to or relates to another, rather than simply adding items to a list. For example, you could connect nuclear waste and recycling by discussing how both involve rethinking waste disposal.

Clarity, Precision, and Tone

Several statements need refinement for logical precision:

"countries that which have many weapons have the power to destroy the world" — awkward phrasing and an overstatement; revise for accuracy and grammar.
"We realize that conserving is not beneficial to our environment but it is also saves money" — this sentence's logic is reversed (it should affirm that conserving *is* beneficial) and contains a grammatical error. Proofread carefully for sentences like this.
"One realizes that animals have rights too" — this is asserted without support. Animal rights is a complex philosophical claim; if you want to include it, you need to justify it, not just state it.
The claim that recycling "helps those w/o homes" is an interesting point but feels like a tangent; either develop it with evidence or remove it.
The conclusion ends on a hopeful but vague note ("hopeful by the 21st century we will have fixed everything we have destroyed!"). This claim is overly optimistic and not earned by the essay's evidence. Aim for a conclusion that reinforces your specific argument rather than a general, uplifting sentiment.

Mechanics

Please avoid text-message abbreviations (w/o, b/c, &) in formal academic writing—spell these out fully. Also watch for run-on sentences and comma splices (eg, "For example deforestation is a very big issue, many groups have formed..."—this should be two sentences or joined with a conjunction).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Narrow your thesis to one clear, arguable claim.
Cut breadth, add depth—develop 2-3 points thoroughly instead of 6 points superficially.
Add concrete evidence—specific laws, events, dates, organizations.
Explain causation—why and how did these changes happen?
Proofread for grammar, logic errors, and informal abbreviations.

You have good instincts about an important topic—now it's time to argue it, not just describe it. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: Computers and Their Impact on Daily Life

Overall Impression

You've chosen a relatable topic and clearly have personal experience to draw on, which gives your essay an authentic voice. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection or journal entry than an academic argument. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, deepen your analysis, and attend carefully to matters of style and mechanics. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening sentence—"Computers have definitely effected peoples lives"—is too vague to serve as a strong thesis. Almost no one would disagree with this claim, so there's no real argument to develop. Ask yourself: *What specific claim am I making about computers' impact?* Are you arguing that computers have fundamentally changed how information is organized and accessed? That they've made previously inefficient tasks nearly effortless? That they've reshaped institutional processes (like your study-abroad application)? Choose a more precise, debatable claim and use it to unify the essay.

Right now, your two paragraphs feel like separate lists of anecdotes (your personal computer use, then your application to the program) rather than parts of a single, developing argument. Consider organizing around 2-3 clear categories of impact (eg, personal productivity, institutional efficiency, communication) with a thesis that names these explicitly.

Structure and Organization

The essay lacks clear topic sentences that signal the function of each paragraph. Paragraph 1 jumps between storage, checkbook-balancing, word processing, and your father's ticket-buying without clear transitions—each of these could be its own point but currently blur together. Consider breaking this into distinct, focused paragraphs, each with a clear controlling idea (eg, one paragraph on information storage/organization, one on task efficiency, one on communication).

Paragraph 2 has strong potential—the idea that a bureaucratic process was made possible/efficient by computers is a compelling example—but it relies heavily on speculation ("I assume," "would seem," "no doubt"). Since you didn't personally witness the computer processing, this weakens your evidence. Can you find any information (even brief) about how study-abroad applications are typically processed? Even a general statement about how universities use databases would strengthen your credibility here.

Development and Evidence

Much of your evidence is anecdotal and drawn only from your own experience (and your father's). This is a reasonable starting point, but for a stronger argumentative essay, consider incorporating outside evidence: statistics on computer/internet adoption, examples from workplaces or other institutions, or brief references to how computing has changed specific fields (education, business, communication). This will move your essay from a personal narrative toward a more persuasive, generalizable argument.

Also, several claims are asserted but not fully explained. For instance, you say computers allow "more information in an organized fashion" but don't explain *how*—what does that organization actually look like or enable? Push yourself to explain the *mechanism* behind each benefit you mention.

Style and Register

This essay uses informal abbreviations throughout ("w/," "&," "info") that are inappropriate for academic writing. Please spell out these words fully. Similarly, phrases like "Well, I applied to the this program" and the conversational question "How did I get there?" suit a diary entry, not an academic essay. Consider revising your tone to be more formal and analytical throughout.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address in revision:

"Effected" should be "affected" in your opening sentence (affect = verb meaning to influence; effect = usually a noun).
"Peoples lives" needs an apostrophe: "people's lives."
"My life has change" should be "has changed."
Several comma splices and run-on sentences appear (eg, "Of course, this was very time consuming & open to error" — check punctuation before "&"; the long sentence beginning "I'm able to store almost all the information..." needs to be broken up).
Subject-verb agreement: "the International Programs Office would inevitably inundated" is missing "be" ("would inevitably be inundated").
Watch for sentence fragments and unclear pronoun references (eg, "this program" appears twice with slightly different referents).

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or having a peer read it, specifically to catch these mechanical issues.

Suggested Next Steps

Draft a clearer, more specific thesis statement.
Reorganize paragraphs around distinct sub-claims with clear topic sentences.
Add at least one piece of outside evidence or research.
Do a dedicated proofreading pass focused solely on abbreviations, apostrophes, and sentence boundaries.

You have good raw material here—now it needs sharper focus and polish. I'm happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and still-relevant claim—that the satellite's ability to transmit events in "real time" has reshaped global politics and accountability. The Tiananmen Square example is well-chosen and emotionally resonant. However, the essay needs substantial revision in terms of structure, argument development, and mechanics before the claim will be persuasive to an academic reader.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim—that the satellite is "the invention of the century"—is a strong, debatable thesis, which is good. But right now it's asserted rather than argued. Consider:

Define your criteria. What makes an invention "the invention of the century"? Is it impact on politics? On daily life? On global consciousness? You gesture toward "nowness" as the key criterion, but this needs to be stated explicitly and used to organize the whole essay.

Address the causal claim carefully. You argue that without the satellite, "the movement for democracy... would have been lost." This is a strong causal claim that needs more support. Was it specifically the satellite, or the combination of satellite technology *with* television cameras and journalists on the ground? You already mention that TV and videotape *preceded* the satellite—so you need to clarify what the satellite added that these technologies alone could not (namely, live simultaneity, as you note). Make this distinction sharper and sustain it throughout.

The concluding predictive claim ("governments... will be less tempted to do wrong") is an interesting idea but currently unsupported speculation. Either provide reasoning/evidence for why surveillance produces accountability, or soften the claim.

The final sentence ("when China is placed down the block, who wouldn't want to help his neighbor?") is a metaphor whose logic isn't fully developed. It gestures at an important idea—the satellite creates a sense of global community/proximity—but as written it feels tacked-on and colloquial. This could actually be a strong secondary claim if you develop it as its own point earlier in the essay, rather than ending on an underdeveloped metaphor.

Evidence and Development

The Tiananmen Square example is your only piece of evidence, and it's doing a lot of work. To strengthen the essay:

Add specific detail: dates, the image of the "Tank Man," how footage was broadcast, who was watching, what actions resulted internationally (sanctions? statements from world leaders?).
Consider a second example to show the pattern is not a one-off (eg, another political event covered live via satellite around this time).
Acknowledge complexity: Was the "movement... crushed" *despite* global visibility, or does that undercut your claim that visibility produces accountability? You raise this tension yourself ("Although the movement for freedom in China was crushed...") but don't resolve it. This is actually a good moment for deeper analysis—engage with the counterargument rather than moving past it.

Organization

The essay is currently one long paragraph. Break it into clear sections:
Introduction: State your thesis clearly and preview your reasoning.
Body paragraph 1: Explain what makes the satellite unique (the "nowness" argument), addressing the comparison to radio/TV/videotape.
Body paragraph 2: Develop the Tiananmen Square case in detail.
Body paragraph 3: Address the counterargument (the movement was crushed—so did visibility really matter?) and your claim about future deterrence.
Conclusion: Return to your thesis and explain its broader significance (this is where the "neighbor" idea could land effectively, restated more clearly).

Style and Mechanics

The essay contains frequent spelling and grammatical errors that will distract an academic reader and undermine your credibility:

Spelling: "evention" → invention; "preceed" → precede; "openned" → opened; "litterally" → literally; "sensorship" → censorship; "mor" → more.
Word choice/typos: "US." should be "vs."; "a like" → "alike"; "now" should likely be "how" ("it has influenced how the world functions").
Sentence fragments: "As it happens- the most profound byproduct of the satellite." This needs to be integrated into a full sentence.
Run-on/comma-spliced sentences appear throughout; reading each sentence aloud will help you catch these.

I strongly recommend a careful proofreading pass *after* you've revised the content, since new errors often creep in during revision.

Priority Revisions

Sharpen and foreground your thesis and definition of "nowness."
Break the single paragraph into a multi-paragraph structure.
Expand and specify your Tiananmen Square evidence.
Directly address the counterargument that the movement was crushed despite global visibility.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

This essay has a genuinely interesting core argument—focus your revision on giving that argument room to breathe through clearer structure and more developed evidence.

Feedback Report on "The Advancement of Technology" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and relevant topic—the influence of computers on institutions and education—and you've organized it into clear sections (companies, students, learning tools). However, the essay currently reads more like a list of assertions than a developed argument. To strengthen this piece, you'll want to focus on sharpening your thesis, adding concrete evidence, and revising for clarity and mechanics.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction states that technology has had a "tremendous influence," but this claim is quite broad and not particularly debatable—few readers would disagree that computers have changed how we work and learn. A strong argumentative essay needs a thesis that stakes out a specific, arguable position. Consider narrowing your focus. For example:

Is your claim that computers have made certain professions *more efficient*, or that they've fundamentally *changed the nature of work itself*?
Are you arguing that computers have made learning *easier*, or that they've made learning *more effective*? These are different claims requiring different evidence.

A sharper thesis might read: "While computers have undeniably increased efficiency in data management, their greatest impact has been in transforming how students engage with and process information." This gives you a specific angle to defend throughout the essay.

Development and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Each paragraph currently offers general claims without much support:

Paragraph 2 (companies): You mention libraries, hospitals, and law firms, but only in passing. What specific efficiencies have resulted? Do you have any statistics, examples, or even hypothetical scenarios that illustrate *how much* time or resources are saved? Even a single concrete example (eg, "A hospital record that once took a clerk 20 minutes to locate can now be pulled up in seconds") would strengthen your claim considerably.

Paragraph 3 (student writing): You focus heavily on spell-check and formatting, which are relatively minor benefits. Consider whether this is your strongest example of computers being "revolutionary" for students. What about research capabilities, collaborative writing tools, or access to information that wasn't previously available? This paragraph would benefit from a clearer connection to *why* these benefits matter for learning outcomes, not just convenience.

Paragraph 4 (learning tools): This paragraph is the shortest and most vague. What does "cosm graph" refer to? This term is unclear and may confuse readers (did you mean "cosmic" something, or perhaps "graphing" in mathematics?). Specific examples—a particular software program, a simulation, an interactive tool—would help readers understand exactly what you mean by computers as "teaching instruments."

Suggestion: For each paragraph, ask yourself: "What is one specific example I can describe in detail?" Rather than mentioning three institutions (libraries, hospitals, law firms) briefly, you might develop just one example more fully with specific details.

Counterargument

Currently, your essay presents only the positive effects of computers. A more persuasive argumentative essay acknowledges potential drawbacks or limitations—even briefly—before reinforcing your position. For instance, has increased reliance on spell-check possibly weakened some students' own spelling and grammar skills? Are there concerns about equitable access to computers among students? Addressing a counterpoint, even in one or two sentences, will make your argument more credible and sophisticated.

Conclusion

Your conclusion feels underdeveloped. The final sentence—"who knows where these machines will take us"—is vague and doesn't leave the reader with a strong sense of closure or the significance of your argument. Consider ending with a specific claim about future implications tied to your thesis, or a call to reflect on how we should thoughtfully integrate these tools going forward.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several errors that need correction before this essay is ready for submission:

Spelling errors: "definantly" → definitely; "accesible" → accessible; "previosly" → previously; "extend" → extent (paragraph 5); "imp" appears to be an abbreviation left in by mistake—did you mean "important"?
Subject-verb agreement: "they is clearly imp" should be "they are clearly important."
Informal abbreviations: "w/o" should be spelled out as "without" in formal writing.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: The sentence "They have enabled to students to learn..." has a grammatical error (should be "enabled students to learn"). Also check the comma splice in paragraph 4 ("...previously used, and for some students...").
Word choice: "cosm graph" is unclear—please clarify or correct this term.

I recommend reading your essay aloud during revision; this often helps catch awkward phrasing and grammatical errors that are easy to miss when reading silently.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Narrow and sharpen your thesis to make a specific, arguable claim.
Add concrete, detailed examples in each body paragraph rather than broad generalizations.
Address a counterargument to strengthen your credibility.
Strengthen your conclusion with a specific, forward-looking claim.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and clarity issues.

With more specific evidence and a tighter focus, this essay has the potential to make a compelling case about technology's role in education and institutions. I look forward to seeing your revision!
