Feedback on Your Essay: Television as a Transformative Invention

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging and defensible topic, and your writing shows real enthusiasm for the subject. The essay has the bones of a solid argument—television's effect on news consumption and its role in entertainment—but it needs more structural discipline, sharper argumentation, and tighter editing to reach its potential. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your thesis (television has significantly changed people's lives) is broad enough to support many possible arguments, but the essay currently reads as two loosely connected points—TV's role in news/global communication, and TV's role in entertainment—without a clear thread tying them together. Consider revising your thesis to state explicitly *how* these two functions relate. For instance: "Television has transformed daily life by democratizing access to information and by shaping our understanding of human behavior through entertainment." This would give your reader a roadmap and help you organize paragraphs around a unified claim rather than a list of separate observations.

Right now, the essay also lacks a concluding paragraph. The entertainment section ends on a defensive note ("TV can be an excellent tool...") without returning to your opening claims about the "global village." A conclusion that synthesizes both parts of your argument—showing how information-sharing *and* entertainment together reshape culture—would strengthen the whole piece considerably.

Development of Ideas

Your strongest material is the discussion of instantaneous global communication. The point about being able to react "while emotions are still strong, before the action is finalized" is a nuanced and interesting observation—but it's asserted rather than demonstrated. Ask yourself: *what specific historical or contemporary example supports this?* Even one concrete case (a protest movement, a breaking news event, a moment when public opinion shifted rapidly because of televised images) would move this from assertion to argument. Academic writing is strengthened enormously by evidence, and right now your essay relies entirely on general claims and hypothetical scenarios ("people in Spain," "gathering of opposition to a certain law").

The entertainment paragraph, by contrast, feels underdeveloped and somewhat rushed compared to the news paragraph. You raise an important counterargument—TV glorifies violence and distorts reality—but then dismiss it in a single sentence ("this sense of reality is not so distorted...") without explaining *why* you believe this. This is where your essay is most vulnerable to a skeptical reader. Consider: what evidence do you have that most viewers maintain "a well-developed sense of reality"? Is this true across all demographics, ages, and levels of media literacy? Engaging seriously with this counterargument—rather than briefly waving it away—will make your position much more persuasive.

Use of Outside Ideas

You reference "one journalist" who coined the term "global village." This is a good instinct—situating your argument in relation to existing thought—but you should name this thinker (Marshall McLuhan) and, ideally, briefly explain what he meant by the term. Vague attribution ("one journalist") weakens your credibility and suggests under-researched writing. If you're drawing on outside concepts, even briefly, proper attribution strengthens your ethos as a writer.

Style and Mechanics

At the sentence level, there are several issues to address in revision:

Comma splices and run-on sentences: Your longest sentence ("While previously people had to wait days...") strings together multiple independent clauses with commas rather than periods or semicolons. Break this into two or three sentences for clarity.
Inconsistent punctuation: You use dashes inconsistently (sometimes with spaces, sometimes without) and periods are occasionally missing after complete thoughts (eg, "as through literature & drama, people learn..." should begin a new sentence). Proofread carefully for sentence boundaries.
Ampersands and abbreviations: Avoid "&" and "thru" in academic prose—write "and" and "through" in full.
Vague qualifiers: Phrases like "In my opinion" and "well, at least everybody who watches" feel conversational. Academic argumentative writing can still express a personal stance, but it should do so with more precision and less hedging.
Paragraph transitions: The shift from news to entertainment ("Not only the news, but also entertainment...") is abrupt. Consider a transitional sentence that explicitly connects the two functions under your unified thesis.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to unify both halves of the essay.
Add at least one concrete, specific example to support your claims about global communication.
Expand your engagement with the counterargument about television's distortion of reality—don't just concede it, argue through it.
Name and briefly explain McLuhan's "global village" concept.
Write a conclusion that ties information and entertainment together into your larger claim about societal change.
Proofread carefully for run-on sentences, punctuation consistency, and informal abbreviations.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here—now it needs tighter structure, sharper evidence, and more confident engagement with the complexities you've already identified.

Feedback Report: The Microwave Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay has an engaging premise—arguing that the microwave represents a revolutionary break from culinary tradition—and your enthusiasm for the topic comes through clearly. However, the essay needs significant revision in three main areas: mechanical correctness, argumentative development, and structural balance. Below, I offer specific feedback organized by category.

Mechanics and Proofreading

This draft requires careful proofreading. Notable errors include:
"culunary" → culinary
"consistant" → consistent
"twentyth century" → twentieth century
"rythm" → rhythm
"cave men" → cavemen (or "early humans," which avoids the outdated term)
"fool of mankind" → food of mankind
"comodity" → commodity
"dizzing" → dizzying
"differents" → different

Recommendation: Read your essay aloud slowly, or use a text-to-speech tool, to catch errors your eyes skip over. Spelling and grammar mistakes undermine your credibility as a writer, even when your ideas are strong.

The Extended Metaphor

Your opening metaphor (ocean/storm/wave) is creative, but it runs too long and becomes strained. Phrases like "crashed upon the rocks of every kitchen" for a wave that "is the microwave" ask readers to stretch credulity. Consider either:
Shortening the metaphor to 2-3 sentences as a hook, then transitioning quickly to your thesis, or
Cutting it entirely and opening with a more direct, arguable claim.

A metaphor should clarify your point, not distract from it. Right now, readers spend more energy parsing the extended comparison than engaging with your actual argument.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your implicit thesis seems to be: *The microwave represents a revolutionary break from tradition because it uses radiation instead of fire, and this innovation has significantly changed how people live.* This is a reasonable claim, but it needs to be stated explicitly, likely at the end of your introduction, so readers know exactly what you're arguing before you dive into support.

Your two body paragraphs correspond to two supporting points:
The microwave breaks from the tradition of using fire (historical/conceptual argument)
The microwave saves time (practical/social argument)

This is a workable structure, but each point needs more development:

Paragraph 2 (Fire vs. Radiation): Your historical survey (fire, spits, wood stoves, gas ranges) is a nice touch, but you assert the significance of the fire-to-radiation shift without fully explaining *why* it matters. What does this shift represent, beyond novelty? Does it change our relationship to food, safety, or scientific method? Push your analysis further—don't just state that the change is "amazing" or "revolutionary"; argue *why*.

Paragraph 3 (Time-saving): Your examples (working mothers, children using the device safely, reheating leftovers) are good real-world illustrations, but they're presented as a list rather than developed points. Consider choosing your two strongest examples and elaborating on them with more specific detail or even a brief anecdote/statistic. Right now, this paragraph reads as a string of quick assertions rather than a sustained argument.

Evidence and Credibility

The essay would benefit significantly from outside sources. Currently, all claims rest on your own assertions ("our society has embraced this," "most of us lead" a fast-paced life). Consider incorporating:
A brief history of microwave invention (Percy Spencer, 1945, accidental discovery via radar research)
Statistics on time saved in food preparation, or on microwave ownership rates
A scholarly or journalistic source discussing the microwave's social impact

Even one or two outside references would substantially strengthen your ethos and move this from opinion essay to argumentative essay.

Logical Consistency

One small issue: you claim the microwave was born from "the daybreak of the twentieth century," but the microwave oven wasn't invented until 1945 and wasn't common in homes until the 1970s. Since you're making a historical argument, accuracy matters here—verify your dates.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates points already made without adding new insight or a sense of closure. Consider ending with:
A forward-looking statement (what does the microwave's success suggest about future kitchen innovations?)
A return to your opening metaphor, if you keep it, to create a satisfying frame
A final reflection on why this "break from tradition" matters beyond convenience—does it reflect something about modern values?

Priority Revisions

Proofread thoroughly for spelling/grammar
State your thesis explicitly
Trim the extended metaphor
Add specific evidence/sources
Verify historical dates
Strengthen the conclusion with fresh insight

You have a clear point of view and some creative writing instincts—now channel that energy into tighter, more evidence-based argumentation. I'm happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Radio

Overall Impression

You have an intriguing hook—the teacher's claim about the machine gun and radio—and a genuine curiosity about how radio has shaped modern life. However, the essay currently reads more as a descriptive reflection than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to clarify your central claim and support it with more rigorous, specific evidence.

Thesis and Argument

This is the most important area for revision. Ask yourself: What are you actually arguing? The essay opens with a provocative idea about repressive governments, but then pivots to a much broader (and vaguer) claim that "radio has changed people's lives." These are two very different arguments:

Radio (and its offshoots) enabled new forms of political control and propaganda.
Radio revolutionized information access and entertainment in everyday life.

Right now, you gesture toward the first idea in your opening paragraphs, then abandon it entirely in favor of the second. Pick one focus—ideally the more original and analytically rich claim about political control—and build your entire essay around it. If you want to keep both threads, you need a thesis statement that explicitly connects them (eg, "Radio's power to reach mass audiences instantly transformed both political control and everyday life, making it one of the most significant inventions of the twentieth century"). As it stands, a reader doesn't know what claim you want them to accept by the end.

Structure and Development

Because the thesis is unclear, the paragraphs feel like a list of observations rather than a logical progression building toward a conclusion:
Paragraph 1–2: machine gun vs. radio and repressive governments (introduced, then dropped)
Paragraph 3: how radio works (technical, but disconnected from your argument)
Paragraph 4: radio as information source
Paragraph 5–6: television as entertainment

Consider restructuring so each paragraph clearly advances your central claim. For example, if you keep the political-control thread, you could organize around: propaganda's use of radio historically (with real examples, such as Nazi Germany or Cold War broadcasting) → how this differs from print media's more limited reach → how the same technology that enables control also enables democratic access to information. This would give your essay a genuine argumentative arc: radio is a double-edged tool, powerful for both control and empowerment.

Evidence and Support

Right now, most claims are asserted rather than demonstrated. Statements like "radio has become a source of information and entertainment" or "the power of the media became evident" need concrete evidence: historical examples, statistics, or scholarly sources. For instance:
If discussing propaganda, name specific regimes or events (eg, Nazi radio broadcasts, Orson Welles's *War of the Worlds* panic as evidence of radio's persuasive power).
If discussing information access, consider citing when radio overtook newspapers in speed or reach, or reference a specific historical broadcast that shaped public opinion.

Personal anecdotes (like your abuela watching TV) can be effective, but they work best as *illustrations* of a larger, well-supported point—not as the primary evidence. Right now, the anecdote about your grandmother, while charming, doesn't advance an argument; it's simply a description.

The "Passive Observer" Point

This is one of your most interesting ideas—that radio turns citizens into "passive observers rather than active participants" in news-making. This deserves much more development. Is this a positive or negative shift? Does it connect back to your opening point about repressive governments (ie, passive audiences are easier to control)? This could become the analytical heart of your essay if developed further.

Style and Mechanics

A few technical issues to address in revision:
Watch apostrophe usage: "It' s," "don' t," "se�ora" (encoding error—should be "abuela" or "grandmother," and double-check for other corrupted characters).
"Marshall Law" should be "martial law."
"forcast" should be "forecast."
Some sentences run together without clear logical connectors (eg, the jump from radio's information function to its entertainment function needs a transitional sentence explaining *why* these are related to your argument).
Proofread for spacing errors around punctuation.

Priority Revisions

Commit to one clear, arguable thesis and revise your title/intro to reflect it explicitly.
Cut or integrate the machine gun comparison—right now it's a hook without follow-through.
Add concrete historical evidence for at least two major claims (propaganda use, information speed, etc).
Develop the "passive observer" idea—it's your most original insight.
Proofread carefully for mechanical errors.

You have a promising angle here; the revision process should focus on sharpening your claim and giving your reader compelling, specific reasons to believe it.

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay has an engaging, personal voice, and the anecdote about your great-uncle is charming and relatable. However, as it stands, this reads more like a personal narrative than an argumentative essay. To meet the expectations of academic argumentation, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, broaden your evidence base, and tighten your structure. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your opening sentence promises a broad discussion of "advances in science and modern technology" that have impacted "peoples lives" during the 20th century—but the essay narrows almost immediately to a single appliance: the microwave. This creates a mismatch between your introduction's scope and your essay's actual content.

Revision suggestion: Decide what your actual claim is. Are you arguing that:
Technological conveniences like the microwave have generally improved quality of life, despite some resistance from those who prefer traditional methods?
Modern time-saving technology reflects a broader cultural shift in how we value time versus tradition?

Whichever you choose, state it explicitly as a thesis at the end of your introduction, and make sure everything that follows serves that claim.

Structure and Organization

Currently, your essay reads as a string of loosely connected observations and one anecdote, several of which are stand-alone one-sentence paragraphs (eg, "For instance a microwave is a brilliant addition to the lives of many people," and "In fact I have a little story to tell about that very thing"). These fragments interrupt the flow of your argument and read more like transitional notes than developed ideas.

Revision suggestion: Consolidate related ideas into fuller paragraphs. For example, combine your points about dual-income families and the microwave's efficiency into one well-developed paragraph with a clear topic sentence, supporting details, and a concluding thought that ties back to your thesis.

Evidence and Support

Right now, your primary evidence is personal—your own household routine and a story about your great-uncle. While personal anecdotes can add color and relatability to an argument, an academic essay needs more substantial support: statistics on dual-income households, historical context about when microwaves became common, or even attitudes toward convenience technology more broadly. Consider integrating at least one outside source (a study, article, or credible statistic) to strengthen your credibility and move beyond anecdote alone.

Handling the Counterargument

You do introduce a counterargument (your great-uncle's perspective), which is a strong instinct for argumentative writing—acknowledging opposing views strengthens your own position. However, the counterargument isn't fully developed or seriously engaged with. You mention his objection but then dismiss it quickly with "But the point is, is that a microwave isn't right for everyone," and then undercut your own counterargument by revealing that he gave the microwave away and your family loves it.

Revision suggestion: Take the counterargument more seriously before rebutting it. Why might someone value the slower, more deliberate process of cooking? What does that say about tradition, family, or mindfulness that a purely efficiency-driven argument might miss? Then offer a thoughtful rebuttal that acknowledges the validity of that view while still supporting your thesis.

Conclusion

Your essay ends somewhat abruptly with the resolution of the anecdote, but there's no broader conclusion that reflects on what this story means for your argument as a whole. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis, synthesize your points, and perhaps gesture toward broader implications (eg, how technology and tradition can coexist, or what this specific example reveals about generational attitudes toward convenience).

Grammar, Mechanics, and Style

A few line-level issues to address in revision:
"peoples lives" → "people's lives" (possessive apostrophe needed)
"left overs" → "leftovers" (one word)
"recieved" → "received" (spelling)
"great Uncle and Aunt" → lowercase "uncle" and "aunt" unless used as part of a proper name/title
"cook a meal... We each take turns cook" → needs correction: "take turns cooking"
"But the point is, is that..." → redundant phrasing; revise to "The point is that..."
Avoid starting too many sentences with "For instance," "In fact," or "In today's society," which can feel like filler transitions rather than substantive connectors.

Additionally, the tone is quite informal (eg, "brilliant addition," "little story," "long and short of the story"). For an academic argumentative essay, aim for a more measured, analytical register while still maintaining your personal voice.

Final Thoughts

You have the seed of an interesting argument here—about convenience technology, generational attitudes, and the tension between efficiency and tradition. Your next revision should focus on clarifying your thesis, deepening your engagement with the counterargument, incorporating outside evidence, and tightening your prose for both structure and correctness.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of ATM Machines

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and relatable topic, and your enthusiasm for the convenience of ATMs comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection than a fully developed argumentative piece. To strengthen it, you'll need to work on developing your ideas more thoroughly, structuring your argument more deliberately, and engaging more seriously with opposing viewpoints. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim—that ATMs have significantly changed people's lives—is a reasonable starting point, but it's quite broad and could be sharpened. What specifically has changed? Is it about time management, safety, financial independence, or something else? Right now, your essay touches on several benefits (time-saving, convenience, safety) without committing fully to any one line of argument. Consider narrowing your thesis to something more precise, such as: "ATMs have transformed everyday financial life by giving people constant access to their money, thereby saving time and reducing the risks associated with carrying cash." A more specific thesis will help you organize your evidence more effectively.

Structure and Organization

The essay's organization needs attention. Currently, your counterargument ("People may argue that ATM machines will take away employment in banks") appears in the first paragraph, sandwiched between other claims, and is dismissed in a single sentence without explanation. Counterarguments are strongest when given their own space—typically a paragraph—where you explain *why* someone might hold that view and then provide a reasoned response. For example, you might acknowledge that ATMs have indeed reduced certain teller positions, but argue that they have also created new jobs in technology and maintenance, or that the efficiency gained outweighs the job losses. Right now, the rebuttal is asserted rather than argued.

Additionally, your third paragraph shifts from your own use of ATMs to their global presence, but the transition is abrupt ("ATM machines appear in all areas of the World") and not well integrated with what precedes it. Consider using clearer topic sentences at the start of each paragraph that signal exactly what point you're making and how it connects to your thesis.

Development and Evidence

Much of your support relies on personal anecdotes ("I have either forgotten my wallet..."). Personal experience can be a valid rhetorical tool, but in an argumentative essay, it should be paired with broader evidence—statistics, expert opinions, historical context, or even hypothetical scenarios that illustrate the point more universally. For instance, when you claim ATMs "appear in all areas of the World," this is a strong claim that needs support: how many ATMs exist worldwide? In how many countries? When did this global expansion happen? A little research here would substantially strengthen your credibility.

Similarly, your point about safety (avoiding large amounts of cash) is a good one, but it's stated rather than developed. You could expand this by explaining *how* ATMs reduce risk—perhaps by allowing people to withdraw only what they need, when they need it, rather than carrying a lump sum.

Counterargument Section

As mentioned above, the counterargument about job loss deserves more space and a real rebuttal. This is actually one of the more compelling angles in your essay, since it introduces some tension into what is otherwise a fairly one-sided argument. Expanding this section will demonstrate that you've thought critically about the issue rather than simply listing positives.

Conclusion

Your conclusion is too brief and introduces a new, much broader claim about "computerized technology" enhancing "our environment" in general. This broadens the scope of your argument at the very end, which can feel like a shift rather than a synthesis. A strong conclusion should circle back to your thesis, briefly remind the reader of your key points, and perhaps end with a forward-looking or reflective statement specifically about ATMs—not technology as a whole. If you want to gesture toward the larger significance of ATMs as part of a technological trend, do so carefully and explicitly connect it back to your main argument.

Style and Grammar

There are a few phrasing issues worth revising for clarity: "I can not comprehend how difficult it would be without this convenience at access to money" is awkward—consider rephrasing to something like "I cannot imagine how difficult it would be to access money quickly without this convenience." Also, watch for inconsistent verb tense and run-on sentences. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch these issues.

Summary of Suggested Revisions

Sharpen your thesis to focus on a specific aspect of ATM impact.
Give the counterargument its own paragraph with a genuine rebuttal.
Add researched evidence (statistics, historical facts) to supplement personal anecdotes.
Strengthen topic sentences and transitions between paragraphs.
Revise the conclusion to stay focused on your thesis rather than broadening to "computerized technology" in general.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a personal reflection toward a more persuasive, well-supported argument.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Cellular Telephone

Thank you for sharing this essay on cellular telephones. You've chosen a topic with rich potential for argument, and your personal anecdote about the four-way call is engaging. However, the essay currently reads more like a list of observations than a focused argumentative essay. Below are some suggestions organized by key areas for revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your introduction states that cellular phones are "a complete luxury and not a necessity," which sounds like it should be your thesis. However, the body paragraphs don't consistently support or develop this claim. Instead, you present a mix of benefits (emergency use, convenience) and drawbacks (accidents, cost) without clearly connecting them back to the luxury-vs-necessity argument.

Revision suggestion: Decide what you actually want to argue. Do you want to claim cell phones are unnecessary luxuries? Do you want to argue they are double-edged—beneficial but dangerous or costly? Once you choose your central claim, every paragraph should work to support it. Right now, the essay feels like it's arguing for luxury, then against safety, then for expense, then marveling at technology—these are four different mini-arguments, not one cohesive one.

Strengthen Paragraph Unity and Topic Sentences

Each paragraph currently touches on multiple ideas. For example, your third paragraph discusses both accident rates *and* cost—two distinct issues that deserve separate treatment. Combining them weakens the impact of each point.

Revision suggestion: Follow the "one idea per paragraph" principle. Consider restructuring into:
One paragraph on the *benefits* of cell phones (emergency access, convenience)
One paragraph on *safety concerns* (accidents)
One paragraph on *cost* as a counterargument to necessity
One paragraph addressing counterarguments or complicating your stance (eg, the anecdote about calling family—does this support "luxury" or does it show necessity for some people?)

Address the Logical Tension in Your Argument

There's an unresolved contradiction: you say cell phones are "not a necessity," yet you also describe their use in emergencies (being stranded, broken down) as a legitimate benefit. Emergency use is precisely the kind of scenario that *supports* necessity, not luxury. This weakens your argument's coherence.

Revision suggestion: Directly acknowledge this tension. You might argue that while cell phones offer occasional necessary functions (emergencies), the *primary* use by most consumers is for convenience and status—making them luxuries in practice, even if they have necessity-adjacent features. This nuance will make your argument more sophisticated and harder to refute.

Reconsider the Anecdote and Conclusion

The story about your four-way call with family is vivid and personal, which is good—but it currently functions as a stand-alone paragraph without clear connection to your thesis. Does this anecdote support your claim that phones are luxuries? Or does it undermine it by showing how meaningful and valuable this connectivity is for families? 

Similarly, your conclusion pivots to a new topic (robots) that isn't connected to your central argument about phones. This creates a sense that the essay trails off rather than concluding with purpose.

Revision suggestion: Use the anecdote deliberately—either as evidence of luxury (a "nice-to-have" that enriches life but isn't essential) or as a complicating example that shows even you use phones for more than "necessity" might allow. Then write a conclusion that returns to your thesis, briefly synthesizes your paragraphs, and makes a final judgment (luxury? necessity? something in between?) rather than shifting to future technology.

Editing and Style Notes

Avoid starting sentences with "So" in formal writing (paragraph 2).
Watch for punctuation errors: "friends houses" should be "friends' houses"; the dash and capitalization after "Madrid -" need correction.
Reduce exclamation points for a more academic tone (eg, "especially when one person is in Spain and another is in a car!!").
The phrase "it' s" has a stray space—proofread carefully for typos.

Summary

Your essay has good raw material—personal experience, awareness of both pros and cons—but needs a clearer thesis, more disciplined paragraph structure, and resolution of the luxury/necessity tension. Consider outlining your argument before revising: What is your one main claim? What three or four points support it? How does your anecdote fit? Answering these questions will help you transform this from a list of observations into a persuasive, unified essay.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Creation/Evolution Classroom Debate

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely complex and controversial topic, and your essay shows real effort to organize multiple lines of argument. However, the essay currently reads as a one-sided advocacy piece dressed in the language of balanced argumentation, and it has significant structural and evidentiary problems that need attention before revision.

Missing Citations Are a Critical Problem

The most immediate issue is the number of placeholder brackets () throughout the essay where quotes and evidence should be. As it stands, I cannot evaluate whether your sources actually support your claims, because the claims themselves are missing. This isn't just a formatting oversight—it's a substantive gap. Attributed statements (Darwin, Carleton, Bliss's conclusions, the Supreme Court case) are the backbone of your argument, and without them, readers have no way to assess their accuracy, relevance, or whether you've represented them fairly. Before revising anything else, fill these in and cite them properly.

The Essay Claims Balance But Doesn't Deliver It

Your thesis states that the two-model approach "presents a strong, effective argument" while the opposition's argument is "increasingly weak." This framing is a problem for an argumentative essay that purports to seriously engage both sides. You've essentially decided the outcome before examining the evidence, which leads to two consequences:

Straw-manning the opposition. The evolutionist position is reduced to a single claim ("creationism is religion, it doesn't belong in science class") and then dismissed through counter-quotes and assertions, rather than genuinely engaged. For instance, the scientific community's actual objections to creationism as *science* (falsifiability, testability, peer-reviewed evidence, the nature of scientific theory versus religious belief) are never substantively addressed. You gesture at these issues near the end ("evolution happened under conditions that do not exist today") but this rebuttal actually reveals a misunderstanding of how scientific inference from indirect evidence works—it needs much more careful handling.

Uncritical treatment of your own side's evidence. The Einstein/Newton example, for instance, is used to argue that "questioning firmly held ideas" justifies teaching creationism alongside evolution. But Einstein's revision of Newtonian physics happened *within* the scientific method—through mathematics, experimentation, and peer review—not by introducing a non-scientific framework as an alternative. This is a significant logical leap that undermines your argument rather than strengthening it. Readers who don't already agree with you will notice this immediately.

Reconsider Your Definition of "Argument"

An argumentative essay is strongest when it demonstrates that you've genuinely wrestled with the counter-evidence and can still defend your position credibly. Right now, the opposition's points appear only to be swatted away with brief responses (often just one or two sentences), while your own side gets paragraphs of elaboration. To make this a stronger essay:

Give the evolutionist/opposition position a full, fair hearing—ideally in its own developed paragraph(s), not just a sentence you then rebut.
Address the strongest version of their argument, not the easiest one to dismiss. For instance: what is the actual distinction scientists draw between "theory" in the colloquial sense and "theory" in the scientific sense? Why do they insist creationism is a religious rather than scientific claim? Engage with this seriously.
Be honest about places where the two-model argument is weaker, even if you ultimately still favor it. Complete balance doesn't require you to abandon your position—it requires you to show why your position holds up even against the best counter-arguments.

Structural and Stylistic Notes

Introduction: The opening paragraph tries to do too much (origins of the universe, education statistics, rhetorical questions) before arriving at your actual thesis. Consider tightening this to 3-4 sentences that move directly toward your claim.
Paragraph transitions: Many paragraphs begin with "The second argument," "Another example," etc This is functional but mechanical—look for ways to connect ideas more organically.
Repetition: Phrases like "two-model approach" and "advocates" are repeated so often that the prose becomes monotonous. Vary your sentence structures and word choice.
Proofreading: There are typos ("fro ma," "E=MC�" formatting issue) and grammatical inconsistencies (eg, "the opposition (two-model approach)" seems to mislabel which side you mean) that need careful proofreading.
Conclusion: The conclusion doesn't grapple with counterarguments at all—it simply restates your favorable position and asserts that action should follow. A stronger conclusion would acknowledge the genuine tension in this debate and explain why, on balance, you still find the two-model position persuasive (or reconsider whether you do, after revising).

Next Steps

Fill in all missing citations with accurate, properly attributed material.
Substantially rewrite the "opposition" sections to represent their strongest arguments fairly.
Reexamine your Einstein/Newton analogy and your response to the "conditions don't exist today" objection—both need more rigorous logic.
Revise the introduction and conclusion for focus and balance.

This essay has ambition and a real attempt at structure, but it needs a more even-handed treatment of both sides and complete sourcing to be persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Feedback on "Mercy Killing" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and important topic, and you clearly did research—citing Kevorkian, Cruzan, and multiple articles. However, the essay currently reads as two different papers stitched together: one is a *rhetorical analysis* of how the media frames euthanasia (as a "values" issue vs. a "consequences" issue), and the other is a *personal argumentative essay* about your own religious and ethical views on mercy killing. Because these two projects have different goals, the essay loses focus. Below are specific areas to revise.

Clarify Your Thesis and Purpose

Right now, your introduction ends with a personal claim ("I... think that an individual should be allowed the freedom that God granted us"), but your second and third paragraphs shift into analyzing *how articles discuss* euthanasia rather than *arguing for* a position. Decide which essay you are writing:

If this is an analytical essay, your thesis should be something like: "Media coverage of euthanasia frames it primarily as a moral/values issue rather than a matter of practical consequences, which shapes public perception in [specific way]." Then every paragraph should support *that* claim.
If this is an argumentative essay, your thesis should state your position clearly (eg, "Euthanasia should be a legally protected personal choice for terminally ill, competcompetent patients") and every paragraph should build a case for that position, using the articles as evidence rather than as the subject of analysis.

Right now you're doing both at once, which confuses readers about what you want them to take away.

Missing Content (the  placeholders)

There are several unfilled placeholders () throughout the draft—after "in cases of euthanasia they," after "useless to society," in the Kevorkian paragraph, in the Cruzan paragraph, and in your conclusion. These are clearly meant to hold specific facts, quotations, or examples, and their absence leaves key sentences incomplete or vague. Before revising anything else, fill these in with concrete material from your sources; several of your paragraphs' logic depends on this missing information.

Strengthen Organization and Transitions

The essay jumps between topics without clear signposting:
The Kevorkian paragraph and the "values vs. consequence" discussion feel disconnected—show explicitly how the Kevorkian case *illustrates* your point about values-based framing.
The paragraph on "The Last Goodbye" introduces a claim (articles are personal/sympathetic rather than analytical) but doesn't develop it with examples from the text itself.
The transition into your religious argument ("Coinciding with values...") arrives abruptly. Explain how your personal religious view relates to the media analysis you just presented—are you agreeing with the "values" framing, critiquing it, or offering a third perspective?

Consider outlining your essay before revising: List each paragraph's main claim in one sentence, then check whether the claims build logically toward your thesis.

Resolve the Contradiction in Your Conclusion

You argue earlier that life is God's gift and freedom is central to human dignity, suggesting support for a patient's right to choose. Yet your final paragraph states euthanasia "should be a restricted process" if there's a chance the person could be "cured and happy." This is a reasonable *nuanced* position, but it needs to be set up earlier in the essay, not sprung on the reader at the end. Consider revising your thesis to reflect this more conditional stance from the start: euthanasia should be a legitimate personal choice specifically in cases of incurable, irreversible suffering.

Use Sources More Rigorously

Attribute quotations properly: "the director of the Center for Medical Ethics... classified Kevorkian's acts as..." needs a name (Siegler, whom you mention two sentences later—introduce him earlier) and a formal citation (MLA or APA, per your assignment).
The block quote "I believe God gave all people the gift of life" appears with no clear source—is this your own words, a quotation from a text, or scripture? Cite it or rephrase it as your own claim.
When you say "In many of my articles," name them specifically rather than referring to them generically; readers need to know which text you're referencing at each point.

Editing for Clarity and Correctness

Watch pronoun agreement: "the person will die anyway... his or her personal choice" mixes singular "person" with "his or her" inconsistently; consider using "they" consistently for a gender-neutral pronoun, or restructure sentences.
Fix minor grammatical slips: "an complex issue" → "a complex issue"; "en a painful life" appears to be a typo—clarify what you mean (end?).
Avoid vague phrases like "Others think" and "Some say" without specifying who—this weakens your credibility as a researcher.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Choose one clear thesis (analytical or argumentative) and align every paragraph to it.
Fill in all  placeholders with real content.
Add transitions connecting your case studies to your central claim.
Foreshadow your conditional final position earlier in the essay.
Properly cite and attribute all quotations.
Proofread for pronoun consistency and typos.

With these revisions, you'll have a much more focused, persuasive, and polished essay. This is a strong start with real research behind it—now it needs a tighter argumentative spine.

Feedback on Capital Punishment Essay

Your essay tackles a complex and enduring debate with genuine conviction, and your thesis—that the anti-death penalty argument is stronger, particularly regarding the value of human life—is clear and consistently maintained throughout. Below are suggestions organized by area to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is clearly stated and you do a good job forecasting your argument in the introduction. The overall structure (pro-arguments, then rebuttals) is logical and easy to follow. However, consider whether presenting *all* the pro-arguments first and *then* all the counter-arguments creates enough direct engagement between them. Right now, the essay reads more like two separate lists than a true point-by-point debate. You might strengthen your analysis by pairing each pro-argument immediately with its rebuttal, which would make the "rebuttal" feel more responsive and less like a second essay bolted onto the first.

Use of Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay contains numerous bracketed placeholders  where quotations from Hoyler, Nygaard, Conway, and Reiman should appear. In the current draft, this means your key evidentiary support is literally missing—your reader cannot yet evaluate whether Nygaard "supports" your claim, because we never see what he actually says. Before further revision, be sure to:

Insert the actual quotations from your sources.
Introduce each quotation (who is this person, what is their expertise/relevance?).
Explain each quotation after you present it—don't just quote and move on. Tell the reader specifically how this quotation supports your claim.
Make sure quotations don't do the analytical work for you. Right now, sentences like "Nygaard states, . This makes the act of capital punishment just as criminal..." suggest the quote should demonstrate this, but you need to build the bridge explicitly.

Logical Development

Several of your claims need stronger support or more careful reasoning:

"The majority of Americans support capital punishment" — this is stated as fact in your opening but never cited. Since your entire argument implicitly pushes against majority opinion, this claim deserves a source.
The "value of human life" argument is your most original and interesting point, but it's asserted rather than fully argued. You claim that executing a criminal "lowers the value" of everyone's life in society—this is a philosophically rich claim that deserves more explanation. *Why* does killing a criminal equate their life's value with the victim's? Walk the reader through this logic more slowly, perhaps with a concrete example.
The deterrence rebuttal is your strongest section because you engage the opposing claim directly and explain why the evidence is lacking. Use this section as a model for how to handle your other counter-arguments.
The claim that "society simply craves violence" in your conclusion is a strong, provocative statement, but it appears in the final paragraph without having been developed earlier. If this is truly part of your argument, introduce it in the body and give it support. If it's more of a rhetorical flourish for the ending, consider softening it or making clear it's a summary observation.

Tone and Word Choice

Your writing is passionate, but be cautious about language that undercuts your credibility as an even-handed analyst:

Phrases like "heinous act," "barbaric," and "society begins to live on spite" are effective rhetorically but can make the essay feel one-sided rather than analytical. Since your assignment (implied) is to *analyze* both arguments before declaring a winner, make sure your treatment of the pro-death-penalty side doesn't feel like a strawman before you dismantle it.
Similarly, granting real concessions to the opposing view (eg, "the deterrence argument, while intuitive, lacks...") rather than fully validating and then swiftly rejecting will make your rebuttal feel more credible.

Sentence-Level Issues

Watch for absolute claims ("It is a known fact," "there simply is no fear") that are hard to support and easy for a skeptical reader to challenge. Qualify these where you can't cite direct evidence.
The essay would benefit from varied transitions between paragraphs; several begin with fairly mechanical phrases ("The most commonly used argument...", "There are several other arguments...").

Priority for Revision

Fill in all missing quotations and integrate them properly (introduce, quote, analyze).
Deepen the "value of human life" argument, which is your most distinctive contribution.
Restructure for more direct engagement between pro and con arguments, if possible.
Moderate tone in a few places to strengthen your ethos as a fair-minded analyst.

Overall, you have a solid foundation and a clear point of view—the next draft should focus on shoring up the evidentiary skeleton and deepening your most original insight about the value of human life.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and important topic, and your roadmap paragraph shows you have a clear plan for organizing the discussion. However, the essay currently reads more like an outline of intended moves ("I plan to...") than a fully developed argument. Revising will mean turning that plan into an executed, evidence-based case. Below are specific areas to focus on.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your introduction promises a balanced discussion ("Both sides present strong cases") but then immediately reveals your conclusion ("because I feel that legislation... is necessary"). This is not a problem in itself—argumentative essays should have a position—but you need to decide *how* you want to frame the essay. Right now it wavers between "objective analysis of a debate" and "advocacy piece." Rewrite your thesis so it openly states your claim (eg, "Affirmative action remains necessary, but its advocates must sharpen their arguments to withstand certain criticisms") and then structure every paragraph to build toward that claim rather than simply reporting what "opponents" and "proponents" say.

The Opening Anecdote

The 1992 riot/black cultural center anecdote is vivid, but its connection to your actual subject—quotas in affirmative action—is not established. Are the campus protests about affirmative action, or about something related but distinct (cultural recognition)? If you keep this opening, you need a transitional sentence that explicitly links the two issues; otherwise, cut it or replace it with an anecdote/statistic more directly tied to hiring quotas.

Logical Development

Several moves need more careful reasoning:
The claim that "by arguing that affirmative action hasn't worked, opponents... are really defending the need for such programs" is a leap. Opponents might believe it hasn't worked *and* should therefore be abandoned—you need to explain why ineffectiveness implies a need for *more* effort rather than *less*.
The assertion that blacks "probably got into these situations as a result of discrimination and oppression" is presented as fact but is unsupported. This is exactly the kind of unproven generalization you criticize opponents for making. Either support it with evidence/sources or qualify it as your interpretation.
Be careful not to let your own side's generalizations go unchallenged while holding opponents to a higher standard—this weakens your credibility as a fair arbiter of the debate.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your body paragraphs sometimes blend two different sections: reporting the opposing view, refuting it, and then advocating for how proponents "could strengthen" their case. Consider separating these three functions more clearly with topic sentences that signal exactly what each paragraph is doing (eg, "Opponents' strongest claim is X," "This claim fails to account for Y," "Advocates could respond by arguing Z"). This will help readers track your argument's logic step by step.

Use of Sources

Right now, the essay contains multiple bracketed placeholders () where quotations should be. Obviously these need to be filled in before submission, but also:
Introduce each source properly the first time you cite them (full name, credentials, and context), not just a last name assumed to be familiar ("Shaw," "Gillepsie").
Make sure quotations are integrated with signal phrases and followed by your own analysis—don't just drop a quote and move to the next point.
Double-check the spelling of "Gillespie" and ensure all citations follow a consistent format (MLA, APA, etc).

Style and Mechanics

The essay needs a careful proofreading pass:
Typos: "tents" (tenets), "achie ved," "stope," "caucasians" (capitalize).
Watch subject-verb agreement and pronoun clarity, especially when switching between "opponents" and "proponents" in close proximity—this can confuse readers about whose argument is being described.

Conclusion

Your closing analogy to slavery is compelling but appears too late to be effective—it could serve as a stronger organizing idea if introduced earlier (perhaps in the introduction) and referenced throughout as a framework for measuring the "success" of thirty years of affirmative action.

Priority Revisions
Sharpen and foreground your thesis.
Clarify the relevance of your opening anecdote.
Fill in and properly integrate all source quotations.
Strengthen logical transitions between claims and rebuttals.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a promising outline toward a persuasive, well-supported argument.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and complex topic, and I can see you did real research into New Age beliefs and the debates surrounding them. However, the essay needs significant revision in several key areas: argumentative objectivity, structure, and the handling of evidence. Below are my main concerns, organized by priority.

Bias and Lack of Objectivity

This is the most serious issue. An argumentative essay should present a fair-minded evaluation of both sides before reaching a conclusion—but your essay reveals its bias almost immediately and never seriously entertains the opposing view. Phrases like "I feel that the Bible is very real and true" insert your personal religious conviction as though it were evidence, which undermines your credibility as an objective analyst. Similarly, dismissing all pro-New Age authors as having "personal" or "fabricated" values, while treating Christian sources as automatically credible because they cite the Bible, is a double standard. You criticize one side for relying on unproven authority and personal opinion, then praise the other side for doing something similar (relying on religious faith as "hard fact"). For this essay to be persuasive to a skeptical reader—not just one who already agrees with you—you need to apply the same evidentiary standards to both sides.

Revision suggestion: Try rewriting your thesis and conclusion without appealing to your personal belief in the Bible's truth. Instead, argue on the grounds of consistency, evidence, and reasoning you established in the paper's methodology (the "question of consequence" and "question of value" framework).

The Anecdote About Tobacco

The story about running to your neighbors after reading a debatable claim about tobacco is engaging and shows critical thinking, but it's overused as a foundation for dismissing an entire body of pro-New Age literature. One error from one author doesn't disprove all arguments in favor of teaching New Age ideas. This is a hasty generalization. If you want to use this anecdote, frame it as *one example* of a pattern of unreliable sourcing, and then provide at least one or two more examples to establish that pattern convincingly.

Structural and Organizational Issues

The essay wanders somewhat before arriving at its central argument. The opening Yoda/yoga joke is fun but takes up space without doing much rhetorical work—consider tightening it or cutting it substantially. More importantly, your thesis (buried at the end of paragraph 3) is quite dense and lists four distinct criteria (values, consequence, firsthand accounts, mainstream sources, hard facts)—this is a lot for a reader to track. Consider:

Stating your thesis more concisely, perhaps naming 2-3 main criteria rather than 4-5.
Using clear topic sentences at the start of each paragraph that explicitly connect back to the thesis criteria (eg, "The first weakness in the pro-New Age argument concerns credibility of authorship...").
Adding brief transitions between sections so the reader always knows which part of your argument is being developed.

Use of Evidence and Quotations

Placeholders like  suggest quotations you intend to insert—make sure these are fully incorporated with proper citation (author, source, page number) in your final draft. Additionally, when you introduce the Christianity Today and Moody magazine examples, you assert they are "mainstream" and highly credible, but you don't explain *why* a Christian magazine constitutes an unbiased, mainstream source on a topic about which Christian and New Age worldviews are in tension. A skeptical reader will notice this same weakness you attribute to the other side.

Clarity and Precision of Language

Several sentences would benefit from tightening:

"Yoga is a relaxation technique typically practiced by what some Westerners would classify as 'new age' type people" — this claim needs a citation or softer, more precise phrasing, since yoga's identity is contested and diverse.
"Because of the large amount of 'personal' values...the objective of arguing this topic as a question of value must be thrown out" — this sentence is confusing. Clarify what you mean: are you saying values-based arguments are illegitimate in general, or only when used by pro-New Age authors specifically?
Watch for comma splices, apostrophe errors ("that families values," "one authors opinion," "families values" should be "family's values" and "author's opinion"), and stray typos ("New< Age," "infotrac 2000" needs explanation for readers unfamiliar with this database).

Consider the Counterargument

A stronger essay would acknowledge the strongest version of the pro-New Age argument before refuting it. Right now, you present that side almost entirely through its weaknesses, which risks looking like you're not engaging fairly. Even one paragraph steel-manning the opposing view, followed by your rebuttal, would strengthen your ethos as a fair-minded writer.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Remove or reframe language expressing personal religious belief as fact.
Apply consistent standards of evidence to both sides.
Tighten the thesis and add clear topic sentences.
Expand your evidence beyond a single anecdote per claim.
Proofread for grammar, punctuation, and clarity.
Add a paragraph representing the opposing view's strongest case.

You have good instincts for finding sources and organizing categories of evidence (values, consequences, credibility)—the next step is applying that framework with more balance and precision. I'd be happy to discuss revision strategies further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Nuclear Power Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting rhetorical question—not whether nuclear power is good or bad, but *how* each side argues its case. This is a sophisticated angle, and your organization around the opponents' values-based appeals versus the proponents' facts-based appeals shows real analytical thinking. However, the essay's execution needs significant work in several areas: factual accuracy, argumentative balance, sentence-level clarity, and consistency of purpose.

Major Issues to Address

Factual Errors Undermine Credibility
Your opening claim that a nuclear bomb has "the explosive power of three hundred tons of TNT" is far too low (many nuclear weapons are measured in kilotons or megatons—thousands to millions of tons of TNT equivalent). Since this is your very first sentence, an error here damages reader trust immediately. Similarly, "meggawatts" should be "megawatts." Please fact-check all technical claims and statistics before submitting, and cite your sources for the cost comparisons and safety statistics you mention (New England, Midwest figures, lives saved per 1000 MW, etc)—right now these numbers float without attribution, making them seem invented rather than researched.

Thesis vs. Execution Mismatch
Your thesis promises to analyze *how* each side argues—opponents via values, proponents via facts. This is a genuinely interesting rhetorical framing. But the essay quickly abandons this analytical stance and instead reads as an argument *for* nuclear power. By the final paragraphs, you're not analyzing the proponents' rhetoric anymore—you're endorsing their conclusions ("nuclear power is much better for the environment... an asset to our revolutionary country"). Decide: is this a rhetorical analysis essay or a persuasive essay? If the former, maintain critical distance from both sides throughout. If the latter, revise your thesis to state your actual position from the start.

Unbalanced Treatment
You devote much more space and specificity to the proponents' arguments than the opponents'. The opponents' section feels like a summary paragraph, while proponents get four detailed sub-sections with evidence. If your thesis claims both arguments are "strong," your essay should demonstrate that through comparable depth and rigor on both sides. Right now, the structure itself reveals your bias, which undercuts the analytical framing you set up.

Unsupported Broad Claims
Phrases like "due to the one-sided way Americans have been brought up" and "the media has obtained the reputation of blowing issues out of proportion" are asserted without evidence. These are exactly the kinds of sweeping generalizations that weaken academic arguments. Either support these claims with specific examples/sources or qualify them more carefully.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Homophone errors: "do to" → "due to"; "meggawatts" → "megawatts"
Verb agreement/tense inconsistencies: "Coal miners can contract... they can be flooded... they can be killed" mixes uncomfortably with "many deaths occur" (tense shifts throughout)
Awkward phrasing: "the common An American" appears to be an editing error—please proofread more carefully before submission
Incomplete sentence: "Though simple, easy-to-understand facts such as:  the average American can easily relate to the amount of waste being produced" — this sentence is broken; there seems to be a placeholder or missing example here
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences, such as "The values center mainly around safety, people do not want to back something..." need semicolons or periods instead of commas

Structural Suggestions

Revise your introduction to clearly state whether this is analysis or advocacy, and make sure your thesis reflects that choice.
Expand the opponents' section with the same level of specific detail (studies, historical incidents like Three Mile Island or Chernobyl, specific cost overruns) that you give proponents.
Add a counter-analysis: If proponents' facts are so strong, why do opponents' emotional appeals still resonate with so many people? Address this tension directly—it would strengthen your analytical framing considerably.
Revise the conclusion to match your thesis. Currently it reads as unambiguous advocacy ("nuclear power will truly be an asset"), which contradicts the analytical, two-sided framing promised in your introduction.

Final Thoughts

You have a promising rhetorical framework here. Your instinct to examine *how* arguments are constructed (values vs. facts) rather than just cataloging pro/con lists shows critical thinking. Focus your revision on: (1) fixing factual errors, (2) committing to either analysis or advocacy and adjusting your thesis/conclusion accordingly, (3) balancing your treatment of both sides, and (4) careful proofreading. With these revisions, this essay could move from a one-sided persuasive piece to a genuinely insightful rhetorical analysis.

Expanding your discussion of the psychological/self-esteem research with more specific findings, not just general claims.

Briefly recap your key pieces of evidence (academic outcomes, self-esteem factors, the Chapter 220 experience, and community attitudes).

"the white communities reluctance" needs an apostrophe: *community's*

Summary

You have the raw material for a compelling essay: real experience, relevant research, and a genuinely complex ethical question. Your next revision should focus on (1) completing your citations, (2) sharpening your thesis into a clear, arguable claim, and (3) using your personal narrative as *evidence* for that claim rather than as a standalone story. Feedback Report: Surrogate Motherhood Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting ethical debate, and your position—that surrogacy causes unacceptable emotional harm to the surrogate mother—is clear and consistently maintained. However, the essay in its current form has a critical structural problem that must be addressed before other issues can be meaningfully evaluated: numerous placeholder gaps (marked with ) appear throughout the draft, including in the opening definition, the thesis-adjacent claims, and nearly every paragraph's topic sentences. Right now the essay reads like a skeleton missing key organs. Revision should begin by filling these in with actual content, evidence, and attribution.

Missing Content and Incomplete Definitions

The essay opens with an incomplete definition: "Surrogate Motherhood is when a." Since your entire argument depends on the reader understanding what surrogacy is and how it works (genetic vs. gestational surrogacy, contractual arrangements, etc), this needs to be fully written out in your first sentence or two.

More seriously, many of your body paragraphs introduce a counterargument or claim and then leave the actual content blank—for example, "Annas argues " or "Others feel that ." These gaps make it impossible to assess whether you are fairly representing opposing views, which is essential for a strong argumentative essay. Please locate your source material (it seems you are drawing on a specific article, possibly Annas and a "Health Services" source) and insert direct quotations or paraphrases with proper in-text citations. Without this, your rebuttals have nothing concrete to rebut.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is scattered across multiple paragraphs rather than stated once, clearly, near the beginning. Consider consolidating your claim into one strong sentence early in the introduction: something like, "Surrogate motherhood is ethically unacceptable because it subjects the surrogate mother to psychological harm that current contractual and legal frameworks fail to address."

Currently, the essay's organization is repetitive rather than progressive. Several paragraphs restate the same core point (the surrogate is treated as a "womb" or "reproductive structure," the emotional toll is severe) without advancing the argument or introducing new evidence each time. Try outlining your paragraphs by function:
Paragraph 1: Background (Baby M case) + thesis
Paragraph 2: Counterargument (bodily autonomy) + rebuttal
Paragraph 3: Counterargument (genetic/biological ownership) + rebuttal
Paragraph 4: Counterargument (baby-selling debate) + rebuttal
Paragraph 5: Weigh the strongest opposing claim honestly, then rebut it
Conclusion: Synthesis, not repetition

This will reduce redundancy and give each paragraph a distinct job.

Engaging Fairly with Counterarguments

A strong argumentative essay does not just assert that opposing views exist—it explains them accurately and with specific evidence before dismantling them. Right now, phrases like "Many people feel that" or "Others feel that" are vague placeholders for actual named perspectives. Ask yourself: Who exactly makes the claim that surrogacy is "just another way of reproduction"? Cite them by name or organization, and explain their reasoning before you counter it. This will make your rebuttals feel earned rather than assumed.

Logical Gaps

Some of your reasoning needs tightening. For instance: "there are many people in the world who's lives have not been altered because they are infertile" is used to suggest infertility isn't a significant need—but this is a weak analogy, since it doesn't address why some infertile individuals *do* experience profound distress. Strengthen this point with evidence (psychological research on infertility, testimonies) or drop it.

Similarly, the claim "Biologically the baby is just as much the surrogate mother's as it is the father's" needs more support, especially since gestational surrogacy (using a donor egg) complicates simple biological ownership claims. Clarify which type of surrogacy you're discussing throughout, since your argument shifts between genetic and gestational scenarios without flagging the distinction.

Style and Mechanics

Watch for repeated phrasing ("emotional strains," "walking womb," "reproductive structure" appear multiple times); vary your language.
Correct minor errors: "who's" should be "whose"; "feels" should be "feel" (subject-verb agreement); "substancial" should be "substantial."
Avoid moralizing statements like "No human being should be looked upon as a reproductive structure who has no feeling" without supporting evidence—let your argument, not your indignation, persuade the reader.

Next Steps

Fill in every  with real content and citations.
Write one clear thesis statement.
Reorganize paragraphs so each introduces new evidence or a new counterargument.
Distinguish between types of surrogacy where relevant.
Proofread for grammar and repetitive phrasing.

Once the placeholders are resolved, I'd be glad to review a fuller draft focused on argument strength and evidence integration.

Feedback on "Drug Pricing and the Need for Federal Regulation"

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a genuinely important policy debate and shows real strength in its use of concrete, specific evidence. Your case studies on Depo-Provera, Coumadin/Percocet, and Clozapril are exactly the kind of detailed, memorable examples that make an argumentative essay persuasive. However, the essay needs work in several areas: completing the draft (there are missing sentences marked with ), tightening the logical structure, strengthening the counterargument section, and cleaning up grammar and citation practices. Below are specific suggestions organized by priority.

Complete the Draft

Most urgently, there are two placeholder markers () where sentences are missing—one in paragraph 2 and one in paragraph 4. These gaps are especially damaging because they occur at key argumentative moments: the first is meant to explain *how* price-gouging shows up in hospital bills, and the second is meant to explain *why* drug prices rose faster than inflation. Right now, readers hit a wall exactly where they need the most explanation. Filling these in should be your first priority before addressing anything else.

Strengthen the Thesis and Roadmap

Your thesis—that federal price regulation is necessary—is clear, but it appears somewhat late and could be sharpened. Consider stating explicitly, early on, the three or four types of evidence you'll use (statistics, international comparisons, case studies) so readers know what to expect. A brief roadmap sentence at the end of your introduction would help orient readers through what is otherwise a fairly long essay with many moving parts.

Handle the Counterargument More Substantively

You raise the pharmaceutical industry's argument (that price boards would eliminate competition) but dispense with it quickly by citing lower prices in Canada and Europe. This is good evidence, but a stronger essay would also acknowledge *why* the industry makes this argument (eg, concerns about funding for R&D) and then explain specifically why that reasoning fails—rather than just asserting "the opposite seems to be true." Right now the rebuttal feels asserted rather than demonstrated. You could also address whether lower prices abroad have had any documented effect on innovation rates, since that is the industry's actual claim.

Clarify and Cite Your Statistics

The statistics in your second paragraph are compelling, but the phrase "the pharmaceutical industry argues that..." is confusing, since $36.4 billion and $751.8 billion are presented as objective figures, not industry claims. Clarify whether these numbers come from an industry source, a government report, or a neutral third party, and cite them properly (author, organization, year). Throughout the essay, you reference sources like "the Senate Special Committee on Aging" and specific percentages—these need proper in-text citations (even informal ones, like "(Senate Special Committee on Aging, 1991)") so readers can verify your claims. This will also strengthen your ethos as a writer.

Revise the Case Study Paragraphs for Clarity

Your three examples (Depo-Provera, Coumadin/Percocet/Percodan, Clozapril) are the strongest part of the essay. To make them even more effective:
Explain *why* the Depo-Provera price hike was unjustified more fully—you assert Upjohn was "mistaken" but don't fully explain why recouping R&D costs is an insufficient justification.
In the Coumadin example, clarify the causal chain: did the packaging change actually save DuPont-Merck money while increasing hospital costs, or did overall costs rise for everyone? This distinction matters for your gouging argument.
In the Clozapril example, note that Clozapril is used for schizophrenia, not "senile dementia"—this is a factual error worth correcting, as it could undermine your credibility with an informed reader.

Reconsider the Ending

Your personal appeal as a nursing student is powerful and adds authentic pathos—keep it. However, the essay currently ends with rhetorical questions ("When does drug overpricing stop? It is up to the American people to decide.") that soften your argument's force. Since your entire essay has argued for a *specific policy solution* (a federal price review board), ending with an open-ended question undercuts that. Consider replacing the final lines with a clear, direct restatement of your policy proposal and a call to action—something that leaves the reader with your solution firmly in mind rather than a shrug.

Proofread Carefully

There are numerous spelling and typing errors (agrues, recup, recieve, negociating, is if) that need correction. Additionally, watch for run-on sentences and comma splices (eg, "As a nursing student, the indications of this are ominous, patients I treat..."). A careful proofreading pass—ideally reading the essay aloud—will catch most of these.

Summary

You have strong, specific evidence and a clear moral stake in this issue. Focus your revision on: completing missing content, tightening your counterargument, citing sources properly, correcting the Clozapril error, and ending with a firmer call to action rather than open questions.

Overall Impression

Your thesis is stated clearly in the introduction, and each body paragraph opens with a clear topic sentence that signals its focus. This is a solid structural foundation. However, there are several significant issues that need attention before this essay is ready for submission.

Missing Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)

For example, you tell us that Archer, Gartner, and Beittel's study "showed that the murder rates of fourteen nations, []" — but showed *what*, exactly? Without the actual findings, this sentence makes a promise it never fulfills.

This is not a minor formatting oversight — it undermines your entire argument. An argumentative essay lives or dies by the specificity and credibility of its evidence. Before revising anything else, go back to your sources and insert the actual data, quotations, and statistics. Make sure each one is properly cited (author, year, and — if using MLA or APA — page numbers or in-text citations).

Fairness to the Opposing View

Directly explain why that evidence is flawed or outweighed (methodology problems, outdated data, correlation vs. causation issues, etc).

Right now, your treatment of the counterargument — especially in the ethics section — is only a few sentences long before you move on to your own view. A stronger essay would engage more seriously with the best version of the opposing argument before dismantling it. The Dann study compares murder rates 60 days before and after five executions. Before treating this as proof that executions *cause* more murders, you should consider (and ideally address) alternative explanations — sample size (only five executions), regional variation, seasonal effects, etc Right now you present it as settled evidence rather than acknowledging its limitations, which actually would strengthen your credibility, not weaken your argument.
The "brutalization effect" section asserts that criminals "model their behavior after the state," but this needs a clearer causal mechanism or supporting evidence, not just assertion. Right now this reads as a plausible theory presented as established fact.

The deterrence paragraph, in particular, reads as a list of studies ("Another study... yet another study...") rather than a synthesized argument. Consider grouping studies by *what they show* (eg, cross-national comparisons vs. before/after comparisons vs. correlational studies) and explicitly stating what each type of evidence contributes to your overall claim, rather than presenting them chronologically or by author name alone.

Style and Mechanics

Capitalize "Both" at the start of the sentence in your introduction ("both sides cite statistics...").
"5.20.1982" — check this date; you state Coleman was convicted in 1982 and executed the same year, which seems historically inaccurate for a case involving lengthy appeals (Coleman was actually executed in 1992). Verify all dates and facts carefully.
The phrase "Social Theory and Practice wrote" is repeated verbatim twice; vary your attribution language ("as noted in," "according to," "the journal argues that").
Avoid vague transitions like "Furthering the idea of the inhumanity..." — be more precise about what point you're building on.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates the introduction almost point-for-point. Consider using this space to address a forward-looking implication — What should happen next? What policy change are you advocating for specifically (a moratorium, judicial reform, legislative abolition)? This would give your argument a stronger sense of purpose beyond restating what's already been said.

Priority for Revision

Fill in all missing evidence with accurate citations.
Strengthen and fairly represent the counterargument before rebutting it.
Address logical/methodological limitations in the studies you cite.
Polish style and verify factual details.

You have a well-organized argumentative skeleton here — the next draft should focus on fleshing it out with credible, fully-cited evidence and more rigorous engagement with opposing views.
