Feedback on "Prozac Should Continue to Be Prescribed"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely debatable topic and organized your essay in a logical sequence: mechanism of the drug, safety, social benefits, and then counterarguments. This structure works well for persuasive writing. However, there are several issues—some mechanical, some argumentative—that need attention before this essay is ready for submission.

Most Urgent Issue: Missing Quotations

The most pressing problem is technical: your essay contains multiple placeholder brackets () where quotations should appear. These appear in nearly every paragraph and are especially critical in your discussion of Dr. Kramer and other physicians. Right now, a reader cannot evaluate your argument because the evidence itself is absent. Before revising anything else, you must locate these quotations and insert them properly, with correct attribution and citation. This is not a minor formatting oversight—without the actual words of your sources, your claims about what "doctors say" or "Kramer says" are unsupported assertions.

Argumentative Structure

Thesis: Your thesis is clear—Prozac should continue to be prescribed because of its positive effects. However, the second sentence of your thesis ("Further discussion will show how people have improved... and devalue the rumors") reveals a potential problem: you are treating opposing viewpoints as "rumors" to be dismissed rather than legitimate concerns to be seriously weighed. This framing weakens your credibility. A stronger thesis would acknowledge that real concerns exist but argue that the evidence still favors continued use.

Use of Evidence: You rely heavily on anecdote (Sam, Tess, the rabbi) and rhetorical claims ("doctors are confident," "most physicians share a common belief"). While case studies from Kramer's book are useful for illustration, they are not proof of a general medical consensus. Consider distinguishing between anecdotal evidence (individual stories, useful for illustrating a point) and statistical/clinical evidence (studies, trial data, comparative rates). Right now these are blended together, which can make your argument feel less rigorous. Where possible, cite actual clinical studies or data with sources, not just Kramer's popular account.

The Suicide Counterargument: This is the weakest part of your essay, and also your most important obligation as a persuasive writer—engaging honestly with the strongest opposing evidence. Your response (900,000 prescriptions vs. "a few confirmed deaths") is a good start toward statistical reasoning, but it needs more development:
Where does this number come from? Cite it.
Correlation/causation is tricky here—you should acknowledge that determining whether Prozac *caused* suicides versus whether depressed patients were already at risk is genuinely difficult, not just dismiss the claims as "highly publicized" rumors.
The word "cannot be cast aside" promises a serious engagement, but the paragraph doesn't deliver one. Slow down here and give this objection its due before countering it.

The "Numbing" Objection: You handle this more fairly, acknowledging Sam's loss of "quirkiness and sarcasm" and the rabbi's incomplete relief. This is good—but then you undercut it by calling these "slight obstacles" without much justification. Ask yourself: is losing part of your personality a "slight" side effect, or a serious philosophical question about what psychiatric drugs are for? Kramer's essay title, "The Personality Pill," actually raises exactly this question. Engaging with it more seriously would strengthen your credibility as a fair-minded arguer, even if you ultimately conclude the benefits outweigh the costs.

Style and Tone

Your opening paragraph contains an interesting historical framing (home remedies vs. modern science) but doesn't clearly connect to Prozac until several sentences in. Consider trimming this preamble or connecting it more directly to your thesis.
Watch words like "skeptics" and "rumors" — these are loaded terms that characterize opposing views dismissively rather than substantively. Academic argument is generally more persuasive when it treats counterarguments with respect, even while refuting them.
Your conclusion ends with a rhetorical question ("what is wrong with making yourself better?") that is emotionally appealing but philosophically underdeveloped. This is actually a fascinating question — the line between treating illness and "enhancing" personality — and gesturing at it in your final line without exploring it can feel like you're dodging the hardest part of your own argument. Consider addressing this tension earlier, even briefly, so the ending feels like a culmination rather than an afterthought.

Concrete Next Steps

Fill in all quotation placeholders with accurate citations.
Add a clear citation system (MLA/APA) for Kramer's article and any statistics.
Expand your engagement with the suicide-risk counterargument—don't just cite numbers, explain their significance.
Reconsider language that dismisses opposing views as "rumors"; aim for fairness even while disagreeing.
Consider explicitly addressing the "enhancement vs. treatment" question your own evidence raises.

This essay has a solid foundation and an interesting angle (medical safety + social benefit). With revision focused on evidence integration and more rigorous counterargument, it will be considerably stronger.

Feedback on Draft Essay: "Gays in the Military" Argument

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and historically significant debate, and you've structured it as a clear point-counterpoint comparison between two sources. This is a solid foundational approach. However, the draft needs substantial revision in four key areas: opening framing, use of evidence, depth of counterargument, and clarity of prose. Below are specific suggestions.

Reconsider Your Opening

The historical references to Dr. King and 1970s feminism are meant to establish context, but they create a problem you may not intend: you're implicitly comparing the fight for LGBTQ rights in the military to two celebrated civil rights struggles, and then your thesis argues *against* extending that right. This creates a jarring contradiction. Either revise the opening to acknowledge this tension directly, or choose a different, less loaded way into your topic—perhaps starting with the actual policy debate (eg, "Don't Ask, Don't Tell" or its historical predecessor) rather than analogies to other movements. A strong opening should set up your argument, not undercut it.

Missing Evidence — This Is Critical

Your essay contains numerous placeholder brackets () where quotations should be. This is the most urgent issue to fix before any other revision matters. An argumentative essay built on textual analysis *depends* on direct evidence from your sources. Right now, a reader cannot evaluate whether your interpretation of Pattullo or Wall is accurate, because the actual words are missing. Insert the specific quotations, then make sure each one is followed by your own analysis explaining *why* it supports your claim, not just a restatement of the quote's content.

Strengthen and Complicate Your Counterargument

Your treatment of Wall's article is currently too dismissive. Calling his tone "lighthearted" and asserting that his comparisons "cannot be made" is an assertion, not an argument. Ask yourself: *why* is the comparison between musical taste and sexual discomfort flawed? Is it a category error? A false equivalence? Spell out the logical mechanics of your objection rather than simply declaring the comparison invalid. Similarly, when you write "How can this be if there are already concerns...?" — this is a rhetorical question standing in for actual analysis. Replace it with a developed argument.

More broadly, your essay would be stronger if you engaged more generously with Wall's civil rights framing before rejecting it. Right now you state that Wall "does not see the point" Pattullo is making — but a good counterargument should show that you understand *why* Wall holds his position, and then explain specifically where his reasoning breaks down (eg, is he ignoring practical realities? Overgeneralizing about rights? Assuming cohesion won't be affected?).

Watch for Slippery-Slope Reasoning

Several claims move quickly from a premise to a dramatic conclusion without intermediate steps — for example, the claim that allowing gay servicemembers would lead to homosexuality being "virtually accepted" and result in the "disintegration of camaraderie." This is a significant logical leap. Academic argumentation requires you to show the causal chain, not just assert the endpoint. Consider: does Pattullo himself provide evidence for this escalation, or are you extending his argument further than he does? Being precise about what your source actually claims versus what you are inferring will make your analysis more credible.

Reconsider the Conclusion's Framing

Ending with "Before reading Pattullo's article it was decided by the writer of this essay that..." shifts the essay into personal narrative, which is inconsistent with the analytical voice used elsewhere. Academic arguments are typically framed as claims supported by evidence, not as an account of the writer's changing opinions. Consider revising this into a synthesis of your strongest points, restating why Pattullo's practical concerns outweigh Wall's civil-rights framing, without narrating your personal journey.

Sentence-Level Clarity

Several sentences need grammatical revision for clarity, eg: "it will be ultimately decided that there should be a ban gays serving in the military" and "Pattullo's strong and convincing claims has had an affect." Read your essay aloud during revision — this will help you catch subject-verb agreement issues and awkward phrasing.

Priority for Revision
Insert all missing quotations.
Deepen your rebuttal of Wall with logical, not just tonal, critique.
Resolve the tension in your opening analogy.
Proofread for grammar and sentence structure.

You have a workable structure — now it needs the evidentiary and logical scaffolding to support it.

Feedback on "Mandatory Drug Testing in Athletics" Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a compelling, well-defined argumentative topic with a clear thesis and logical structure. Your essay moves through several strong supporting points—ethics, health risks, and the protection of fair competition—and you've clearly done research into multiple sources. However, the essay needs significant revision in three key areas: citation completeness, source integration, and paragraph focus. Below is detailed feedback organized by these priorities.

Critical Issue: Missing Quotations

The most urgent problem is the repeated use of  placeholders where quotations should appear. In an argumentative essay relying heavily on expert testimony, these gaps are fatal to your credibility—readers cannot evaluate your evidence. Before any other revision, you must insert the actual quotations from Chaplin, Thompson, Wadler, and the other sources you reference. Without these, the essay currently reads as an outline rather than a finished argument.

Source Integration and Attribution

Even once quotations are inserted, work on integrating them more smoothly:
Avoid dropping quotes with only "he stated" or "the article states." Instead, use signal phrases that indicate *why* the quote matters (eg, "Chaplin argues that random testing is necessary because...").
Follow every quotation with your own analysis explaining its significance to your argument. Right now, several paragraphs quote and then move immediately to a new point without unpacking the quote's meaning.
Double-check names and titles for accuracy—"National Collegiate Association of Athletes" should be the "National Collegiate Athletic Association (NCAA)," and "Lial Alsado" appears to be a misspelling of Lyle Alzado.

Paragraph Organization and Focus

Several paragraphs try to do too much at once. For example, the third paragraph moves from ethics to fairness to Dr. Thompson's quote to judgment impairment—four ideas competing for space. Consider:
Dedicating one paragraph solely to the ethics/privacy argument, fully developing the counterargument (privacy rights) before refuting it with your evidence.
Separating the fairness-in-competition argument into its own paragraph, distinct from health risks.
Keeping the health effects paragraph focused only on physical/psychological harm, without introducing new claims about role models (which could become its own paragraph or be merged with the Ben Johnson example).

The Ben Johnson and Borlange/Test Case Sections

These are your strongest, most concrete pieces of evidence—real-world cases and specific policy proposals. However, they currently feel like separate mini-reports rather than fully connected to your thesis. After describing the Borlange proposals or the Athletic Congress program, explicitly return to your argument: *how does this specific policy support mandatory testing?* Don't just summarize the program—evaluate it. You gesture toward this with "pros and cons," but the cons (athletes skipping tests due to distance) actually undermine your case for mandatory testing's practicality. Address this tension directly: acknowledge the logistical challenge and explain how stricter enforcement would resolve it, rather than letting the con sit unaddressed.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Word choice errors: "lesson" should be "lessen"; "meathod" should be "method"; "strickter" should be "stricter." Proofread carefully in your final pass.
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences merge independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, "The pros are that more athletes illegally using drugs would be caught however, the cons seem to be a drawback"). Break these into separate sentences or use a semicolon.
Rhetorical questions: You use several ("What will it take for athletes to think differently about steroids?" "What are fans...supposed to think?"). Use these sparingly—one strong rhetorical question is more effective than several scattered throughout, which can feel like padding rather than argument.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates your thesis almost verbatim rather than synthesizing your argument or leaving the reader with a final, memorable insight. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement: What should happen next? What is at stake for the future of sports if mandatory testing isn't universally adopted?

Priority Revision Checklist

Insert all missing quotations (non-negotiable).
Verify and correct names/titles (NCAA, Lyle Alzado).
Split overloaded paragraphs into single-focus units.
Explicitly connect each source/example back to your thesis.
Proofread for spelling and comma splices.
Rewrite conclusion to synthesize rather than repeat.

You have strong research and a clear, defensible thesis—the raw material for a solid essay is here. The next draft should focus on making your evidence fully visible and your paragraph logic tighter.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely complex, multi-sided debate, and you've done solid work identifying three distinct positions (Civil Liberties Group, Conservative Religious Group, and Anthony Lewis's "moment of silence" compromise). The structure—laying out two opposing views before proposing a third way—is a sensible strategy for this kind of argument. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: thesis clarity, use of evidence, and mechanics/proofreading.

---

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is currently buried inside one very long, overloaded sentence at the end of the first paragraph. Readers need to know *immediately* what you are arguing, not have to extract it from a sentence trying to summarize three articles at once. Consider breaking this into two sentences: one that briefly frames the debate, and one clear thesis statement, eg, "While the Civil Liberties Group presents more substantive reasoning than the Conservative Religious Group, both fail to offer a workable solution. Anthony Lewis's proposal for a moment of silence best balances the concerns of both sides."

The body paragraphs generally follow a logical pattern (claim → quote → your analysis), which is good. But each section would benefit from a stronger topic sentence that tells the reader *why* this point matters to your overall argument, not just what the group claims.

Use of Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Right now, your paragraphs rely very heavily on direct quotations, and in several places you critique the Conservative Religious Group for "just stating opinion without evidence"—but your own paragraphs sometimes do the same thing by quoting a source without unpacking *why* the quote is persuasive or flawed. For every quotation you include, add 2-3 sentences of your own analysis: What is the underlying assumption? Is the logic sound? What evidence would strengthen or weaken it?

Also, be careful about fairness. You praise the Civil Liberties articles as "detailed and unbiased" while calling the Conservative articles "biased" — but bias should be demonstrated through analysis of the *reasoning*, not simply asserted. If Applebome or Klinghoffer are citing statistics or expert opinions, evaluate those specifically rather than dismissing the whole argument as biased. This will make your critique more credible and rigorous.

Paragraph on "Ethics and Religion"

This paragraph has multiple quotations stacked together without enough of your own connecting language. Try paraphrasing at least one of these points in your own words, then follow with a shorter, more targeted quotation. This will improve flow and demonstrate that you understand the material rather than are stitching quotes together.

The Solution Paragraphs (Lewis)

These paragraphs are actually your strongest, because you engage more directly with the debate (eg, addressing Gary Bauer's counterargument). Expand this section—it's your thesis's centerpiece, yet it currently receives less development than the two "problem" sections. Explain more precisely *how* a moment of silence answers the constitutional concerns of the Civil Liberties Group *and* the spiritual/moral concerns of the Conservative Religious Group. This is the crux of your argument and deserves the most detailed treatment.

Clarity and Word Choice

Several sentences would benefit from more precise vocabulary. For example, "This moment of silence will sustain from hurting anyone" should likely read "will prevent hurting anyone" or "will avoid harming anyone." Similarly, phrases like "give a good reason" or "this is a good statement" appear repeatedly—vary this language and be more specific about *what makes* the statement effective (eg, "This appeal to statistics strengthens the argument because…").

Grammar and Mechanics

There are numerous spelling and typographical errors that need correction before this essay is submission-ready: *arrise → arise; know as → known as; torwads/torward → towards; conatin → contain; ow → how; stengthen → strengthen; mionorities → minorities; titles → titled; Gary Baur → Gary Bauer* (verify spelling). Proofread carefully, ideally aloud, to catch these.

Also watch subject-verb agreement and sentence length—several sentences run long and lose clarity partway through (eg, the opening sentence of the essay). Breaking these into shorter units will make your argument easier to follow.

---

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite the introduction to state a clear, concise thesis.
Add analytical commentary after every quotation—don't let quotes speak for themselves.
Balance your treatment of "bias": support this claim with specific reasoning, not just assertion.
Expand the Lewis/solution section, since it's your main argumentative contribution.
Proofread thoroughly for spelling and word choice.

With these revisions, your essay will move from summarizing three articles to making a sharp, well-supported argument of your own. You have good instincts about structure and comparison—now the goal is to make your *own voice and reasoning* more prominent throughout.

Feedback Report: Death Penalty Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, genuinely debated topic, and your three-pronged thesis (ineffective, immoral, flawed in practice) gives the essay a clear organizational backbone. The paper shows real engagement with the issue. However, there are several areas—citation, logical development, tone, and structure—that need attention before this reaches its full potential.

Citations and Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)

The bracketed placeholders () appear throughout the essay where quotations or statistics should be. This is a serious problem: an argumentative essay lives or dies by its evidence, and right now readers cannot evaluate your claims about Forst, Solomon, or Jim Gittings because the actual words are missing. Before revising anything else, you must:

Insert the actual quotations, with page numbers and full citations.
Introduce sources with more context. Who is Forst? What kind of study did he conduct (how many states, what years, what methodology)? Credibility depends on specifics, not just a name and a date.
Make sure quotations are integrated smoothly and followed by your own analysis, not just dropped in.

Logical Development

Several claims need stronger support or more careful reasoning:

The "murderers will kill witnesses" argument: This is an interesting inference from Forst's data, but you present it as your own speculation ("I feel that Forst's argument is very logical because...") rather than something Forst actually demonstrated. Be clear about where the source's argument ends and your inference begins.
The claim about crime rates increasing "especially murder": This needs a citation. Broad claims about crime trends are often contested and should not be asserted without data.
The appeals/wealthy lawyers argument: This is a good point about inequality in sentencing, but it's an argument against *how* the death penalty is administered, not against the death penalty itself in principle. Consider distinguishing between "capital punishment is wrong in theory" and "capital punishment is applied unjustly in practice"—these are different claims and deserve separate treatment.
Wrongful convictions: This is your strongest practical argument. Give it more space and at least one concrete example (there are well-documented cases) rather than a general assertion.

Structure and Paragraph Focus

Your paragraphs sometimes contain multiple ideas that would be clearer if separated:

The morality paragraph shifts between the "eye for an eye" counterargument, Gittings's quotation, and the claim that execution doesn't satisfy victims. Each deserves its own space, with a clear transition explaining how they connect back to your thesis.
Consider giving the "flawed system" paragraph (money/lawyers, backlog, wrongful convictions) three distinct sub-arguments, each with its own evidence, rather than moving quickly through all three.

Counterargument Treatment

You raise opposing views (deterrence, "eye for an eye," political motivations) but sometimes dismiss them too quickly ("No, to me no murder is right, not even the death penalty"). A stronger essay would:

State the counterargument fully and fairly before rebutting it.
Rebut with evidence or reasoning, not just assertion ("No" is not a rebuttal).
Consider dedicating a full paragraph to the strongest counterargument (likely deterrence or victims' rights) and systematically dismantling it.

Tone and Word Choice

Phrases like "full-proof" should be "foolproof."
"the increases crime we face" has a grammatical error—revise to "the increased crime."
Rhetorical questions ("is it moral for the government to turn right around and murder them") work but should be followed by developed reasoning, not just a quick "No."
The essay occasionally slips into casual phrasing ("give people something to talk about," "take no prisoners"). This is fine in moderation but revisit whether it undermines the seriousness of your argument in an academic context.

Introduction and Conclusion

The introduction is somewhat long before arriving at the thesis. Consider trimming the list of organizations and getting to your argument more directly.
The conclusion introduces a new idea (education as a solution) that isn't developed anywhere else in the essay. Either remove it or add a paragraph earlier that builds this point, so the conclusion isn't introducing fresh material.
Strengthen your final paragraph by more directly echoing your three main points (ineffectiveness, immorality, systemic flaws) before pivoting to your closing statement.

Priority Revision Steps

Fill in all citations with real, properly cited evidence.
Separate compound paragraphs into single-focus paragraphs.
Strengthen counterargument treatment with evidence-based rebuttals.
Fix grammar/word choice issues.
Align conclusion with body content (remove or develop the education point).

You have a solid foundation—now it's about deepening the evidence and tightening the logic. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report: Prayer in Public Schools Essay

Overall Impression

This essay demonstrates a clear structural strategy: presenting the pro-prayer argument, then systematically countering it with opposing evidence. This claim-and-rebuttal pattern is effective for argumentative writing and shows genuine engagement with multiple sources. However, the essay currently has one glaring technical problem and several substantive issues that need attention before this can be considered a polished piece of work.

Critical Issue: Missing Quotations

The most urgent problem is the repeated use of  placeholders throughout the essay where direct quotations should appear. This happens at least ten times and severely undermines the essay's credibility, since an argumentative essay relying on synthesis of sources cannot function without the actual textual evidence. Before any other revision, you must return to your sources and insert the specific quotations you intended to use, along with proper in-text citations (author, page number, or paragraph number depending on your citation style). Readers currently cannot evaluate whether your characterization of these sources is accurate.

Strengths to Preserve

The opening scene is vivid and effectively humanizes the abstract policy debate—showing three different students' reactions to mandatory prayer illustrates your thesis before you've even stated it.
The organizational logic (claim, then rebuttal, repeated for each pro-prayer argument) gives the essay a predictable and easy-to-follow structure.
Your alternative solution (voluntary before/after-school prayer groups) is a thoughtful synthesis that moves beyond simple "for/against" framing and shows independent thinking.

Areas Needing Revision

Historical accuracy. You state that the 1962 Supreme Court decision "prohibited prayer in the public school classrooms." This is imprecise. *Engel v. Vitale* (1962) prohibited *school-sponsored, officially organized* prayer—not all forms of prayer (silent or voluntary prayer, for instance, remains constitutionally protected). Since your own proposed solution rests on this distinction between mandatory and voluntary prayer, you need to state the actual holding correctly. This strengthens rather than weakens your argument.

Logical inconsistency in your causation argument. You rightly critique the pro-prayer side for committing a correlation/causation fallacy: the decline of prayer in schools does not prove prayer *caused* social order. This is a sharp and valid critique. However, you then use Klinghoffer's argument about the Episcopal Church's declining membership (correlated with the 1979 prayer book revision) and the *Churching of America* thesis without applying the same scrutiny. If correlation isn't causation for the pro-prayer side, you need to either apply the same standard to your own evidence or explain why this instance is different (eg, stronger mechanism, more controlled comparison, converging evidence from multiple denominations). Right now this reads as inconsistent standards applied to different sides.

Uneven treatment of the strongest counterarguments. Your rebuttal of the "75% of Americans support prayer" statistic is somewhat thin—you note the sample might be biased, but don't offer a competing statistic or deeper analysis. Since this is likely the pro-prayer camp's most persuasive piece of evidence (broad public support), it deserves a more substantive response, not just speculation about sampling bias.

Word choice and precision. Some phrasing softens your argument unnecessarily. "This claim is quite weak" is an assertion, not an argument—show me why through logic or evidence in the same sentence, rather than asserting weakness and then providing evidence after the fact. Similarly, "with these numerous examples, it is clear" is a common transitional overreach; let the reader draw that conclusion from evidence rather than declaring it.

Proofreading. There are several errors to correct in revision:
   - "many writes" → "many writers"
   - "intrest" → "interest"
   - "Sid duf" → likely "Siddur" (check spelling/spacing)
   - "oppotunity" → "opportunity"
   - "parishoners" → "parishioners"
   
   These are minor individually, but accumulate to suggest insufficient proofreading, which can undermine credibility with an academic reader.

The conclusion restates rather than synthesizes. Your final paragraphs largely repeat points already made (freedom of religion vs. freedom from religion; prayer didn't prevent past social ills). Consider using the conclusion to address a complication: What about students whose only exposure to community prayer would be through school-based voluntary groups? Does your solution adequately address equity of access, or could extracurricular prayer groups create their own form of social pressure? Engaging with a residual complexity would strengthen your ending rather than simply restating the thesis.

Priority Revision Steps

Insert all missing quotations with proper citations.
Correct the description of the *Engel v. Vitale* ruling.
Address the causation-fallacy inconsistency between your critique of the pro-prayer side and your use of the Episcopalian/Klinghoffer evidence.
Strengthen your rebuttal of the 75% statistic.
Proofread carefully for spelling and word-choice errors.

You have a solid argumentative skeleton here—the revision work is about strengthening the logical consistency and technical completeness of the essay, not rebuilding its structure.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay engages a genuinely important debate and takes a clear position: prayer should not be instituted during the school day because it imposes religion without producing meaningful religious experience. This is a defensible, focused thesis, and you support it with several distinct lines of reasoning (parental responsibility, lack of student engagement, freedom of expression, generic/meaningless prayer). However, the essay needs significant revision in four areas: source integration, argumentative structure, counterargument handling, and prose clarity.

Source Integration (Most Urgent Issue)

Nearly every quotation in your essay appears as  rather than actual text. This is a serious problem—right now, a reader cannot evaluate your evidence at all. Before you revise anything else, go back to your sources and:

Insert the actual quotations.
Introduce each quotation with a signal phrase that tells the reader *who* is speaking and *why their authority matters* (eg, "Ralph Reed of the Christian Coalition argues that...").
Follow every quotation with your own explanation of what it means and how it supports your specific claim. Right now you sometimes do this ("She is stating that..."), but do it consistently and more analytically—don't just restate the quote, interpret its significance for your argument.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is buried at the end of a long introductory paragraph. Sharpen it into one clear sentence early on, and consider forecasting your main supporting reasons so the reader knows the shape of the argument to come (eg, "because it usurps parental responsibility, produces only rote recitation rather than genuine reflection, and violates the pluralism the Constitution protects").

Several paragraphs raise multiple ideas that deserve separate treatment. For example, the paragraph beginning "If the government actually writes a universal prayer..." moves from meaninglessness, to academic time lost, to the "decadent" school system—three different claims crammed together. Give each major idea its own paragraph with a clear topic sentence, and make sure every paragraph clearly answers "how does this prove my thesis?"

You also repeat the same point (that morals come from parents/community, not school) in both paragraph 2 and paragraph 3. Consolidate these into a single, fuller paragraph rather than restating the idea.

Counterargument Paragraph

This paragraph is currently the weakest part of your argument. You acknowledge that some students—those without strong family/community moral guidance—might genuinely benefit from prayer time. Rather than refuting this, you essentially agree and pivot to proposing after-school religious activities. This is a reasonable policy alternative, but structurally it undercuts your thesis instead of defending it. Two options:

Reframe it as a genuine rebuttal: explain why even these students would not benefit from *in-class* prayer (eg, because it would still be rushed, generic, or coercive), and *then* offer the after-school alternative as your counter-proposal, explicitly stating that this shows your position is not opposed to religious expression, just to *government-imposed, in-class* prayer.
Move it to a separate "solution" section near your conclusion, clearly labeled as an alternative policy, so it doesn't read as capitulation in the middle of your argument.

Logical Precision

Watch claims that are asserted but not fully supported. For instance: "there is a little effect on the thoughts of most students" after discussing crime rates needs evidence, not just assertion. Similarly, "the previously stated source is quoted saying" needs a clearer logical bridge—explain *why* the shallow content of the sample prayer proves your broader claim about lack of meaning.

Also reconsider the line, "This is a contradiction, because the institution of prayer... would hinder the student's ability to freely express their faith." Make explicit *how* mandated prayer contradicts free expression—spell out the reasoning (mandated prayer imposes a specific religious form, which by definition limits the freedom to express one's *own* faith or non-faith).

Style and Mechanics

Fix minor errors: "moral values i children" → "in children"; "Even is there is" → "Even if there is."
Avoid loaded language that weakens academic tone, such as calling the school system "decadent"—this editorializes rather than argues.
Some sentences are overly long and stack multiple clauses. Break these into shorter, more direct sentences for clarity.
Vary sentence openings—many paragraphs begin with "Prayer..." or "The government..."—for better flow.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph restates the thesis but doesn't synthesize your strongest points or leave the reader with a memorable closing idea. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement—what *should* happen instead (referencing your after-school alternative)—to give the essay a constructive close rather than a repetition of the opening claim.

Priority for Revision: (1) insert and analyze real quotations, (2) reorganize paragraphs for one-idea-per-paragraph clarity, (3) strengthen the counterargument section, (4) proofread for grammar and tone.

Feedback Report: Animal Testing Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult ethical topic and you clearly have researched material to work with (Traystman's research, the Draize test, thalidomide, the dog experiments). However, the essay needs significant revision in three key areas: establishing a clear, consistent thesis; supporting claims with properly cited evidence; and engaging more fairly with the opposing viewpoint.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction spends time on dilemmas in general before narrowing to animal testing, but your actual position doesn't emerge clearly until well into the essay. State your thesis explicitly in the introduction: something like, "While animal rights activists raise legitimate ethical concerns, animal testing remains ethically justifiable when the human benefit substantially outweighs animal suffering."

This leads to a bigger problem: your argument is inconsistent. You claim humans have a God-given right to use animals "as they please," which suggests testing is always justified. But later you concede that the dog-shock depression experiments were unjustified because "the benefit to humans does not substantially outweigh the pain." These are two different ethical standards—one absolute, one consequentialist/cost-benefit. Pick one framework and apply it consistently. The cost-benefit approach is more defensible academically; consider building your whole argument around it and dropping the religious authority claim, which will only persuade readers who already share your faith background.

Use of Evidence

Several missing citations (marked ) need to be filled in before this essay is complete—we don't know what Bible passage, what Waddams quote, what Traystman quote, or what Bromey said. These aren't minor gaps; they're central pieces of evidence.

The thalidomide paragraph is confusing—you're using a case where *lack of* testing caused harm to argue for *more* testing, but this needs clearer signposting so readers see the logical connection you intend.

Treatment of Counterarguments

You do include activist viewpoints (pain avoidance, the Draize test), which is good practice. However, your engagement often tips into dismissiveness rather than fair rebuttal. For example:

The closing line ("who's life is more sacred, the life of a three year old boy, or a pig?") is a false dichotomy. No animal rights activist is arguing that pig lives matter more than child lives; they're asking whether *harming animals is always necessary or justified*. Rephrasing your conclusion to reflect the actual debate will make your argument more persuasive to a skeptical reader, not less.

The comparison to bullfighting and hunting ("these methods aren't under the same controversy") is a *whataboutism*—it deflects from your topic rather than defending it. Consider cutting this paragraph or reframing it to make a substantive point (eg, contrasting entertainment-based animal killing with testing that has documented medical outcomes).

The essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Right now, paragraphs shift between topics (ethics definitions, specific tests, researcher rebuttals, living conditions, slippery-slope arguments) without strong transitions. Consider organizing around 2-3 clear criteria for ethical testing (eg, necessity, proportionality of harm to benefit, availability of alternatives), then apply each criterion to your examples.

Style and Tone

Phrases like "Thank God for little Timmy" and framing activists as obstacles to saving children undermine the analytical tone expected in academic argument. You can still argue for your position passionately while representing the opposing side charitably—this actually strengthens your credibility.

Mechanics

Missing apostrophes ("friends side," "One's")

Run-on and fragmented sentences in the second paragraph

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Condom Distribution in Schools

Overall Impression

You've built a solid argumentative structure here, and your choice to organize the essay around three specific counterarguments (moral objections, abstinence-only advocacy, and condom reliability) gives readers a clear roadmap through a complex debate. Your rebuttals are logical and you clearly did research to support your position. That said, several issues need attention before this essay is ready for a final draft.

Strengths to Build On

Your concession-and-rebuttal structure is a mature rhetorical choice—rather than ignoring opposition, you name it directly and then answer it with evidence. This shows you understand that strong argumentation requires engaging with the strongest counterarguments, not just the weakest ones. Your topic sentences also do good work signaling the shift from one counterargument to the next ("Another criticism...," "The final argument...").

Areas Needing Revision

Missing Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)
Your essay currently has numerous bracketed placeholders  where statistics, quotations, and study results should appear. Right now, a reader cannot evaluate your argument because the actual evidence is absent. This needs to be your top priority: every claim that depends on a specific number, quotation, or finding must be filled in with the actual source material, properly cited (author, publication, page/paragraph if available). Until these are complete, it's impossible to assess whether your evidence truly supports your claims or whether it's being used selectively.

Weak the Analysis of Evidence
In several places, you present a finding and then move directly to a conclusion without explaining the logical connection. For example: "The results they received consisted of . Other studies in progress are finding similar results... This leads one to believe that if condoms were more readily available, students would be more apt to practice safer sex." The phrase "this leads one to believe" is doing a lot of work without showing your reasoning. Once you fill in the actual data, spend a sentence or two explaining *why* that data supports your claim—what is the mechanism connecting condom availability to reduced risk?

Undermining Your Own Argument in the Conclusion
Your final paragraph opens with: "there is no evidence available concerning how effective the distribution of condoms in schools is in decreasing the rate in which AIDS is spreading." This is a significant admission to make in your conclusion, where readers expect reinforcement of your thesis, not a concession that weakens it. If this is true, you need to address it earlier in the essay (perhaps when discussing the Baltimore study) and reframe it as a *reason for cautious optimism based on related evidence* rather than a last-minute admission of uncertainty. As written, it risks leaving readers with doubt rather than conviction.

Introducing New Material Too Late
The Robert Rygor quotation appears in your final paragraph, which is a strong emotional appeal but arrives without any setup. Since he has AIDS and his testimony seems compelling, consider moving this quotation (or at least referencing him) earlier in the essay—perhaps in your introduction to establish stakes, or in one of the body paragraphs as evidence. Save the conclusion for synthesizing your argument, not launching a new piece of evidence.

Citation Consistency
You cite Time magazine twice, Consumer Reports once, and reference the CDC, Epstein's article, and the Johns Hopkins study (note: "John Hopkins" should be "Johns Hopkins")—but the citation style is inconsistent. Decide whether you're using in-text citations with a Works Cited page, or simply naming sources in the prose, and apply that method uniformly throughout.

Repetitive Phrasing
Phrases like "sexually active teens," "high schools," and "combat the spread of AIDS" recur frequently, sometimes within the same paragraph. Vary your language to keep the prose engaging—for instance, alternate between "students," "adolescents," and "teenagers," and use pronouns or synonyms for "AIDS" (the epidemic, the disease, HIV transmission) where appropriate.

Strengthen the Abstinence Counterargument
Your abstinence section could be strengthened by acknowledging *why* abstinence-only advocates believe their position is realistic before you refute it—right now you assert "there is evidence to prove that the goal of abstinence is realistic" but immediately pivot to your rebuttal without engaging that evidence. A stronger paragraph would spend a sentence explaining their reasoning before dismantling it.

Next Steps

Fill in all bracketed citations with real, properly attributed evidence.
Add analytical sentences connecting each piece of evidence to your claim.
Revise the conclusion to avoid conceding uncertainty; end on a note of synthesis and conviction.
Relocate the Rygor quotation to an earlier, more strategically impactful position.
Proofread for repetition and citation consistency.

Once these revisions are made, this essay will have a much stronger evidentiary foundation to match its already-clear structure. Good work on the framework—now it's time to build out the walls.

Feedback on Your Essay: AIDS Discrimination

Your essay tackles an important and still-relevant topic, and your case studies (Woodley, Lentz, the Valhalla pharmacist) give the argument real human stakes. Below are areas to strengthen before your next draft.

Critical Logical Error to Fix Immediately

In your fourth paragraph, you write: "This is more than enough proof that HIV, and later AIDS, is not, and cannot be transmitted through casual contact. Therefore, co-workers, patients, friends, family, or anyone else in close contact with the victim is in danger of contracting the disease from everyday occurrences, or contact."

This sentence says the opposite of what you mean and directly undermines your entire thesis. You clearly meant to say they are *not* in danger. This kind of error is easy to make when drafting quickly, but it's the kind of mistake a careful reader will seize on to dismiss your whole argument. Reread your work sentence-by-sentence for logical consistency, especially around transition words like "therefore."

Fill in Your Sources

The placeholders (<\*>) throughout the essay indicate you intend to insert quotations later, but as it stands, your key pieces of evidence—Woodley's statement, the Macy's response, the Maclean's article, etc—are missing. A reader cannot evaluate an argument built on absent evidence. Before revising further, complete these citations with full, properly attributed quotations (author, publication, date), and make sure each source is introduced with context (who is Nora Underwood? What is her expertise or role?).

Strengthen Your Opening Claim

Your very first sentence—"AIDS is the most common disease of our world today"—is both unsupported and factually questionable (compare this to conditions like the common cold, cardiovascular disease, etc). Starting with a shaky, unverifiable claim weakens your credibility from the outset. Consider opening instead with a more defensible claim about the scope of AIDS-related discrimination, or a striking statistic (properly cited) about employment/insurance discrimination cases.

Clarify and Front-Load Your Thesis

Your actual thesis—that discrimination against people with HIV/AIDS in employment and insurance is legally and morally wrong—doesn't fully crystallize until several sentences into paragraph one, after a discussion of transmission methods. Consider restructuring so your thesis appears early and clearly, then use the transmission-method information as supporting evidence afterward, since it's actually part of your argument (low health risk) rather than throat-clearing.

Represent the Opposition Fairly

You describe the opposing argument as "weak" and call it a "fallacy" before you've actually presented it in good faith. Academic argumentation is more persuasive when you state the counterargument as strongly and fairly as its proponents would, and *then* refute it with evidence. Simply labeling it weak up front can come across as dismissive rather than rigorous.

Watch Your Rhetorical Tone

Phrases like "a martyr to the AIDS community," "in its ongoing quest to promote image above real people," "society would just as soon bury someone with AIDS alive," and the repeated rhetorical questions ("What is the difference? What makes the other terminal patients so righteous?") are emotionally charged. This kind of language can be effective in advocacy writing, but in an academic argumentative essay it risks undercutting your credibility—it can read as more sensational than analytical. Consider trimming some of this and replacing it with measured, evidence-based language, letting the facts of the Woodley and Lentz cases carry the emotional weight themselves.

Reconsider the ADA Paragraph

You write, "The government has recognized that a person with AIDS has a disability much like someone who is paralyzed has a disability. This is not true, but unfortunately it's the only way to legally keep employers...from discriminating." This is confusing and potentially damaging to your argument—you seem to be conceding that the legal protection you're relying on is built on a fiction. Clarify your position: are you arguing that AIDS *should* count as a disability under the law (a legal/functional classification), or that the comparison is inaccurate? As written, this passage weakens the legal argument you need for your conclusion.

Proofread Carefully

There are several typos and inconsistencies to catch in revision: "Wookley" vs. "Woodley," "A AIDS," "insurance car" (care), "final fays" (days), and inconsistent capitalization/spacing. These are minor individually, but accumulate to suggest the essay needs another close editing pass.

Overall

Your case studies are compelling and your instinct to combine legal, moral, and factual (medical transmission) arguments is a sound strategy. Focus your revision on: (1) fixing the logical contradiction, (2) completing your citations, (3) tightening your thesis placement, and (4) dialing back rhetorical flourishes in favor of precise, evidence-driven claims. This will make an already promising essay significantly more persuasive.

"difficult to content with" → likely "difficult to contend with"

Reorganize using Cooper's three criteria as a structural backbone, if possible.

Overall Impression

Goodrich, advertising practices). However, the essay currently reads more like a rough draft than a polished argument. Since your entire argument depends on external support to be persuasive, this is the first thing to fix. Beyond that, there are opportunities to sharpen your thesis, deepen your analysis, and tighten your prose.

Consider stating this thesis explicitly and specifically at the end of your introduction. Right now the introduction spends time defining "business" and "ethics" in general terms before revealing your actual argument. Try condensing the definitional material into one or two sentences and using the rest of the paragraph to preview your specific claim and the examples you'll use to support it.

Evidence and Citations

This is the area requiring the most urgent attention. You reference Fortune, Time, *Business Ethics and Common Sense*, Norton's article, and a case study on B.F. Goodrich—all promising sources—but the actual quotations and data are missing (marked with ). Without this evidence, readers cannot evaluate whether your sources actually support your claims. When you revise:

Insert the actual quotations/paraphrases.
After each piece of evidence, add 1-2 sentences of analysis explaining *how* the evidence proves your specific point (you do this well after the Fortune reference in paragraph 2—use that as a model).

Organization

The essay moves through several examples (free market relationships, ethical guidelines, Walton/Wal-Mart, advertising, Norton's article, B.F. Goodrich) but the connections between paragraphs are sometimes abrupt. Consider:

Informal tone: Phrases like "everyone is happy" and "irritates business" are too casual for academic argument. Aim for more formal, precise diction.
Run-on sentences: Several sentences (eg, the long sentence in the advertising paragraph beginning "This also hurts...") pack in multiple ideas without clear separation. Break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Pronoun clarity: "This" is used often without a clear antecedent (eg, "This shows...", "This also hurts..."). Specify what "this" refers to each time.

With these revisions, your essay's genuinely interesting argument will be much more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

You've chosen a manageable, well-defined task: comparing the argumentative strength of two authors on school prayer. Your organizational structure—examining claim, evidence, and treatment of opposition for each author—is a sound and appropriate framework for this kind of comparative analysis. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: sentence-level clarity, the depth of your analysis, the objectivity of your evaluation, and the completion of textual support (several quotations appear only as placeholders, which must be filled in before this essay is complete).

Strengths

Your criteria for evaluating "strong" argumentation (clear claim, supporting evidence, fair treatment of opposition, absence of fallacies) are appropriate and consistently applied.
You correctly identify Methvin's appeal to unqualified authority as a fallacy, and you explain *why* it weakens his argument rather than simply naming it.
Your discussion of Shriver's three-part structure (theological, educational, and social/minority-rights reasons) shows you understand how he builds momentum toward his central point.

Areas for Revision

Complete the Evidence
The placeholders () throughout the essay indicate that direct quotations have not been inserted. This is the most urgent fix—without the actual language from Shriver and Methvin, a reader cannot verify your claims about clarity, strength, or fallacy. Make sure every quotation is followed by *your own analysis* explaining specifically how the wording supports or undermines the author's argument.

Deepen Your Analysis, Don't Just Assert
In several places you tell the reader that a quote is "strong" or "hard to argue with" without explaining *why*. For example, you say Shriver's second reason "is hard to argue with" because "people would agree that learning takes place everywhere"—but you don't explain how this claim logically supports excluding prayer from schools. Strong analytical writing shows the *reasoning chain*, not just the verdict. Ask yourself after each quotation: What logical work is this evidence doing? Does it actually prove the claim, or does it merely sound persuasive?

Reconsider Objectivity
Your essay sets out to evaluate the *quality of argumentation*, but at times it reads as though you already agree with Shriver's conclusion and are working backward to justify that agreement. For instance, you note that Shriver's second reason "supports his claim more than his opponent's claim"—an admission that this is somewhat one-sided reasoning, yet you count it in his favor rather than scrutinizing it as a bias. A rigorous comparative analysis should hold both authors to the same standard and be willing to critique Shriver's weaknesses as readily as you critique Methvin's. Consider: does Shriver ever oversimplify, or rely on assumption rather than evidence? Even a small acknowledgment of Shriver's limitations would strengthen your credibility as an evaluator.

Revise the Introduction
Your opening paragraph contains several sweeping historical claims ("Religion essentially was the foundation of society of the past," "people learned through war and consequence that church and state do not mix") that are asserted without support. These generalizations distract from your actual thesis and risk sounding like unearned authority. Trim this opening considerably and move more quickly to the specific context (the 1962 Supreme Court ruling) and your thesis about Shriver vs. Methvin.

Strengthen the Conclusion
Your final paragraph largely repeats points already made rather than synthesizing them into a final insight. Consider ending with a reflection on *why* this comparison matters—what does it teach us about effective argumentation in public debates more broadly? This would give your essay a stronger sense of closure.

Proofread Carefully
The essay contains numerous grammatical errors that interfere with readability:
"it continues to be influential factor" → "an influential factor"
"people and learned through war" → "people have learned through war"
"This ruling ha become" → "has become"
"A research for his argument he conducted" → unclear; revise for clarity (perhaps: "As research for his argument, he conducted...")
"the strong Are was learned" — unclear fragment; check for missing/garbled text
Subject-verb agreement: "He defines the two most prominent voices in the debate are those of piety" — this sentence needs restructuring (eg, "He identifies the two most prominent voices in the debate as...")

A careful line-edit pass, ideally reading the essay aloud, will catch many of these issues.

Summary

Your comparative framework is solid, and your instinct to look for fallacies and evaluate claim-clarity is exactly the right approach for this assignment. Focus your revision on (1) inserting and analyzing the actual quotations, (2) explaining *why* evidence supports or weakens each claim rather than simply asserting it, (3) applying your critical standards more evenly to both authors, and (4) cleaning up sentence-level errors. These changes will transform a promising draft into a rigorous, persuasive essay.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, well-focused topic, and your position—media access yes, cameras no—is clear and defensible. The Harris case is a strong central example. However, the essay currently reads more like a research summary than a fully developed argument, and there are structural and mechanical issues that need attention before this reaches its potential.

Major Issues

Missing Quotations/Citations
The bracketed placeholders () appear throughout the essay in place of actual quotations from Vasquez, the media brief, Brelsford, the First Amendment text, Sherwood, and Morain. This is the most urgent issue to fix. These quotes are doing crucial argumentative work—they're meant to show *what* the warden's justification was, *why* the court ruled as it did, and *what* the First Amendment actually guarantees. Without them, readers cannot evaluate whether your claims are supported. Please insert the actual language before this essay is considered complete.

Thesis Needs Sharpening
Your opening claims that "the public has a right to be informed about anything and everything they want to be informed about." This is an overstatement that a critical reader will immediately push back on (does the public have a right to classified national security information? Private medical records?). Narrow this claim specifically to matters of public accountability—like how the state carries out capital punishment, a governmental action taken in citizens' name. This is a much stronger and more defensible position than an unqualified "right to know everything."

Underdeveloped Analysis of the Counterargument
You raise the "ratings" objection but dismiss it quickly by asserting it "does not override the First Amendment" and by asserting KQED "is not out for ratings" based on a spokesperson's own claim (which is not neutral evidence—of course a network representative would deny profit motives). Strengthen this section by:
Explaining *why* First Amendment protections for press access can outweigh discomfort with the content (perhaps drawing on the government-accountability rationale, or case law on public trials)
Acknowledging that even if ratings *were* a motive, this wouldn't necessarily disqualify the constitutional right to report

The Conclusion is Too Casual
"If you find the event grisly and gruesome, turn it off; it is that simple" undercuts the academic register of the rest of the essay. This flippant tone weakens your credibility. Consider replacing this with a more measured restatement of why public oversight of state executions matters, and why the print/camera distinction reasonably balances competing interests.

Structural Suggestions

Add a clear roadmap in your introduction. State explicitly that you will (1) narrate the Harris case, (2) explain the legal ruling and its rationale, (3) address and rebut the "ratings" objection, and (4) explain why cameras specifically (as opposed to reporters) can be restricted.

The security-risk paragraph (para. 6) is your strongest material—it offers concrete, specific reasons (staff identification, inmate agitation, physical safety near the gas chamber) for why cameras are treated differently than notebooks. Consider moving some of this reasoning earlier, or referencing it when you first introduce your thesis, since it's the crux of your print/camera distinction.

Attribute sources more consistently. You cite Brelsford, Sherwood, and Morain, but it's not always clear whether these are direct quotes, paraphrases, or your own interpretations of their views. Clean transitions like "According to..." or "As Sherwood explained..." will help readers track whose argument is whose.

Sentence-Level Issues

"There access should be restricted" → *Their* access
"One such case which receive national attention" → *received*
"the warden, Vasquez, had made to effort to justify" → *made no effort*
"Inmate are more tense" → *Inmates*
Proofread carefully for subject-verb agreement and homophone errors (there/their); these recur enough to distract from your argument.

Priority Revision Checklist

Fill in all missing quotations—this is essential, not optional
Narrow your opening claim about the public's "right to know"
Strengthen your rebuttal to the ratings objection with reasoning, not just assertion
Revise the conclusion to match the essay's academic tone

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing such a personal and vulnerable piece of writing. Your willingness to draw on your own experience gives this essay real emotional power, and that personal narrative is genuinely one of your strongest assets. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas to become a more effective piece of argumentative writing. Below are my main observations, organized by category.

Structure and Argument Development

Your thesis—that schools should teach sexual morals and values because parents and other institutions are failing to do so—is clear enough, but the essay's structure doesn't build a sustained case for it. Right now, the essay reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a logical progression of evidence toward a conclusion. Consider outlining your argument before revising: What is your claim? What are your 2-3 strongest pieces of evidence? What is the counterargument, and how do you refute it? Right now the counterargument section (paragraph 5) feels underdeveloped and disconnected from the rest.

Additionally, the personal narrative at the beginning and end works well as a frame, but the middle section shifts into a more generic, impersonal argumentative voice. Try to find ways to weave your personal experience more consistently throughout, or at least transition more smoothly between the personal and the general.

Missing Evidence

This is the most urgent issue. Several key sentences contain placeholder brackets  where statistics, study results, or quotations should be:

The statistic about pregnancies before 1989
The results of studies on sex education effectiveness
The claim about churches "hiding" from sex education

These gaps are currently fatal to your argument. An argumentative essay relies on evidence to be persuasive, and right now readers cannot evaluate your claims. Before doing any other revision, track down these sources, integrate the actual data, and cite them properly (check with your instructor on the required citation style). Vague phrases like "according to one article researched" also need to be replaced with specific, properly cited sources.

Logical Consistency

A few of your claims contradict each other or your own experience, and this weakens your credibility:

You state that "casual sex is unacceptable... degrading, animalistic," yet your personal narrative describes your own experience with premarital sex as ultimately leading to a marriage, a child, and (eventually) a lesson learned—not something purely "animalistic." Consider whether this judgment is essential to your argument or whether it oversimplifies your own more complex experience.

Your proposed sexual ethics curriculum includes both "abstinence... without commitment toward marriage" AND "the use of condoms to promote safe sex." These messages can coexist, but you should explain *how* schools would teach both without one undermining the other. Right now they sit side by side without connection.

You ask, "Why are these couples getting married if their intentions aren't to commit to each other?"—but this rhetorical question doesn't clearly connect to the surrounding argument about pregnancy statistics. Clarify the logical link.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring errors worth addressing systematically:

Apostrophes with plurals: "bird's and the bee's," "peer's," "youths'" should be "birds and bees," "peers," "youths" (no apostrophe needed for simple plurals).
Subject-verb agreement and verb tense: You shift between past and present tense somewhat inconsistently, especially in the final paragraph.
Sentence fragments: eg, "The loving compassion two people should give each other" is a fragment, not a complete sentence—it needs a verb or should be joined to the previous sentence.
Capitalization: "Father" and "Him" are capitalized when they shouldn't be (unless "Father" is being used as a direct address/name).

I'd recommend reading the essay aloud, which often reveals these issues more readily than silent reading.

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all evidentiary gaps with real, cited sources before revising anything else.
Strengthen the counterargument section—give your opposition's view more space and more respect before refuting it. Right now it appears only briefly and is dismissed rather than engaged with.
Reconsider absolute language ("unacceptable," "animalistic") that may alienate readers or contradict the nuance of your own story.
Proofread carefully for the grammar issues noted above, particularly apostrophe usage.
Strengthen your conclusion by connecting it more explicitly back to your thesis about *schools'* role, since right now it focuses almost entirely on your own parenting rather than on the institutional argument you're making.

Final Note

The emotional honesty here is compelling, and your firsthand experience with teen pregnancy gives you an authentic voice that many writers on this topic lack. With more rigorous evidence, tighter logic, and careful proofreading, this essay could be genuinely persuasive. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to bring a draft to office hours.

Feedback Report: "The Right to Refuse Life-Support"

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses a genuinely difficult and important ethical question, and your personal investment in the topic comes through in your final body paragraph, which is the strongest part of the essay. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of reflective musings than a tightly argued position paper. Below are specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis—"The right to refuse life-support is an individual one"—doesn't appear until the very end of the introduction, and even then it's somewhat lost among broader statements about medical advancement. Consider moving a clearer, more assertive version of this claim to the beginning, and make sure every subsequent paragraph visibly supports it. Right now, several paragraphs (on defining life, defining death, and physicians' attitudes) feel like independent essays on related topics rather than building blocks for your central argument. Ask of each paragraph: "How does this prove that the decision belongs to the individual?"

The Missing Evidence Problem

I notice repeated placeholders () throughout the draft. These represent a serious gap: essays on ethical and medical topics need concrete support—quotations from ethicists or physicians, references to specific medical or legal cases (eg, landmark right-to-die cases), statistics, or definitions from credible sources. Right now the essay relies almost entirely on generalized personal assertions ("Everyone has their own attitude toward life," "I think most physicians are very sensitive..."). These claims need to be grounded in something outside your own impression—a cited case, a philosopher's argument, a medical definition—or the essay will read as opinion rather than argument. This is the single most important revision priority.

Organization

The essay's structure loosely follows: define life → define death → competing views on life support → physicians' perspective → your personal view → conclusion. This is a reasonable arc, but the transitions between sections are weak and sometimes abrupt (eg, "In time the focus was on breathing" appears with no lead-in explaining whose focus, or when). Add clear topic sentences that state the purpose of each paragraph and connect it explicitly back to your thesis. The physicians' paragraph, in particular, feels disconnected—if you keep it, clarify why the medical profession's attitude matters to your argument about *individual* rights (perhaps as a counterpoint, or as evidence that physicians already defer to patient autonomy).

Development

Several paragraphs assert ideas without developing them. For example: "Our memories of childhood define how we look upon events" is stated but never explained or illustrated. Similarly, the cryogenics reference is intriguing but dropped almost as soon as it's raised. Choose two or three of your strongest points and develop them fully with examples, rather than listing many undeveloped ones. Your final paragraph on personal criteria (breathing, feeling, loving, communicating) is your best material—consider expanding this earlier in the essay as a concrete anchor for your abstract discussion of "defining life."

Style and Clarity

Your prose sometimes relies on short, choppy declarative sentences that state similar ideas repeatedly without advancing the argument (eg, the string of sentences in paragraph two: "Our memories... Our personality... Our memories, our personality..."). Combine and vary these sentences, and cut redundancy. Rhetorical questions are used effectively in places ("Where do you draw the line between life and death?") but overused by the end of the essay; reserve them for moments of real emphasis.

Grammar and Mechanics

A revision pass should address:
"People with this frame of mind have even tried cryogenics" — needs a transition connecting to the previous sentence.
"people o want" — typo for "who want."
"considered when deciding what makes our life important to use" — "use" should be "us."
"Would we want someone else to make this decision without regarding our wish(es)" — awkward phrasing; consider "without regard for our wishes."
Watch for comma splices, especially in the concluding paragraph ("...the answer must be our own decision.")

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen and relocate your thesis to the end of a more focused introduction.
Replace every  placeholder with actual cited evidence—medical case studies, legal precedents (eg, end-of-life rulings), or expert commentary.
Cut or repurpose the physicians paragraph so it clearly serves your argument.
Move your personal criteria for "essential life" earlier, and use it as a recurring anchor.
Revise transitions so each paragraph clearly answers "why does this matter to my claim that the decision is an individual one?"
Proofread carefully for typos and awkward phrasing.

With stronger evidence and tighter organization, this essay has real potential—your final paragraph shows you can write with clarity and conviction. Bring that same voice to the rest of the essay.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a complex and important issue, and your personal experience with the welfare system gives it authenticity and emotional resonance that many academic papers lack. You clearly care about this topic, and that passion comes through. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen your argument significantly.

Strengths to Build On

Your personal anecdote about being encouraged to quit your job is powerful and serves as effective evidence for your central claim about how welfare can inadvertently discourage self-sufficiency. Your proposed solutions (job training, subsidized childcare, transitional support) are concrete and reasonable, and citing Spring Meadows Children's Home gives your argument a real-world model to point to.

Areas Needing Revision

Missing Citations and Quotations
You have two placeholder brackets  where quotations should appear—one for Hoover's statement and one for the social worker's comment. These are significant gaps. The Hoover quote is meant to be a cornerstone of your historical argument, and its absence weakens your credibility. Please locate and insert the actual quotation with proper citation, or remove the reference to it if you cannot verify it.

Historical Accuracy
Your claim that "The Welfare System was begun by President Herbert Hoover in the early 1930s" needs verification. Hoover was largely opposed to federal relief programs and favored local/private charity; the Federal Emergency Relief Administration and other New Deal programs began under FDR, not Hoover. This is a factual error that undermines your historical argument. Please research this more carefully—your library's databases or a source like the Social Security Administration's history page would help.

Logical Gaps and Oversimplification
You move quickly from "welfare has existed for decades" to "welfare has eroded family values" without establishing the causal mechanism clearly. Consider:
What evidence (beyond your personal experience) supports the claim that welfare *causes* dependency rather than correlating with it?
Are there studies, statistics, or expert testimony you could cite to strengthen this connection?
Have you considered counterarguments? Some critics argue that poverty itself, not welfare, drives these patterns. Addressing this directly would make your argument more persuasive to skeptical readers.

Characterization of Opponents
Your portrayal of Gingrich and Republicans relies heavily on characterization ("driven by a growing coldness toward the poor," "obvious that Mr. Gingrich does not understand") rather than analysis of their actual stated reasoning. Even if you disagree with their proposals, presenting their arguments more fairly (and then refuting them with evidence) will make your critique more credible to readers who don't already share your view. Consider: what reasons *do* they give for their proposals, even if you find those reasons unpersuasive?

Structure and Organization
The essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Consider revising your introduction to explicitly preview your argument's structure: (1) history of welfare, (2) how it changed, (3) why current reform proposals fail, (4) what should be done instead. This will help readers follow your reasoning.

Mechanics and Proofreading
There are several recurring errors to address:
"It's" is used where "its" (possessive) is needed multiple times (eg, "It's purpose," "it's traditions," "it's values")
Inconsistent capitalization ("Country," "Society," "Government" are capitalized inconsistently)
Some sentence fragments and run-ons, particularly in the list of Gingrich's proposals
"food rent" appears to be missing a comma or word

Suggestions for Next Draft

Verify and properly cite all historical claims and quotations
Add 2-3 outside sources—statistics on welfare dependency, studies on child outcomes, or scholarly perspectives on welfare reform—to supplement your personal experience
Strengthen your counterargument section: dedicate a paragraph to steelmanning the opposing view before dismantling it
Revise the Gingrich section to quote or paraphrase actual policy language rather than paraphrasing critically; this will make your rebuttal more credible
Proofread carefully for the it's/its issue and punctuation

Final Thought

Your core argument—that meaningful welfare reform must address underlying family and community structures, not just benefit cuts—is a sophisticated and defensible position. With stronger evidence, more careful historical grounding, and fairer treatment of opposing views, this essay could be quite persuasive. Your voice and personal investment are assets; now pair them with more rigorous research and argumentation.
