Feedback on Your Draft: Avenue Commons Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely, locally relevant topic, and your passion for it comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected complaints than a unified argument. To strengthen this piece, focus on clarifying your central claim, tightening your logic, and supporting your assertions with proper evidence.

Thesis and Central Claim

Your title concept—that Avenue Commons is "unethical"—needs to be defined much more precisely. Right now, "ethical" seems to mean different things in different paragraphs: sometimes financial burden, sometimes fairness to non-resident taxpayers, sometimes institutional priorities (grass vs. sports vs. academics). Ask yourself: what specific ethical principle is being violated? Is it that students weren't consulted? That the cost-benefit analysis is flawed? That it prioritizes image over substance? Choose one clear ethical framework (eg, fairness, transparency, or cost-benefit to the community) and build your paragraphs around it.

The opening comparison to UCLA's diversity courses is especially confusing and risks alienating readers—it's unclear what the two situations have in common, and the phrasing ("so-called psychological differences") suggests a dismissiveness that could distract from your actual argument. Consider cutting this comparison entirely.

Structure and Organization

The essay moves through several topics—transit costs, dorm residency, campus identity, existing green space, football programs, the rifle team, and tuition—but the connections between them are often implicit rather than stated. Each paragraph should open with a topic sentence that ties directly back to your thesis. For example, when you pivot to discussing the rifle team, explain explicitly how this relates to your critique of Avenue Commons (presumably: "Marquette should not fund niche projects when core academic and financial needs go unmet"). Without that bridge, readers may see this as a tangent rather than supporting evidence.

Similarly, the football team proposal complicates your argument rather than reinforcing it. You spend a paragraph criticizing the rifle team for being an expensive program that serves few students, then propose a Division I football program—one of the most expensive undertakings a university can pursue. This creates a logical inconsistency: if your argument is "don't spend on programs serving small numbers of people," a football team (with recruiting costs, scholarships, stadium needs, etc) doesn't clearly satisfy that standard, even if it has "broad appeal" as a spectator sport. You need to explain *why* football is different—cost recovery through ticket sales? Recruitment? Be explicit and quantify this if possible.

Evidence and Sourcing

This is the area needing the most attention. You cite specific figures ($10 million, $1 million to $600,000 in transit losses, tuition of $12,500) and quote Todd Clausen, but there are no citations, and several bracketed placeholders () are left unfilled. Before revising further, go back and:
Attribute every statistic to a source (a Marquette Tribune article, city council minutes, transit authority report, etc)
Fill in the missing quotations and mottos referenced with brackets
Verify numbers like the "$5,000 average student income" and "50 shells for $25–$250"—these claims need citations or they will read as unsubstantiated guesses

Without sourcing, your strongest points (the financial burden argument) lose credibility, which is unfortunate because they are your most persuasive material.

Logical Consistency

Reconsider the transit argument: you assert the U-Pass system "won't help" but don't explain the mechanism by which it would fail. Walk the reader through your reasoning—are current riders already paying into a system that would be diluted? Show the math or logic, not just the conclusion.

Also reconsider the "no one uses the existing green space" claim—this is anecdotal. If you want it to carry rhetorical weight, cite usage data or survey results, or reframe it explicitly as your personal observation.

Tone and Style

Several phrases are quite informal for an academic essay ("the gesus," "this hoopla," "I hate to rain on many a person's parade," "You get the idea"). While a personal voice can be effective, you'll want a register that signals credibility to an academic audience. I'd suggest revising these into more measured phrasing while keeping your directness.

Mechanics

There are numerous typos and grammatical errors that need correcting in revision: "physcological" → psychological; "effected" → affected; "It you go" → If you go; "Not once have I seen... unless they ran track" is a confusing sentence; "he well have to" → he will have to; "off spending" → of spending. A careful proofreading pass is essential before this is submission-ready.

Suggested Revision Plan

Rewrite your introduction to state one clear, defensible ethical claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each supports that specific claim, with explicit transitions.
Add real citations for all statistics and quotes.
Resolve the football/rifle team tension by explaining your standard for "worthwhile" programs.
Proofread thoroughly for grammar and word choice.

You have real insight into this issue and clearly care about affordability and campus identity—channeling that into a tighter, better-supported argument will make this essay much more persuasive.

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely difficult and emotionally charged case, and your essay demonstrates real engagement with the material. Your central argument—that the Illinois Supreme Court's decision failed to account for Richard's psychological development—is compelling and timely. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: sharpening your thesis, strengthening your use of evidence, and tightening your prose. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Your strongest claim is buried in paragraph four: "The Illinois Supreme Court made an immense mistake in transferring custody of Richard." Consider moving a clearer version of this thesis to your introduction so readers know exactly where you stand from the outset. Currently, your opening paragraph is mostly narrative background; it doesn't signal your argument until quite late.

Your comparison between Baby Jessica and Baby Richard is a genuinely strong analytical move—using Erikson's stages to differentiate the two cases shows sophisticated thinking. Push this further: explain *why* the initiative-vs.-guilt stage is more psychologically damaging to disrupt than whatever stage Jessica was in. Right now you assert the four-year-old's attachment is stronger than the toddler's, but you should substantiate this claim with more than intuition or rhetorical questions.

Speaking of which: rhetorical questions like "Think back to when you were four years old..." and "Nobody likes to be hurt, so why go through with it?" feel more like emotional appeals than argumentative support. Academic essays can certainly use pathos, but it should be earned through evidence, not substituted for it. Consider cutting or significantly revising these moments.

Use of Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay contains numerous bracketed placeholders () where quotations, citations, or paraphrased information should appear. Before your next draft, these all need to be filled in with actual source material, properly cited (check whether your instructor wants MLA, APA, or another format).

More importantly, when you do have source information (the Newsweek article on Baby Jessica, the Erikson reference, the ABA Journal piece by Mark Hansen), you tend to *mention* the source rather than *analyze* it. For example, after introducing Erikson's "initiative vs. guilt" concept, walk the reader through his actual argument about what happens when this conflict goes unresolved, then connect that explicitly to Richard's specific situation—rather than moving quickly to your own inference ("he will not take the initiative").

Similarly, the paragraph on the Uniform Adoption Act references Hansen's article but doesn't tell us what the article actually says or how it supports your argument. Right now this paragraph reads as informational rather than argumentative—what is the connection between this legal reform and your core claim about Richard's psychological welfare?

Structure and Paragraphing

Your organization mostly follows a logical progression: background → comparison to Baby Jessica → psychological analysis → effects on the Does → broader effects on adoption as an institution → conclusion. This structure works, but a few adjustments would help:

The paragraph on adoption's broader social value (mothers avoiding abortion, infertile couples finding children) feels like a tangent. Either cut it or explicitly tie it back to your thesis—perhaps by arguing that the court's ruling threatens this larger social good.
The claim that "more people are turning their backs on adoption" is a significant generalization that needs a source. Do you have data, surveys, or expert commentary to support this, or is it speculative? If speculative, soften the language.
Your conclusion largely restates points already made rather than synthesizing them into a final, forceful statement. Consider ending with a clear articulation of what you think *should* have happened, or what stakes remain for future cases like this one.

Style and Mechanics

A few line-level issues to address in revision:

"whom are referred to as Jane and John Doe" should be "who are referred to..."
"ripping him away form the only family" — typo: "from"
"these laws will no longer very from state to state" — typo: "vary"
The sentence beginning "when Daniela Janikova gave birth" should be capitalized (it follows a period).
Watch your hedging language ("highly likely," "may very likely," "usually")—in psychological/legal argumentation, vague probability markers can weaken your credibility if overused. Where you can find actual support (from Erikson or other developmental psychologists), replace hedges with cited claims.

Summary

You have a promising argument with real emotional and intellectual stakes. Your next draft should focus on: (1) sharpening and front-loading your thesis, (2) replacing placeholders with fully integrated, properly cited evidence, (3) cutting or connecting tangential material, and (4) tightening prose at the sentence level. With these revisions, this essay could be quite persuasive.

Feedback Report on Euthanasia Essay

Overall Impression
You've tackled a genuinely difficult ethical issue and organized your paper around three clear claims. However, the essay needs significant revision in argument structure, evidence integration, and logical reasoning before it will be persuasive to an academic audience.

Thesis and Structure
Your introduction promises three distinct arguments: (1) society isn't "ready" for euthanasia, (2) it's too difficult to determine when suffering justifies death, and (3) the law is too vague. This is a reasonable roadmap, but your body paragraphs don't fully deliver on it. Paragraphs 1 and 2 (the monkey protest and the abortion comparison) both essentially argue that America isn't "ready"—they don't address your second promised idea about the difficulty of measuring suffering. That argument disappears after the introduction. Either revise your thesis to reflect the two arguments you actually develop (social unreadiness and legal vagueness), or add a dedicated paragraph on the suffering-measurement problem, since it's actually one of your more interesting philosophical points (the coma patient question, for instance, deserves its own developed paragraph).

Logical Reasoning
Several of your key inferences need more support:

The "not ready" argument conflates protest with majority opinion. The existence of vocal opponents (whether animal-rights activists or pro-life protesters) doesn't prove the *majority* of Americans reject euthanasia. Polling data on public opinion would strengthen this claim far more than anecdotes about protests.
The animal-to-human analogy is shaky. Many people who support euthanizing suffering animals actually *oppose* human euthanasia for different reasons (autonomy, dignity, sanctity of human life)—the ethical categories aren't equivalent, so this comparison needs more justification, not just assertion.
The abortion analogy conflates correlation with causation. You argue that because abortion and euthanasia raise "similar ethical questions," euthanasia will provoke similar violence. This is a slippery-slope argument that needs stronger warranting—why would controversy over one necessarily translate to comparable violence over the other? Consider: what specific mechanism connects the two? Right now the logic reads as "X happened with Y, and Y is somewhat like Z, so X may happen with Z," which is not fully convincing on its own.
The monkey example is confusing and undersourced. Without a citation, this anecdote reads as unverifiable, and it's unclear how three monkeys dying "of their sickness" while protesters waited outside actually demonstrates societal unreadiness for *human* euthanasia specifically. Clarify the source and tighten the connection to your claim.

Source Integration
This is the most urgent issue: your paper contains several  placeholders where quotations should appear. As it stands, readers cannot evaluate whether McKown's or Americans Against Human Suffering's statements actually support your claims. Fill these in fully, and make sure each quotation is followed by your own analysis explaining *how* it supports your point—don't let quotes speak for themselves.

Clarity and Precision
Define your terms early. "Euthanasia" covers voluntary/involuntary and active/passive forms; your argument would be sharper if you specified which form(s) you're critiquing, since the ethical and legal issues differ significantly between them.
The phrase "God-given life" introduces a religious premise that not all readers will accept. Since your paper otherwise argues from ethics and law, consider removing or reframing this to keep your reasoning grounded in secular reasoning accessible to all audiences.
Some claims need updated verification—double-check the 1988 "ruling on euthanasia" date, as landmark euthanasia legislation (eg, Oregon's Death with Dignity Act) occurred in 1994, and lack of precision here could undermine your credibility.

Style and Mechanics
The essay would benefit from careful proofreading:
"there own lives" → "their own lives"
"a live is worth living" → "a life is worth living"
"American" (singular) → "Americans" in the second sentence
Watch subject-verb agreement ("groups... continued their protest" is fine, but check similar constructions throughout)
Several sentences run long with multiple clauses; consider breaking these up for clarity.

Conclusion
Your closing rhetorical question ("Is it worth losing lives to violent protests just to follow a few others to lose their lives to euthanasia?") is emotionally charged but presents a false dichotomy. Consider ending instead with a clear restatement of your strongest evidence-based claim, perhaps calling for specific legal reforms (family consultation, competency evaluation) that you mention only briefly in the body.

Priority Revisions
Fill in all missing quotations with full citations.
Realign body paragraphs with your stated thesis (or revise the thesis).
Strengthen the logical connections in your analogies.
Define what type(s) of euthanasia you're discussing.
Proofread for grammar and word choice errors.

Feedback Report on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and timely topic, and your opening anecdote about Amy is an effective hook that personalizes an abstract legal debate. Your central claim—that KQED's argument for televising executions is weaker than the opposition's because it relies on value-based reasoning rather than concrete evidence of consequences—shows real analytical sophistication. This is a promising foundation. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: clarifying your thesis, integrating evidence properly, tightening organization, and correcting mechanical errors.

Missing Evidence

The most urgent issue is the placeholder brackets  scattered throughout the essay. These indicate that direct quotations were never inserted. Right now, a reader cannot evaluate your argument because the evidence you're building it on is literally absent. Before addressing anything else, you must:
Insert the actual quotations from KQED, Sherman, Smolowe, and Clark.
Follow each quotation with your own analysis explaining *why* it supports your point.
Introduce speakers properly on first mention (eg, "Jonathan Sherman, author of...") and use consistent capitalization for names (you write "pat Clark" instead of "Pat Clark").

Without this evidence, your argument—however sound in outline—remains an assertion rather than a demonstration.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Central Distinction

Your key conceptual move—distinguishing between arguments of "value" (is this right or wrong?) and arguments of "consequence" (what will happen as a result?)—is a strong analytical framework, likely drawn from stasis theory. But you introduce this distinction somewhat abruptly in paragraph two without fully explaining it to readers who may not know this framework. Consider:
Defining "value" versus "consequence" arguments early in your introduction, so readers understand the lens you're using to evaluate the debate.
Returning to this framework explicitly at the end of each body paragraph to reinforce how the evidence you've just presented fits into it.

Your thesis statement itself is currently buried in dense, run-on syntax. Consider isolating it into a clear, single sentence: something like, "Because KQED's argument rests on an abstract claim of value rather than demonstrated consequences, it is less persuasive than the opposition's evidence-based argument against televised executions."

Organization and Paragraph Focus

Paragraphs 4 and 5 feel repetitive and underdeveloped—each is short, introduces a new voice (Clark, Sherman, Smolowe) without context, and doesn't build toward a clear point. Consider:
Combining these into one paragraph that addresses the "public benefit" counterargument (that televised executions might educate voters) and then rebuts it with the opposition's evidence.
Making sure each paragraph has one clear controlling idea, introduced by a topic sentence, rather than shifting between multiple sources with minimal transition.

Additionally, the essay would benefit from a paragraph explicitly addressing the counterargument in more depth—right now you gesture at the "wake America up" argument but move past it quickly. Engaging seriously with the strongest opposing point (even briefly) will make your own argument more credible.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are several errors that need correcting in revision:
Typos/word errors: "routing" should be "routine"; "be drooms" should be "bedrooms"; "effect" should be "affect" ("how televising this execution will affect their ratings").
Repetition: The phrase "is not a valid argument" appears twice in one sentence ("This is not a valid argument because only one aspect is being considered and is therefore not a valid argument"). Revise for variety and precision.
Pronoun/capitalization inconsistency: "their argument is more thoroughly defined" should begin with a capital "Their."
Awkward phrasing: Sentences like "Amy took a great deal of time to try and express what she had seen" could be tightened ("Amy struggled to describe what she had seen").

A careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud, will catch many of these issues.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively returns to the Amy anecdote, which creates a nice circular structure. However, it currently just restates your thesis rather than synthesizing your argument's implications. Consider adding a sentence on why this distinction (value vs. consequence) matters beyond this single case—for instance, what it suggests about how media organizations should argue for access rights in future cases involving sensitive content.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Insert and analyze all missing quotations.
Clarify and foreground your value/consequence framework early.
Consolidate and refocus paragraphs 4–5.
Proofread carefully for typos and repetitive phrasing.
Strengthen the conclusion's broader implications.

With these revisions, this essay has strong potential to make a clear, well-supported argument. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and important topic, and it's clear you've done research to support your position. You have a discernible structure—moving from establishing fetal life, to fetal separateness, to medical ethics—which shows organizational thinking. However, the essay currently reads more as advocacy than academic argument, and there are several structural, evidentiary, and logical issues that need attention before this meets college-level standards.

Missing/Incomplete Citations

This is the most urgent issue. Throughout the draft, you have placeholder brackets () where quotations, statistics, and source information should be. As it stands, I cannot evaluate your use of evidence, and neither could any reader. Every specific claim—especially quantitative ones like "200,000 women" and "ninety-eight percent"—needs a properly cited source. Academic writing lives or dies by the credibility and traceability of its evidence; right now, the essay's entire argument rests on citations that don't yet exist on the page. Prioritize filling these in with full, accurate attributions (author, credentials, publication) before revising anything else.

Thesis and Framing

Your thesis—"abortion cannot be justified as a moral act... because it is the senseless killing of an innocent human life"—is stated clearly, which is good. However, the opening paragraph undermines your credibility before you even reach that thesis. Phrases like "sadly enough," "endure," and dismissing pro-choice reasoning as a "simple" solution to a "complex problem" signal bias rather than argument. Academic essays can absolutely take a position, but they should do so through evidence and reasoning, not through loaded language that presupposes the conclusion. Consider revising your introduction to present both positions more neutrally before asserting your own.

Logical Structure and Fallacies

Several sections rely on assumptions that need to be argued, not asserted:

Begging the question: You state the goal is to prove the fetus is "human and living," but biological development (heartbeat, brain activity, etc) doesn't by itself resolve the *moral* question of personhood or rights—which is the actual crux of the abortion debate. You move from "it is biologically alive" to "therefore killing it is murder" too quickly. Pro-choice advocates generally don't deny fetal life; they contest what moral and legal status that life should have relative to the woman's autonomy. Engaging with that distinction will make your argument far stronger and fairer.

Straw man risk: Your characterization of the pro-choice position as claiming a woman can do "what she wants with her body when she wants to do it" is reductive. Representing the opposing view in its strongest form (before you argue against it) is a hallmark of persuasive academic writing—it shows you've engaged seriously with the counterargument rather than dismissing it.

Appeal to authority: You cite several experts (Callahan, Grisez, Liley), but simply naming their credentials isn't the same as explaining *why* their specific claims are logically sound. For each quotation, add a sentence unpacking the reasoning behind it, not just stating that a "reputable" person said it.

The Hippocratic Oath Section

This section is interesting but needs more nuance. Historically, the Hippocratic Oath's stance on abortion has been debated and revised across centuries and versions; modern oaths taken by physicians often omit this language entirely. Presenting the Oath as a settled, static text weakens your credibility if a reader knows this history. Briefly acknowledging this complexity would make your argument more sophisticated rather than more vulnerable.

Tone and Word Choice

Words like "senseless killing," "innocent child," and "turn their backs" are emotionally charged and more suited to persuasive rhetoric (eg, an op-ed) than to an academic argumentative essay, which should persuade primarily through logic and evidence. I'd encourage a revision pass focused solely on tone: replace emotionally loaded phrases with precise, analytical language. This doesn't mean abandoning your position—it means making the argument itself carry the persuasive weight.

Conclusion

The conclusion currently restates that the debate is intractable and ends with the aphorism "You can't legislate morality," which feels disconnected from your paper's actual argument (that abortion is immoral and, implicitly, should be restricted). This ending seems to concede the opposite of what you've spent the essay arguing. Reconsider whether you want to end on resignation ("we'll never resolve this") or reassert your thesis with a call to action or reflection that logically follows your argument.

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all missing citations with full source information.
Revise the introduction and word choices for a more neutral, analytical tone.
Strengthen the transition from "the fetus is alive" to "therefore abortion is immoral"—this logical gap is the heart of your essay and deserves the most development.
Represent the pro-choice position more fully and fairly before refuting it.
Rewrite the conclusion so it aligns with, rather than undercuts, your thesis.

You have the bones of a serious argument here—now it needs tightening, sourcing, and a more rigorous engagement with the opposing side.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You're tackling an ambitious and provocative topic—questioning whether modern medicine's focus on prolonging life at all costs is actually beneficial to society. This is a genuinely interesting philosophical question, and your willingness to challenge a widely-unquestioned assumption is commendable. However, the essay currently reads as a first draft: there are unfilled citation placeholders (), the argument shifts direction several times, and some key claims rest on shaky logical ground. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim doesn't crystallize until the final paragraph: that treating symptoms rather than causes is degrading the value of life and misallocating resources. This is a strong, debatable thesis—but it needs to appear explicitly in your introduction, and every subsequent section needs to visibly serve it.

Right now, the essay moves through several semi-connected topics: genetic disease (PKD), genetic counseling, antibiotic resistance, and viral mutation. Each is interesting, but they support different (sometimes contradictory) versions of your argument:

The PKD section suggests we're weakening the *gene pool* by keeping people alive.
The antibiotic section is about *individual noncompliance* causing resistance—a behavioral problem, not really about whether we should treat illness at all.
The genetic counseling section argues something more specific: that parents *choosing* to have a genetically at-risk child are being irresponsible.

These are three distinct arguments with different implications. Decide which one is your true focus, or explicitly organize the essay to show how they build toward one unified claim. As it stands, a reader can lose track of what you're actually arguing.

The Evolutionary Argument Needs More Care

Your use of "survival of the fittest" to argue that medicine is degrading the human gene pool is the most vulnerable part of your essay, and likely to draw strong pushback. A few problems:

Natural selection operates on reproductive success, not simply survival—people with PKD often live long enough to reproduce even without modern medicine, since it frequently manifests in adulthood. Your claim needs stronger data support.
This argument edges close to eugenic reasoning (suggesting some genetic traits should be "bred out" by allowing death), which is ethically fraught territory. If you want to make this argument, you need to explicitly grapple with the ethical implications rather than let the biological framing do the work for you. Readers will (fairly) ask: are you saying disabled or chronically ill people are less valuable? Address this directly rather than leaving it implicit.
You should also acknowledge the counterargument: medical treatment doesn't just prolong life passively—it often allows people to contribute meaningfully to society, care for families, and pursue happiness (which you invoke in your opening). Ignoring this weakens your credibility.

Tone and Word Choice

Some language undermines your academic ethos:

Calling a parent's choice "selfish" and genetic counselors "disgraceful" is editorializing rather than arguing. Show *why* through evidence and reasoning, and let the reader draw the conclusion.
The second-person "you" address (especially in the antibiotics section) is engaging but shifts your essay's register toward informal persuasion rather than academic analysis. Consider whether this fits your assignment's expectations.
Phrases like "cost an arm and a leg" are casual; aim for precision instead.

Evidence and Citation

The  placeholders indicate missing quotations/citations that are structurally important—readers can't evaluate your argument without them. When revising:

Fill in and properly cite all sources (Heimer's review, Bosk's quoted parent, statistics on PKD incidence, Hantavirus information).
Consider adding sources beyond a single book review to discuss genetic counseling ethics—bioethicists and disability studies scholars have written extensively on this exact tension, and engaging them would strengthen your credibility and show awareness of the debate you're entering.
The antibiotic resistance information is accurate but is common knowledge in this context; make sure you're using it to support your specific thesis rather than as a stand-alone digression.

Structural Suggestions for Revision

Introduction: Tighten this significantly. The philosophical musing on taxes/death is a nice hook but takes too long to reach your actual claim. State your thesis clearly by the end of the first paragraph.
Reorganize body paragraphs around one consistent line of argument (likely: resource allocation and the ethics of symptom-based treat3ment), using PKD, antibiotics, and viral mutation as *supporting examples* of the same principle rather than separate arguments.
Add a counterargument paragraph: Address the disability-rights or humanistic objection to your position directly. This will strengthen your ethos and show you've engaged seriously with the difficulty of your claim.
Conclusion: Rather than repeating your claims, synthesize what you've shown and clarify what policy or attitude shift you're actually recommending.

This essay has real intellectual ambition—now it needs sharper focus, stronger ethical framing, and complete evidentiary support to make its case persuasively.

Feedback on Your Essay: Assisted Suicide and Dr. Kevorkian

Thank you for tackling this challenging and ethically complex topic. You've identified a genuine controversy and organized your essay around a "strengths and weaknesses" framework for analyzing both sides. Below are observations to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Thesis and Purpose

Your introduction poses two thoughtful questions, but your thesis—that both sides have "strengths and weaknesses" that "help to adequately debate" the issue—is more descriptive than argumentative. As an argumentative essay, readers need to know *your* position, not just a summary of others'. Are you arguing that society should proceed cautiously? That better pain management should replace euthanasia? That some middle ground exists? Your conclusion gestures toward "finding a median," but this needs to be established as your thesis from the start and defended throughout, not introduced only in the final paragraph.

Structural Balance

Notice the imbalance: you devote three full paragraphs to opponents' arguments (suicide contagion, better alternatives, doctor-patient trust) but only one paragraph to supporters' side, which focuses mainly on Kevorkian's personal conviction rather than substantive arguments (eg, patient autonomy, quality of life, the right to die with dignity). This imbalance undermines your claim to be fairly weighing both sides. Consider adding a paragraph on autonomy-based arguments for assisted suicide—this is the strongest philosophical case supporters make, and its absence weakens your analysis.

The Missing Evidence

The placeholder  appears throughout in place of actual quotations. This is a critical gap I cannot ignore: without the real quotes, I cannot assess whether your analysis of the evidence is accurate or whether you're using sources effectively. When you revise, make sure every quotation is properly integrated, attributed, and followed by real analysis (not just "This is significant because..." repeated as a formula). Vary how you introduce and unpack quotations so the essay doesn't feel mechanical.

Logical Consistency

A few reasoning issues need attention:

The MLK comparison (paragraph 5) is a weak analogy. King was arrested for peaceful civil disobedience against unjust laws protecting racial discrimination; Kevorkian was arrested for ending patients' lives. Comparing the two implies a moral equivalence that many readers will reject outright, and it may alienate rather than persuade. Consider cutting this or significantly qualifying it.
The "no weaknesses" claim (paragraph on doctor-patient trust) contradicts your stated method of finding strengths *and* weaknesses in every argument. Even strong arguments have vulnerabilities—for instance, one could argue that honoring patient autonomy strengthens rather than undermines trust in doctors. Push yourself to find a genuine weakness here for consistency and credibility.
The suicide-contagion argument you critique as "a bit steep" (comparing it to fast food) is a good example of real critical engagement—do more of this kind of specific, evaluative pushback throughout, rather than simply cataloging strengths and weaknesses in general terms.

Style and Mechanics

The essay needs careful proofreading. Some specific issues:

Spelling/typos: "neglection" → *neglect*; "af raid" → *afraid*; "i doctors" → *in doctors*; "physicians stands" → *physician stands*
Sentence fragments: "While opponents believe that Kevorkian is trying to play God" is a fragment when preceded by a period—consider joining it to the previous sentence or restructuring.
Repetitive phrasing: "definite strength," "definite weakness," and "This is significant because" recur so often that they become mechanical. Vary your sentence openings and analytical vocabulary (eg, "a notable limitation," "this claim falters when," "a more persuasive aspect of this position is").
Capitalization: "when his case was first released" should begin with a capital letter.

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into a clear, defensible claim you will argue for, not just a description of the debate.
Balance your treatment of supporters' and opponents' arguments—add substantive detail to the pro-Kevorkian side, especially arguments about autonomy and dignity.
Insert and properly cite all quotations, ensuring they are analyzed rather than just dropped in.
Revisit the MLK analogy and the "no weaknesses" claim for logical consistency.
Proofread carefully for spelling, fragments, and repetitive phrasing.
Strengthen your conclusion so that it follows logically from a thesis stated in your introduction, rather than introducing a new idea (finding a "median") at the last minute.

You clearly have a solid grasp of the debate's key tensions—religious belief, patient autonomy, medical ethics, and slippery-slope concerns. With a clearer argumentative stance and more balanced, well-supported analysis, this essay can become a much more persuasive piece of writing.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an interesting rhetorical question—not whether corporal punishment is right or wrong, but whether its opponents *argue* effectively. This is a sophisticated angle for an argumentative essay, and your opening anecdote about Tommy is vivid and draws the reader in immediately. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of evidence, structure, clarity, and mechanics before the argument will be persuasive. Below are the key areas to address.

Missing Evidence

The most pressing issue is the placeholder brackets (<\*>) where quotations should appear. Currently, you assert that Jambor, Johns and MacNaughton, and Diamantes define or discuss corporal punishment inconsistently, but you never show us their actual words. Since your entire argument rests on the claim that these authors use "ambiguous terminology" and hold "wavering positions," the quotations are not optional—they *are* the evidence. Without them, the essay is a set of assertions rather than an argument. When you revise, insert the exact language from each source and then explain *specifically* how the definitions differ or how the authors contradict themselves within the same paragraph. Right now you tell the reader that Diamantes contradicts himself "in the same paragraph," but you don't quote the two statements so the reader can see the contradiction for themselves.

Clarify the Thesis and Scope

Your thesis—"The opponents' argument is ineffective because of ambiguous terminology and a wavering position"—is a strong, specific claim, which is good. But be careful about scope: you are making a sweeping generalization about *all* opponents of corporal punishment based on what appears to be three or four sources. Consider either (a) narrowing your claim to "the opponents I examined" or "much of the literature I reviewed," or (b) acknowledging that some opponents may argue more rigorously, which would make your critique of the *representative* weak arguments more credible and less like a straw-man attack. Right now, a skeptical reader could ask, "Have you surveyed the strongest opponents, or just the weakest ones?"

Strengthen the "Alternative" Section

Your paragraph proposing what opponents *should* do (agree on a definition, take a firm stand) is currently thin and general. This is actually the most constructive part of your essay and deserves more development. What would a strong, unified definition look like? Can you point to any author who *does* argue effectively, to use as a model of what "good" looks like? Even one contrasting example of effective argumentation would strengthen your credibility here.

Revisit the Anecdote and Direct-Address Questions

The Tommy narrative is effective as a hook, but the essay returns to rhetorical questions ("Were you a tommy?") several times, including in the conclusion. This device loses power through repetition. Consider using it once memorably at the opening and once at the close, rather than three or four times throughout, so it doesn't feel like a filler transition.

Historical/Statistical Support Needs Updating and Integration

The 1988 statistic about states outlawing corporal punishment is useful context, but it's dropped in abruptly near the end and is quite dated for any essay written after that year. If this is a historical paper, that's fine—but make clear why you're using data from that specific year. If not, update this with more current figures, since this data point could undermine your credibility if a reader assumes you haven't researched recent statistics.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are several errors to clean up:
"several of the authors fail to make any attempt" should be joined properly (currently a comma splice: "...do not apply the same definition, several of the authors fail...").
"took he seat" should be "took her seat" (assuming it's Tommy—check pronoun consistency, since "Thomas" is addressed as male but this may be a typo).
"unteeteringly" is not a standard word; consider "unwaveringly" or "without wavering."
"subjected the paddle" should be "subjected to the paddle."
"Are you from the old school?" and subsequent questions in the introduction are engaging, but proofread for consistent verb tense and pronoun agreement throughout.

Structure and Transitions

Your paragraphs are logically organized (definition problem → contradiction problem → consequences problem → path forward → stakes), which is a real strength. To make this even clearer, consider adding a brief transitional sentence at the start of each paragraph explicitly linking back to your thesis (eg, "A second failure of the opponents' argument is inconsistency regarding consequences...").

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Insert and analyze real quotations in place of every <\*>.
Either narrow or qualify the sweeping claim about "the opponents."
Expand your constructive alternative section with concrete examples.
Trim repetitive rhetorical questions.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice errors.

This essay has a genuinely interesting angle and a clear structure—your job now is to back up each claim with textual evidence and tighten the prose. Good luck with your revision!

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic, and you've clearly done research to support your position. You've incorporated multiple sources (studies, articles, surveys) and specific examples from television programming. However, the essay needs significant revision in several key areas before it fully achieves its argumentative goals. Below is feedback organized by priority.

Clarify Your Argumentative Stance and Voice

This is the most pressing issue. Throughout the essay, you write about what "advocates for censorship" or "proponents" claim, rather than making the argument yourself. Phrases like "the advocates for censorship show," "proponents prove," and "supporters realize" create distance between you and your thesis. This single change will strengthen the entire essay's persuasive force.

Present the strongest version of the opposing view (not just one talking point)

The essay contains numerous grammatical errors that affect readability and credibility:
Subject-verb agreement issues ("is censorship does not increase")
Misspellings ("deminish," "caniving," "bout," "suppose to")
Comma splices and run-on sentences throughout
Inconsistent verb tense (shifting between past and present)

Suggestion: After revising for content and argument, do a dedicated proofreading pass. Reading the essay aloud can help you catch awkward phrasing and grammatical errors.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion re-states claims already made rather than synthesizing them into a compelling final statement. Consider ending with a specific call to action or a vivid image that reinforces urgency—something that leaves the reader with a clear sense of stakes and next steps.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Take ownership of the argument—write as yourself, not as a reporter on others' claims.
Sharpen your thesis into a specific, arguable claim.
Deepen the counterargument section.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely complex and important topic, and you've clearly done research into the ethical debates surrounding genetic research. However, the essay in its current form has a significant structural issue that undermines its effectiveness as an argumentative piece. Below are my main observations and suggestions for revision.

Major Issue: This Is a Meta-Argument, Not an Argument

The most pressing problem is that your essay doesn't actually argue *against* genetic research—it argues about *how one should construct* an argument against genetic research. Notice how often you use conditional and instructional language: "One possible way of presenting the most effective argument...," "This would make for an effective argument...," "Citing his example...would be excellent for this argument." You are describing a hypothetical essay rather than writing one.

This creates distance between you and your claims. Readers need to know what *you* think, not what quotes *could* be used to persuade someone. Revise by removing this meta-commentary and committing directly to the positions. Instead of "This reference to the Nazis deals with Hitler's plan of genocide... This obvious atrocity would be effective in an argument," write something like: "The historical exploitation of eugenics by the Nazi regime demonstrates that humanity cannot be trusted with control over its own genetic inheritance." Own the argument.

Missing Quotations

There are numerous placeholders () throughout the essay where actual quotations should appear. Since your entire argument relies on these sources (Sinsheimer, Maddox, Annas, Aldhous), the essay cannot be evaluated in its current state, and more importantly, cannot function rhetorically without this evidence. Prioritize filling these in with precise, well-integrated quotations, and make sure each one is followed by your own analysis of its significance—not just a restatement of what it says.

Structural Suggestions

Your three-part structure (history, refutation, consequences) is a reasonable organizational plan, but it currently reads as a *plan* rather than an executed essay. Consider:

Introduction: Trim the historical overview of Mendel-to-genome-mapping; get to your thesis faster. Right now the thesis doesn't fully emerge until late in paragraph two.

Thesis clarity: State directly and early what your essay will argue—presumably that the risks of genetic research (eugenics history, loss of genetic diversity, privacy violations, commodification of children, inadequate oversight) outweigh its benefits, and that research should proceed cautiously or not at all.

Refutation section: This is your strongest material conceptually (sickle-cell/malaria tradeoff, diversity loss), but it needs your own voice asserting these as your conclusions, not just as strategies for a hypothetical writer.

Consequences section: Similarly strong in content (privacy, "designer children," lack of international regulation via the Aldhous example) but needs the same conversion from third-person strategy to first-person argument.

Logical Development

A few claims need more support or nuancing:
The Nazi eugenics comparison is powerful but risks being a slippery-slope fallacy if not carefully qualified. How do modern genetic research safeguards differ from 1930s Germany? Address this directly rather than assuming the analogy is self-evidently strong.
The sickle-cell/malaria example is excellent—keep this, but make sure you draw out the broader principle (that gene elimination is not cost-free) more explicitly.
The discrimination argument (AIDS analogy) is a good start, but could be strengthened with a clearer causal chain: why would genetic information transfer to insurers/employers, and what specific mechanisms of harm follow?

Style and Mechanics

"Mendal" should be "Mendel."
"doing three doings" is awkward phrasing—revise to something like "the argument should proceed in three parts."
"One consequences deals with privacy" has a subject-verb agreement error.
Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences, particularly in paragraphs discussing Maddox and Sinsheimer.
The final paragraph's "It is vital that the argument be strong because " needs to be resolved—this is presumably meant to be a significant closing point, so don't leave it as a placeholder.

Overall Recommendation

Your research and organizational instincts are sound. The core task now is converting your outline of a persuasive strategy into the persuasion itself. Read through your draft and change every instance of "this would be effective for an argument" into an actual argumentative claim. Once you commit to your position directly and integrate your quotations, this essay has strong potential.

Overall Impression

As written, it appears in your introduction and resurfaces occasionally, but you never fully argue *why* selective emphasis constitutes exploitation rather than simply appropriate audience awareness. Right now the essay wavers between them, which weakens your argumentative focus.

The "audience" paragraph, in particular, feels like a preview of arguments you make more fully later; it could be cut or condensed into a transitional sentence.

Additionally, the physical context paragraph makes good points about charts and visuals but ends abruptly with "we will look at that," which is a weak transition. Revise transitions between paragraphs so each builds on the previous point rather than simply moving to a new subtopic.

Your conclusion largely restates the introduction rather than synthesizing your findings or explaining their significance. Consider ending with a reflection on what this comparison reveals about media literacy or how readers should critically evaluate science reporting in popular press.

Evidence and Support

This is the area needing the most attention. Several placeholders () appear where you clearly intended to insert direct quotations—these must be filled in with actual language from the articles before submission. Direct quotes are essential for a rhetorical analysis like this one; right now, your claims about "emotional" or "shocking" language are asserted but not demonstrated.

You also need full bibliographic information for both articles (authors, titles, publication dates) either in-text or in a works-cited list, both for credibility and to allow readers to verify your claims. Currently, you refer to "the JAMA article" and "the Newsweek article" without ever naming them precisely.

When you do use statistics (eg, "56% or men and 34% of women"), double-check accuracy and clean up transcription errors (e56% "or" men should be "of men"). Precision matters enormously when your argument centers on how data gets used.

Style and Mechanics

A few patterns to address in revision:

Grammar/agreement errors: "todays," "writers assumptions" need apostrophes (today's, writer's/writers'). "Any give time" should be "any given time." "It uses its physical context... to hook it audience" needs "its."
Word choice: "have an affect" should be "have an effect."
Pronoun consistency: You shift between third person analysis and first-person plural ("we will look at that," "as we have seen"). Choose a consistent voice—academic writing typically favors third person or careful first-person singular, avoiding the conversational "we."
Wordiness: Sentences like "This kind of thoroughness is expected by the audience, and the writers know that" could be tightened. Look for redundant phrasing throughout (eg, "the writers for the journal give the readers a complete report... They include statistics on all sides" repeats the same idea twice).
Topic sentences: Some paragraphs open with general statements ("Audience is one of the most important factors...") rather than sentences that clearly connect to your thesis. Sharpen these to guide readers more explicitly.

Suggestions for Revision

Clarify whether your thesis is purely analytical or also evaluative/critical of Newsweek.
Eliminate redundancy between the audience and social-context paragraphs.
Insert all missing quotations and citations.
Proofread carefully for grammar, apostrophes, and word choice.
Strengthen the conclusion to explain the broader significance of your analysis (eg, what should readers/consumers of media learn from this comparison?).

You have the analytical instincts for strong rhetorical analysis—now it's a matter of substantiating your claims with evidence and tightening the prose.

Feedback on Draft Essay: Abstinence-Only vs. Comprehensive Sex Education

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely, debatable topic and have clearly done research into both sides of this controversy. However, the essay in its current form has several structural and rhetorical issues that need attention before it can function as an effective argumentative essay.

Missing Source Material

The most immediate problem is the number of bracketed placeholders () throughout the draft. These appear to mark spots where you intended to insert direct quotations or specific data but never returned to fill them in. As it stands, the essay cannot be evaluated on its evidentiary merits because so much of the actual evidence is missing. Before revising anything else, go back to your sources and insert the specific quotes, statistics, and claims these placeholders represent. Readers cannot assess your reasoning about "misleading questions," "catchy phrases," or "illogical reasoning" without seeing what was actually said.

Clarify Your Thesis and Stance

Your introduction frames this as an even-handed look at "the controversy," but by the second paragraph, the essay reads almost entirely as an argument *against* abstinence-only education. This is fine—an argumentative essay should take a position—but you need to be honest with your reader about that position from the start. Right now, the essay seems caught between two modes: (1) a report *describing* what each side claims, and (2) an argument *siding* with comprehensive sex education. Choose one. If your true purpose is to argue that abstinence-only education relies on flawed reasoning and misinformation, say so explicitly in your thesis, and then structure every paragraph to support that claim rather than to "summarize" both positions.

Attribute Claims Fairly and Critically

You frequently write phrases like "opponents argue," "opponents propose," and "opponents feel" without ever independently evaluating whether these counter-arguments are sound. Since your paper leans heavily toward the comprehensive sex education side, you are essentially channeling that side's talking points through the mouths of "opponents" without scrutiny. To strengthen your credibility, you should:
Explicitly analyze *why* a given argument is fallacious rather than simply asserting that it is.
Name the specific logical fallacy when you invoke one (eg, false dilemma, hasty generalization, appeal to fear) rather than using vague language like "facile reasoning" or "illogical reasoning."
Consider briefly acknowledging a strong point from the abstinence-only side, even if you ultimately reject it. This will make your critique of their weaker arguments more persuasive, since readers will see you are engaging fairly rather than cherry-picking.

Strengthen Paragraph Organization

Each paragraph currently mixes multiple claims, studies, and rebuttals without a clear topic sentence to orient the reader. For instance, paragraph three jumps between the Dawson study, the Project Respect study, and the San Marcos High School controversy without transitions explaining how these examples build a cumulative case. Consider giving each paragraph one clear controlling idea (eg, "Abstinence-only studies often lack independent verification") and then use your evidence to support only that idea before moving to the next.

Revise for Sentence-Level Clarity

There are numerous grammatical and syntactical issues that obscure your meaning:
Subject-verb agreement errors ("proponents have used," but later "does not meet")
Misused verb forms ("not being informed," "opponents have beared")
Sentence fragments and run-ons, especially in the second paragraph ("since, why should teens even listen...")
Inconsistent verb tense (mixing past and present when describing studies and claims)

I'd recommend reading the essay aloud slowly, sentence by sentence, to catch these issues. A peer reviewer or writing center consultation would also help significantly here.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion raises a new question ("Should we teach prevention methods in addition to abstinence?") rather than reinforcing the argument you've built. Since your essay has effectively been arguing that abstinence-only programs rely on faulty logic and misinformation, your conclusion should circle back to that claim with a clear, confident restatement—not introduce a fresh, unresolved question. Consider ending with a stronger claim about what responsible sex education *should* look like, based on the evidence you've presented.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in all missing quotations and data.
Clarify your thesis and take a consistent stance.
Add critical analysis, not just attribution, when evaluating opposing claims.
Reorganize paragraphs around single, clear ideas.
Proofread carefully for grammar and syntax.
Rewrite the conclusion to reinforce your argument rather than raise new questions.

With these revisions, your essay will move from summarizing a debate to making a compelling, well-supported argument of your own.

Feedback on "Biological vs. Adoptive Parents" Essay

Overall Impression

You've taken on a genuinely difficult rhetorical analysis task: evaluating *how* each side argues (through values, consequences, and appeals to public opinion) rather than simply summarizing *what* each side believes. This is a sophisticated approach, and your instinct to hold both sides accountable to the same analytical standard is a real strength. The essay shows real critical thinking—particularly in your discussion of hypothetical consequences and your recognition that extreme cases create a "false dilemma." However, the essay currently has some structural and evidentiary issues that are undermining your argument's persuasiveness.

Major Issues to Address

Missing Evidence (Critical Issue)
The placeholder brackets () appear multiple times where quotations, statistics, or specific source material should be. This is the single most damaging issue in the draft. Your analytical claims—about media portrayal, the National Council for Adoption statistic, the Newsweek article title—depend entirely on the reader trusting your characterization of evidence that isn't actually shown. Before revising anything else, fill these in with actual quotations and cite your sources properly (author, publication, date). Your analysis is only as strong as the evidence it rests on.

Clarify Your Analytical Framework Early
You're evaluating arguments based on three criteria: *values*, *consequences*, and *attempts to shape public opinion*. This is a smart organizing principle, but you introduce it in the thesis without explaining what you mean by each term. A reader unfamiliar with rhetorical analysis won't know what counts as a "value" versus a "consequence" appeal. Add a sentence or two early in the essay (perhaps in the introduction) defining these three categories, so readers know what lens to apply as they move through your analysis.

Sharpen the Thesis and Opening
Your introduction opens with a personal, somewhat sentimental framing ("Blood is thicker than water," rhetorical questions about grief) that doesn't match the analytical, evaluative tone of the rest of the essay. Consider opening more directly with the rhetorical situation: what is the public debate, and what analytical claim will you make about *how* each side argues? Save the emotional resonance for your conclusion, where it will feel earned rather than like throat-clearing.

Reduce Repetitive Sentence Patterns
Many paragraphs follow an almost identical template: "This is the reasoning behind [X]... This is a good/weak argument because... They should have done Y instead." While the analysis itself is often sound, the repeated phrasing makes the essay feel mechanical by the midpoint. Try varying your sentence openings and combining some of these evaluative moves into single, more complex sentences. For example, instead of "This is somewhat strong. Yet, to make an even stronger argument, they should..." try: "Although the abandonment case exposes real inconsistency in the courts, the argument would gain force if paired with additional examples and a clearer account of the decision's likely harms."

Watch Vague Pronoun Reference
Phrases like "This is..." and "This point..." appear frequently without a clear antecedent, forcing readers to reconstruct what "this" refers to. Replace vague pronouns with specific nouns ("this abandonment case," "this psychological claim") to keep your analysis precise.

Conclusion Introduces New Material
Your final paragraph raises a compelling idea—that the whole conflict might have been avoided by "one simple signature" or "one more question"—but this point appears nowhere earlier in the essay. A conclusion should synthesize what's already been argued, not introduce a new claim. Either develop this idea in the body (perhaps in your discussion of the Baby Jessica case, where the incomplete paperwork is central) or cut it from the conclusion.

Smaller Issues

Some sentence fragments and comma splices ("This is a somewhat strong argument for the opponents of biological parents. Even though they use a rare case as an example, it shows the inconsistency…") could be tightened.
Terms like "proponents of biological parents" and "opponents to biological parents" are used somewhat interchangeably in places, which is confusing—make sure you're consistent about which term refers to which side.
The Baby Richard/Jessica case details are assumed to be familiar to the reader; a brief contextual footnote or sentence early on would help readers unfamiliar with these cases follow your analysis.

Strengths to Preserve

Your closing insight about oversimplification and the "false dilemma" created by media coverage is the strongest analytical move in the essay—it ties together your critiques of both sides elegantly. Similarly, your point about hypothetical consequences weakening the adoptive-parent side's argument shows genuine rhetorical sophistication. Build the rest of the essay's precision and evidence up to match the quality of these insights.

Next step: Fill in all missing citations, add a brief definitional passage for your three analytical categories, and revise the introduction/conclusion to bookend the essay more tightly around your central claim.

Feedback on "Cheating in College" Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely interesting question—which anti-cheating strategy works best—and you clearly did real research (library sources, examples like UVA and the XF grade). However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of sources than an argument you're driving. Below are the main areas to strengthen in revision.

Thesis and Argument Consistency

Your introduction claims that honor codes *and* revamped academic codes are "the two strongest methods," but by the conclusion you argue honor codes are clearly superior and the academic code's flaws are "too strong." This is a good position to take, but you need to commit to it from the start. Revise your thesis so it previews the actual argument: that honor codes, despite some weaknesses, outperform other proposed solutions. Right now the thesis is vague ("their effectiveness can be increased by improving the faults")—this sounds like a different, weaker claim than the one you actually defend in your conclusion. Make the thesis and the final position match.

Missing/Unclear Evidence

There are several places marked with  where evidence or explanation seems to be missing entirely. This is a serious problem: claims like "Mr. Levine of Harvard believes that..." trail off without content, and "the claim is weakened" appears with no explanation of *why*. Before submitting a final draft, go through every one of these gaps and either supply the missing quotation/paraphrase with a citation, or delete the sentence if it doesn't hold up. An argumentative essay cannot have unsupported assertions floating without their justification.

Evaluate, Don't Just Summarize

Much of the essay describes what sources say (UVA's application essay, the XF grade, Hiley Ward's suggestion) without analyzing *why* this evidence supports your thesis. For example, you note that the XF grade reduced cheating by 50% at several schools—this is a strong statistic in favor of revamped codes. But then you dismiss the method because of a loophole (ethics classes removing the X), without weighing whether a 50% reduction still outweighs that flaw. Since your conclusion argues honor codes are superior, you need to make this comparison explicit: directly weigh the loophole in academic codes against the peer-pressure weakness you identify in honor codes, and explain why one flaw is more damaging than the other.

Personal Anecdote vs. Evidence

Your personal experience ("I have seen others cheat and get a better grade," "If we had an honor code at Marquette...") is valuable for establishing your stake in the topic, but it currently functions as a substitute for argument rather than a support for it. Consider trimming these moments and using them strategically—perhaps one clear anecdote early to establish credibility/motivation, and then let researched evidence carry the analytical weight.

Organization and Paragraphing

The essay's structure (honor codes → academic codes → faculty involvement → judiciary/other failed methods → conclusion) is logical, but paragraphs would benefit from clearer topic sentences that state the point *before* the evidence. Right now, paragraphs often open with a general statement, then list examples, then pivot to a weakness, then to your opinion—all without clear signposting. Add transitional sentences that tell the reader why you're introducing a weakness ("However, this success is undermined by...") rather than shifting abruptly (eg, "However, when taken in account the effects of peer pressure...").

The section on judiciary/corporal punishment feels underdeveloped compared to the rest—it's introduced late and treated briefly. Since it's a "failed" solution, consider either expanding it with an example (what case, what court, what outcome) or integrating it earlier as a contrast to honor codes and academic codes.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively restates your final position, but it repeats earlier points nearly verbatim ("show how cheating is becoming out of control," "show ways to curb this problem") rather than synthesizing them into a final, forward-looking statement. Consider ending with a recommendation: what should universities like Marquette actually do, based on your argument? This gives the essay a stronger sense of purpose.

Style and Mechanics

Watch subject-verb agreement: "the range of punishments are focused" should be "is focused."
"ensure and honest academic environment" should read "ensure an honest academic environment."
Avoid vague attributions like "the authors" and "Mr. Levine of Harvard" without full names/titles and proper citation format.
Some sentences are choppy; combine short, related sentences for better flow (eg, the UVA description could be tightened into two sentences instead of four).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in all missing evidence ( placeholders).
Sharpen thesis to match your actual conclusion.
Add explicit comparative analysis between honor codes and academic codes.
Reduce reliance on anecdote; increase analytical use of evidence.
Strengthen topic sentences and transitions.
Proofread for grammar and citation consistency.

You have solid research and a clear personal investment in this topic—now the task is to make your argument, not just your sources, the star of the essay.

Feedback on Draft Essay: Fourth Amendment Interpretation in the O.J. Simpson Case

Overall Impression

You've chosen a rich, high-profile case to explore a genuinely difficult constitutional question, and your instinct to examine *how* each side argues (not just *what* they argue) shows sophisticated thinking. However, the essay currently sits uneasily between two different genres—a rhetorical analysis and an argumentative essay—and this tension is the most important issue to resolve in revision.

Major Issue: Clarify Your Purpose

Your opening question ("How should the Fourth Amendment be interpreted?") promises an argumentative essay in which you will take a position. But the body of your essay is actually a rhetorical analysis of how the prosecution and defense construct persuasive narratives in news coverage of the case. These are legitimate but distinct projects. As written, the essay never answers its own opening question, and the conclusion pivots to a policy recommendation (police should get warrants) that isn't really supported by the analysis that precedes it.

Revision options:
If you want to write a rhetorical analysis, revise your introduction to signal this clearly (eg, "This essay examines how competing media narratives use ethos, pathos, and appeals to authority to shape public opinion on the admissibility question") and drop the implication that you'll resolve the constitutional debate.
If you want to argue a position on the Fourth Amendment question, you need a clear thesis stating your stance, and the body paragraphs should build a case rather than simply summarizing both sides "objectively."

Right now the essay tries to do both and fully accomplishes neither.

Structural and Evidentiary Issues

Missing quotations: Several key moments meant to anchor your analysis are marked with  placeholders rather than actual quotations (eg, Fuhrman's testimony, Judge Ito's comment). These are essential—your argument about emotional appeal and credibility depends on the *actual language* used. Fill these in and analyze the specific word choices, not just the fact that a quote exists.

Citation practice: You mention article titles and authors in-text, but you should adopt a consistent citation style (MLA, given the subject matter) with a Works Cited page. This also invites you to specify publication dates, which matters since the case unfolded over months and your evidence should be chronologically clear.

Terminology: "Proposition" and "opposition" are debate-team terms that may confuse readers unfamiliar with that convention. Since you're really discussing the prosecution's argument for admissibility versus the defense's argument against it, just use those terms, or clearly define your usage in the introduction.

Logical and Factual Concerns

"Exigent circumstances" is not constitutional text. The Fourth Amendment doesn't contain this phrase; it's a judicially created exception developed through case law. Your first sentence should be corrected to avoid misleading readers about what the Amendment actually says.
Factual accuracy: "Ronald Goldwin" should be "Ronald Goldman," and "Los Angelas" should be "Los Angeles" throughout—these need careful proofreading.
The ethical framing is underdeveloped. You introduce the essay by saying each side has "different ethical reasons," but you never clearly define what ethical framework is at stake (eg, consequentialism—guilty should be punished regardless of means—versus a rights-based view—process matters more than outcome). Naming these frameworks explicitly would sharpen your analysis considerably and give the essay real analytical teeth.
Your conclusion's policy suggestion (obtain warrants in advance) is reasonable but appears without groundwork. If you want to end on this note, you should build toward it earlier, perhaps by evaluating which side's argument is more legally/ethically sound and why the warrant requirement exists in the first place.

Style Notes

Several sentences use hedging or informal phrasing ("scratching to find anything," "backed into a corner") that undercuts the analytical tone. Consider a more measured, academic register throughout.
Paragraphs are generally well-organized around a single point, but topic sentences could more explicitly state the *analytical move* you're making (eg, "The prosecution's use of authority appeals is especially evident in..." rather than simply narrating events).
The essay would benefit from explicit naming of rhetorical strategies (ethos, pathos, logos) if you're keeping the analytical approach—this gives your reader a framework to follow your observations.

Suggested Next Steps

Decide: rhetorical analysis or argumentative essay on constitutional interpretation?
Rewrite the introduction with a clear, specific thesis matching that choice.
Fill in all missing quotations and cite sources properly.
Correct factual errors (names, the "exigent circumstances" claim).
Either eliminate the "ethical" framing or develop it with named philosophical frameworks.
Revise the conclusion so it follows logically from your thesis and evidence, rather than introducing a new, unsupported claim.

This essay has real potential—your observations about selective quotation and character attacks are sharp. Tightening the framework will let those insights shine.

Feedback on Your Essay on Suicide and the Arguments Against It

Overall Impression

You've chosen an ambitious and difficult topic, and your central analytical move—examining *how* opponents of suicide argue rather than simply agreeing or disagreeing with them—is a sophisticated approach. Focusing on the *quality of evidence* rather than just the conclusions is exactly the kind of critical thinking college-level argumentation calls for. However, the essay needs work in several areas: thesis clarity, quotation integration, logical consistency, and variety in how you develop each point.

Thesis and Framing

Your actual argument—that opponents of suicide raise legitimate concerns but fail to support them with evidence—doesn't emerge clearly until partway into the introduction, and even then it's stated somewhat awkwardly ("The opponents of suicide often neglect to prove their statements"). Consider stating this thesis explicitly and early: something like, "While arguments against suicide raise valid concerns, they largely fail because they rely on assertion rather than evidence." This would give readers a clear lens through which to read everything that follows.

Also, reconsider your opening sentences. "Suicide means the end of all problems (Though those problems are usually solved easily.)" is confusing and undercuts your credibility right away—it's unclear whether you're being ironic, dismissive, or literal. Cut or revise this.

The Missing Quotations

This is the most serious technical problem in the draft: every direct quotation is replaced with . Without the actual words of Rauscher, Forbes, Ingram and Ellis, Parks and Weiss, and Hurley, readers cannot evaluate your claims about what these authors argue or judge whether your paraphrases are fair. This needs to be fixed before anything else—your entire essay depends on textual evidence that isn't there. When you insert the quotations, make sure to:
Introduce each with enough context (who is this author, what is their expertise?)
Follow with your own analysis of *why* the quotation supports your reading
Avoid quoting and then simply restating what the quote said in different words (see below)

Repetitive Argumentative Structure

Each paragraph follows nearly the same pattern: (1) introduce an author's claim, (2) explain what the author "wants to convey" or "intends," (3) conclude that the argument lacks evidence. This becomes predictable by the third paragraph and mechanical by the fifth. Vary your sentence structures and analytical moves. For example, sometimes you might challenge the *logic* of an argument rather than only its evidentiary support (you do this well with the slippery slope discussion of the AIDS paragraph—more of that kind of specific logical critique would strengthen the essay).

Also, be careful with phrases like "Mr. Rauscher hopes to convey," "Forbes intends this point to establish," and "Ingram and Ellis want to show"—you use variations of this construction so often that it starts to feel like padding. Trust your readers to infer intent once you've quoted and briefly glossed the passage.

Logical Tension in Your Central Claim

There's a puzzling tension in your argument: you say these claims "can be rationalized" with "some thought," implying they're logically sound, but then argue they fail because they lack evidence. These are two different kinds of failure—one is about *logical validity*, the other about *empirical support*. Clarify which failure you're diagnosing in each case. For instance, the slippery slope fallacy is a logical problem, not an evidentiary one; conflating the two weakens your overall claim. Consider explicitly distinguishing "arguments that are logically sound but empirically unsupported" from "arguments that are logically fallacious regardless of evidence."

The AIDS Paragraph

This section introduces genuinely interesting material (rational suicide, assisted suicide, slippery slope reasoning) but feels underdeveloped and disconnected from the rest of the essay. The paragraph about HIV/AIDS transmission routes, in particular, doesn't connect clearly to your argument about evidence and religion—it reads as background information without analytical purpose. Either cut it or explicitly tie it back to your thesis (eg, "because religious opponents rarely address suffering like that caused by AIDS, their arguments about redemptive suffering seem incomplete").

Strengthen the Ending

Your conclusion pivots toward a broader, more compassionate claim about understanding suicide rather than judging it. This is a compelling note to end on, but it appears rather suddenly. Consider foreshadowing this shift earlier in the essay—perhaps in your introduction—so the ending feels like a natural culmination rather than a new argument.

Revision Priorities

Insert and properly integrate all quotations.
Sharpen and foreground your thesis.
Distinguish "lack of evidence" critiques from "logical fallacy" critiques.
Vary your paragraph structure and analytical vocabulary.
Either integrate or cut the AIDS/HIV background material.

You have a genuinely interesting critical framework here. With stronger textual support and more precise reasoning, this essay could be quite compelling.
