Feedback Report: Adolescent Suicide Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and sensitive topic, and you've clearly done research to support your claims (the frequent citation markers indicate engagement with sources). You've also made an ambitious attempt to connect sociological theory—Durkheim's typology and Marxist conflict theory—to a real social problem. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: argumentative clarity, structural organization, sentence-level grammar, and depth of analysis. Below I address these systematically.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening paragraph buries the thesis in a string of statistics. What is the *specific claim* you're arguing? Is it that adolescent suicide is under-addressed compared to other youth problems? That theoretical frameworks explain *why* it happens? That certain theories are more useful than others? Right now the essay reads as descriptive (reporting facts and summarizing theories) rather than argumentative. 

Revision suggestion: State explicitly in your introduction which theory (or combination) offers the *best* explanatory power for adolescent suicide, and why this matters for prevention or policy. This gives your essay a spine that the rest of the paper can support.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay follows a reasonable trajectory (statistics → Durkheim → Marx → synthesis on depression), but transitions between sections are abrupt. Each theoretical section (egoistic, anomic, altruistic, conflict theory) reads as a separate mini-essay rather than a connected argument building toward a conclusion.

Consider adding a topic sentence to each paragraph that ties back to your thesis, not just introduces the next theorist.
The paragraph on altruistic suicide, which you say "doesn't" relate to adolescents, is a missed opportunity. Why include a theory you're dismissing? Either cut it, or use it comparatively—what does its *absence* in adolescent cases tell us about adolescent psychology versus adult psychology?
Your concluding paragraph is too brief and vague ("maybe one has to look backwards to better understand their movements forward") for an essay of this scope. Expand this into a real synthesis: What do Durkheim and Marx, taken together, tell us that neither does alone?

Evidence and Citations

You have many citation markers (`<R>`) but the surrounding text often doesn't specify *who* said what or *where* the data comes from in-text (author names, years). Academic readers need this woven into your prose, not just marked externally.

Additionally, some evidence is asserted without support—eg, "There are no theories, theorists, researchers, or even case studies that argue that adolescent suicide isn't a direct result of depression." This is a sweeping claim. Either cite scholarship confirming this consensus, or soften the claim ("Most researchers agree...").

The anecdote from Rabkin's book is left underdeveloped—you introduce the quote but don't analyze it. What does the girl's statement about feeling "different and crazy" *demonstrate* about egoistic suicide specifically? Unpack the connection.

Clarity and Precision of Theoretical Explanation

Your definitions of egoistic and anomic suicide are serviceable but could be sharper. For instance, you write that egoistic suicide involves "a lost sense of bondness"—this is close to Durkheim's concept of weakened social integration, but state it more precisely using standard terminology (social integration/regulation) so readers familiar with Durkheim recognize the concepts clearly.

Similarly, your discussion of Marx and dialectics is abstract and disconnected from adolescent suicide until the very end of the paragraph. Slow down and explicitly walk the reader through *how* dialectical tension (eg, between family expectations and adolescent autonomy, or economic pressures and family stability) produces the outcomes you're describing.

Grammar, Mechanics, and Style

This essay requires careful proofreading. Notable issues include:

Subject-verb agreement: "are our young people," "rates were," "attempts are"
Verb tense inconsistency: shifting between past and present tense when discussing Durkheim/Marx's theories
Word confusion: "contraversal" (controversial), "programed" (programmed), "appropiatly" (appropriately), "bondness" (should be "belonging" or "connectedness")
Repeated phrases: "adolescents who have experienced the death of a parent and adolescents who have experienced the death of a parent and adolescents who has moved..." — this sentence needs to be rewritten; it currently repeats itself and has a subject-verb agreement error.
Comma splices and fragments: eg, "This is a basis for Durkheim's position on suicide." reads as disconnected from surrounding sentences.

Recommendation: After revising for content and structure, do a dedicated proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch these issues.

Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis into an explicit, arguable claim.
Strengthen paragraph transitions and topic sentences to maintain argumentative thread.
Expand your synthesis/conclusion significantly.
Integrate citations more smoothly into your prose with named sources.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice errors.

You have strong raw material here—solid research and an interesting theoretical angle. The next revision should focus on tightening the argument and making your analytical voice more prominent alongside your research.

Feedback on "Water Pollution and Sociological Theory"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an ambitious and genuinely interesting angle—connecting an environmental issue to Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory. However, the essay currently reads more like three loosely connected papers (a science overview, a policy summary, and a theory review) than a unified argument. The biggest priority for revision is establishing a clear thesis and using it to drive every subsequent paragraph.

Thesis and Structure

Your second paragraph states the paper's "focus" but doesn't make an *argument*. What are you claiming? Possible theses might be: "Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory reveal that water pollution persists because polluters lack sufficient information about costs, and reducing pollution requires restructuring incentives rather than appealing to goodwill." Whatever you choose, state it explicitly and use it as a roadmap.

Right now the essay's structure is additive rather than argumentative:
Introduction (pollution examples)
Water scarcity facts
Categories of pollutants
Point vs. non-point sources
Rational Choice Theory
Exchange Theory
Conclusion

Paragraphs 3 and 4 (pollutant categories, point/non-point sources) are useful background, but they aren't yet connected to your theoretical argument. Consider explicitly linking them: for example, non-point pollution (individual, diffuse, hard to trace) might connect naturally to Rational Choice Theory's emphasis on individual decision-making, while point-source pollution (institutional, structural) might connect to Exchange Theory's focus on costs/rewards for organizations. Making this kind of connection would transform your background information into evidence for your argument.

Missing Citations

There are numerous placeholder markers ` <R>` throughout the draft where source citations should be. This is a serious issue—as it stands, unsupported claims about diseases, statistics, and even theory definitions have no attribution. Before your next draft, fill in every one of these with a proper in-text citation and ensure they appear in your Works Cited/References list. An essay that leans this heavily on statistics *must* be fully sourced.

Developing the Theoretical Analysis

Your explanations of Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory are a good start, but they need sharper analytical use:

Rational Choice Theory: You state individuals act on "personal beliefs," but classical Rational Choice Theory is usually framed around self-interest and cost-benefit calculation, not belief per se. Double-check your source's definition and make sure your application matches it precisely. Currently, the paragraph shifts between describing the theory, citing statistics, and making policy recommendations, without clearly showing *how* the theory explains company or individual behavior regarding pollution. Try structuring this paragraph as: (1) define theory, (2) apply theory to a specific behavior (eg, dumping used oil), (3) explain what the theory predicts would change behavior, (4) support with evidence.

Exchange Theory: The car-washing and bad-tasting-water examples are strong, concrete illustrations—more paragraphs would benefit from this kind of specificity. However, the paragraph runs long and repeats itself (eg, multiple restatements that behavior is "individualized"). Tighten this section by cutting repeated phrasing and combining redundant sentences.

Consider also directly comparing/contrasting the two theories in one dedicated paragraph: where do they agree, where do they diverge, and which do you find more persuasive as an explanation for real-world pollution behavior?

Conclusion

The Milwaukee cryptosporidium example is powerful, but it appears for the first time in your conclusion. Strong essays introduce major evidence in the body, not the conclusion. Move this example earlier (perhaps as a case study illustrating consequences of unchecked pollution) and use the conclusion to synthesize your argument and reiterate your thesis, rather than introducing new material.

Style and Mechanics

Watch for repeated ideas/phrasing (eg, "individualized" and "personal beliefs" recur many times without adding new insight each time).
Some sentences contain grammatical errors: "then" vs. "than," "war pollution" (likely "water pollution"), inconsistent apostrophes ("its'" should be "its").
Vary sentence openings—many paragraphs rely on "Therefore" or "Also" as transitions, which weakens flow.
Proofread carefully for subject-verb agreement and pronoun consistency (eg, "if she is deprived...then she will").

Action Items for Revision

Write an explicit, arguable thesis statement and revise topic sentences to support it.
Fill in all missing citations.
Connect background sections (pollutant types, point/non-point sources) explicitly to your theoretical framework.
Tighten each theory paragraph using a clear structure: definition → application → evidence → implication.
Move the Milwaukee example into the body as supporting evidence.
Proofread for grammar and eliminate repetitive phrasing.

You have strong raw material here—concrete statistics, relevant theories, and real-world examples. The next draft should focus on integration and argumentative clarity rather than adding more content.

Feedback on "The Case for Legalizing Marijuana"

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research, and your paper covers an impressive range of angles—medical, economic, criminal justice, and industrial. This breadth is a strength, but it also creates the essay's central problem: you're trying to do too much, and as a result, no single argument gets the depth it deserves. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Thesis and Focus

Your thesis statement (paragraph 3) reads more like a table of contents than an argument. Sentences like "This paper will focus on..." repeated three times signal that you haven't yet distilled your argument into a single, sharp claim. Consider revising to something like: "The criminalization of marijuana produces greater social harm than its legalization would, as demonstrated by its effects on healthcare, the justice system, and the economy." Then let each body section serve that unified claim rather than reading as separate mini-essays stapled together.

Given the length constraints of an undergraduate paper, you would be better served by choosing two of your five topics (medical use, prisons, judges, industrial fiber, revenue, crime) and treating them with real depth, rather than covering all five superficially.

Application of Sociological Theory

This is the area needing the most attention. You mention Structural-Functionalism, Exchange Theory, and Conflict Theory, but the application is often just a label attached to a claim rather than genuine analysis. For example, "Therefore according to the Exchange Theory rewards should overpower the costs" doesn't actually explain the mechanics of exchange theory (rational actors weighing costs/benefits in social interactions)—it just asserts your conclusion and tacks the theory's name onto it.

For each theoretical application, ask yourself: *What specific concept from this theory am I using, and how does it illuminate something non-obvious about my evidence?* For instance, your Conflict Theory point about class disparities in marijuana arrests is genuinely promising—develop this with data on arrest rates by race/class rather than just asserting that "the lower class will become frustrated." Cite actual sociological studies on differential enforcement.

Evidence and Citations

Your placeholder citations (`<R>`, ) need to be replaced with proper in-text citations before this is submission-ready—remember to check your required citation style (APA, MLA, etc).

More substantively, your evidence sometimes overstates its case. The claim that "not a single death has ever been credibly attributed directly to smoking marijuana" is a strong claim that needs a strong, recent source—this is exactly the kind of assertion a skeptical reader will want verified. Similarly, opening with pop-culture nicknames for marijuana is a soft, informal start for an academic paper; consider opening instead with a striking statistic or the historical fact about the 1,600-year-old burial you mention later—that's a genuinely compelling hook.

Balance and Counterargument

Your essay is persuasive but one-sided. An academic argumentative essay is strengthened, not weakened, by seriously engaging opposing views. You gesture at counterarguments (eg, PHS concerns about AIDS patients and unsafe sex) but then dismiss them quickly. Spend more time steelmanning the opposition—what are the *strongest* arguments against legalization (addiction potential, gateway drug theories, effects on adolescent brain development)? Addressing these directly will make your eventual rebuttal more persuasive, not less.

Organization

Right now, sections feel loosely sequenced rather than building on each other. Consider a clearer architecture:
Introduction with focused thesis
Historical/legal background (your current paragraphs 2 and background material)
Medical case (strong section—keep this)
Economic case (revenue + industrial fiber could be combined)
Justice system costs (prisons + judges could be combined, since both stem from mandatory minimums)
Counterargument and rebuttal
Conclusion

This would cut redundancy and let related points reinforce each other.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are numerous typos and errors that need proofreading: "cannavis" (Cannabis), "heroine" (heroin), "foe" (for), "win" (will), "mar on drugs" (war on drugs), ":pot's," "Once again the rewards are many" (informal filler phrase). Read your paper aloud or use a grammar-checking tool for a full pass. Academic writing also calls for more formal diction—avoid phrases like "getting high" or overly casual transitions.

Voice and Objectivity

Because your topic is contentious, be mindful of language that reveals advocacy rather than analysis—phrases like "obviously," "clearly shown," and "amazing" tell the reader how to feel rather than letting evidence do that work. Academic argumentation is most persuasive when it appears to arrive at conclusions through careful reasoning rather than starting from a firm conviction and marshaling only supporting evidence.

Priority for Revision

Narrow your thesis and scope (choose fewer subtopics, go deeper)
Strengthen theoretical application with real conceptual engagement
Add a genuine counterargument section
Fix citations and proofread thoroughly

You have the raw material for a strong paper here—the next draft should focus on depth over breadth.

Feedback Report: Homelessness Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay demonstrates genuine engagement with the topic of homelessness and shows you have done substantial research into its history and sociological explanations. You clearly understand conflict theory and symbolic interactionism and can apply them to a real-world problem. However, the essay currently reads more like a well-organized research report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need a sharper thesis, tighter structure, and more deliberate argumentation throughout. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argumentative Purpose

Your introduction raises several possible claims—that homelessness is a moral issue, that debates over statistics are unproductive, that action must be taken immediately—but it never settles on one clear, arguable thesis. An argumentative essay needs a specific claim that you defend with evidence and reasoning, not just a topic you describe.

Revision suggestion: Decide what you are actually arguing. Are you claiming that conflict theory offers the most useful framework for understanding and solving homelessness? That structural/economic factors (not individual failings) are the primary cause? That policy should shift from moralizing about the "deserving poor" toward addressing systemic causes? Choose one central claim and make it explicit in your final introductory sentences. Then use it as a thread that ties together your historical overview, your causes section, and your theoretical discussion.

Structure and Balance

Currently, the essay divides into three large, loosely connected sections: (1) a history of homelessness in the U.S., (2) causes of homelessness, and (3) a survey of sociological theories (conflict theory and symbolic interactionism). Each section is informative, but they read as separate mini-essays rather than parts of a unified argument. The history section, in particular, is long and mostly descriptive—it doesn't yet feed into your argument about what should be done or which theoretical lens is most useful.

Revision suggestion: Trim the historical section significantly. You don't need a chronological survey from colonial settlers to the 1990s; instead, select two or three historical moments that best illustrate your thesis (eg, the "deserving vs. undeserving poor" distinction, deinstitutionalization in the 1970s, changing public attitudes in the 1980s–90s). Use these as evidence, not as a self-contained narrative.

Use of Evidence and Citation

You reference *Homelessness: A Sourcebook* by Fantasia and Isserman, but the direct quotation is missing (marked with ), and this is your only cited source despite the essay's heavy reliance on historical and sociological claims. Statements like "Real estate agencies have also conspired against the poor" or "Drug use has increased dramatically in the homeless community" are strong claims that need citations to be credible in academic writing.

Revision suggestion: Go back through the essay and identify every factual claim that isn't common knowledge—economic trends, statistics, historical events, theoretical definitions—and add a citation. This will also help you avoid the appearance of asserting opinion as fact (eg, "conspired against the poor").

Application of Sociological Theory

This is the strongest part of your essay. Your explanations of conflict theory and symbolic interactionism are clear, and your application of labeling theory to homelessness is a genuine insight. However, the connection between theory and your actual argument is underdeveloped. You explain what each theory says, but you don't clearly argue why one is more useful than the other for understanding or solving homelessness—until your brief concluding paragraph, which raises this comparison for the first time.

Revision suggestion: Move your evaluative comparison of the two theories (micro vs. macro-level analysis) earlier, and let it guide your discussion throughout rather than appearing only in the conclusion. If you believe the conflict theory's macro perspective is more useful for policy (as your final paragraph suggests), build your entire essay toward that conclusion rather than presenting both theories neutrally.

Paragraph Focus and Repetition

Several paragraphs repeat points made elsewhere (eg, the 1980s vs. 1990s attitude shift appears three times). Some paragraphs also drift—the paragraph on African-Americans and homelessness, for instance, introduces an important point about racial disparity and education but doesn't connect back to your thesis or receive further development.

Revision suggestion: After drafting, outline each paragraph's single main point in one sentence. If two paragraphs share the same point, combine them. If a paragraph's point doesn't clearly serve your thesis, either cut it or explicitly explain its relevance.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are several typos and unclear phrasings to fix:
"lobs" should be "jobs"
"consent unrest socked endures" is unclear—likely a typing error that needs to be rewritten
"Contact theorists" should probably be "Conflict theorists"
"in these factories the workers had a great insecurity" — awkward phrasing; consider "workers experienced significant job insecurity"
Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences, particularly in the historical section.

Revision suggestion: Read the essay aloud during revision to catch awkward phrasing and missing words.

Summary of Priorities

Write a clear, arguable thesis and revisit it in each section.
Cut redundant historical narrative; keep only what supports your argument.
Add citations for factual and statistical claims.
Integrate your theoretical analysis with your argument rather than presenting it as a separate survey.
Proofread carefully for typos and unclear sentences.

You have strong raw material here—particularly your grasp of sociological theory. Focus your revision on shaping this material around one clear argument, and the essay will be significantly more persuasive.

Feedback Report: Welfare System Essay

Overall Impression

This is an ambitious essay that tackles a genuinely complex policy question by putting two major sociological theories—Spencer's Social Darwinism and Merton's Matthew Effect—into dialogue with each other. Your research is extensive, and you clearly understand both theoretical frameworks well enough to apply them to contemporary data. However, the essay currently reads more like a well-organized research report than an argumentative essay, because your own position doesn't emerge clearly enough, and several structural and technical issues weaken its persuasive power. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argumentative Stance

The essay's biggest structural problem is that it presents Spencer's view, then Merton's view, and gives each roughly equal, sympathetic treatment—almost like a compare/contrast paper—before arriving at a conclusion that still hedges ("it is also not fair that some welfare recipients candidly admit they could work more"). Readers finish the essay unsure of what you actually believe should happen to the welfare system.

Your introduction poses the question "Why does welfare exist?" but never explicitly states your answer or your argumentative claim. Revise your introduction to include a clear, specific thesis—for example, one that argues welfare should be reformed (not abolished) through targeted opportunity-based programs, while acknowledging legitimate concerns about dependency. Then make sure every section supports that thesis, rather than presenting Spencer and Merton as equally weighted "sides" you're merely surveying.

Strengthen the Structure Around Your Argument, Not the Theorists

Right now the essay's organization follows the theorists (background → Spencer → Merton → conclusion) rather than your argument. This makes the Spencer section feel like it's arguing *for* abolition for several pages before you pivot. Consider restructuring so that Spencer's view functions clearly as the counterargument you are engaging with and ultimately complicating or rejecting—signal this earlier. Right now, a reader could reach the end of the Spencer section believing that is your position.

Additionally, the transition into your conclusion is abrupt. You introduce a strong claim ("the grand illusion of welfare reform") and then immediately shift to disability/immigrant comparisons without clearly synthesizing what this means for your overall argument. Add a paragraph that explicitly reconciles Merton's and Spencer's insights: perhaps some individuals do lack motivation, but structural barriers explain most cases, and therefore policy should address structural barriers first.

Fix Missing and Inconsistent Citations

There are numerous placeholder marks (`<R>`, ) throughout the draft where quotations or citations were clearly meant to be inserted but were left blank. This is a serious issue for an argumentative essay—readers cannot evaluate evidence (eg, the Ann Clark quote, the Aletha Townsend anecdote, the Bill Bennett quote, the Toby Herr quote) if the actual words are missing. Before resubmitting, every one of these must be completed with the actual quotation and a proper citation (author, source, page/date). Additionally, statistics like "sixty percent of welfare recipients are abused" or "25–40% have learning disabilities" need specific, named sources—right now they read as floating assertions, which weakens credibility even though the numbers are likely accurate.

Balance Statistics with Analysis

Several paragraphs (particularly under the Merton section) are essentially lists of statistics with brief connecting sentences. While the data is compelling, you should slow down and analyze more of it—explain *how* a given statistic supports Merton's theory rather than simply stating it and moving to the next one. For instance, after citing the Holzer study on employer hiring practices, spend a sentence or two unpacking why this specifically illustrates a "lack of opportunity" rather than a "lack of effort."

Address Contradictions and Overgeneralizations

The essay sometimes undercuts its own argument. You write that people without diplomas, non-English-speaking immigrants, and disabled individuals all manage to work "without the support of welfare," which risks reinforcing the very "personal failure" narrative you spent most of the essay countering with Merton's theory. Consider whether this comparison strengthens or contradicts your broader argument, and revise accordingly.

Proofread Carefully

There are numerous typos and errors that need correction: "Me United States" (paragraph 1), "different men" (not "deferent"), "he no useful place" (Spencer paragraph), a double period after "family by herself," and inconsistent comma/period placement around citations. A careful proofreading pass is essential before this is submission-ready.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Write an explicit, arguable thesis and make sure the essay argues *toward* it throughout.
Restructure so Spencer functions clearly as counterargument, not as an equally endorsed position.
Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Add analytical commentary connecting statistics to your claims.
Resolve internal contradictions in the conclusion.
Proofread thoroughly.

You have strong raw material here—now it needs a clearer argumentative spine.

Feedback on Your Essay on Divorce

Overall Impression

This essay demonstrates real effort to engage with sociological theory and covers an impressive range of material—symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, and exchange theory are all brought to bear on the question of why divorce rates have risen. However, the essay currently reads more like an annotated summary of course material than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need a clearer thesis, tighter organization, and more critical engagement with the theories rather than simply describing them one after another.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, it's unclear what you are actually arguing. Is your claim that divorce is a "social problem" rather than a collection of "personal troubles"? Is it that multiple theories are needed to fully explain divorce? Is it that certain interventions (strengthening family ties, clarifying gender roles) could reduce divorce rates? Each of these is a viable thesis, but the essay tries to do all of them at once without deciding which is primary.

*Revision suggestion:* Write a single sentence that states your central claim, then check whether every section supports it. For example: "While divorce is often treated as a personal failure, sociological theory shows it to be a predictable outcome of structural changes in family life—and understanding these theories points toward specific ways of strengthening marriages." This would justify the current three-part structure (causes, consequences, solutions) but give it a spine.

Structure and Organization

The essay's organization is essentially a list: symbolic interactionism, then functionalism, then conflict theory, then exchange theory, followed by a parallel list of "what can be done" for each theory. This is logical but mechanical. The reader never sees you compare or evaluate the theories against one another—which one explains divorce best? Where do they overlap or conflict? A stronger essay would include a short paragraph, perhaps before your conclusion, that synthesizes these perspectives rather than simply restating them.

Also, the discussion of children and custody arrangements after divorce feels like a separate essay tacked onto the main argument. Either integrate it more explicitly into your thesis (eg, "the consequences for children reveal the same structural pressures that caused the divorce") or consider trimming it if it's pulling focus from your central claim.

Use of Sources

You cite statistics and scholars (Ogburn, Burgess and Locke, Spanier and Thompson) but many citations are marked simply as `<R>`, meaning the actual reference is missing. Before submission, every one of these needs a proper citation. Additionally, some claims need more development: the Spanier and Thompson study, for instance, is listed as a set of bullet-pointed conflict issues but never analyzed—how do these findings support conflict theory specifically? Show the reader the connection rather than assuming it's obvious.

Theoretical Application

Your explanations of each theory are generally accurate, but they remain at the level of definition and description. Strong analytical writing would apply the theory to a specific claim and then interpret it. For example, in the symbolic interactionism section, you explain that expectations of marriage have changed, but you don't clearly link this back to the *mechanism* the theory highlights (that shared meanings shape behavior). Consider adding a sentence like: "Because partners now interpret 'true love' as a guarantee of total satisfaction, any dissatisfaction is read as evidence that the marriage—not the expectation—has failed." This kind of explicit theoretical reasoning will elevate your analysis.

Style and Mechanics

There are frequent grammatical errors that need correcting in revision:
"coning to depend" should be "coming to depend"
"the possibilities for remarriage, or will they live a satisfying single life" needs rephrasing for parallel structure
"As a couple moves forward divorce" should be "moves toward divorce"
"Piety needs to make the family unit" is unclear—likely a typo for "People" or "Policy"
Watch subject-verb agreement: "husband and wives" should be "husbands and wives"

I recommend reading the essay aloud, which will help you catch these errors more easily than silent proofreading.

The "Solutions" Section

This section is a nice addition, but it currently just restates the theories in the imperative mood ("we need to look at symbols," "couples need equal exchange") without offering concrete, actionable proposals. What would strengthening "the family unit" actually look like in practice? Are there policy implications (premarital counseling, changes to no-fault divorce law) suggested by the theories you've discussed? Grounding your solutions in real-world specifics will make this section more persuasive.

Priorities for Revision

Draft a clear, one-sentence thesis and revise each section to visibly support it.
Add a synthesis paragraph comparing the four theories.
Fill in all missing citations.
Deepen theoretical analysis rather than definition.
Proofread carefully for grammar.

You have gathered strong material—the next draft should focus on shaping it into a unified argument.

Feedback on "Running Up the Score" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a lively, debatable topic, and your knowledge of college football gives you material to work with. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of loosely connected observations than a unified argument. My feedback below focuses on strengthening your thesis, tightening your logic, and cleaning up style and mechanics.

Clarify Your Thesis and Central Claim

Your actual argument doesn't appear until the final paragraph: coaches aren't "guilty" of running up the score; they're just trying to secure their programs financially and professionally. This is a strong, specific claim—but it needs to appear early, ideally at the end of your introduction, so readers know what you're arguing before they wade through six paragraphs of examples.

More importantly, there's a contradiction at the heart of your essay. You claim coaches "really aren't guilty of anything" and don't score points "purposely." But then you spend three paragraphs explaining that coaches deliberately keep starters in for Heisman consideration, deliberately recruit score-running players for "job security," and deliberately let scores run because it pleases fans and generates revenue. These are all descriptions of *intentional* score-running. You need to decide: are you arguing that running up the score is unintentional (a side effect of playing hard), or that it's intentional but justified by legitimate institutional pressures? Right now you're arguing both, and they don't fit together. The second claim is actually more interesting and defensible—consider building the whole essay around it.

Strengthen and Verify Your Evidence

The Spurrier/Florida-Carolina anecdote is your strongest piece of evidence, but it needs work:
Include a date/season so readers can verify it.
Fix the tense inconsistency—you shift between present tense ("will they go for it?") and past tense throughout this passage. Pick one and stay consistent, most likely past tense since you're recounting a specific historical event.
The ranking dispute (Florida vs. Ohio State) is confusing as written. Walk the reader through it more slowly and explicitly: what were the rankings before, what happened in each team's game, and why did the NCAA's system produce this outcome? Right now the logic is compressed and hard to follow.

Your essay would also benefit from at least one outside source—an NCAA statement, a sports journalist's commentary, or a coach's own quote about the ethics of running up scores. Right now the essay relies entirely on your own reasoning and one extended anecdote, which weakens its persuasive authority.

Improve Paragraph Organization

Each paragraph raises a distinct reason (second-string players, passing plays, the Heisman, recruiting/job security, fan interest), but they read as a list rather than a developing argument. Consider:
Adding a topic sentence to each paragraph that explicitly ties back to your thesis (eg, "Another reason coaches appear to run up the score is financial pressure, not malice.")
Adding transitions between paragraphs that show logical progression, not just chronological add-ons ("Some coaches even go further...").
The Heisman paragraph and the "job security" paragraph make similar points (money, incentives) and could potentially be combined or more clearly distinguished.

Address the Counterargument More Directly

You mention that the NCAA "discourages" running up the score, but you never explain *why* the NCAA objects, nor do you seriously engage with the idea that blowing out an opponent by 50+ points might genuinely be poor sportsmanship. A strong argumentative essay acknowledges the opposing view's best points before refuting them. Consider adding a paragraph that fairly represents why critics see this as unsportsmanlike, then pivot to your explanation of institutional pressures.

Style, Tone, and Mechanics

A few issues to fix in revision:
Informal phrasing ("getting their butts beat bad," "who could blame him") should be replaced with more academic diction appropriate for an argumentative essay.
There's a placeholder or missing word/symbol in the third paragraph ("You could give them the ball and say "). Make sure this is filled in before submission.
Watch homophone errors: "it's" vs. "its," "to" vs. "too."
The stray apostrophe after "Bowden," should be removed.
Several sentences are comma splices or run-ons; reading your paragraphs aloud will help you catch these.
"Unsurmountable" should be "insurmountable."

Next Steps

I'd suggest starting your revision by rewriting your thesis statement to resolve the contradiction I flagged above, then reorganizing your paragraphs so each one visibly supports that single claim. Strengthening your Florida/Carolina example with clear dates and verified details will also make your strongest piece of evidence even more persuasive. This has the bones of an engaging essay—the revision work now is about clarity and consistency.

Feedback on "The Confederate Battle Flag Debate"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult and personally meaningful topic, and your willingness to write from a place of complicated, lived experience is a real strength. The essay is readable and your voice comes through clearly. However, the argument currently works against itself in several places, and tightening the logic will make this a much more persuasive piece. Below are the main issues to address in revision, organized by priority.

Clarify and Stabilize Your Thesis

Your introduction promises a fairly clean argument: *regardless of what the flag means to different groups, it should come down.* But the essay doesn't consistently deliver on that promise. By the final paragraph, you're actually arguing for a different flag to go up in its place, accompanied by an explanatory plaque about Southern heritage. This is a much softer, more complicated position than your opening suggests, and it risks confusing readers who expected a straightforward removal argument.

Ask yourself: is your real claim (a) the battle flag should come down and nothing should replace it, or (b) the battle flag should be replaced with a different, "more accurate" Confederate flag? These are not the same argument, and they pull in different directions. Choose one and revise your introduction and conclusion so they match.

Resolve the Contradiction Between Paragraphs

You state early on that you personally see the flag as connected to Southern heritage, and that "the South does have a very rich heritage that should be remembered." Later, you write that the battle flag "stands for nothing but racism and hatred" and that this heritage claim shouldn't be made at all. These two positions are in direct tension. If the flag is *nothing but* hatred, your earlier concession about heritage becomes hard to justify—and vice versa. Revision should either soften the "nothing but" language or explain more precisely how you reconcile your personal attachment with your conclusion that the symbol is irredeemable. Right now it reads as if two different essays were merged.

Support Your Historical Claims with Evidence

The distinction you draw between the "battle flag" and the "real Confederate flag" is an interesting point that many readers won't know, but you present it purely from memory ("in my mind that stands for...") rather than from research. Similarly, your claim that the flag was raised in 1964, "the day after the civil rights movement started," is a striking historical detail that needs a citation—and as stated, it's not quite accurate. (The flag was actually raised over the S.C. State House in 1961–62, tied to the Civil War centennial and resistance to desegregation, not literally the "day after" the civil rights movement "started," which itself is hard to date to a single day.) This is exactly the kind of claim an academic argument needs to source carefully, both to be accurate and to be persuasive. Find and cite a credible historical source for the flag's origin and use precise dates.

Reconsider the "Let Them Experience It" Passage

The paragraph suggesting that outsiders "cannot be expected to understand" Southern life and should be left to "experience it for themselves" risks sounding dismissive of the legitimate objections many people—including many Southerners—have to the flag. If your goal is to persuade readers who see the flag as a hate symbol, this passage may alienate them rather than build common ground. Consider reframing this section around shared civic values (eg, what message the state wants to send about who it welcomes) rather than the idea that critics simply don't understand Southern culture.

Tighten Structure and Transitions

Right now the essay moves: heritage vs. hatred → flag-type distinction → renewed claim of hatred → historical origin → economic/reputational argument → proposed replacement flag. Each of these is a reasonable point, but the sequence doesn't build cumulatively toward one governing claim. Consider organizing around your strongest, most concrete argument—the 1961/62 origin during the civil rrights era—and using it as the backbone, with the heritage-vs-hatred discussion and the flag-type distinction functioning as supporting context rather than competing side arguments.

Proofreading Note

Watch for "succeeded" where you mean "seceded"—an easy mix-up but one that matters in a historical argument. Also check subject-verb agreement in a few spots ("they should agree as a group") for consistency.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Decide on one clear thesis (remove entirely vs. replace with alternate flag) and align intro/conclusion.
Resolve the heritage/hatred contradiction explicitly.
Add citations for historical claims, especially the flag's origin date.
Revise the "let them experience it" section for tone.
Reorganize paragraphs around a single throughline.

You have the makings of a compelling, personal argument here—the revision work is mostly about consistency and evidence rather than fundamentally rethinking your position.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles an ambitious and controversial topic, and it's clear you've done research to support your position. However, the essay currently reads more as a moral polemic than an academic argument, and there are significant issues with evidence, logic, tone, and mechanics that need attention before this could be considered college-level analytical writing. Below is feedback organized by area.

Argument and Thesis

Your thesis claims premarital sex is "overall destructive" because it causes loss of respect for women, illegitimate families, and STDs. The problem is that you consistently conflate correlation with causation. For instance, you cite statistics about STD rates and family structure changes occurring *after* the sexual revolution, but you never demonstrate that premarital sex itself—rather than other social, economic, or educational factors—is the causal mechanism. A rigorous argument needs to rule out alternative explanations, not just note that two trends coincide chronologically.

Additionally, the essay would be stronger if you engaged with counterarguments. Right now, you mention that premarital sex "does little or no harm... physically" almost as a concession, but you never seriously address the strongest opposing views (eg, that education, contraception, and personal responsibility—not the act of premarital sex itself—determine outcomes like STDs or unwanted pregnancy). Academic argumentation is strengthened, not weakened, by acknowledging and refuting the best version of the opposing case.

Use of Evidence

This is the essay's weakest area. Several statistics are used in ways that don't actually support your claims:

You state that "38% of single men and 45% of single women would like to become parents even if they never marry," then use this to argue family structures are becoming "dysfunctional." But wanting to be a parent outside marriage doesn't necessarily correlate with dysfunction—you need actual evidence (eg, studies on outcomes for children in these households) to make that leap.
The statistic that "over 90% of people believe family is the most important institution" seems to contradict rather than support your argument that family values are eroding. You need to explain this apparent tension rather than gloss over it.
Citations like "(Reiss 175)" and "(Janus 13 5)" are inconsistently formatted and sometimes garbled (is it page 13 or 135?). Every citation should be checked for accuracy and formatted consistently according to whatever style guide (MLA, APA) you're using.

When revising, ask of each statistic: *Does this number actually prove the specific claim I just made, or does it just sound related?*

Tone and Word Choice

The essay's tone undermines its credibility as an academic argument. Phrases like "the foundation of this nation... has crumbled," "moral solvents," and the closing call to return to "Christian values" and "God given" reason shift the essay from persuasive analysis into religious/moral exhortation. This is fine for a sermon or op-ed, but in an academic essay, it will alienate readers who don't share your religious framework and will read as an appeal to authority rather than an argument. Consider whether your claims can be supported through secular sociological, psychological, or public-health reasoning instead. This would broaden your audience and strengthen your ethos as a careful, fair-minded arguer.

The language describing women ("brazier cup sizes than IQs") and the casual use of slurs even when analyzing their misuse, could be reframed with more precision and less repetition of the offensive language itself. When quoting or analyzing derogatory terms, make sure the analytical framing is clearly separated from the term itself.

Organization

Your body paragraphs are organized thematically (revolution history, STDs, pregnancy, disrespect), which is reasonable, but each section needs a clearer topic sentence that states the specific sub-claim before diving into statistics. Right now the reader is often given a fact ("in the Janus Report...") before understanding why that fact matters. Consider a structure of claim → evidence → analysis/explanation of significance for each paragraph.

The conclusion introduces a new, unargued policy solution (return to Christian values, use of reason) that isn't developed anywhere else in the essay. If this is your ultimate point, it needs its own body paragraph with support, not just an assertion in the final lines.

Mechanics

There are frequent errors that need careful proofreading:
"lost" should be "loss"; "there" should be "their" (recurring error); "affects" should be "effects" (recurring error)
"have sex" should be "having sex"; "din-finishing" appears to be a typo
Sentence fragments and comma splices appear throughout (eg, "Those stats would lead to less structured relies, Now, since...")

A careful proofreading pass—reading the essay aloud—will catch many of these.

Overall Recommendation

Focus your revision on: (1) separating moral/religious conviction from empirical argument, (2) ensuring every statistic logically supports the specific claim it's attached to, (3) engaging seriously with counterarguments, and (4) proofreading for grammar. The topic is worth exploring, but the argument needs a more rigorous, evidence-based foundation to be persuasive to an academic audience.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely complex and debated topic, and you clearly have personal conviction about your position. That passion comes through in your writing. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas to strengthen its academic rigor and persuasive power. Here is feedback organized by key areas.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your central claim—that women can serve valuably in the military without serving in combat roles—is a defensible position, but it gets diluted by several tangential claims that aren't fully developed: (1) standards are lowered for women, (2) equality efforts distort "reality," (3) differences should be embraced rather than "fixed," and (4) equalization risks destroying femininity itself. Each of these is a substantial claim requiring its own evidence and reasoning. Right now, the essay tries to argue all of them simultaneously, which weakens the overall focus. Revision suggestion: Choose one or two of these threads as your primary argument and develop them thoroughly, rather than gesturing at several.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's greatest weakness. You make several assertive claims—that standards are "sometimes lowered," that physical demands "would not be feasible," that women's "emotional makeup" differs from men's in ways relevant to combat—without citing any sources, studies, data, or concrete examples. In academic argumentative writing, especially on a topic this contested, you need to support claims with evidence: military policy documents, physical fitness studies, historical examples of women in combat roles (many countries already integrate women into combat units), or expert testimony. Without this, the essay reads as personal opinion rather than an academic argument. Revision suggestion: For every major claim, ask yourself "How do I know this?" and find a source or example to answer that question.

Addressing Counterarguments

The essay would be considerably stronger if it directly engaged with opposing viewpoints. For instance, what would you say to the argument that individual women who meet the same physical standards as men should be allowed to serve in combat roles, even if this doesn't apply to all women? What about countries with fully integrated combat forces—what has their experience shown? Engaging with counterarguments demonstrates critical thinking and strengthens your credibility, even if you ultimately reject them.

Consistency and Logical Clarity

There are some internal tensions worth resolving. You state that "standards are lowered" to accommodate women (suggesting they cannot meet existing standards), but later argue that differences should be "accepted" rather than "fixed"—implying separate standards might be appropriate. These aren't necessarily contradictory positions, but the essay doesn't clarify the relationship between them. Are you arguing for equal standards with no exceptions, or for different but respected standards? Being explicit about this will help readers follow your reasoning.

Tone and Word Choice

Some language choices undermine your credibility with a general academic audience. Phrases like "physical weakness of the weaker sex" and repeated framing of women as inherently limited may alienate readers and can come across as reinforcing stereotypes rather than making a reasoned case. Consider whether you can make your argument about role differentiation without language that sounds dismissive of women's capabilities. You can argue for different roles without characterizing women as "weaker" in ways that feel value-laden.

Structure and Organization

There is a noticeable repeated passage in your second paragraph (about intellectual capability and lowered standards) that appears to be a drafting error—you state the same idea twice in close succession. Please proofread carefully for this kind of duplication.

Additionally, the essay would benefit from a clearer structural roadmap. Consider organizing paragraphs around discrete claims: one paragraph on physical differences and their relevance to combat, one addressing the "why fix what isn't broken" argument, one addressing counterarguments, and a conclusion that synthesizes rather than simply restates your feelings.

Personal Voice vs. Academic Argument

Your concluding paragraph relies heavily on personal feeling ("I love having feminine characteristics...I have a difficult time understanding a woman who wants to be treated like a man"). While personal perspective can enrich an essay, an argumentative essay should ultimately rest on reasoning and evidence that would persuade someone who doesn't share your personal feelings. Consider moving some of this personal reflection to your introduction (as motivation for the topic) while ensuring your body paragraphs carry the analytical weight.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Narrow and clarify your central thesis
Add concrete evidence and sources for all major claims
Address at least one significant counterargument
Revise language that risks reinforcing stereotypes
Fix the duplicated passage and proofread for clarity
Strengthen the balance between personal conviction and evidence-based reasoning

This essay has a clear point of view—now it needs the scaffolding of evidence and counterargument to become a persuasive academic piece.

Feedback Report

Your essay tackles an important and complex topic, and you've done solid work incorporating multiple sources (Farrell, Masters, Friedan, Poilit) to support your argument. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen the essay significantly.

Thesis and Argument Consistency

Your thesis states that American culture "should maintain gender identities, but only to a certain extent." This is a promising nuanced position, but the essay doesn't fully deliver on it. Instead, your body paragraphs and conclusion argue almost entirely *against* traditional gender roles, cataloguing their psychological and sociological harms. By the conclusion, you're advocating that people should be free to be "who they want to be"—which sounds like you're arguing against maintaining distinct gender roles altogether, not for maintaining them "to a certain extent."

Revision suggestion: Clarify what "to a certain extent" actually means. Which aspects of traditional gender roles are worth preserving, and which aren't? For example, do you think there's value in raising boys to be responsible or girls to be nurturing, even as you reject rigid enforcement of these traits? Right now, the essay reads as a fairly one-sided critique of gender stereotyping. If that's your real position, revise the thesis to match. If you genuinely believe some traditional elements should remain, you need at least one paragraph that concretely defends this.

Structure and Balance

The essay is heavily weighted toward the *disadvantages* of gender stereotyping (several paragraphs), while the *advantages* are addressed only briefly (one or two sentences each) before pivoting quickly to disadvantages. This imbalance undermines your stated "to a certain extent" position. Consider giving equal weight to genuine advantages, or reconsider whether your thesis should more directly reflect the critique you're actually making.

Use of Sources

You cite several credible sources, which is a strength. However:
Some citations are incomplete or vague (eg, "Poilit feels that " and "primary concern of psychiatrist, as they think "). These placeholders need to be filled in with actual content, or removed.
Introduce sources with more context. For example, when citing Masters, briefly explain *why* his research is relevant/credible.
Ensure each source is followed by *your own analysis* explaining how it supports your argument, rather than letting the citation speak for itself.

Clarity and Paragraph Focus

Some paragraphs try to do too much. For instance, the paragraph on female stereotypes moves from bored housewives, to women dropping out of college, to bad marriages, to Friedan's research—each of these could be its own point with more development. Consider breaking dense paragraphs into smaller, more focused ones, each with a clear topic sentence.

The paragraph beginning "I feel that both genders should work for financial stability" feels disconnected—it introduces a new argument (about widowhood and financial security) very late and briefly. Either develop this point earlier and more fully, or integrate it into your discussion of the disadvantages of the traditional female role.

Sentence-Level Issues

There are numerous errors that need correcting in your next draft:
Spelling: "genre" (should be "gender"), "femine" (feminine), "famimlies" (families), "mascularity" (masculinity), "abnomal" (abnormal), "unfemine" (unfeminine), "enstranged" (estranged), "definaton" (definition)
Typographical errors: "alot," "afterall" (should be "after all"), stray commas and periods (eg, ",,Ieadig-.to")
Word choice: "judge" should be "judged"; "Psychologist have also conceded" should be "Psychologists have also found" (conceded implies reluctant agreement, which isn't quite right here)
Missing words/incomplete citations throughout

Suggestion: After revising content, do a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch awkward phrasing and errors.

Conclusion

Your conclusion is compelling in its appeal to American values of liberty, but it doesn't circle back to your original "to a certain extent" thesis. Make sure your conclusion mirrors and reinforces the nuanced position you set up at the start—or revise the thesis to match the stronger anti-stereotyping argument you actually make throughout the essay.

Priority Revisions

Resolve the tension between your nuanced thesis and your essentially one-sided argument—pick one and commit to it.
Fill in all incomplete citations and ensure proper attribution.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and punctuation.
Balance or restructure your discussion of advantages versus disadvantages.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs to show how each point builds toward your thesis.

With these revisions, this essay has strong potential—your source material is good, and your instincts about the psychological and sociological costs of rigid gender roles are well-supported. Focus on aligning your argument and cleaning up the prose.

Feedback Report on "Resident Hall Rules and Regulations"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a topic you clearly care about, and your firsthand knowledge of USC's dorm policies gives you material that many student writers lack. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal complaint than an academic argument. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, tighten your organization, support claims with evidence rather than assertion, and revise for a more formal, controlled tone.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim—that dorm rules prevent students from maturing—gets lost amid several loosely related complaints (visitation, quiet hours, gender discrimination, LGBTQ "advantages"). Each of these could be a strong paper on its own, but combined without clear transitions, they weaken rather than reinforce your main point. Revise your thesis into one sentence that states your specific claim (eg, "Residence hall visitation and noise policies infantilize students rather than helping them develop the independence college is meant to foster") and make sure every paragraph explicitly supports it.

Some sections also undercut your own argument. You spend a paragraph explaining that roommate contracts allow students "a little choice," which seems to concede that student input *does* exist in some form—this needs to be reconciled with your larger claim that rules ignore student voice.

Logical Consistency

A few arguments need more careful reasoning:

The LGBTQ "advantage" argument relies on a hypothetical you present as fact and risks sounding like you're arguing *for* more surveillance of LGBTQ students rather than for fairness. Clarify whether your point is that visitation rules should be enforced equally for everyone, or abolished for everyone—right now it reads as the former, which sits oddly next to your larger complaint that rules are too restrictive.
The rape-prevention paragraph conflates two different issues: whether men can be rape victims (a valid point) and whether posting warnings in female dorms is "unfair" to men. Consider whether statistics support your claim, and separate the emotional appeal from the factual one.
Watch for hasty generalizations ("nine times out of ten," "several universities," "many cases") — these need actual sources or should be reframed as your informed opinion rather than fact.

Evidence

Your strongest moments occur when you cite a *specific* policy (the 10 a.m.–2 a.m. visitation rule, the roommate contract process). Build on this. Where possible, incorporate:
Actual data on dorm policy violations or student satisfaction surveys
Comparisons to policies at other universities (you gesture at this but never follow through)
A counterargument you take seriously and rebut with evidence, not just assertion

Right now, your evidence for the "opposing view" (rules protect residents) is stated but dismissed too quickly ("nine times out of ten... disturbance is the last thing on their minds"). A stronger essay would engage more seriously with *why* colleges implement these rules before arguing they fail.

Organization

Consider restructuring around your strongest 2–3 points rather than covering visitation, quiet hours, gender equity, and LGBTQ policy in one essay. Each new topic currently gets a paragraph, but paragraphs sprawl and drift (eg, the visitation paragraph moves from money spent on campus living to a guest's long drive to worry about accidents). Give each paragraph one controlling idea and stick to it.

Tone and Voice

The essay relies heavily on direct address ("you," "I know you're thinking...") and rhetorical questions. This creates a conversational, persuasive-speech feel rather than an academic essay. For a more polished argumentative essay:
Replace "you" with "students" or "residents"
Convert rhetorical questions into topic sentences that state your claim directly
Reduce exclamations ("That's ridiculous!") in favor of reasoned critique

Grammar and Mechanics

The essay needs careful proofreading. Common issues include:
Word confusion: "rolls" → "roles"; "guest" → "guests" (consistently plural needed); "there" → "their" ("voice there opinions")
Spacing/spelling: "Atleast" → "At least" (appears twice)
Subject-verb agreement: "Men are rape also" → "Men are raped also"; "The rules don't give you a chance" (check consistency of subject throughout)
Sentence fragments and run-ons: several sentences need periods or conjunctions where currently joined awkwardly
Comma splices: eg, "It is as if your parents still live at home" needs a transition to the prior sentence

Next Steps

Narrow your thesis to one clear, arguable claim.
Choose your two strongest supporting arguments and cut or condense the rest.
Add at least one credible outside source per major point.
Revise for academic tone—eliminate direct address and rhetorical questions.
Proofread line-by-line for the grammar issues noted above.

You have a genuine, developed perspective here—the revision task is mostly about focus, evidence, and control of tone.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a strong, debatable topic and clearly have detailed knowledge of college football history. Your case studies (1992-93, 1993-94, 1994) provide compelling, concrete evidence for your argument. However, the essay would benefit significantly from tighter organization, more balanced treatment of counterarguments, and careful proofreading. Below are specific areas to address.

Structure and Organization

Your essay spends roughly 80% of its length on historical case studies before arriving at your actual proposal (the 16-team playoff format) near the end. While these examples are valuable evidence, consider restructuring so your thesis and proposed solution are established early, with the case studies serving to *support* that solution rather than delaying it. Right now, readers must wait a long time to understand what you're actually proposing.

Suggestion: Introduce your specific playoff proposal (16 teams, bowls as neutral sites) in your introduction or shortly after, then use the case studies to demonstrate *why* this specific system would have resolved each controversy. For example, after describing the 1993-94 Florida State/Notre Dame dispute, briefly explain how your 16-team playoff would have settled it.

Depth of Counterargument

You mention opposing views (loss of bowl tradition, extended season, financial concerns) only briefly in one paragraph near the end. For a strong argumentative essay, you need to engage these objections more seriously:

Financial concerns deserve a real response. You claim a playoff would generate more revenue, but bowl executives and conference commissioners have historically argued the opposite (existing bowl contracts, corporate sponsorships, etc). Address this tension directly rather than asserting your side without engagement.
Season length is a legitimate concern for player welfare (academics, injury risk over a longer season). Bowden's hesitation is mentioned but not explored—what would you say to him?
Consider dedicating a full paragraph to the strongest counterargument and directly rebutting it with evidence, rather than listing objections in passing.

Use of Evidence

Your quotations (Reilly, Osborne, Paterno, Spurrier, Bowden) are effective, but:

Citation format is inconsistent and unclear. "(18)" and "(C3)" appear to be page/section references, but there's no clear citation system explained (no works cited, unclear if these are newspaper articles, magazines, etc). Establish a consistent citation method and include a works cited or references list.
Some quotes have formatting errors (stray "11" characters before quotes—likely a formatting/typo issue from quotation marks). Proofread carefully to fix these.
The Spurrier/Bowden anecdote is not cited at all—where does this information come from? Attribute your source.

Clarity and Sentence-Level Issues

The essay contains numerous run-on sentences, comma splices, and word-choice errors that undermine your credibility. Examples:

"college football can develop a playoff system and keep their bowl games at the same time" — this claim is asserted early but the mechanism (bowls as playoff sites) isn't explained until much later. Consider connecting these ideas explicitly and early.
"Another reason the poll writers couldn't really Florida State ahead of Alabama" — missing word ("rank").
"going to going to the Cotton Bowl" — repeated words.
"implied this rule" should likely be "applied this rule."
"Both teams won their bowl games on." — incomplete sentence.

I recommend a careful line-edit pass focused solely on grammar and word choice after you've revised content and structure.

Strengthening the Proposal Section

Your actual playoff proposal (16 teams, four-week format, bowls as neutral sites) is the heart of your argument, but it's underdeveloped compared to the historical narrative. Expand this section:

How would the 16 teams be selected? By committee? Poll ranking? This is the very problem you've spent pages criticizing (subjectivity of polls)—address how your system solves this.
What happens to the "non-playoff" bowls? You mention them briefly but don't explain how this preserves bowl tradition, which is central to your thesis ("keep their bowl games at the same time").
Provide more detail on how revenue would specifically increase (ticket sales, TV contracts, sponsorships)—right now this claim is asserted rather than demonstrated.

Revision Priorities

Reorganize to introduce your proposal earlier and use case studies as supporting evidence for a clearly stated plan.
Deepen counterargument engagement—give real weight and rebuttal to the strongest objections (finances, season length, bowl tradition).
Fix citations—establish a consistent, complete system with a source list.
Proofread thoroughly for grammar, run-ons, and typos.
Expand the proposal section with more mechanical detail and stronger connection to your thesis about preserving bowls.

You have excellent raw material here—detailed knowledge and genuine passion for the topic. With tighter structure and more rigorous argumentation, this essay could be very persuasive. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report

Your essay draws on genuine first-hand experience, and that authority is your greatest asset—few writers can say they've actually been hit by a go-cart while explaining why safety rules matter. Below are suggestions organized by area to help you strengthen the essay's structure, argument, and prose.

Thesis and Organization

Your introduction gestures toward a thesis ("There are many reasons why you should follow the rules") but doesn't clearly preview the specific reasons you'll cover. Consider revising your final introductory sentence to map out your argument more precisely—something like: "Following the rules protects riders from injury, prevents damage to the cars, and keeps park employees safe while they work." This gives readers a roadmap and helps you organize body paragraphs around these three categories rather than moving rule-by-rule in a somewhat listlike fashion.

Right now, your body paragraphs read as a sequence of separate rules (seatbelts, gas/brake, bumping, wrecks, pit entry) without much transition or connection back to a central argument. Try grouping related rules together and adding transitional sentences that tie each paragraph back to your thesis. For example, the seatbelt and brake paragraphs could be combined under a broader point about "operating the cart safely," while the bumping and wreck paragraphs could be grouped under "protecting others on the track."

Development and Evidence

Your strongest moments are the specific anecdotes—being hit by a go-cart, seeing kids get hurt by bumping. Lean into these more. Right now many claims are asserted rather than illustrated ("I have seen a good bit of people get hurt"). Consider adding one or two more concrete, detailed stories (with specifics—what happened, what injury resulted, how it could have been prevented) rather than general statements. A single vivid, well-told anecdote is more persuasive than several vague ones.

Also consider addressing a counterargument directly: you gesture at this when you say bumping "for fun" isn't actually fun, but you could strengthen this by acknowledging why riders want to break the rules (thrill, competition with friends) before explaining why the risk isn't worth it. This makes your argument feel less like a lecture and more like a persuasive conversation with your actual audience—likely teenage or young adult riders.

Audience and Tone

Your opening line ("I don't know how to express how important it is...") undercuts your authority right from the start. Since your essay demonstrates that you *do* know how to express this—through experience and detail—cut this hedge and open with something more confident, perhaps starting directly with an anecdote or a clear statement of your position.

The tone occasionally shifts between formal argument and casual conversation ("I know you have heard the saying, 'buckle up'"). This isn't necessarily wrong for this audience, but be consistent. Decide whether you're writing a persuasive essay for a general audience or an informal appeal directly to go-cart riders, and adjust word choice accordingly throughout.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring errors worth addressing in revision:

Comma splices and run-ons: Sentences like "I know the rules are very important to follow because I am living proof" run multiple ideas together without clear separation. Read your paragraphs aloud to catch places where you need a period or semicolon instead of a comma.
Subject-verb agreement and verb tense: "The seatbelts shouldn't be taking off during the race" should be "taken off." "Having all these rules are for the safety" should be "is for the safety" (singular subject "having...rules" as a gerund phrase takes a singular verb).
Homophone/spelling errors: "annoucements" (announcements), "their for" (there for), "who ever" (whenever), "get in fights" is fine but check "provoking other people" for clarity.
Pronoun consistency: You shift between "you," "I," and general statements. Since your audience seems to be direct ("you"), try to maintain second person more consistently rather than sliding into general statements or first-person tangents that don't reinforce your argument.

Conclusion

Your conclusion repeats ideas from the introduction (rules = safety, fun, family) without adding a new insight or call to action. Consider ending with a more specific, memorable image—perhaps returning to your own injury, or picturing a family's day ruined by a preventable accident—to leave readers with a stronger emotional final impression.

Summary

Focus your revision on: (1) sharpening your thesis and organizing paragraphs around 2-3 clear categories, (2) adding more specific anecdotal evidence, (3) maintaining consistent tone and person, and (4) proofreading carefully for the grammar issues noted above. Your personal experience gives this essay real credibility—the revision process should be about making sure your structure and prose do that experience justice.

Feedback on Your Essay on the Susan Smith Case

Thank you for sharing this draft. You clearly have strong feelings about this case, and your compassion for both the victims and the complexity of Susan Smith's psychological history comes through. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the essay's argument, structure, and academic rigor.

Thesis and Focus

Your introduction promises to address "the justness of the sentence," but the essay's actual argument shifts midway from a legal/psychological justification (mental illness, abuse history) to a theological one (God's forgiveness, heaven and hell). These are two very different kinds of arguments, and mixing them without transition confuses your reader. Consider choosing one central line of argument and making it explicit in your thesis statement. If you want to combine them, you need a sentence that explicitly bridges the two—for example, arguing that the legal system's mercy mirrors a Christian principle of redemption. Right now, the essay reads as two separate essays stitched together.

Factual Accuracy

You state that "the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty" and later "the jury made the right decision." These are contradictory claims. The case was decided by a South Carolina jury, not the Supreme Court. This is an important factual error to correct, as it undermines your credibility on a legal argument. Double-check all case details (dates, names, procedural facts) before revising.

Use of Evidence

You reference a psychiatrist's testimony and Smith's biography, which is good—this is exactly the kind of evidence an argumentative essay needs. However, you should cite your sources (news reports, trial transcripts, biographies) rather than presenting these facts as common knowledge. Since this appears to be a research-based argument, proper citation will strengthen your credibility and is likely required for the assignment.

Additionally, be cautious about the logical leap: "If she had been thinking rationally as a mother she would have given her husband custody of her children." This sentence risks sounding like you are excusing the murders as a rational alternative to divorce, which is likely not your intent. Reconsider how to frame Smith's mental state without implying that custody transfer was a real alternative she "should" have chosen—this makes the mitigating psychological argument sound flippant.

The "Death Would Be Easy" Argument

Your claim that death would be "quick, easy, and more economical" and that life imprisonment is a harsher punishment is an interesting rhetorical move, but it needs more development. Are you arguing that life in prison is a *better deterrent or more moral punishment* than execution? Or are you arguing she deserves to suffer more? Clarify your reasoning here, and consider addressing the counterargument: many people feel execution is *not* "easy" and that life imprisonment is not guaranteed to produce guilt or suffering. Engaging with this opposing view would make your argument more persuasive.

Religious Argument

The shift to a specifically Christian theological argument (citing the Ten Commandments, forgiveness, heaven, and hell) is a significant turn. This is a legitimate personal perspective, but in an academic argumentative essay, you should consider your audience. Not all readers share your religious framework, so if you want this argument to be persuasive to a broader audience, you might:
Explicitly frame this section as "From a Christian perspective..." to signal the shift in reasoning.
Consider pairing it with a secular argument (eg, restorative justice, redemption narratives, rehabilitation) so readers who don't share your faith tradition can still follow your logic.
Also, the placeholders  need to be filled in with actual Bible verses and properly cited (book, chapter, verse).

Structure and Paragraphing

Your essay would benefit from clearer topic sentences that map onto a thesis. Right now, paragraphs blend narrative (Smith's backstory), argument (why the sentence was just), and theological reflection without clear signposting. Consider an outline like:
Introduction with clear thesis
Smith's psychological/family history as mitigating factor
Why life imprisonment is an appropriate legal outcome
Counterargument (why some believe death penalty was warranted) and rebuttal
Broader reflection (theological or philosophical) on forgiveness/redemption
Conclusion

Style and Mechanics

There are several spelling errors to correct: "recieve" → receive; "prision" → prison. Also, watch for repetitive phrasing (eg, "Her boyfriend dumped her" appears twice in consecutive sentences). A careful proofreading pass will elevate the writing's professionalism.

Overall Suggestion

Your emotional and moral engagement with this topic is a strength—lean into it, but ground it more firmly in accurate facts, cited evidence, and a single clear thesis. Consider whether this is fundamentally a legal-ethical argument, a theological argument, or an argument about restorative justice, and structure the whole essay around that central claim.
