Feedback on "Lowering the Drinking Age" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely debatable and interesting policy question, and your thesis is clear: lowering the drinking age would reduce crime, encourage responsibility, and improve public health. You've also made an effort to organize your essay around these three claims. However, the essay's persuasive power is significantly weakened by unreliable sourcing, logical gaps, and repetitive reasoning. Below are the main areas to focus on for revision.

Source Credibility (Most Urgent Issue)

This is the most serious problem in the essay. Several of your citations are not legitimate academic sources:

"Rick Finley@rfinleygmail.vt.edu" appears to be a personal email address, not a citable source. Academic arguments require peer-reviewed research, government data, or reputable journalism—not personal correspondence with unclear credentials.
"Netscape.Young Drinkers Homepage" and "nttp.www.kfu.com/nsayer" (note the typo "nttp") are outdated or informal web sources with no clear authorship or credibility.
Government sources like the US Department of Health and Human Services and US Department of Justice are appropriate—lean more heavily on these, and cite them with page numbers and full titles/years so readers can verify them.

Action step: Replace informal sources with peer-reviewed studies, government reports, or data from organizations like the NIAAA, CDC, or NHTSA. A instructor or librarian can help you find current, credible research.

Logical Gaps and Unsupported Claims

Several key claims are asserted without evidence or clear reasoning:

You claim lowering the drinking age would reduce crime because youths won't "break the law" buying alcohol—but this is circular. If the law changes, then by definition fewer people violate *that* law, but this doesn't address whether *other* crimes (DUIs, assaults, vandalism) would decrease.
The claim that eighteen-year-olds will suddenly "act responsibly" once given back their rights needs support. What evidence suggests responsibility follows from legal permission? Consider addressing counterarguments: opponents would point to research on adolescent brain development and impulse control.
The comparison to Canada and international drinking cultures is promising but underdeveloped. You assert other countries have less alcoholism due to cultural norms, but you don't explain *why* this transfers to a U.S. context, nor do you cite comparative statistics.

Action step: For each major claim, ask "why would this be true?" and "what evidence supports this?" Anticipate and address the strongest counterarguments (eg, research showing MLDA 21 has reduced traffic fatalities).

Repetition and Structure

The essay repeats the same ideas multiple times, especially the point about eighteen-year-olds having adult responsibilities (military service, contracts) and the point about crime reduction. This padding weakens the argument rather than strengthening it.

Action step: Outline your essay after drafting. For each paragraph, identify its *one* main claim. If two paragraphs make the same point, merge them and add new evidence rather than restating the claim.

Paragraph Organization

Your three main arguments (crime, responsibility, health) are good organizing categories, but the paragraphs under "crime" and "responsibility" blur together, and evidence often doesn't clearly connect back to the topic sentence. For example, the discussion of reform programs feels disconnected from the crime argument until the final sentences.

Action step: Begin each paragraph with a clear topic sentence, follow with evidence, then explicitly explain how the evidence supports the claim before moving to the next point.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Watch apostrophe use: "eighteen-year old's" should be "eighteen-year-olds" (no apostrophe for simple plurals).
"Effect" vs. "affect": "Lowering drinking age...would effect the United States" should be "would *affect*."
"Amoung" should be "among."
Capitalization inconsistencies (eg, "on most college campuses" should begin with a capital letter).

Action step: Do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on grammar and mechanics after you've revised content.

The Conclusion

Overall Impression

Strengths

Practical thinking: You've considered real logistical solutions (partnering with local pools, using certified instructors, seasonal economics).

Signaling more clearly in a transition sentence that you're moving from illustration to argument.

Conclusion
Your final paragraph is too brief and general ("a simple one that could be remedied quickly and effectively") to serve as a satisfying close for such a serious topic. Consider:
Returning to your opening scenario with a "what if" reframing (what if this child *had* learned to swim?).

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a compelling and still-relevant topic: the limitations of rigid gender roles. Complete the Masters, Johnson, and Kolodny citation with their actual finding.
Provide the actual Friedan quotation and page/source citation.
Complete Warren Farrell's narrative and Ralph's quotation, with proper attribution.

Without this evidence, readers cannot evaluate whether your sources actually support your claims. This is the single most important revision to make.

Sharpen and Clarify Your Central Claim

Your thesis — that we should "abolish distinct gender identities" — is a strong claim, but the term "distinct gender identity" is defined loosely and shifts meaning throughout the essay. Sometimes you seem to mean *gender stereotypes* (fixed social expectations), and other times you seem to mean *gender roles* (behaviors assigned by tradition). These are related but distinct concepts. Consider explicitly defining your key term in the introduction and using it consistently. You might also soften "abolish" to something more precise, such as "we should stop treating gender traits as if they belong exclusively to one sex," since your evidence actually supports flexibility rather than elimination of gendered concepts altogether.

Strengthen the Logic of Your Body Paragraphs

Your single-parent paragraph is actually your strongest evidence, but it complicates your thesis in an interesting way: it shows that *both* traditionally masculine and feminine traits remain useful and necessary — just not gender-exclusive. This example doesn't really argue for abolishing gender identity; it argues for recognizing that all humans, regardless of sex, benefit from a full range of traits. Make this nuance explicit — it will make your argument more sophisticated and harder to refute.

Similarly, the Friedan and Farrell paragraphs would benefit from a clearer causal link between "psychological burden" and your recommended solution. Right now, the leap from "stereotypes caused unhappiness" to "therefore we should abolish gender identity" happens too quickly. Consider adding a sentence that explains *why* abolishing the categories (rather than simply loosening or expanding them) is the necessary solution.

Address Counterarguments

Currently, the essay presents only one side. Even a brief paragraph acknowledging why some might value traditional gender roles (eg, cultural identity, tradition, or arguments that some psychologists make about innate differences) — followed by your rebuttal — would strengthen your credibility and demonstrate critical engagement with the topic.

Style and Mechanics

Repetition: You use "Likewise" and "however" very frequently, sometimes multiple times in one paragraph. Vary your transitions.
Sentence-level errors: Several sentences begin with a lowercase letter after a period (eg, "if these traits were abolished..." and "it would be ridiculous..."). Proofread carefully for capitalization.
Word choice: In the single-parent paragraph, you list "dependent" as a masculine trait — this is likely a typo for "independent." Recheck word choices throughout for precision.
Wordy phrasing: Phrases like "in this instance" and "stated differently" can often be cut or replaced with more direct transitions.
Conclusion: Your conclusion currently summarizes rather than synthesizes. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement — what would a society without rigid gender identities look like, and why does that matter now?

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Replace all placeholder markers with complete, properly cited evidence.
Define "distinct gender identity" clearly and use the term consistently.
Clarify the logical connection between your evidence and your proposed solution (abolition vs. flexibility).
Add a brief counterargument and rebuttal.

Feedback on "Media, Society, and the Standards of Beauty"

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an important and still-relevant topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You've gathered striking statistics and you write with a genuine, persuasive voice. The essay's main weakness is not its ideas but its organization and use of evidence—both of which are very fixable. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Focus

Your introduction moves quickly from supermodels to a broad claim that "society and media shouldn't persuade us as to what true beauty is." This is a reasonable thesis, but it's stated almost in passing, buried in the middle of the first paragraph rather than positioned as the essay's guiding claim. Consider revising your introduction so that it builds toward one clear, specific thesis sentence—something like: "The media's promotion of an unrealistic body ideal has driven women toward disordered eating, dangerous cosmetic procedures, and diminished self-worth, and it is time we redefine beauty on our own terms." This gives readers a roadmap for what's coming.

Organization

Right now the essay moves through several related but distinct topics—models' measurements, eating disorders, plastic surgery, beauty products, and economic discrimination against obese women—without clear transitions signaling how they connect back to your thesis. Each of these could be its own well-developed paragraph, but currently some paragraphs (especially the one on disorders) try to cover too much ground at once, listing anorexia, bulimia, plastic surgery, liposuction, and bad beauty products all in a single paragraph. Consider breaking this into two or three paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence: one on eating disorders, one on cosmetic procedures, one on beauty products. This will let you develop each point with more depth rather than moving through them as a list.

Additionally, the paragraph on obese women and economic discrimination feels somewhat disconnected from the rest of the essay, which has focused on models and thinness. You might explicitly bridge this by noting that the same media culture that idealizes extreme thinness also punishes women who don't fit that mold economically and socially—this ties the paragraph back to your central argument about media-driven standards.

Evidence and Sources

This is the area needing the most attention. You cite many specific statistics (the average model's measurements, percentages of anorexia and bulimia cases, income disparities for obese women), which is a real strength—but none of these statistics are sourced. As an academic essay, you need to tell readers where these numbers come from (a study, an organization, a book). Without citations, readers may doubt the accuracy of your claims, and it also raises questions of academic integrity. Look up the original sources for each statistic and add in-text citations and a works cited page. This will significantly strengthen your credibility.

Use of Personal Voice

Your personal admissions—wanting to look like Cindy Crawford, knowing you'll never resemble Christie Brinkley—are effective and humanize the essay. However, be careful not to let the personal voice slide into casual or conversational asides, such as "(yeah right.)" and "I think not." These undercut the essay's argumentative tone. Academic argumentative writing can still be passionate, but aim for phrasing that maintains a more formal register, eg, "Yet the promise is often hollow" rather than "(yeah right.)"

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

There are several recurring errors worth addressing in revision:
Spelling: "Cambell" (Campbell), "adolecense" (adolescence), "enwnel" (enamel)
"there job" should be "their job"
"the average model is... weighs 1 10 pounds" appears to be a typo for "110 pounds"
Watch subject-verb agreement: "there are many disorders which alter these females' state of mind, health, and body" is a bit awkward—consider "many women develop disorders that alter..."
Some sentences are fragments used for rhetorical effect ("Disorders so severe that..."). This can work stylistically but should be used sparingly and intentionally, not as a substitute for a complete sentence in expository prose.

I'd recommend a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud, to catch these issues.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively returns to your central belief that beauty comes from within, but it becomes somewhat repetitive with restated questions ("Do we have to go to such extreme measures?"). Consider tightening this section to end on your strongest, most memorable point rather than a string of rhetorical questions.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen and reposition your thesis statement.
Break dense paragraphs into more focused ones with clear topic sentences.
Add citations for all statistics.
Revise casual asides for a more consistently academic tone.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

You have compelling material here—with structural revision and sourcing, this essay could be quite persuasive.

Overall Impression

Paragraph 4 introduces a serious real-world example (the murder incident), but it needs a citation/source and clearer transition explaining *why* this example matters to your argument.
Paragraph 5 raises the important question of responsibility but ends abruptly. This could be your strongest analytical paragraph—expand it.

Suggestion: After revising your thesis, create an outline:
Intro with clear thesis
Benefits of talk shows (with examples)
How talk shows manufacture drama (mechanics of the genre)
Risks/consequences (the murder example, developed with more detail and a citation)
Who bears responsibility—hosts, producers, or guests?
Conclusion that returns to your thesis and answers the debate you raised in your intro

Your essay relies heavily on general claims ("many people feel," "many faithful viewers feel") without citing specific shows, hosts, or sources. Since you reference a real tragic incident, this suggests you're capable of researching specifics—do more of this.

Which shows are you referring to? (Jerry Springer? Maury? Dr. Phil?)
Can you name the incident in paragraph 4 and cite where you learned about it?
Do you have any statistics on talk show viewership or studies on their psychological effects?

Concrete evidence will make your argument far more persuasive than general impressions.

Grammar and Mechanics

This essay needs careful proofreading. Common issues include:

Comma splices and fragment errors: eg, "Many people feel that talk shows, trashy or not. are for entertainment purposes only" — the period after "not" should be a comma.
Spelling errors: "audeinces" (audiences), "agressively" (aggressively), "thier" (their), "fueding" (feuding), "nieghbor" (neighbor), "thay" (they), "heath" (health).
Subject-verb agreement: "These differences in opinions leads" should be "lead."
Awkward phrasing: "The host dies deep into the minds" — likely meant "delves."

Suggestion: After revising content, do a dedicated proofreading pass—perhaps reading the essay aloud, which often catches missing words and awkward sentences.

Feedback on "The Legal Drinking Age" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely debatable topic and you clearly feel passionate about it—that passion is a strength. The paper needs revision in several areas: sharpening the thesis, strengthening logical connections between claims and evidence, addressing counterarguments more fully, and cleaning up grammar and word choice. Below are specific areas to focus on.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your thesis—that eighteen should be the legal drinking age—is stated clearly in the introduction. However, your conclusion undermines it by saying "Drinking should be prohibited to kids eighteen or older," which contradicts your entire argument. This is likely a typo, but it's a serious one: your conclusion is the last thing your reader remembers, so it must reinforce your thesis, not reverse it. Proofread your conclusion carefully to make sure it echoes your opening claim.

Additionally, the opening sentences about the Wild West, the Civil War, and the Declaration of Independence don't clearly connect to your argument. Are you claiming drinking has *always* been legal for eighteen-year-olds historically? If so, you need evidence. If this is meant as general background, consider cutting it, since it doesn't support your central claim and makes the introduction feel unfocused. A strong introduction should move efficiently toward your thesis.

Logical Development

Your strongest point is the military/voting rights comparison: if eighteen-year-olds are old enough to fight and die for their country, they should be considered adults in other respects too. This is a compelling emotional and logical appeal, but it needs to be *argued*, not just asserted. Ask yourself: what is the *connecting principle*? Is it that military service demonstrates maturity? That the government is inconsistent in how it defines adulthood? Make that reasoning explicit so readers can follow your logic rather than just feeling your frustration.

The diploma paragraph is the weakest section because the connection between a high school diploma and readiness to drink responsibly is unclear and asserted rather than argued. Why would having a diploma indicate someone can handle alcohol responsibly? This paragraph would benefit from either being cut or significantly developed with reasoning.

The crime/drinking-and-driving paragraph makes a claim—that legalizing drinking at eighteen would reduce crime—that needs *evidence*. Do you have statistics comparing DUI rates in states/countries with lower drinking ages? Right now, this reads as speculation ("Most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving") rather than a supported claim. Even one or two statistics or a comparison to another country (eg, many European nations have lower drinking ages) would strengthen this significantly.

Addressing Counterarguments

You do attempt to address a counterargument (the diploma issue), which shows good instinct—strong essays anticipate objections. However, your essay never addresses the *strongest* counterargument: that eighteen-to-twenty-year-olds have higher rates of alcohol-related accidents and risk-taking behavior, which is the primary reason the drinking age was raised to twenty-one in the 1980s. Engaging with this argument directly and explaining why you still believe eighteen is the right age would make your essay far more persuasive and show that you've researched the issue seriously.

Structure and Paragraphing

Each paragraph would benefit from a clear topic sentence stating its main claim, followed by evidence, and ending with a sentence linking back to your thesis. Right now, paragraphs sometimes drift (eg, the diploma paragraph shifts into a policy proposal that isn't fully connected to your main point).

Voice and Tone

You frequently address the reader directly ("You can say that...," "You can be condemned..."). This creates an inconsistent point of view. Decide whether you want a more formal, argumentative voice (avoiding "you" and speaking generally about "eighteen-year-olds" or "young adults") or a more conversational essay. For academic argument, the formal approach is usually stronger and more persuasive.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several errors to correct in your next draft:
"Every since" → "Ever since"
"Declaration of Independance" → "Independence"
"dieing" → "dying"
"too young" (not "two young")
"gonvernment" → "government"
"affects" → "effects" (when referring to the impact of alcohol)
Watch for comma splices and run-on sentences—several sentences string together independent clauses without proper punctuation or conjunctions.

Suggestions for Revision

Fix the contradictory conclusion.
Cut or clarify the historical opening.
Add real evidence/statistics for the crime and military arguments.
Directly address the strongest counterargument (safety statistics).
Choose a consistent point of view and revise "you" statements accordingly.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

With these revisions, your essay will have a much clearer, more persuasive argument. Good luck with your next draft!

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic—how early gender socialization affects adult identity and relationships—and you've done solid work gathering relevant sources (Pollitt, Ferrell, Friedan, Gray). However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas before the argument will be persuasive and clear. Below is feedback organized by priority.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your thesis is currently diffuse. You open with claims about early childhood gender identity, pivot to media criticism, then to adult regret narratives, then to Gray's communication theory. Each of these could be a paper on its own. Ask yourself: what is the *one* claim that ties all of this together? Based on your conclusion, it seems to be something like: *"Children should be taught that a flexible, evolving gender identity—rather than rigid stereotypes—leads to healthier adult lives."* State this explicitly and early, and then make sure every paragraph explicitly supports it rather than simply summarizing a source.

Revision tip: Write a one-sentence thesis, then write one sentence per paragraph explaining how it will support that thesis. If a paragraph doesn't clearly serve the thesis, cut or refocus it.

Organization and Structure

The essay moves chronologically through your sources (Pollitt → Ferrell/Friedan → Gray) rather than logically through an argument. This creates a "book report" feel rather than an analytical essay. Consider reorganizing by *idea* rather than by *source*:

Paragraph on the problem: children are taught rigid gender messages (use Pollitt here)
Paragraph on the consequences: adults suffer when they conform to these rigid roles (use Ferrell and Friedan together, since you rightly note their parallel)
Paragraph on the solution: flexible, mutually respectful gender roles (use Gray)
Conclusion that synthesizes and makes a call to action

This restructuring will also help you avoid the awkward one-line paragraphs (eg, "Pollit pints out that even the more enlightened Public Broadcast stations engage in male gender bias.") which should be integrated into surrounding paragraphs rather than left as isolated sentences.

Use and Integration of Evidence

You cite sources, but often you summarize *what they say* rather than *why it matters* to your argument. For example, after noting that Pollitt discusses PBS bias, explain: What does this reveal about the broader pattern you're describing? How does it connect to the Ferrell/Friedan sections that follow?

Additionally, your citations are inconsistent and sometimes unclear:
"(548)" and "(29)" appear with no clear signal of what page range or edition they refer to—make sure you're using a consistent citation style (likely MLA, given the subject matter) and introduce quotations with signal phrases ("As Masters and Johnson note...").
Several claims are asserted without full support—eg, "Pollit contends that the message is more straight forward for the boys" needs elaboration. *What* message? *How* is it more straightforward? Show your reader the evidence, don't just gesture at it.

Clarity and Logical Connections

Some transitions are unclear or overly casual for academic writing:
"Now that our children are off to a wonderful start they are ready for adulthood" — this ironic tone is fine conceptually but reads as flippant; sharpen it so the irony clearly serves your argument.
The Gray paragraph introduces a new concept (planetary metaphor) somewhat abruptly. Explain *how* this solves the problems raised earlier rather than just describing his book.

Grammar, Spelling, and Mechanics

This essay needs careful proofreading. Repeated errors include:
Misspelled proper names: Pollitt (not "Pollit"), Friedan (not "Fredian"/"Friedon"), Farrell (not "Ferrell"—verify correct spelling), Gray (not "Grey," if that's the actual author name—verify), Venus (not "Venue"), California (not "Califorian").
Typos: "relazed" (relaxed?), "ammend" (amend), "sponse" (spouse), "scarry" (scary), "metophor" (metaphor), "piont" (point), "roller" is fine but "rolds" (roles).
Homophone/word-choice errors: "there's" vs. "their's," "your" vs. "you're."
Punctuation issues: stray colons (eg, "Pollit contends:that"), inconsistent comma usage, run-on sentences.

Action step: After revising content, do a dedicated proofreading pass—reading the essay aloud often catches these errors more effectively than silent reading.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen and foreground your thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around ideas, not sources.
Deepen analysis of each piece of evidence—explain significance, not just content.
Proofread carefully for spelling, names, and punctuation.
Smooth transitions so the logical flow between paragraphs is explicit.

You have the makings of an interesting, well-researched essay—the next draft should focus on tightening the argument and polishing the prose so your ideas come through as clearly as your reading clearly went into it.

Feedback Report: Drug Legalization Essay

Overall Impression
Your essay tackles a genuinely complex and controversial topic, and you clearly have opinions and some real-world observations to draw on (eg, the anecdote about your high school's smoking policy). However, the essay needs significant revision in four key areas: thesis clarity, organization, logical consistency, and evidence/citation. Below are specific suggestions.

Sharpen and Maintain a Consistent Thesis
Your introduction promises an argument about legalizing marijuana specifically, but the essay repeatedly slides into discussing "drugs" broadly, then "marijuana among other drugs," without ever specifying what those "other drugs" are. This vagueness undermines your credibility, since legalizing marijuana and legalizing, say, heroin or crack cocaine involve very different arguments and risks (you even admit you don't feel strongly about legalizing crack cocaine, which contradicts your own thesis).

Revision suggestion: Decide clearly whether your argument is about marijuana only, or about drug policy in general, and state this in your thesis. Then use that exact scope consistently. If you want to discuss other drugs, do so explicitly and justify why they should be included.

Strengthen Paragraph Structure and Topic Sentences
Several paragraphs are only one or two sentences long (eg, "Most violent crimes stimulate from selling illicit drugs..." and the standalone line "The legalization of marijuana among other drugs, would keep some people out of the streets"). These read as unfinished ideas rather than developed arguments. Each paragraph should have a clear topic sentence, supporting evidence or reasoning, and a concluding thought that ties back to your thesis.

Revision suggestion: Combine related short paragraphs (eg, merge the crime/violence point with the jail overcrowding point, since they're related) and expand each with concrete evidence—statistics, studies, or specific examples—rather than general assertions.

Resolve Logical Inconsistencies
This is the most significant issue. Several of your claims undercut one another:
You argue legalization would decrease drug use, but earlier state that drug use is "here to stay" and people will "always" find ways to get high—these are contradictory.
You argue legalization would reduce crime and let police focus on other cases, but then suggest that if drugs were legal, people would simply "find other ways to do illegal things"—which suggests crime wouldn't actually decrease.
The paragraph on age limits wanders into a tangent about eliminating drinking and smoking age laws entirely, which is a separate (and much larger) argument that isn't tied back to your main thesis on marijuana.

Revision suggestion: Go through each paragraph and ask, "Does this directly support my thesis, or does it complicate/contradict it?" If a point cuts against your argument, either address the tension explicitly (strong essays often anticipate counterarguments) or cut it.

Improve Use of Evidence
You use one quotation (attributed to "Wibotsky"), but the citation is incomplete—no first name, no clear source title, and the quotation itself contains transcription errors ("use" instead of "us," "injuries" instead of "injures," missing words like "constitutional"). Additionally, this is your *only* piece of outside evidence in an essay that makes many empirical claims (about AIDS transmission, crime rates, jail overcrowding, economic impact).

Revision suggestion: 
Proofread all quotations carefully against the original source.
Use full, correctly formatted citations (author's full name, source, page number).
Add at least 2–3 more pieces of evidence—statistics on incarceration rates for drug offenses, studies on marijuana's health effects, or economic data from states that have already legalized marijuana (eg, Colorado, Washington). This will make your claims far more persuasive than assertions alone.

Address Grammar and Mechanics
There are frequent errors that distract from your argument:
Subject-verb agreement: "Most violent crimes stimulate from..." should be "stimulate from" → likely "originate from" or "result from."
Typos: "hann" (harm), "injuries" (injures).
Sentence fragments: "Issues such as permanent damage to the brain..." is not a complete sentence.
Comma splices and run-ons throughout.

Revision suggestion: Read your essay aloud slowly, or have a peer read it, to catch these errors. A grammar-checking tool can help, but careful re-reading is essential since some errors change your meaning (eg, "injuries us all" vs. "injures us all").

Strengthen the Conclusion
Your conclusion relies heavily on rhetorical questions rather than synthesizing your argument. While rhetorical questions can be effective, ending an argumentative essay primarily with questions leaves the reader without a clear final statement of your position.

Revision suggestion: End with a strong, declarative restatement of your thesis and perhaps one forward-looking call to action (eg, what specific policy change you'd like to see).

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Clarify whether your thesis is about marijuana specifically or drugs broadly—be consistent.
Resolve contradictions between paragraphs.
Develop underdeveloped paragraphs with more evidence and analysis.
Fix citation and quotation accuracy.
Proofread carefully for grammar and typos.

You have a genuine argument here with real conviction behind it—the revision work now is about making that argument airtight and consistent from start to finish.

Your essay tackles a real and relatable issue—the challenge of language barriers between professors and students—and your personal anecdote gives the argument a human dimension that pure abstraction would lack. However, the essay would benefit from tighter structure, more varied evidence, and significant proofreading. Below are specific areas for revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—that professors should be required to demonstrate English proficiency before teaching—doesn't appear until the end of your second paragraph, after a lengthy anecdote and general observations about "difficulties in classes." Consider stating your claim earlier, perhaps in the first paragraph, so readers know from the outset what argument the anecdote is building toward. Right now, the anecdote functions as a story rather than as evidence explicitly in service of a stated claim.

Additionally, the anecdote itself, while effective, is quite long (nearly two full paragraphs). Consider condensing it to the most essential details—the professor's inability to explain concepts, the failed office-hours meeting, and the lack of alternative sections—so you can spend more time developing your analytical argument.

Evidence and Support

This is the area needing the most attention. Your entire argument rests on a single, secondhand anecdote about a friend. While personal narrative can be a powerful entry point, an academic argumentative essay needs additional support:

Data or statistics: How many international faculty teach at USC? Are there documented complaints or studies about ITA (International Teaching Assistant) language proficiency?
Institutional policy: You mention that TAs must pass a language-proficiency exam—this is a great point. Can you cite the actual USC policy or program name? This would strengthen your credibility and give the Board of Trustees comparison more force.
Additional perspectives: Consider mentioning research on ITA programs at other universities, or quoting a student affairs representative, to show this is a broader, documented issue rather than one bad experience.

Repetition

Several ideas are repeated across paragraphs without adding new insight—particularly the tuition/consumer argument ("students pay, so they deserve good teaching") appears in paragraph 3 and again implicitly in paragraph 4 and the conclusion. Consolidate this reasoning into one strong paragraph rather than restating it. Similarly, the point about professors having "expertise" is made twice with similar phrasing ("expert," "trained expert," "authority"). Trim redundant sentences and let each paragraph advance a *new* piece of the argument.

Counterargument

You raise the TA-substitution solution but dismiss it in only two sentences. This is a missed opportunity. Strengthening your engagement with this counterargument—explaining more specifically *why* TAs cannot substitute for professors in upper-level courses (eg, depth of expertise, ability to answer novel questions, credibility for research-based instruction)—would make your rebuttal more persuasive and show you've seriously considered the opposing view rather than brushing it aside.

Clarity and Logical Flow

Some sentences overstate the logic. For example, the line "A professor who speaks a different language other than his students can't teach them unless he has a sufficient amount of knowledge of the students' language" is a bit circular and could be tightened. Similarly, the comparison "When a person buys an item from a store, he pays and receives the item he paid for" is a simple analogy but risks reducing education to a transactional relationship. You might briefly acknowledge that education isn't quite like buying a product, then argue why the *comparison still holds* for the purposes of accountability—this preempts a reader's objection.

Mechanics and Proofreading

The essay contains numerous typographical and formatting errors that distract from your argument: stray periods and spacing issues (eg, "went to.  USC," "began-to have difficulty," "who-,@ spoke English," "large 1, university"), comma splices, and inconsistent capitalization. These need careful proofreading—read the essay aloud or use a grammar-checking tool to catch these before your final submission. Precision in language is especially important in an essay *about* the importance of clear communication.

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates the thesis but doesn't add a new dimension—consider ending with a forward-looking statement (eg, what happens if the university doesn't act, or a call to specific action for the Board of Trustees) to give the essay a stronger sense of closure and urgency.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
State your thesis earlier and more concisely.
Trim the anecdote; add outside evidence (policy, statistics, other institutions).
Eliminate repetitive reasoning across paragraphs.
Deepen your engagement with the TA counterargument.
Proofread carefully for typos and formatting errors.

With these revisions, your essay's compelling premise will be matched by a more rigorous and polished argument.

Feedback Report: "Business is Business"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, emotionally resonant topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. The essay raises an important question about loyalty, civic identity, and corporate greed in professional sports. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of argumentative clarity, organization, and mechanics before it will be persuasive to an academic audience.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim is buried and inconsistently stated. You open with several rhetorical questions ("Is business just business or do fans, tradition, and loyalty count?") rather than a clear thesis statement. By the essay's end, you've clearly landed on a position—Modell is greedy and betrayed Cleveland—but this argument should be stated explicitly and early, ideally at the end of your introduction. Consider a thesis like: "Despite claims that professional sports are purely a business, Art Modell's relocation of the Browns represents a betrayal of civic loyalty that no financial justification can excuse."

Organization

The essay's structure is its biggest weakness. You move between multiple team relocations (Colts, Oilers, Raiders, Rams) without clearly explaining why these examples matter to your Browns argument. This creates confusion rather than context. I'd recommend:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly, perhaps with one brief contextual reference to other relocations.
Background paragraph: Cleveland's investment in itself (Gateway Project, Rock & Roll Hall of Fame) to establish stakes.
Counterargument paragraph: Present the opposing view fully (business necessity, Cleveland's own choices) before rebutting it—right now these counterarguments appear as a single rushed sentence in paragraph four.
Main argument: Modell's specific betrayals (broken promises, financial details, refusal to negotiate).
Conclusion: Return to your thesis and its broader implications.

Evidence and Support

You provide compelling factual details (the $200 million stadium offer, the 70% sin tax vote, attendance figures), which is good. However:

Cite your sources. Where did these statistics come from? Academic writing requires you to attribute facts, quotes, and figures to specific sources (news articles, press releases, etc).
The Bob Costas quote needs a citation—when and where did he say this?
Verify your facts. The Houston Oilers became the Tennessee Titans, not the "Tennessee Cherokees"—this is a significant factual error that will undermine your credibility with informed readers. Double-check all team names, dates, and figures before submitting.

Counterarguments

You mention opposing views ("Some try to blame the city of Cleveland...") but dismiss them too quickly, in a single sentence, without genuine engagement. Strengthen your essay by giving these counterarguments a full paragraph and then systematically rebutting them with evidence. This will make your ultimate position more persuasive, not less.

Style and Tone

Your voice is passionate, but some phrasing undermines your credibility:
"take it like a man" and "gave Cleveland the one finger salute" are too casual/colloquial for academic argumentation.
Calling Modell "a liar" and stating he's "greedy, pure and simple" are assertions that need more measured, evidence-based framing (eg, "Modell's own words suggest a contradiction between his stated values and his actions").

Mechanics (Priority for Revision)

This essay has numerous errors that need correction:

Its vs. it's: "it's tax subsidized teams" should be "its." Review this rule carefully.
Word confusion: "abdication" should likely be "abandonment"; "rapid" should be "rabid" (describing fans); "novel" is an odd word choice for "new."
Spelling: "Rains" should be "Rams"; "inwhich" should be two words, "in which."
Punctuation: Comma splices appear throughout (eg, "The Rock'n Roll Hall of Fame opened this year, and now they are building..."). Quotation marks are misplaced ("The Renaissance of Cleveland."—period should be inside the closing quotation mark, and the comma before it should be outside).
Team name error: "Tennessee Cherokees" should be "Tennessee Titans" (fact-check this).

I strongly recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool, then doing a careful proofreading pass focused solely on mechanics.

Next Steps

Draft a clear, one-sentence thesis.
Reorganize using the structure suggested above.
Add citations for all factual claims and quotes.
Expand your counterargument section significantly.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors.
Verify all factual details (team names, dates, figures).

You have real potential here—an interesting topic and genuine evidence. With revision focused on structure, sourcing, and precision, this essay could be quite strong.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a genuinely interesting and relevant question: how societal expectations shape—and sometimes derail—personal choices about career and family. You clearly have a thoughtful perspective on the topic, and the comparison between Friedan's era and Farrell's "Ralph" is a promising structural device. However, the essay currently reads more like an extended reflection than a tightly argued piece. Below are specific areas to strengthen for revision.

Thesis and Central Claim

Your introduction gestures toward an argument—that people suffer when they let others' expectations dictate their life choices—but this claim doesn't crystallize into a clear, arguable thesis statement. By the final paragraph, you conclude that "there has to be a middle road," which is a reasonable point, but it isn't clearly set up as the essay's destination from the start. Revise your opening paragraph so that it states explicitly what you will argue: for example, that societal expectations (whether feminist or traditional) create false binaries that trap both men and women, and that recognizing this trap is the first step toward a more authentic "middle path." Make sure every subsequent paragraph visibly advances this claim.

Missing Citations

There are two placeholders marked with  where quotations from Friedan and Farrell should appear. These are essential to your argument—you're building your case on these sources, so the actual quoted language matters. Please locate and insert the specific quotations, with proper page citations, before submission. Right now, the essay's evidentiary foundation is incomplete.

Development of the Ralph/Friedan Comparison

The parallel between Ralph and Friedan's suburban housewives is your strongest analytical move, but it needs more explicit unpacking. Right now you assert "the similarity is unmistakable" without fully explaining *why*. Consider adding a paragraph that directly compares the mechanism of entrapment in both cases: Is it the same kind of pressure (economic necessity, gendered role expectations, fear of social disapproval)? Or are the pressures on Ralph and on Friedan's housewives actually different in kind, even if the outcome (regret, lost selfhood) is similar? Drawing this distinction would sharpen your analysis considerably.

Logical Gaps and Unsupported Generalizations

Several claims are asserted rather than argued:
"Of course, most men helped around the house" — this is presented as fact but isn't substantiated, and it also seems to undercut your point about women's overburdened lives. Clarify or remove.
"Today a woman is almost required to work outside the home" — this is a significant economic claim. Consider citing a source or softening it to "many women feel."
The claim that Ralph "would have had... more love and respect from his family in the long run" is speculative. Since Ralph's story is nonfictional (per Farrell's account), check whether the text itself supports this counterfactual, or flag it clearly as your own speculation.

Strengthening these moments with evidence—or explicitly signaling where you are speculating—will make your argument more credible.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph unity. Several paragraphs shift between describing Friedan's era, describing Ralph's story, and offering your own commentary within the same paragraph, which can disorient the reader. Consider organizing by function: one section establishing the historical/textual background (Friedan and Farrell), one section analyzing the shared pattern of entrapment, one section addressing counterarguments or complications (eg, economic necessity), and a final section proposing your "middle road." Clear topic sentences at the start of each paragraph will help signal these shifts.

The "Middle Road" Conclusion

Your ending—"It's up to each man or woman to search his or her own heart"—is a satisfying emotional note but a somewhat vague intellectual conclusion. Since much of your essay has argued that individual choice is constrained by social pressure, simply telling readers to follow their hearts doesn't fully resolve that tension. Consider addressing directly: what would it take, socially or structurally, to make the "middle road" more viable? You gesture at this with the mention of remote work, but this idea deserves more development, perhaps as a body paragraph exploring practical/systemic changes, not just an aside.

Style and Mechanics

Watch subject-verb agreement and typos ("erected of them," "reouired," "tine-in," "a. corporate cog-in-the-wheel"). A careful proofreading pass is needed.
Some sentences are quite long and loosely joined by commas; consider breaking these into shorter units for clarity.
Avoid inserting personal asides ("It seems to me," "Men of my acquaintance") without signaling whether these function as evidence or personal reflection—academic essays should distinguish claims supported by scholarship from your own observations.

Revision Priorities

Insert missing quotations and citations.
Write a clear, specific thesis statement.
Deepen the comparative analysis between Friedan and Farrell's texts.
Substantiate or qualify sweeping claims.
Reorganize paragraphs around clear topics.
Expand the conclusion to address systemic/practical solutions, not just individual choice.

With these revisions, this essay could move from an engaging personal reflection to a well-supported academic argument. You have real insight into this topic—now it needs the scaffolding of evidence and structure to fully persuade your reader.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an ambitious and genuinely interesting question: how should society balance the celebration of sex differences with fairness between the sexes? This is a topic with real intellectual potential, but the essay currently struggles to deliver a coherent argument because the examples don't clearly work together, the central claim is fuzzy, and there are significant issues with clarity, logic, and mechanics. Below are the main areas to focus on in revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Central Argument

Your introduction promises an argument about "enjoying differences" while maintaining "fairness," but it's not clear what specific claim you're making. Are you arguing that:
Legal equality and physical/social difference can coexist without contradiction?
Sexualized entertainment (strip clubs, Hooters) is not sexist?
Sports funding should reflect revenue rather than equal treatment?

These are three very different arguments, and your essay drifts between them without showing how they connect. Revise your thesis into a single, specific claim, and make sure every paragraph supports *that* claim rather than a loosely related idea.

Address the Logical Gaps in Your Examples

Several of your examples need stronger reasoning:

Sports funding: You call the 50/50 split "communism" and suggest revenue alone should determine funding — but you don't grapple with the deeper issue: female sports have historically received less investment and exposure, which affects revenue in a circular way (Title IX exists precisely because of this). Acknowledging this counterargument would strengthen your case rather than weaken it.

Strip clubs and Hooters: You assert there is "nothing nasty" about these industries, but you don't respond to the actual arguments critics make (objectification, workplace harassment, economic exploitation). A stronger essay would name the counterargument and directly refute it, not simply assert your view.

Barkley vs. Madonna: This comparison is thought-provoking, but the claim that Barkley "brings an aura of violence" while Madonna brings "love and affection" feels asserted rather than argued. Also, be cautious: comparing an athletic career to displays of nudity as equivalent "talents" needs more careful justification, since readers will likely push back here.

Male/female athletic competition: This section, while interesting, doesn't clearly connect to your argument about "enjoying differences." Consider either cutting it or explicitly tying it back to your thesis (eg, separate leagues *are* the fair way to honor difference).

Strengthen Your Counterarguments

A persuasive essay must seriously engage opposing views, not just dismiss them. For instance, the Hooters lawsuits are dismissed in one sentence ("This is not discrimination") without addressing the actual legal or ethical arguments involved. Try the following structure for each example: state the opposing view fairly, then explain specifically why you disagree.

Reconsider Tone and Register

Phrases like "warring cologne," "human body part," and "open one called buttocks for women" undercut the essay's credibility. Academic argumentative writing benefits from a more measured, serious tone — even when discussing a provocative topic. You can still be direct and even a little playful, but avoid language that reads as flippant, since it may make readers dismiss your more serious points.

Editing and Mechanics

The essay has frequent errors that distract from your argument and need careful proofreading:
Homophone errors: "there" vs. "their," "were" vs. "where," "hear" vs. "here," "resent" vs. "recent," "scene" vs. "seen," "sells" vs. "sales."
Verb/spelling errors: "underlining" should be "underlying," "trask" should likely be "trash," "returmed" should be "returned," "Cline" should be "Klein."
Run-on and comma-splice sentences: Several sentences string together independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, "While men and women are naturally different, the laws of the land must treat everyone equally" is fine, but many others need revision).

I strongly recommend reading the essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool, before your next draft.

Suggested Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis that captures your specific claim.
Choose your two or three strongest examples (I'd suggest sports funding and Hooters) and cut or condense the rest.
For each remaining example, add a paragraph addressing the strongest counterargument.
Do a full proofreading pass focused on homophones and sentence boundaries.
Revisit the conclusion — right now it repeats slogans ("let us enjoy...") rather than synthesizing your argument. Try ending with a specific claim about what should change in policy or attitude.

This essay has an interesting core idea. With a tighter thesis, more serious engagement with counterarguments, and careful editing, it will be significantly more persuasive.

Feedback on Welfare Reform Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely complex policy debate and shows real effort to synthesize opposing viewpoints into a compromise position. This is an ambitious and worthwhile argumentative strategy. However, the essay currently reads more as a summary of sources than an argument you are making and defending. The revision should focus on strengthening your own analytical voice and creating a tighter, more logical structure.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis promises four specific components: time limits, mandatory training, reduced funding, and eliminating incentives for larger families. This is a clear, specific, and debatable claim—good work. However, the essay does not treat these four elements evenly. Time limits and training programs receive substantial development, funding cuts get moderate treatment, but the fourth element (ending incentives for having more children) is only mentioned in passing within the AFDC section and never developed as its own point. Revise by giving each of the four elements its own clearly signaled section, ideally with a topic sentence that states what you will argue and why it matters, rather than opening paragraphs with a source's claim.

Use of Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Currently, many paragraphs are essentially strings of quotations with minimal connective analysis. For example, the paragraph on training programs moves from one quotation to another (Economist, Whitman, Economist again) without you explaining *how* these pieces of evidence build toward your claim. Ask yourself after every quotation: "So what? Why does this support my argument specifically?" Add a sentence of analysis after each piece of evidence rather than letting the quotation speak for itself.

Additionally, some evidence is used uncritically. The Princess Rowell anecdote, for instance, actually complicates your argument—she is *still* on welfare after entering a program, and lost benefits for refusing to look for work. Is this a success story or a cautionary tale? You should acknowledge this ambiguity rather than presenting it simply as proof that training programs work. Anticipating and addressing this kind of complexity will make your argument more persuasive, not less.

Logic and Argumentation

Several claims are asserted without being argued. For example: "If these time limits were enforced there would [be] no chance for recipients to become dependent on the welfare system." This is a strong causal claim that needs support—why would a time limit alone prevent dependency? What happens to people who hit the limit without a job? Your essay would be stronger if you directly addressed this kind of objection, since it is the most common criticism of time limits (ie, that they cut off aid without guaranteeing self-sufficiency).

More broadly, your essay states in the introduction that combining both parties' ideas will "solve" the welfare issue, but it never really argues why this particular combination is superior to either party's plan alone, or to other alternatives. Since your core argument is about *synthesis*, you need at least one paragraph explicitly explaining why blending these four specific elements addresses the weaknesses of each individual party's plan. Right now, the essay reads as "Democrats say X, Republicans say Y, here's evidence for both"—but not clearly "therefore Z is the best solution because it fixes the flaws in X and Y."

Counterargument

The essay would be significantly stronger with even one paragraph raising a serious objection to your compromise—for instance, critics who argue that cutting AFDC harms children regardless of parental behavior, or that time limits punish people during economic downturns when jobs aren't available. Address this counterargument directly. This will demonstrate that you've thought through the complexity of the issue rather than simply selecting convenient facts.

Style and Mechanics

There are numerous citation and formatting errors that need careful proofreading:
"Silverstein" is misspelled as "Sliverstein" in multiple places
Quotation marks are frequently rendered incorrectly (using commas or apostrophes instead of proper quotation marks, eg, "199911," "systems," ",good-bye,")
Punctuation with quotations needs to be standardized (periods/commas inside quotation marks, per MLA style)
Some sentences have grammatical errors: "would no chance" should be "would be no chance"

I recommend a dedicated proofreading pass focused solely on quotation formatting and citation accuracy, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch these issues.

Revision Priorities

Develop the fourth thesis component (ending the incentive for more children) into its own full section.
Add analytical sentences after quotations explaining their significance to your argument.
Include at least one counterargument-and-response paragraph.
Strengthen the conclusion by returning explicitly to how your four-part plan improves on each party's individual proposal.
Proofread carefully for quotation marks, spelling, and punctuation.

With these revisions, this essay can move from summarizing the debate to making a genuinely persuasive case for your compromise position.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay, which tackles a topic you clearly feel strongly about. Your personal investment in the issue comes through clearly, and this passion is an asset. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas to make your argument more persuasive and academically rigorous. Below is feedback organized by area of concern.

Thesis and Focus

Your central claim is unclear and shifts throughout the essay. You begin by arguing for overnight visitation rights, but by the conclusion you're arguing against having *any* curfew at all. These are related but distinct arguments requiring different kinds of support. Decide which claim you want to defend—or explicitly argue for both if you can connect them clearly—and state this thesis directly in your introduction. Right now, a reader isn't sure what you're asking the university to change until well into the essay.

Organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer structure. Consider organizing your body paragraphs around distinct reasons, such as:
Overnight visitation already happens informally, so policy should reflect reality
Curfews infringe on students' developing autonomy as adults
Curfews create safety risks rather than preventing them
Family circumstances (like your cousin's visit) create legitimate need for exceptions

Currently, related ideas are scattered across different paragraphs (the safety argument appears late but could strengthen an earlier point), and the anecdotes about your male classmate and your cousin could be combined or more clearly connected to specific claims.

Evidence and Reasoning

This is the area needing the most development. Your argument relies almost entirely on personal anecdotes (your friend at Bates House, your late-night study session, your cousin's visit). While these are effective for illustrating your point, they aren't sufficient to persuade a skeptical reader or an administrator who must weigh policy for an entire student body. Consider:

Addressing counterarguments: Why might the university have instituted curfews? Safety concerns? Liability issues? Distinguishing your dorm from truly co-ed housing? Address these directly rather than dismissing them implicitly.
The safety argument is currently self-contradictory. You argue that walking at night is dangerous, therefore the curfew should be removed—but a reader will likely respond that this is an argument *for* curfews, not against them. Clarify your reasoning here: perhaps you mean that forcing people to leave at a fixed hour, rather than allowing them to leave when convenient, creates unnecessary risk. Make this logical connection explicit.
The alcohol reference also needs clearer connection to your argument. Are you saying curfews force intoxicated people into risky situations? This point needs more development to land effectively.

Consistency

You state "I'm not writing this to advocate alcohol, sex, or anything else," but the essay's examples (overnight guests, sneaking around) inevitably raise these issues for readers. Rather than disclaiming this, you might address it more directly: explain why overnight visitation doesn't need to be about those things, or explain why the university's assumption that it does is misguided.

Tone and Audience

Consider who you're trying to persuade—likely campus administrators. Some phrases ("We deserve a bit of freedom," "this should be one thing that does") read as more informal or personal than persuasive writing to decision-makers typically requires. You might strengthen your ethos by acknowledging legitimate institutional concerns while still advocating for your position.

Mechanics and Proofreading

The essay needs careful proofreading. Errors that stood out include:
"he" for "be" (a typo, easily fixed)
Missing or misplaced periods and capitalization (eg, "In," "Is," "It")
Inconsistent capitalization of "USC"
Comma splices and run-on sentences in several places

I recommend reading your draft aloud slowly, which often catches these errors more effectively than silent reading.

Suggested Revision Plan

Write a clear, one-sentence thesis stating your specific claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each addresses one clear reason supporting that thesis.
Add at least one paragraph addressing the university's likely counterarguments.
Strengthen the connection between your safety argument and your conclusion.
Proofread carefully for grammar and punctuation.

Your topic is compelling, and your personal examples show real problems with the current policy. With a sharper thesis and stronger logical connections between your evidence and claims, this essay could be considerably more persuasive. I encourage you to revise with these suggestions in mind and feel free to visit office hours if you'd like to discuss further.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Recognizing and Rewarding Teachers"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely important topic, and your passion for teachers—especially your favorite high school teacher—comes through clearly. The personal anecdotes about staying after school and collecting elephant pins add warmth and authenticity. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of related reflections than a tightly organized argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen for your revision.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—that students, parents, and schools should recognize and reward teachers—is stated early, but the essay doesn't consistently build toward proving it. Instead, it moves through several related but distinct claims: teachers deserve recognition, teachers deserve better pay, teachers deserve respect comparable to doctors, and specific groups (students, parents, schools) bear responsibility for rewarding teachers.

Revision suggestion: Choose 2-3 main supporting arguments and dedicate a clear paragraph to each. For example:
Teachers deserve recognition because of their emotional/mentoring impact
Teachers deserve recognition because of the difficulty of their work
Specific parties (students, parents, schools) have specific obligations to provide this recognition

Right now, paragraphs sometimes introduce a topic (like pay) and then pivot without fully developing it before moving to the next idea.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Much of your support relies on personal anecdote and general claims ("I've heard that..." "I've been told that..."). While personal experience is valuable, academic arguments are strengthened by:

Concrete data: What is the actual teacher turnover rate? What is average teacher pay compared to other professions requiring similar education?
Named sources: Instead of "I've heard" or "I've been told," cite where this information comes from—an article, a study, an interview.
Specific incidents: Your "elephant pin" story is charming, but explain more precisely how this action affected your teacher's morale or motivation. Show, don't just tell.

Address Logical Gaps

Several claims need more support or qualification:

"High school teachers deserve the most recognition" — This is a strong claim that needs justification. Why more than elementary or middle school teachers? You explain that high schoolers are "harder to deal with," but this deserves more development or should be softened if you can't fully support it.
The doctor comparison — This is a compelling analogy, but you assert similarities without naming them. What specifically do teachers and doctors share? Years of training? High-stakes decision-making? Direct impact on life outcomes? Spell this out.
"If there aren't good teachers in schools, then there aren't good students" — This is a significant claim. Consider softening ("good teachers contribute to good outcomes") or providing evidence.

Consider Counterarguments

Your essay would be much stronger if you acknowledged opposing viewpoints. For instance:
Why do some people believe teachers already have manageable workloads (summers off, set hours)?
Are there practical or budgetary reasons why raising teacher pay is difficult?

Engaging with these ideas—even briefly—would show a more sophisticated understanding of the debate and strengthen your credibility.

Polish Language and Mechanics

There are several areas needing editing attention:

Sentence fragments: "A material gift or a kind word of expression" should be integrated into the previous sentence.
Typos/unclear phrases: "They too deserve their flowers while they live just as many moms ask of their children" is a lovely image but needs clarification for readers unfamiliar with this expression.
Redundancy: Several ideas repeat (the point about students teasing "teacher's pets" could be trimmed; the "kind word vs. material gift" comparison appears more than once).
Missing content: The bracketed  suggests missing text—make sure to fill this in before submission.
Pronoun clarity: "It teachers that have helped made these figures" appears to be a typo—likely meant "It's teachers who have helped make these figures."

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion has a nice rhetorical question ("When are teachers going to be recognized more?"), but the answer feels vague ("When everyone realizes..."). Consider ending with a more concrete call to action—what should the reader *do* differently after reading this essay? A specific, actionable takeaway will leave a stronger final impression.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Reorganize around 2-3 clear supporting arguments
Add concrete evidence (statistics, sources) alongside personal anecdotes
Develop the doctor analogy with specific points of comparison
Address a counterargument to strengthen your credibility
Proofread carefully for fragments, typos, and redundancy
Sharpen your conclusion with a specific call to action

Your voice and conviction are the essay's greatest strengths—now it's time to give that passion a more disciplined structure to make your argument as persuasive as your feelings clearly are.

Feedback on "Violence on Television" Essay

Thank you for tackling this timely and complex topic. Television violence and its regulation remain widely debated, and you've gathered some useful sources. However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of facts than an argumentative essay. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen your argument.

Establish a Clear Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Right now, your essay presents information about TV violence, the V-chip, and Congressional proposals, but it never clearly states *what you are arguing*. Your final paragraph gestures toward a position—that violence "adds texture" to shows and that children will inevitably encounter violence regardless of censorship—but this claim appears too late and isn't set up as your thesis.

Revise your introduction to state a specific, debatable claim. For example: "While concerns about television violence are legitimate, government censorship measures like the V-chip and content-based bans are ineffective solutions that infringe on creative freedom without addressing the root causes of childhood aggression." A clear thesis like this will help you organize every paragraph around supporting evidence rather than general information.

Improve Paragraph Unity and Logical Flow

Each paragraph currently functions as a separate mini-report rather than a step in a developing argument:

Paragraph 1 introduces statistics on violence.
Paragraph 2 describes the V-chip and Senate Committee.
Paragraph 3 lists proposals and the NAB's objection.
Paragraph 4 shifts to defending violent content for adult viewers.
Paragraph 5 discusses cartoon violence.
Paragraph 6 offers a conclusion that contradicts some earlier framing.

Consider reorganizing around your argument's logic: (1) the scope of the problem, (2) the regulatory responses and their flaws, (3) counterarguments about creative/artistic necessity, (4) your response to counterarguments, (5) conclusion. This will help readers see *why* each fact matters to your claim.

Clarify Attribution and Strengthen Source Use

You frequently use vague phrases like "some people" or "many children" without specifying who. Be more precise:
"Some people, such as the Senate Commerce Committee, are not satisfied with it" — the Senate Commerce Committee is not "some people"; it's a specific institutional actor. Name it directly.
"Many children watch violent tv shows because the action thrills them and can sometimes cause the child to become violent" — this is an empirical claim that needs a citation. Do you have evidence connecting viewing to actual behavioral change, or is this your inference? If the latter, reframe it as an interpretation rather than a stated fact.

Additionally, when you quote the NAB's characterization of the Senate's proposals as building "an unprecedented federal censorship bureaucracy," you don't analyze or respond to this claim. Since this quote closely aligns with the position you seem to hold, use it more deliberately—introduce it, explain its significance, and connect it to your thesis.

Address Counterarguments More Substantively

Your strongest paragraph is the fourth one, where you begin to argue that violent content serves narrative purposes. However, this argument needs development:
Why *must* a murder be shown for a plot to work? Give examples or cite media scholars who address artistic necessity.
Your rhetorical question ("how many parents actually watch television with their teenager?") raises an important point about parental responsibility versus government regulation—this could become a key pillar of your argument. Develop it rather than leaving it as a single sentence.

Fix Factual and Mechanical Errors

"the 1995-1995 season" is likely a typo (probably 1994-1995 or 1995-1996). Verify and correct.
"Bugs Bunny" and "Elmer Fudd" cartoons from earlier decades being grouped with "Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles" and "Spider-Man" in a 1995 study seems questionable—double-check this source detail for accuracy.
Check quotation punctuation: "sinister combat violence' pervades" is missing an opening quotation mark.
Review subject-verb agreement and sentence-level clarity throughout (eg, "they will have to deal with it, rationally or not" needs a clearer antecedent).

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your final paragraph introduces a new, potentially compelling claim—that violence serves a narrative "texture" function and that children will inevitably confront violence regardless of protective measures—but this arrives without groundwork. A strong conclusion should *synthesize* your argument, not introduce fresh claims. Consider moving this idea earlier (perhaps into your counterargument section) and using your conclusion to restate your thesis in light of the evidence you've presented, then suggest what your argument implies for policy or parental responsibility.

Summary

Your essay has good raw material—relevant statistics, an awareness of competing viewpoints, and pertinent sources—but needs a clearer argumentative spine. Focus your revision on (1) crafting a specific thesis, (2) reorganizing paragraphs to build a logical case, (3) engaging more critically with your sources, and (4) polishing mechanical errors. I'd be happy to discuss these revisions further in office hours.

Feedback on "The Case for Government Arts Funding"

Overall Impression

You clearly have passion for this topic, and that enthusiasm comes through in your writing. You've assembled a good range of angles—anthropological, historical, social, economic, and therapeutic. However, the essay currently reads more like a list of reasons why art is valuable than a persuasive argument for why *government* specifically should fund it. Sharpening this distinction, along with tightening your evidence and structure, will substantially strengthen your case.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your thesis sentence ("there are some areas...which should keep, if not receive more, support from government agencies") is grammatically awkward and doesn't give readers a clear preview of your reasoning. Consider revising to something like: "Government funding for the arts deserves protection—and even expansion—because the arts provide cultural, social, economic, and psychological benefits that private funding alone cannot guarantee." This kind of thesis signals your structure and, importantly, addresses *why government* must be the funder, not just why art matters.

This is the essay's biggest gap: many of your points (friendship among artists, stress relief, personal enjoyment) explain why art is good, but not why *taxpayers* should fund it rather than private patrons, nonprofits, or the market. Strengthen the connection explicitly in each section—eg, "because these benefits serve the public good broadly, they justify public investment," etc

Organization

Your paragraphs are organized by theme (anthropology, history, community, economics, psychology, prevention), which is a reasonable structure. However:

The paragraph on Louis XIV and Napoleon is a double-edged example. Both were autocratic rulers using art as propaganda for personal and imperial glory—this could easily be read as an argument *against* government-sponsored art (state art as vanity project), so you need to explicitly redirect this example toward a democratic public good, or replace it with an example more clearly relevant to modern government funding (eg, public arts programs in schools).
The paragraph distinguishing art from pornography feels like a tangent. It raises a serious and complicated question (who decides what counts as "art" worth funding?) but resolves it in a single sentence. Either develop this into a fuller discussion of funding criteria, or cut it—as it stands, it risks derailing your argument rather than reinforcing it.
Consider adding a paragraph that anticipates and responds to counterarguments (eg, "Isn't this a waste of taxpayer money when there are more urgent needs?" or "Shouldn't private philanthropy carry this burden instead?"). Addressing this directly will make your argument much more persuasive to skeptical readers.

Evidence and Support

This is an area needing significant attention. Several claims are stated as established fact without citation:
"As psychologists and pschosanalysts have told us for years..."
"As researchers have found..."
"Continual research has been done linking..."

These vague appeals to unnamed authorities weaken your credibility. For a persuasive essay, you need specific studies, statistics, or named experts (eg, a specific study on prison arts programs and recidivism rates, or economic data on museum tourism revenue). Even one or two well-chosen statistics would strengthen your argument considerably more than several unsupported generalizations.

Your personal anecdote about painting as a "means of escape" is a nice touch of authenticity, but since this is an argumentative essay, keep it brief and pair it with more objective evidence rather than relying on it as a stand-alone proof point.

Style and Clarity

The opening image (funding cuts as a "storm cloud") is a cliché; consider a more original or concrete opening—perhaps a striking statistic about a recent funding cut, or a specific program at risk.
Watch transitional phrases that don't do real logical work (eg, "Following from the idea of sharing" doesn't clearly connect to the previous paragraph's content).
There are several grammar and spelling issues to proofread carefully: "pschosanalysts," "oftentiines," "artists ideas" (needs an apostrophe: *artist's*), "an ever-more complex" (should be "a" before "ever-more"), and "Necessarily, it's interesting to note" (unclear use of "necessarily").
Some sentences are overloaded with qualifiers and could be simplified for directness (eg, in the introduction and conclusion).

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates ideas rather than synthesizing them into a final, forceful claim. Rather than ending on a general statement about government understanding "the interaction and basic needs of the people," try closing with a vivid image or call to action that reinforces your central claim—perhaps returning to your opening metaphor of funding cuts, now reframed to show what society stands to lose.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to explicitly justify *government* (not just private) funding.
Cut or clarify the Louis XIV/Napoleon and pornography paragraphs.
Add specific, cited evidence in place of vague appeals to "researchers."
Add a counterargument/rebuttal paragraph.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

With these revisions, you'll have a much more persuasive and polished argument. Keep building on the genuine enthusiasm you clearly have for this subject!
