Feedback on "Should There Be a Salary Cap on Professional Football Players?"

Overall Impression

You clearly have strong feelings about this topic, and your essay touches on several legitimate angles—economic fairness, stadium conditions, player mobility, and the broader cultural effects of money in sports. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of grievances about football players than a focused argument for a salary cap. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, tighten your logic, and support your claims with evidence rather than assumptions.

Thesis and Focus

Your introduction promises three reasons for a salary cap: (1) players are overpaid compared to college graduates, (2) money should go toward fan facilities, and (3) a cap would stop players from negotiating their own salaries. This is a reasonable structure—but the essay doesn't follow it. Instead, entire paragraphs veer into tangents: whether football players deserve fame, the O.J. Simpson trial, the history of when "money and power" entered football. These are interesting observations, but they don't build your case for a *salary cap* specifically. 

Revision tip: After drafting, go through each paragraph and ask, "Does this paragraph give a reason to support a salary cap, or does it just complain about players?" If it's the latter, either cut it or explicitly connect it back to your policy proposal.

Logical Gaps

Several of your arguments rely on assumptions that need to be examined or supported:

The doctor/football player comparison is emotionally compelling but logically shaky. Salaries are determined by revenue generation and market demand, not by a job's moral or social "importance." A stronger essay would acknowledge this economic reality and then argue why football's revenue-based pay structure is still problematic (eg, inequitable distribution, effects on team competitiveness, fan costs).
The O.J. Simpson reference conflates a legal outcome with salary levels. Even without a salary cap discussion, this is a controversial and unsubstantiated claim (his acquittal was about a jury verdict, not "his salary"). Consider removing this example, as it distracts from your actual argument and requires evidence you haven't provided.
The stadium/bathroom anecdote is vivid, but it doesn't logically connect to salary caps. Poor facilities could result from mismanagement, ownership decisions, or ticket pricing, none of which are directly solved by capping player salaries. If you want to keep this point, you need to explain the *mechanism*: how would capping salaries redirect money specifically toward fan facilities? Right now, that connection is assumed rather than argued.
The Deion Sanders paragraph actually makes a good argument for a salary cap (preventing bidding wars and free-agent movement), but it undercuts your earlier claim by suggesting star players and bench players should be paid the same. You might want to clarify whether you're arguing for a *team* salary cap (limiting total payroll) or *individual* salary equality—these are different policies with different implications.

Tone and Word Choice

Phrases like "greedy football players," "players that sit the bench," and "doesn't do anything that is considered rewarding" come across as dismissive rather than persuasive. Academic argumentation is more effective when it treats the opposing view fairly before refuting it. For instance, instead of asserting players "don't do anything rewarding," you might acknowledge the physical demands and short career spans of professional athletes, then pivot to your economic argument.

Structure and Paragraphing

Right now, paragraphs are organized loosely by topic (doctors vs. players, greed, stadiums, salary negotiation) rather than by a clear line of reasoning. Consider this structure:

Intro: State the specific type of salary cap you're proposing and preview your strongest 2–3 reasons.
Body 1: Economic argument (revenue distribution, competitive balance—see NFL/NBA salary cap models as real-world evidence).
Body 2: Effects on team stability and bidding wars (your Deion Sanders example fits well here).
Body 3: Address the counterargument (star players "deserve" more) and refute it with reasoning, not just assertion.
Conclusion: Return to your thesis and summarize the practical benefits.

Evidence

The essay would benefit greatly from actual facts: NFL salary data, examples of existing salary cap systems (NBA, NFL's real salary cap history—which actually already exists!), or reports on stadium funding. Right now, most claims are supported by personal opinion or a single anecdote. Even brief research citations would substantially strengthen your credibility.

Mechanics

Watch for repeated rhetorical questions used as filler ("Is this really fair? In many ways it is not fair.")—vary your sentence structures. Also proofread for typos ("wee," "ant," "affective" instead of "effective") and inconsistent capitalization (Earth).

Final Suggestion

Pick your strongest, most factually groundable argument (likely competitive balance/bidding wars) and build the essay around that, using the other points only as secondary support.

Feedback on Your Gun Control Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, contentious issue and gathered substantial research to support a gun control position. Your essay shows real effort to address constitutional questions, statistical evidence, and policy solutions. However, the essay needs significant revision in organization, argumentation, and mechanics before it fully succeeds as academic writing.

Thesis and Structure

Your opening paragraph actually contains your entire argument in miniature—it previews the constitutional argument, the crime-reduction argument, and the policy solution all at once. This creates a structural problem: the rest of the essay essentially repeats these points rather than building on them. Consider restructuring so your introduction states your thesis clearly and concisely (eg, "Congress should pass stricter federal gun control legislation because it is both constitutional and effective at reducing gun violence"), and then let each body paragraph develop *one* line of argument in depth.

Currently, the essay reads as a series of loosely connected points: constitutional analysis, the Brady assassination attempt, statistics on gun deaths, arguments about statistical persuasiveness, policy recommendations, and a conclusion. Many of these ideas circle back on each other (the constitutional argument appears at least three times). Try outlining your essay with clear topic sentences for each paragraph, and make sure each paragraph has a distinct purpose that moves the argument forward rather than restating earlier points.

Argumentation and Evidence

Several claims need stronger support or more careful reasoning:

The statistics paragraph is confusing. You claim pro-control statistics are more "verifiable" than pro-gun statistics, but you don't explain *why*—you simply assert that corpses and injuries are more real than claims about crime prevention. This is an opinion dressed as a statistical judgment. Either develop this comparison with more rigor (what specific studies are being compared?) or cut this paragraph, as it currently weakens rather than strengthens your case.

"Law-abiding citizens do not need handguns since very few use them to defend themselves" is a significant claim that needs its own evidence and explanation, not just a citation. What does "very few" mean numerically? This is exactly the kind of claim that invites counterargument, and you should anticipate that pushback.

You acknowledge but don't fully engage the "slippery slope" counterargument (that gun control leads to gun bans). You dismiss it in one sentence without offering a substantive rebuttal—this is a missed opportunity to strengthen your argument by taking the opposition seriously.

The conclusion's claims about Founding Fathers' "vision of peace and tranquility" are unsupported by any of your historical evidence. This feels like a rhetorical flourish rather than an argument—cut it or ground it in the Second Amendment history you discussed earlier.

Use of Sources

Your citations follow MLA-style parenthetical format, which is appropriate, but there are problems:

Quotations are sometimes introduced without context. When you quote "the right of the people to keep and bear arms," tell readers *whose words these are*—is this the Amendment's text? A court's interpretation? A specific source?
You rely heavily on Cozic and Cole—make sure you're representing their arguments fairly and not simply stringing quotations together. Paraphrase more and quote more selectively, choosing quotations that are especially precise or authoritative.
Double-check that every source in your parenthetical citations appears in a Works Cited page, and standardize the spelling (I noticed "Cosec" appears to be a typo for "Cozic" in several places).

Mechanics and Proofreading

This essay needs careful proofreading. Some specific issues:

"sawed of shotguns" should be "sawed-off shotguns"
"definatly" should be "definitely"
"riffles" should be "rifles"
"prevent identification" should likely be "present identification"
The paragraph beginning "lnl981,JimBrady..." has a formatting error that garbles the opening—this needs to be fixed so readers can follow the Reagan assassination attempt example clearly.
Watch subject-verb agreement and comma splices throughout (eg, "More commonly, Guns lead to violence"—capitalization error mid-sentence).

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, or having a peer read it, specifically to catch these mechanical errors, as they currently interrupt the reader's ability to follow your argument.

Suggestions for Revision

Cut redundancy: Consolidate your constitutional argument into one strong paragraph rather than three scattered ones.
Strengthen transitions: Help readers see how each paragraph builds on the last.
Engage counterarguments more seriously: Give the strongest opposing view its due before rebutting it.
Proofread carefully: Fix typos, spelling, and the garbled opening of the Brady paragraph.
Sharpen your thesis: State it once, clearly, and organize body paragraphs around distinct supporting arguments.

You have good raw material here—now the task is to organize it with more discipline and polish the prose so your argument's strength isn't undercut by structural and mechanical issues.

Feedback Report

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and important topic, and you clearly have strong personal convictions about it. You also make an effort to complicate your position by considering exceptions (rape, health risks), which shows you're thinking through the ethical nuances rather than offering a flat, unexamined stance. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of structure, argumentation, tone, and clarity. Below are the key areas to focus on.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is unclear. You open by saying abortion should have "strict rules," but you spend much of the essay arguing abortion is morally equivalent to murder—these are different claims requiring different support. Decide: are you arguing for a total ban with narrow exceptions, or for tighter regulation? State this clearly in your introduction, and then organize each paragraph around one clear supporting point for that specific claim. Right now the essay moves through contraception statistics, moral claims about personhood, exceptions, and a personal anecdote without clear transitions or a unifying thread connecting them back to your thesis.

Logical Gaps

Several key inferential leaps need support:

Contraception → no excuse for abortion. Even with contraceptive failure rates as low as you cite, some pregnancies will still occur despite responsible use. Your argument implies that any failure is the woman's fault, but this doesn't follow logically from statistics about effectiveness.
Adoption as a simple solution. Carrying a pregnancy to term and placing a child for adoption is not emotionally, physically, or medically equivalent to not being pregnant. This deserves more careful treatment—acknowledge the real burdens involved rather than presenting it as an easy alternative.
Financial hardship and welfare. You dismiss financial concerns by pointing to welfare, but this oversimplifies the economic and social realities many people face. Consider engaging with counterarguments more seriously rather than dismissing them quickly.

Strengthening these transitions will make your argument feel earned rather than asserted.

Tone and Language

The tone undermines your credibility in places. Phrases like "if a person decides to play the game" and "horseplay" trivialize a serious subject and can alienate readers, especially given that later in the essay you discuss rape and medical risk with real gravity. This inconsistency in tone (casual/judgmental early on, empathetic later) suggests the essay was written in different emotional registers without revision for consistency. I'd recommend adopting a more measured, respectful tone throughout, even when expressing strong disagreement.

Also reconsider language describing the rape scenario ("vicious sperm," "wild maniac," "fiend")—while your intent is clearly to convey the violation and trauma, overly graphic or sensational phrasing can distract from the emotional and ethical seriousness of the point you're making. A more restrained description would carry more persuasive weight.

The Personal Anecdote

The story about your cousin is the most powerful material in the essay, but it's used inconsistently. You present it as evidence *against* abortion (regulation would have prevented her mother's decision), but the actual takeaway seems more about *bodily autonomy*—your cousin didn't consent to the abortion; her mother forced it on her. This story actually illustrates the danger of someone else controlling a woman's reproductive decisions, which complicates rather than supports your argument for government-imposed rules. Consider whether this anecdote truly serves your thesis, or whether it reveals a tension in your position worth exploring explicitly.

Claim About Jail for "Murder"

The claim that women who have abortions should be "put in jail for murder" is a serious legal and ethical claim that needs much more support. Consider: What legal precedent exists for treating fetal personhood as equivalent to personhood after birth? What would enforcement look like? This claim, as stated, reads as an assertion of anger rather than a reasoned position. If you want to keep this claim, it needs its own paragraph with legal/philosophical grounding.

Proofreading

The essay needs careful proofreading for grammar, word choice, and clarity (eg, "she got pregnant for a twenty-two year old married man," "I does think," missing words and punctuation in several sentences, comma splices). These errors distract readers from your argument and should be corrected in revision.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify your thesis and make sure every paragraph supports it directly.
Strengthen logical connections between claims (contraception, adoption, welfare).
Revise tone for consistency and respectfulness.
Reconsider what the cousin's story is actually demonstrating.
Support the "jail for murder" claim more rigorously, or soften it.
Proofread carefully for grammar and clarity.

You have real conviction and are willing to grapple with hard cases—now the essay needs a clearer logical skeleton to hold that conviction together.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a relevant and engaging topic—the pervasiveness of sexual content in media and its potential effects on teenage behavior. You provide concrete, specific examples (Beverly Hills 90210, R. Kelly songs, Paul Mitchell ads) that ground your argument in real evidence, which is a strength. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of thesis clarity, organization, argumentative logic, and mechanics to reach academic standards.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your central claim is unclear. The title suggests sex is "overrated" in media, but your actual argument seems to be that media's *unrealistic* portrayal of sex (without consequences or precautions) contributes to teen pregnancy and STDs. These are two different claims:

Sex is overused/excessive in media (quantity)
Media portrays sex irresponsibly, without showing precautions (quality/realism)

Your strongest material actually supports the second claim, not the first. Consider revising your thesis to something like: "While media saturates teenagers with sexual content, its failure to depict responsible sexual behavior—condom use, discussing sexual histories—contributes to rising rates of teen pregnancy and STDs." This gives you a clearer, more arguable thesis with a logical throughline.

Organization

Currently, your essay moves through media types (TV, radio, magazines) before pivoting to consequences (teen pregnancy/STDs) and solutions. This structure works, but the transitions are abrupt ("Television and radio are not the only places sex is overrated" is a weak transitional sentence used mechanically). Consider:

Opening with a stronger, more specific thesis in your introduction
Using topic sentences that connect each section back to your central argument (not just "here's another example of sex in media" but "this example demonstrates how media normalizes consequence-free sex")
Strengthening your conclusion—right now it restates points rather than synthesizing them into a final insight

Logical Development

Some of your reasoning needs more support:

You claim media causes teen pregnancy/STDs, but this is correlation, not demonstrated causation. Consider acknowledging other factors (peer influence, lack of sex education, family communication) to make your argument more credible and nuanced. You gesture at this ("A lot of teenagers don't talk to their parents") but don't develop it.
The magazine ad analysis (Paul Mitchell, Gravity cologne) is vivid but drifts into critiquing advertising strategy rather than sexual content's harm. Clarify how these examples connect to your main argument about teen behavior.
Research or statistics about teen pregnancy/STD rates would strengthen your causal claims considerably. Right now you're relying entirely on personal observation ("I have never seen a show where...").

Style and Mechanics

Several issues need attention in revision:

Word choice/typos: "R. Kellye" (R. Kelly), "f eaturing" (featuring), "saids" (says), "soak and wet" (soaked and wet), "want see" (won't see)
Missing content: The Gravity cologne slogan is marked "" — you need to insert the actual quote
Repetitive sentence starters: Multiple paragraphs and sentences begin with "You can't..." in the final section. Vary your syntax for better flow.
Colloquial tone: Phrases like "Its funny how" and "The only things else that needs to be said" are conversational rather than academic. Aim for more formal diction throughout.
Apostrophe errors: "Its funny" should be "It's funny"; watch for consistent possessive/contraction usage
Sentence fragments and run-ons: Some sentences need restructuring for clarity (eg, "Nothing but a sheet." works stylistically but should be used sparingly and intentionally)

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to make a single, clear, arguable claim
Add a counterargument – acknowledge that media isn't the sole cause of teen sexual behavior, then explain why it's still significant
Incorporate outside sources: even one or two statistics or expert opinions on teen pregnancy/STD rates would substantially strengthen your credibility
Revise topic sentences so each paragraph explicitly connects to your thesis
Proofread carefully for typos and fill in the missing quotation
Strengthen the conclusion by synthesizing your argument rather than repeating it, and perhaps expanding on your "understood sex" solution with more concrete suggestions

Final Note

You have a genuine, developed perspective and good instincts for using specific examples. The next revision should focus on tightening your central argument and supporting it with more than personal observation. Consider: what is the *one* claim you most want your reader to believe, and does every paragraph clearly serve that claim?

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Psychology of Fashion"

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've clearly done substantial research, and you're engaging with an interesting topic—the psychological dimensions of clothing choice. However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of sources than an argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to focus on as you revise.

Develop a Clear, Arguable Thesis

Right now, your essay makes several different claims—clothing reflects mood, clothing reflects self-concept, clothing affects adolescent behavior, clothing matters to the elderly, modesty is psychologically complex—but none of these coalesce into a single governing argument. An argumentative essay needs a thesis that takes a *position* that a reader could reasonably disagree with. For example: "Clothing choices are not simply personal expression but are fundamentally shaped by the psychological need for social belonging" is arguable. "Clothing can influence how a person thinks and feels" is simply a fact, not an argument. Revise your introduction to state one focused, debatable claim, and then make sure every paragraph works to support *that* claim specifically.

Cut the Summary; Add Analysis

Much of this essay reports what Ryan, Flugel, and Davis say, but it rarely explains *why* this matters to your argument or how the sources relate to each other. For instance, the paragraph on the elderly and the paragraph on adolescents each present interesting facts, but you don't connect them back to a central claim—are you arguing that social approval is the primary psychological driver of dress across all age groups? If so, say that explicitly and use these examples as *evidence*, not as standalone facts. After nearly every citation, ask yourself: "So what? How does this prove my thesis?" Add a sentence of analysis after each piece of evidence.

Reconsider the Herrick Poem and the Flugel List

The inclusion of the full "Delight in Disorder" poem and the five-part list from Flugel are interesting, but they are dropped into the essay without interpretation. Long quotations should be the exception, not filler—if you include them, you must explain in your own words what they show and why they matter to your argument. Consider whether you need the entire poem, or whether a shorter, more targeted quotation with your own analysis would serve you better. The same applies to Flugel's five-part list: don't just present it and move on—apply it to your argument.

Improve Paragraph Structure and Transitions

Each paragraph currently jumps between topics (self-concept, adolescents, consumer motivation, the elderly, modesty) without clear transitions or topic sentences that connect back to your thesis. Revise so that each paragraph opens with a topic sentence stating the point you will prove, follows with evidence, and closes with analysis that ties back to your central claim. Right now the essay reads as a list of loosely related sub-topics on "psychology and fashion" rather than a unified argument.

Address Grammar, Tone, and Word Choice

There are a number of grammatical issues to fix in revision:
Subject-verb agreement: "what a person feel," "she probably feel," "may fell self-pity" should be "feels" and "feel."
Informal/judgmental language: calling someone "a slut" or "a pimp" is inappropriate for academic writing and undermines your credibility. Replace with neutral, analytical language (eg, "may be judged based on perceived social role or class").
The personal anecdote ("if I were a person who dresses the way society wants...") shifts tone abruptly into first person speculation. If you want to use personal reflection, integrate it deliberately and consistently, not as a one-off aside.
Citation format: Check whether your instructor wants author-page citations consistently (eg, "(Ryan 84)")—right now some citations drop the author's name once introduced, which is fine, but be consistent and make sure page numbers are accurate throughout.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates various threads from the body without synthesizing them into a final statement about your thesis. A strong conclusion should return to your argument and explain its broader significance—why does it matter that fashion is psychologically driven? What does this reveal about human identity, society, or consumer culture? Avoid ending on a general, feel-good statement like "clothes are an important part of our everyday lives and they always will be," which doesn't advance an argument.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Write one clear, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around that thesis, not around your sources.
Add analysis after every piece of evidence.
Trim/interpret long quotations rather than letting them stand alone.
Revise for academic tone and grammar.
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize, not summarize.

You have good raw material here—focus your next draft on argument and analysis rather than information gathering.

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult topic and takes a clear stance—that the decision should rest with the woman. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of clarity, argument development, and mechanics before it will be persuasive to an academic reader. Below are specific areas to address.

Right now, the essay reads as a loosely connected set of points rather than a unified argument building toward a conclusion.

This paragraph contains a critical logical and grammatical breakdown: "For example some anti-abortionist, think that even woman women who have abortions. Rape is something that the woman has no control over..." The sentence about rape victims is incomplete—you never finish the thought about what anti-abortionists actually believe regarding rape. Use of Evidence

Statistics on unsafe/self-induced abortions in places where legal abortion is unavailable
Psychological research on outcomes for unwanted children (you assert this confidently in your conclusion—cite a source)
A specific philosopher or scholar who makes the "genocide" argument, so you're responding to an actual position rather than a general "anti-abortionist" strawman

Academic arguments are strengthened significantly when they engage with specific sources rather than an unnamed collective "they."

Addressing Counterarguments

You gesture toward opposing views but don't fully engage with the strongest versions of them. For instance, why do pro-life advocates believe personhood begins at conception? What is their strongest argument, and why does it fail? Right now you're addressing relatively weak or vague pro-life arguments (the genocide comparison, an unclear point about rape) without engaging with more substantive claims about fetal personhood or moral status. A stronger essay would identify the best opposing argument and explain specifically why your position still holds.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are numerous errors that need editing:
Run-on sentences: "These debates began over two decades ago, they have done nothing, but enlarge throughout the years." (comma splice)
Missing/incorrect words: "The pro-choicers, as well as proffers" (did you mean "pro-lifers"?)
Inconsistent spacing/punctuation: "the true meaning of ,-genocide"
Subject-verb and pronoun errors: "shows there tendencies" (should be "their")
Sentence fragments and unclear referents throughout

I strongly recommend reading the essay aloud slowly, sentence by sentence, to catch these issues, or using a grammar-checking tool as a first pass before a final human review.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces new claims (about death rates from self-induced abortion, psychological damage to unwanted children) that should really appear earlier in the body, supported with evidence, rather than being asserted for the first time at the end. Save the conclusion for synthesizing your argument and reinforcing your thesis, not introducing new unsupported claims.

Priority Revisions

Fix the incomplete rape paragraph—this is likely your strongest point
Add specific evidence/citations throughout
Engage with a stronger, more specific pro-life argument
Proofread carefully for grammar and sentence structure
Move conclusion's new claims into the body with support

With revision, particularly around clarity and evidence, this essay has the potential to make a much more compelling case.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and relevant issue—how children are exposed to adult content because of scheduling gaps in children's programming—and your personal experience as a parent gives you credibility and concrete examples. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of related observations than a tightly argued essay. To strengthen it, you need a clearer thesis, more disciplined organization, stronger evidence, and careful editing for grammar and clarity.

Thesis and Argument

Your strongest and most original claim appears only in the final paragraph: television networks should schedule more age-appropriate programming during the hours when children are most likely to be watching, rather than assuming parents can monitor every minute. This is a specific, arguable, and constructive thesis—but you don't state it until the end, and the rest of the essay wanders through several related but distinct claims (parents are too busy, children imitate what they see, sexuality education comes too late, cartoons aren't always safe, etc).

Revision suggestion: State your thesis explicitly in the introduction. Something like: "Because networks schedule so little age-appropriate programming during the after-school hours when children are most likely to be watching TV unsupervised, broadcasters—not just parents—bear responsibility for reducing children's exposure to sexual content, violence, and profanity." Then organize each body paragraph around one piece of evidence supporting *that* claim, rather than restating "children aren't mentally ready" in multiple paragraphs.

Organization

Right now, several paragraphs repeat the same basic point (children lack the maturity to process adult content) using different examples. This creates repetition rather than development. Consider organizing around distinct sub-claims:
Paragraph 1: The scheduling problem (your statistic about 98%/2% and the daily program breakdown)
Paragraph 2: Evidence that children imitate what they see (Bart Simpson, Ricki Lake example)
Paragraph 3: Evidence that children misinterpret what they see (your daughter's "kissing means married" example)
Paragraph 4: Why this matters more now than before (sexuality not taught until 8th grade; media has gotten more explicit)
Conclusion: Your proposed solution (more children's programming)

This would eliminate redundancy and let each paragraph build toward your final recommendation.

Evidence and Support

Your statistics (98% of programming isn't for children, 2% is) are compelling but need a source—where did these numbers come from? Unsupported statistics can undermine credibility rather than strengthen it. If this is your own estimate based on the schedule you describe, say so explicitly ("Based on a typical weekday schedule...") rather than presenting it as a verified fact.

Your personal anecdotes (your daughter's assumption about kissing, your decision to ban *The Simpsons*) are valuable—they're vivid and specific. But right now they function as your *only* evidence for claims about all children. Consider distinguishing between what you know from personal experience and what would need broader support (child development research, media studies, or even the National Institute on Media and the Family, which was active in this period). Even one or two outside sources would significantly strengthen your argument's credibility.

Also, be careful with causal claims. You suggest that watching Ricki Lake could make an 8-year-old think "it's all right to have sex and get pregnant"—this is a significant leap. Consider softening the claim ("may lead children to believe...") or providing more reasoning about *why* this connection would form.

Style, Tone, and Mechanics

The essay shifts between formal argument and casual, conversational asides ("I do not allow my four year old..."), which is fine if intentional, but the transitions need to be smoother. Right now they read as digressions rather than deliberate evidence.

There are also numerous mechanical issues that need editing:
Inconsistent and incorrect use of quotation marks (eg, "11 Big people shows"—likely a typo for opening quotation marks; "The Simpsons" is written as "11 The Simpsons"")
Comma splices and run-on sentences (eg, "Children starting at age four, generally watch...")
Missing apostrophes ("moms," "child's," "Opra" for "Oprah")
Repetition of filler phrases ("For an example," "Also," used too frequently as transitions)
The phrase "the the" and other typos suggest the essay needs a careful proofreading pass

Suggested Next Steps

Write a clear, one-sentence thesis and place it in your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one supports a distinct part of that thesis, eliminating repeated points.
Add at least one outside source (statistic, expert quote, or study) to support your claims about children's media consumption or developmental readiness.
Do a full proofreading pass focused on sentence boundaries, apostrophes, and quotation marks.
Revise the conclusion so your proposed solution feels like the natural culmination of the essay's argument, not an afterthought.

You have real insight here, especially from firsthand parenting experience—sharpening the structure and argument will help readers see that insight clearly.

Feedback Report: Recycling Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled an important topic with genuine enthusiasm, and your essay demonstrates solid research effort—you've gathered specific, compelling facts about various recyclable materials. However, the essay currently reads more as an informational survey of recycling categories than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, reorganize for persuasive impact, and address some significant issues with sourcing and structure.

Major Issues to Address

Unclear Argumentative Focus
Your introduction promises an argument about why "recycling needs to become commonplace uniformly," but the body reads as a catalog of facts about different materials (plastic, glass, batteries, motor oil, etc) rather than a sustained argument. Ask yourself: What is the *one* claim I'm defending? Is it "recycling should be mandatory"? "Individuals must change daily habits"? "Corporations bear primary responsibility"? Right now, the essay tries to do too much informationally without building a persuasive case with reasons, evidence, and rebuttals to counterarguments.

Revision suggestion: Narrow your thesis. Consider organizing around 2-3 main *reasons* recycling matters (eg, landfill capacity, energy conservation, toxin prevention) rather than moving material-by-material.

Source Reliability Concerns
This is critical: your primary source, "Recycler's Bill of Rights," and "One Earth" are never fully identified (no author, publication, or date given), and the Superbag Corporation is a company with an obvious commercial interest in promoting plastic bags over paper. Using a plastic bag manufacturer to argue "plastic is better than paper" is a significant credibility problem—this is like citing a cigarette company on the health effects of smoking. 

Revision suggestion: Seek out peer-reviewed studies, EPA data, or reports from independent environmental organizations (eg, EPA.gov, peer-reviewed journals, established NGOs) to support the plastic vs. paper claim. If you keep the Superbag quote, you must acknowledge and address its bias explicitly.

Structural Organization
The essay moves through material types (plastic → paper → glass → refrigerators → batteries → oil → cartridges → household toxics) somewhat mechanically, without clear topic sentences connecting each paragraph back to your central argument. Each section feels like a mini-essay rather than a building block in a larger case.

Revision suggestion: Add transitional topic sentences that explicitly link each material back to your thesis. For example: "Just as plastic recycling reduces landfill waste, glass and steel recycling offers even more dramatic energy savings—demonstrating that recycling program expansion should be a policy priority."

The Plastic vs. Paper Tangent
The extended argument that plastic bags are superior to paper bags is a significant digression from your recycling argument. This is a genuinely contested environmental debate (plastic bags have well-documented problems with ocean pollution, wildlife entanglement, and persistence) that deserves its own essay—but including it here without addressing counterevidence weakens your credibility on the broader recycling issue.

Revision suggestion: Either cut this section significantly or acknowledge the complexity (both materials have environmental costs; the real solution is reducing consumption and recycling either material properly).

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Several typos/errors need correction: "fun" should be "full," "@" appears to be a formatting error, "m" should be "in" (multiple instances), "win" should be "will," "fight bulb" should be "light bulb," "Freon should be first drained" needs revision.
Quotation formatting is inconsistent—some quotes lack clear introduction ("Most plastic can be recycled..." appears without context).
The long block quote from Superbag is not properly formatted or contextualized before it appears.

Revision suggestion: Do a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading aloud, to catch these errors.

Strengths to Build On

Your statistics are vivid and memorable (the World Trade Center comparison, the light bulb energy comparison)
You cover an impressive breadth of recyclable materials
Your call to action in the conclusion has genuine persuasive energy

Priority Revisions

Rewrite your thesis to state one clear, arguable claim
Replace or contextualize the Superbag source
Cut or shorten the plastic-vs-paper section
Add topic sentences linking each paragraph to your central argument
Proofread for the errors noted above

Focus your revision energy on making this less "here's everything I learned about recycling" and more "here's why you must act, and here's the evidence that proves it." You have the raw material for a compelling essay—now it needs a stronger argumentative spine.

Feedback on Your Essay: Pink, Blue, and Gender Stereotypes

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging, relatable topic, and your instinct to use concrete commercial and product examples (Skintimate, Secret, Huggies, Always) is a good one—specific evidence is exactly what strong argumentative writing needs. However, the essay currently reads more like a description of a pattern than an argument about it. To move this to the next level, you need a clear central claim and deeper analysis of your evidence. Below are specific areas to work on.

Develop a Clear Thesis

Right now, your essay tells us *that* pink is coded feminine and blue is coded masculine, and it shows many examples of this. But it never tells us why this matters or what claim you are making about it. Ask yourself:
Is this stereotype harmful? Limiting? Arbitrary?
Are you arguing that advertising *creates* this association, or that advertising merely *reflects* an association society already holds?
Are you arguing that this stereotype is changing, weakening, or getting stronger?

Your final paragraph gestures toward an interesting claim—that as people age, women have more freedom to cross the color line than men do—but this appears only in your last two sentences. This asymmetry (women can "get away with" masculine colors, men can't "get away with" pink) is actually the most original and analytical idea in your essay. Move a version of this claim to your introduction as your thesis, and then organize the body paragraphs to build toward proving it, rather than saving it for the end.

Strengthen Structure and Topic Sentences

The essay currently moves through examples in a list-like fashion: commercials for women, then babies, then baby boys. Each section needs a topic sentence that connects back to your thesis, not just introduces the next category of example. For instance, instead of "Commercials always show this stereotype," try something like: "Advertising reinforces the pink-for-women, blue-for-men divide by consistently coding feminine products in soft, pastel tones." This signals *why* the paragraph matters, not just *what* it contains.

Also consider reorganizing so that your strongest, most analytical point (the asymmetry between how men and women can cross gender-color lines) appears early, and the body paragraphs serve as evidence for it, rather than appearing as an afterthought.

Deepen Your Analysis

Many of your examples are stated but not analyzed. For example, you note that the Secret deodorant slogan is "Strong enough for a man, but pH balanced for a woman"—this is a rich example because the *language* itself reinforces gender difference, not just the color scheme. Push further: What does it suggest that "strength" is implicitly masculine while women need something gentler? This kind of close reading of your evidence will make your argument more persuasive than simply cataloguing product colors.

Similarly, ask *why* pink became associated with softness and femininity in the first place. A little research here (there's actually a well-documented history showing pink was once considered a *boys'* color) would add depth and credibility, and could even become a counterargument you address—showing that this stereotype is culturally constructed rather than natural, which would strengthen an argument about its arbitrariness.

Add a Counterargument or Complication

Strong argumentative essays acknowledge complexity. You could ask: Are there exceptions? Is this stereotype weakening with unisex baby products or gender-neutral marketing? Addressing this would show critical thinking and prevent the essay from feeling one-note.

Editing and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues to fix in revision:
Capitalization after periods: "because it is a very soft color" should begin with a capital B; "on the Secret deodorant commercial" needs a capital O; "once a baby has developed" needs a capital O.
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "The background has soft colors. on the Secret deodorant commercial..." — check sentence boundaries throughout.
Informal register: "(jam referring to babies)" appears to be a typo for "I am referring to babies"—proofread carefully, and avoid parenthetical asides like this in formal writing; instead, integrate the clarification into the sentence.
Repetition: Words like "soft colors" and "stereotypical" appear very frequently—vary your language.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, arguable thesis (likely built from your final paragraph's insight).
Reorganize paragraphs around that thesis, with analytical topic sentences.
Analyze your examples more deeply—don't just list them.
Add a counterargument or historical complication.
Proofread carefully for capitalization, sentence boundaries, and repetitive phrasing.

You have good raw material here—now it's a matter of shaping it into a focused, analytical argument. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report: "Drug Legalization" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely important and complex policy debate, and you've organized your argument around several distinct benefits of legalization, which gives the paper a clear roadmap. However, the essay currently reads as a rough draft rather than a finished argument. The most pressing issue is structural: there are numerous placeholder marks () throughout the body paragraphs where evidence, examples, or transitional reasoning should be. These gaps make it impossible to evaluate your actual argument, since entire logical steps are missing. Before addressing style or grammar, this is the priority for revision.

Content and Argumentation

Fill in the missing evidence. Every paragraph contains at least one  where you seem to have intended to insert a statistic, example, or quotation but never completed the thought. For instance, in your crime paragraph, you assert that lower drug prices lead to less crime, but the causal mechanism connecting price to crime is left blank. Readers cannot follow your logic without these connective explanations. Revise by drafting complete sentences for each of these gaps, and make sure each one is supported by a specific source (data, expert testimony, or documented case).

Strengthen your logical connections. Even where the prose is complete, some causal claims move too quickly. For example, you state that legalization would decrease crime because drug prices would fall, but you don't explain *why* lower prices reduce crime (eg, users would no longer need to steal to afford drugs). Similarly, the paragraph on drug users being "responsible for their own debts" needs a topic sentence that actually defines what this benefit is before you jump to taxation. Right now the connection between personal financial responsibility and tax revenue is unclear—these feel like two different points awkwardly merged.

The public health paragraph needs revision for coherence. This section has a sentence fragment ("Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers") that doesn't make grammatical sense and disrupts the logic of substitution between drugs and alcohol. Clarify: are you arguing that heroin users who can't access heroin turn to alcohol? Spell this out in a complete sentence, then explain how legalization would prevent this substitution.

Address counterarguments. A persuasive essay on such a contested topic should acknowledge the strongest objections—for example, concerns that legalization would increase addiction rates, harm public health, or send the wrong message to young people. Even briefly addressing and rebutting one or two counterarguments would make your position more credible and show that you've engaged critically with the issue rather than presenting only one side.

You cite Duke once early in the essay, but no other benefit is explicitly attributed to a source, even though several claims (eg, "one third of people" incarcerated for drug offenses, the claim about tobacco/alcohol substitution) sound like they are drawn from research. Every statistic or specific claim needs an in-text citation. Without this, readers cannot verify your evidence, and the essay risks appearing unsupported or even plagiarized if the ideas originated elsewhere. Since this essay clearly draws on outside reading (the substitution effect between heroin/alcohol reads like it's paraphrased from a specific study), make sure to cite consistently throughout, not just in the introduction.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your paragraph organization by "benefit" is a reasonable strategy, but each paragraph would benefit from a clearer topic sentence stating the specific benefit before you explain it. Right now, several paragraphs open with vague transitional phrases ("Another benefit would be...") without immediately clarifying what that benefit *is* in concrete terms. Consider revising each opening sentence to name the benefit precisely (eg, "Legalizing drugs would reduce violent crime associated with the illegal drug trade" rather than just "the decrease in crime").

Style and Mechanics

Watch for run-on sentences and comma splices, such as in your introduction: "They have come to reality and decided that ,drug prohibition has not worked..." Revise punctuation here.
Avoid casual phrases like "get hooked up in the drug business" and "that big money"—these read as informal for academic writing. Consider more precise, academic phrasing.
The phrase "otherwise productive citizens" and later "socially and economically margin" needs revision—the latter is grammatically incomplete (should likely read "marginalized").
Proofread for typos ("imprisonmemt," "beneficiary" instead of "beneficial").

Priority Revision Steps

Complete every  placeholder with full, evidence-based sentences.
Add citations for every specific claim or statistic.
Clarify causal logic between price, crime, and drug availability.
Add at least one paragraph addressing a counterargument.
Proofread for sentence-level clarity and grammar.

With these revisions, you'll have a much stronger, more complete argument. The bones of a good essay are here—now it needs the evidence and polish to support your claims fully.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, high-stakes topic, and your personal connection to it—especially the story about your uncle—gives the essay emotional power that many student writers struggle to achieve. Your instinct to ground the argument in local, real-world examples (Blackville-Hilda, Branchville, Orangeburg) is a strength. However, the essay needs significant revision in structure, argumentation, and use of evidence to move from an emotional appeal to a persuasive academic argument.

Strengths to Build On

Personal investment: The Glover killing and your uncle's death are powerful, memorable examples that ground abstract policy debates in human consequences.
Local research: You clearly did work to find local news examples, which shows initiative.
Clear thesis attempt: You state your position early—that gun control would reduce crime, save lives, and lessen fear.

Key Areas for Revision

Strengthen and Clarify Your Thesis
Your thesis is currently buried in a long, run-on introduction and is vague about *what* "gun control" actually means. Does it mean background checks? Waiting periods? Banning civilian ownership entirely (as your conclusion suggests)? Your examples (a school shooting, a bank robbery, two murders) involve very different circumstances that would require different solutions. Decide on a specific policy proposal and make sure your thesis names it clearly. Right now, your evidence doesn't clearly support the specific solution you propose in the conclusion (only police and military carrying guns)—this is a significant leap that needs its own supporting argument.

Address the Logical Gap Between Evidence and Claim
This is the essay's biggest weakness: your examples show that guns are misused, but they don't show that gun control (as a policy) would have prevented these specific crimes. For instance:
Would a five-day waiting period have stopped Toby Sincino, a 15-year-old, from getting a gun? How did he get it?
Would background checks have prevented the bank robbery or your uncle's murder, which resulted from an argument, not a background-check failure?

You need to draw an explicit causal link between the policy you're proposing and the prevention of these tragedies. Otherwise, a skeptical reader will say, "These are sad stories, but they don't prove gun control works."

Address the Counterargument
You mention the NRA's opposition in one sentence and dismiss it without explaining their actual reasoning (eg, Second Amendment rights, self-defense, enforcement concerns). A strong argumentative essay must fairly represent the opposing view before refuting it. Consider adding a paragraph that outlines the counterargument's strongest points, then explains why your position is still more compelling.

Fix Citation and Source Issues
"Multimedia Resources–CD-ROM" is not a specific or traceable source. Identify the actual database, article title, or organization that produced the statistic.
The hate crime statistics you cite (robbery 2.5%, murder 16.3%) seem to be measuring different things than gun violence broadly—clarify what the "4,755 crimes" refers to and how it connects to your argument about gun control specifically.
Personal interviews and recollections are valid sources for this kind of essay, but label them consistently and consider adding a Works Cited page.

Reorganize for Clarity
Currently the essay moves example-by-example without clear signposting. Consider this structure:
Introduction – clear thesis with specific policy proposal
Body Section 1 – the problem (misuse of guns), using your strongest 2-3 examples
Body Section 2 – how your proposed policy would address the problem (the causal link mentioned above)
Body Section 3 – counterargument and rebuttal
Conclusion – restate thesis, call to action

Proofreading
There are numerous typos and formatting issues (eg, "goverment," "techers," "misue," inconsistent quotation marks, a stray paragraph break in the bank robbery quote). Read the essay aloud or use a grammar checker before your final submission—these errors distract from your credible, well-researched content.

Final Suggestion

Your emotional examples are the heart of this essay—don't lose them. But an argumentative essay needs to do more than move the reader; it needs to convince them through logical connections between evidence and claims. Revise by asking, after every example: *"So what? How does this specific policy I'm proposing address this specific problem?"* That question will help you tighten the argument throughout.

Feedback on Capital Punishment Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely important debate with a clear structure: you identify three common pro-death-penalty arguments and attempt to refute each one. This organizational approach is sound and shows you understand how to build a counter-argumentative essay. However, the essay needs significant revision in three key areas: tone/credibility, evidence support, and mechanics. Let me address each.

Tone and Credibility

Your passionate opposition to capital punishment comes through clearly, but the essay's persuasive power is undermined by language that sounds more like a rant than an academic argument. Phrases like "obscene form of punishment," "as ridiculous as the source they are trying to endorse," and "medieval philosophy" signal to readers that you've already decided the other side is foolish rather than demonstrating this through careful reasoning. Academic argumentation is most persuasive when it treats opposing views with intellectual respect even while refuting them. Consider revising your language to be more measured—you can still be firm and clear without being dismissive. This will make skeptical readers more receptive rather than defensive.

Similarly, direct address to the reader ("Let me throw a couple of statistics at you," "Correct me if I'm wrong") creates an informal, conversational tone that doesn't match academic argumentative writing. Academic essays typically maintain a more formal register even when the writer feels strongly about the topic.

Evidence and Support

This is the area most in need of revision. You state that you have statistics "from a paper last year," but you never cite an actual source. Claims like "it costs five times the amount" and "death row lasts a little over eighteen years" need attribution—which study? Which state or year? Published where? Without citations, these numbers are unverifiable and readers may dismiss them as fabricated, even if they're accurate.

The same problem appears in your deterrence section: "They have done studies in states..." Who conducted these studies? When? Which states? Vague references to unnamed research weaken your argument considerably. For a strong revision, I'd recommend:

Finding and citing 2-3 specific, credible sources (eg, Death Penalty Information Center, peer-reviewed criminology studies, or government reports)
Using proper in-text citations in whatever format your instructor requires (MLA/APA)
Being specific: "A 2011 study found..." is far more credible than "They have done studies..."

Argumentation

Your retribution paragraph is your strongest conceptually—the point about consistency between condemning killing while practicing it as punishment is a genuinely interesting philosophical argument. However, you could strengthen it by acknowledging the counterargument more directly: proponents would say punishment and murder differ because one is sanctioned by law and applied to guilty parties after due process, while the other is not. Address this distinction explicitly rather than assuming your reader will agree it's contradictory.

Your deterrence paragraph would benefit from actually naming which states were studied and by whom, and perhaps directly citing a comparison (eg, murder rates in states with vs. without capital punishment).

Mechanics and Proofreading

The essay contains numerous errors that need correction before submission:
Homophone errors: "untill," "it's" (should be "its" in most instances), "Ounce" (should be "Once")
Spelling errors: "individ ual," "midevil," "murdeers," "prols and con's," "statels," "deference" (should be "deterrence")
Missing words/awkward phrasing: "so therefore for a punishment society is turning around and killing the offender for doing the exact same thing that it is supposedly create justice" (this sentence needs to be rewritten for clarity)

I strongly recommend using spell-check and reading your essay aloud before submission—many of these errors would be caught this way. Errors of this frequency distract readers from your argument and can undermine your credibility, even when your ideas are strong.

Revision Priorities

For your next draft, focus on:
Adding proper citations for every statistic (this is essential)
Toning down inflammatory language while keeping your firm stance
Proofreading carefully for spelling and word-choice errors
Strengthening the retribution section by directly addressing the counterargument

Your argument has real potential—the underlying reasoning is logical and the three-part structure works well. With more rigorous evidence and cleaner prose, this could be a compelling essay.

Feedback on Euthanasia Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done research on this topic and have assembled useful background information—the different types of euthanasia, the Kevorkian case, May's four social forces, and the Hippocratic Oath. However, the essay currently reads more like an informative report than an argumentative essay. Your actual position doesn't emerge clearly until the final paragraph, and even then it feels disconnected from everything that came before it. The essay needs a clearer thesis, tighter organization around that thesis, and more analytical engagement with the material rather than summary.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your introduction ends with a question ("Should it be legalized to allow doctors to assist in suicides...or should they be bound by their Hippocratic Oath?") rather than a thesis statement. A question is not a claim—it doesn't tell your reader what you will argue. Based on your conclusion, it seems your actual position is nuanced: doctors shouldn't *initiate* the suggestion of euthanasia, but may *cooperate* with a patient's informed decision. This is a defensible and interesting position, but it needs to be stated explicitly in your introduction so readers know where the essay is heading. Revise your opening to state this claim directly, then structure the body paragraphs to build toward it.

Structure and Organization

Right now the essay moves through topics in a way that feels additive rather than argumentative: definitions of euthanasia → May's four forces → the Kevorkian case → the Hippocratic Oath → your conclusion. Each section is informative on its own, but the connections between them are left for the reader to infer. Ask yourself, after every paragraph: *How does this material support my thesis?* For example:

The typology of euthanasia (living will, refusal of treatment, self-inflicted suicide) is useful background, but you should explicitly state which type(s) your argument concerns. Right now it's unclear whether your final claim applies to all three forms or only physician-assisted suicide.
The Kevorkian narrative is detailed and interesting, but it functions as a case study—you need to tell the reader *why* it matters to your argument. Does Kevorkian's case support or complicate your position? Right now it reads as neutral reporting.
May's four forces are listed but never analyzed. Do you agree with these forces? Are they still relevant today? You introduce them and then move on without evaluation.

Consider organizing your body paragraphs by argument (eg, "the ethical limits of physician authority," "patient autonomy," "legal precedent") rather than by source or topic. This will help you build a sustained case rather than a series of summaries.

Use of Sources and Evidence

There are several bracketed placeholders () where quotations should appear—these need to be filled in before this draft is submission-ready. Beyond that mechanical issue, when you do quote sources like May, Fletcher, or the Hippocratic Oath, don't just present the quotation and move on. Analyze it: What does this quotation mean for your argument? Do you agree or disagree with it? For instance, you mention that Fletcher found "contradictions" in the Hippocratic Oath, but you never tell us what those contradictions are or whether you find his critique persuasive. This is exactly the kind of moment where your own argumentative voice should take over.

Also, be careful with unsupported factual claims (eg, specific dates and details about Kevorkian's case, Judge Breck's ruling)—make sure each is properly cited with a source, not just narrated as if common knowledge.

Conclusion

Your final two paragraphs contain your strongest, most original thinking—the claim that doctors lack the moral authority to initiate euthanasia but may ethically cooperate with a patient's own decision. This is a good place to *end* the essay, but it shouldn't be the *first* time this idea appears. Bring this claim into your introduction, and use the body paragraphs to build the case for it step by step.

Sentence-Level Issues

Watch apostrophe usage: "patient's" should often be "patients'" or "patients" depending on context.
Several run-on or comma-spliced sentences need to be broken up (eg, the sentence about Kevorkian's court order violations).
"Christains" should be "Christians"; check for similar typos throughout.
Avoid vague phrases like "This view has struck the heart of the medical community"—be specific about *how* it has done so.

Priority Revisions

Write a clear, arguable thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Fill in all missing quotations and properly cite them.
Reorganize body paragraphs around your argument, not around your sources.
Add analysis after every piece of evidence—tell the reader what it means and why it matters.
Proofread for grammar and punctuation errors.

Feedback on "The Wild Card" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely, well-defined topic and you clearly know the subject matter in detail. The essay demonstrates genuine enthusiasm and command of factual detail about the 1995 season. However, the essay currently reads more as an explanatory report on how the wild card system works than as an argumentative essay defending a claim. To strengthen this into a persuasive piece, you'll need to rebalance the proportion of description to argument, tighten your evidence, and address counterarguments.

Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Your thesis—stated at the end of the first paragraph—is clear: the wild card is good for baseball because it (1) adds teams to the playoffs, (2) creates a pennant-race-like competition, and (3) increases competitiveness. This is a solid three-part structure, and your body paragraphs do map onto these three claims. Good organizational instinct.

The problem is proportion. Nearly half the essay (the explanation of the old system, the new Division Series rules, and the home-field-advantage rotation) is pure exposition with no argumentative work being done. While some background is necessary for readers unfamiliar with baseball's playoff structure, you should compress this substantially—perhaps to one paragraph—so more space can go toward developing and defending your claims.

Evidence: Strengths and Weaknesses

Your use of specific statistics (Rockies' four-game run total vs. Reds', the Yankees-Mariners extra-inning game, the last-day scenarios) shows real research effort and adds vividness. However, several evidentiary problems weaken your argument:

Single-season sample size. Your entire case rests on one season (1995). A structural change to a sport should be evaluated over multiple years, or at least you should acknowledge this limitation explicitly. Right now the essay implies these are permanent, structural benefits, but the evidence only shows what happened once.

The runs-scored comparison is questionable logic. You cite the Rockies scoring more runs than the Reds as evidence the wild card team was "more competitive," but the Rockies lost their series 3-1 while the Reds' series was against a different opponent (Braves) under different circumstances—actually, correct: both series were against the Braves, but the comparison still doesn't clearly establish causal competitiveness tied to *being* a wild-card team versus a division winner. Consider whether this is really evidence that wild-card teams generically are more competitive, or just that these two specific teams performed differently.

The star-player list (Larkin, Vaughn, Gant, etc) is effective as a concrete illustration, but you should explain *why* fan interest in stars translates to "good for baseball" beyond the advertising analogy—consider linking this more directly to attendance or TV ratings data if available.

Counterarguments

The essay does not acknowledge any drawback or objection to the wild card. Baseball traditionalists at the time argued that the wild card diluted the meaning of winning a division, or that a team could reach the World Series without ever finishing first. Addressing and rebutting even one such objection would make your argument feel more balanced and convincing, rather than one-sided cheerleading.

Sources and Citations

Your parenthetical citations ("Major League Baseball," "The Wildcard...," "Final 1995 Wild Card Standings") suggest you're using outside sources, but:
There is no Works Cited/References list included.
The citations are inconsistent in format (compare to MLA or whatever style you're required to use).
Some sources are vague ("Major League Baseball" is not a proper citation—what document, website, or press release specifically?).

Please include full citations and ensure in-text citations follow a consistent style guide.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

"the team from each league with the best record the three Division winners" — this sentence is missing a word/phrase and needs to be rewritten for clarity.
Typos: "wild car spots" (wild card), "National Leagued Championship," "Andres Galaragga" (Galarraga).
Final paragraph contains a contradiction: you first say baseball "remained behind football and basketball," then later say baseball was "losing popularity to football and baseball"—this should read "football and basketball."
The "Without the wild card..." repetition in the conclusion is a nice rhetorical device, but revise for grammatical parallelism and proofread carefully, as repetition amplifies the visibility of errors.

Priority Revisions

Cut the expository middle section by half; move any necessary rules explanation into a compressed background paragraph.
Add a paragraph addressing at least one counterargument.
Clarify or replace the runs-scored comparison with a stronger competitiveness metric.
Fix the football/basketball contradiction and proofread for typos.
Add a complete, correctly formatted Works Cited list.

With these revisions, you'll shift the essay's center of gravity from *reporting* to *arguing*—which is exactly what this assignment calls for.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely important issue—the tension between media's "right to know" and individuals' right to privacy—and you clearly feel passionate about this topic. This passion is an asset, but the essay needs significant revision to become a more effective piece of argumentative writing. Here is feedback organized by key areas.

Thesis and Focus

Your essay currently lacks a clear, arguable thesis statement. The personal anecdote about "The State" newspaper is a good hook, but readers don't know what specific claim you're making until well into the essay. Consider crafting a thesis that appears early on—perhaps at the end of your first or second paragraph—that clearly states your position. For example: "The modern media's aggressive pursuit of the 'right to know' has crossed an ethical line, sacrificing personal privacy and even national security for profit." This gives readers a roadmap for what follows.

Organization and Coherence

The essay reads as a list of grievances against the media rather than a structured argument. You move through several distinct examples (Grenada, the Surgeon General nominee, the USC English professor, the O.J. Simpson trial, Colin Powell) without clear transitions explaining how each example builds your case. Consider:

Grouping your examples by theme. For instance, group Grenada and Desert Storm together under "national security"; group the Surgeon General nominee, the USC professor, and Colin Powell together under "destroying reputations/deterring good candidates from public service."
Adding transitional sentences between paragraphs that explicitly connect back to your thesis. Right now, each paragraph feels like a separate mini-essay.
Cutting the digressions. The personal anecdotes about your newspaper subscription and the closing line about "good news" are engaging but don't strengthen your argument. Consider whether they're serving your thesis or simply venting.

Evidence and Development

Your examples are compelling, but they need more development to be persuasive:

Add specific details and sources. When was Colin Powell reportedly deterred from running? What exactly did the Spectator article on the USC professor say? Concrete details (dates, quotes, specific incidents) will make your claims more credible and harder to dismiss.
Address counterarguments. A strong argumentative essay acknowledges the other side. You mention "the people's right to know" in passing, but you don't seriously engage with *why* transparency matters (accountability, informed citizenship, preventing corruption). Address this directly and then explain why, despite these benefits, the costs outweigh them in the cases you cite.
Distinguish between different issues. You conflate several different problems: national security leaks, personal privacy invasions, sensationalism for profit, and character assassination. These are related but distinct issues. Clarifying which specific harm you're addressing in each example will sharpen your argument.

Tone

Your outrage comes through clearly, but at times the tone becomes so heated that it may undermine your credibility with skeptical readers. Phrases like "Was this a rejection or a crucifixion?" and "Watch out, future warriors of this country" are rhetorically effective but risk sounding more like a rant than a reasoned argument. Consider tempering some of the most emotional language and letting your evidence do more of the persuasive work. Academic argument is often more convincing when the tone is measured even while the content is forceful.

Clarity and Mechanics

The essay contains numerous typos and errors that need correction in revision (eg, "successfiil," "fives," "gwne," "dodt," "hwnburgers," "inunune," "0. J. Simpson"). Proofread carefully, ideally aloud, to catch these. Also review punctuation—several sentences have comma splices or missing punctuation (eg, "Nobody was inunune to these incessant facts, They found...").

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces new claims (phone tapping, cameras in homes) that feel like slippery-slope speculation rather than a natural culmination of your argument. Consider ending instead by returning to your thesis and summarizing your strongest points, perhaps closing with a call to action that's grounded in your evidence rather than speculative fears.

Overall Suggestion

For your revision, I'd suggest: (1) draft a clear thesis, (2) reorganize your examples thematically, (3) add specific evidence and address counterarguments, (4) moderate the tone where it becomes overly emotional, and (5) proofread carefully. You have real conviction and interesting examples here—the revision should focus on shaping that energy into a more disciplined, persuasive argument.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Marijuana Legalization

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and debatable topic, and your personal voice comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a stream-of-consciousness reflection than an academic argument. To strengthen it significantly, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, organize your points more logically, replace anecdotes with credible evidence, and address some internal contradictions. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Establish and Maintain a Clear Thesis

Your opening—"What is marijuana? It is a plant, and I think it should be legalized"—is too casual and vague to function as a thesis statement. An academic argument needs a specific, arguable claim that previews your reasoning. Consider something like: "Marijuana should be legalized because it poses fewer health risks than currently illegal classification suggests, and current enforcement disproportionately targets low-level offenders while ignoring larger systemic issues." Whatever claim you choose, make sure every paragraph works to support it.

Resolve a Key Contradiction

You write early on that marijuana "is a drug" in the political sense, but later state, "I don't believe marijuana is a drug. I consider it to be an herb." This is a significant inconsistency. Pick one position and build your argument around it. If your claim is that marijuana shouldn't be classified as a dangerous drug, you need to define what you mean by "drug" and support that distinction with evidence (eg, legal/pharmacological classifications, comparison to alcohol or tobacco), not just assertion.

Replace Anecdotes with Evidence

Much of your essay relies on personal anecdotes—stories about your parents, "Black Moses" the Jamaican man, friends who ace tests after smoking, dealers with nice cars. While personal experience can add color to an essay, it cannot substitute for verifiable evidence in an academic argument. For instance:

Instead of claiming marijuana "can be used to slow down certain eye ailments" or is "highly non-habit forming," cite actual medical studies or health organizations (eg, NIH, DEA scheduling reports, peer-reviewed journals).
Instead of your friend's anecdote about studying, cite actual research on cognitive effects of THC.

This will make your argument far more persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Address Unsupported Claims and Logical Leaps

Several claims are stated as fact without support:
"The Columbian suppliers make millions every year" (likely true, but needs citation).
"Government officials who see and don't see make mega bucks" — this implies a conspiracy without evidence. Even if you don't intend to argue a "grand scale cover up" (as you say later), the accusatory language undermines your credibility unless backed by sources.
"It is highly non-habit forming" — this is contested; you should qualify it and cite research rather than state it as settled fact.

A strong argumentative essay anticipates counterarguments and addresses them with evidence, rather than dismissing them or ignoring them.

Reconsider Your Use of Celebrity Examples

Listing the President, The Notorious B.I.G., and Snoop Dogg as evidence that marijuana isn't harmful is a logical fallacy (appeal to popularity/authority). Fame or past drug use doesn't demonstrate that a substance is safe or should be legal. Cut or reframe this section—perhaps instead discuss how stigmatization affects real communities, or examine racial disparities in enforcement, which is a stronger and more evidence-based angle hinted at in your "little man vs. big suppliers" point.

Tone and Audience

The tone throughout is very informal—phrases like "talking out of his head," rhetorical questions like "Did you know...," and the direct address "Do you ever notice" work in casual conversation but weaken academic credibility. Consider revising these into declarative, evidence-based statements. Save the Bob Marley quote for your introduction or as a rhetorical flourish, not as your concluding "evidence."

Structure and Paragraphing

Right now the essay moves chronologically through your personal history with marijuana rather than logically through supporting reasons for legalization. Consider reorganizing around 3–4 clear reasons (eg, medical benefits, economic factors, enforcement inequality, comparison to legal substances like alcohol), each with its own paragraph and supporting evidence.

Proofreading

There are several typos and grammatical errors to fix in revision: "their" vs. "there," "cession" instead of "session," "Moses" is misspelled as "Mosses," and "critisize" should be "criticize." A careful proofreading pass will elevate the professionalism of your final draft.

Final Note

You clearly have personal investment in this topic, which is a strength—use it to fuel your research rather than replace it. Revise with a clear thesis, credible sources, and logical structure, and this could become a compelling, persuasive essay.
