Feedback on "Protecting Journalists' Sources" Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research on shield laws and have gathered a rich set of case studies—Maryland's original law, the Decker case, Watergate, Charles Stuart, Procter & Gamble, and Rodney King. This is impressive groundwork. However, the essay currently reads more like an informative report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need a sharper thesis, more analytical use of evidence, and engagement with the counterarguments your own examples raise.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Argument

Your introduction sets up the trust relationship between reporters and sources, but it takes several paragraphs before we reach anything resembling a claim ("Keeping sources secret... has always been a problem"). Ask yourself: what is the *specific* claim you want the reader to accept? Is it "Shield laws should be absolute and never overridden by courts"? "Congress should pass a federal shield law"? State this explicitly in your first paragraph, and use it to organize everything that follows.

Let Evidence Serve the Argument, Not Replace It

Right now, many paragraphs simply narrate a case (Maryland, Decker, Ohio, Stuart, King) without telling the reader *why* it matters to your argument. For example:

The Decker case actually shows a shield law *failing* to protect a reporter. You present this neutrally, but it directly complicates your thesis. You need to explain why you think the court was wrong here, or acknowledge it as a legitimate limit and explain why such limits should still be rare.
The Procter & Gamble/Swasy case is compelling, but you never connect it explicitly to shield laws—was Ohio's law inadequate? Nonexistent? Clarify this so the example supports your point.
The long block quotes (the 1896 Maryland statute, the phone-search description, the Henry quotation) take up significant space without analysis afterward. After each quote, add 2-3 sentences explaining its significance to your argument.

Address the Counterargument

A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections. Courts don't ask reporters to reveal sources arbitrarily—they often cite legitimate concerns: a defendant's right to a fair trial, the need to enforce court orders (as in Decker), or preventing journalists from being used as tools to launder illegally obtained information. You gesture at this only briefly and dismissively ("If the justice system were better..."). This is your weakest point—it's speculative and doesn't engage with the real tension between press freedom and judicial authority. Revise this section to seriously consider *when*, if ever, a source should be revealed, and explain why you still favor near-absolute protection despite these concerns.

Strengthen Structure and Transitions

The essay moves through cases somewhat chronologically but not always logically. Consider reorganizing around your *reasons* for supporting shield laws:

Reason 1: Confidentiality enables the flow of information the public needs (use Watergate, Stuart).
Reason 2: Without protection, sources dry up and important stories go untold (use the Henry quote about subpoenas).
Reason 3: Current legal patchwork (35 vs. federal law) creates inconsistent, sometimes abusive enforcement (use Ohio, Kamins/King).

This would let you address the Decker case as a complication within your reasoning rather than a stray anecdote.

Revise the Conclusion

Your final paragraph relies on short imperative sentences ("Protect the Shield Laws," "Don't keep them from doing their job") that feel more like slogans than a reasoned conclusion. A strong conclusion should synthesize your argument and perhaps propose a concrete solution (eg, a federal shield law, or specific criteria courts must meet before compelling disclosure). This gives your essay a forward-looking, persuasive close rather than an emotional appeal.

Mechanical and Citation Issues

There are several errors to fix in revision:
Inconsistent and garbled punctuation (eg, "�a journalist's sources were stripped bare," stray commas, missing closing quotation marks). Proofread carefully for typographic errors—these likely resulted from formatting issues but need cleanup.
Comma splices ("Morris was a police reporter and he wrote a story...").
Inconsistent capitalization ("deep Throat" should be "Deep Throat").
Verify all your citations follow a consistent format (you're mixing MLA-style parenthetical citations with unclear attribution for some quotes).
The 1989 statistic ("almost 4,500 subpoenas") needs a citation and clarification of source/date.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write an explicit, arguable thesis in your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs around reasons/claims, not just chronological cases.
Analyze each piece of evidence—especially Decker—rather than just narrating it.
Add a genuine counterargument section and refute it.
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize argument and suggest a solution.
Proofread carefully for punctuation and citation consistency.

You have strong raw material here. With sharper argumentative framing and more analysis woven through your excellent case studies, this essay could be quite persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay: Women's Admission to the Citadel

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, real-world topic and clearly have a personal stake in the argument, which gives your writing energy and conviction. Your instinct to address counterarguments (tradition, physical strength, distraction) is exactly the right approach for persuasive writing. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: thesis clarity, argument development, and mechanics. Below is guidance organized by priority.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Introduction

Your introduction currently just announces that a debate exists ("there has been one question that has appeared in the courts"). This is a *topic announcement*, not a *thesis*. A strong argumentative essay needs a clear, specific claim in the first paragraph—something like: "The Citadel's refusal to admit women violates constitutional principles of equal access and rests on outdated assumptions about gender capability; therefore, it should be required to admit qualified female applicants."

Also, avoid vague phrases like "many objections" and "many firm believers." Name the actual arguments you'll be addressing so the reader knows what's coming.

Strengthen and Organize Your Body Paragraphs

You have four good potential arguments buried in this essay:
Tradition is not a valid reason to discriminate
Physical/intellectual capability varies by individual, not gender
Public funding legally requires nondiscrimination
Coeducation works elsewhere, so the "distraction" argument fails

Each of these deserves its own clearly focused paragraph. Currently, your second paragraph tries to do too much—it moves from tradition, to societal equality, to physical strength, to individual capability, without clear transitions. Consider splitting this into two paragraphs: one on tradition, one on capability/strength.

Use evidence. Right now your strongest claims (eg, "The Citadel is publicly funded, therefore...") are asserted but not supported. You should:
Cite the actual legal case (this is clearly referencing *United States v. Virginia* or the Citadel's own admission of Shannon Faulkner in the 1990s—research the specific case and cite it).
Include a statistic or quote about physical training standards, or about outcomes for women in similar institutions (eg, VMI after it was forced to integrate).
Reference the actual legal principle you're invoking (Title IX, or the Equal Protection Clause) rather than saying vaguely "it can't discriminate."

This will move your essay from personal opinion to persuasive, evidence-based argument.

Fix Logical Gaps

A few claims need tightening:
The tradition paragraph makes a strong analogy (slavery, housewives) but then abruptly pivots to physical strength without a transition sentence. Add a sentence explicitly linking the two ideas.
The paragraph on public funding makes a hypothetical about race/ethnicity ("hispanic or polish") that is a bit confusing and not fully developed—clarify that you're making an analogy, not a real scenario, and connect it back to Citadel funding explicitly.
The "distraction" paragraph ends with a strong point about teaching inequality ("less superior" should be "inferior") — this is actually your most powerful idea and deserves more development, perhaps even its own paragraph with examples of workplace integration.

Add a Real Conclusion

Your essay currently ends on the distraction paragraph with no conclusion. Add a final paragraph that:
Restates your thesis in new words
Briefly synthesizes your strongest points
Ends with a forward-looking statement (why this matters for society going forward)

Proofreading and Mechanics

This draft has frequent errors that distract from your argument and need careful editing:
Spelling: "appearred," "alao," "desciniinate," "pubfically," "eqaul," "learening," "excellence discipline" (should be "excellent")
Word choice: "less superior" should be "inferior"; "Everyone deserves" (not "Every deserves")
Comma splices: "That may be true in some cases, but others might be able to handle the challenges, Everyone does deserve the chance to try." (period needed, not comma)
Subject-verb agreement: "There has been many objections" → "There have been many objections"
Consistent point of view: You shift into first person ("as a woman I especially know") — this is fine occasionally for emphasis, but make sure it's a deliberate choice, not accidental.

Suggestion: Read your essay aloud after revising—this will help you catch missing words and awkward phrasing that are easy to miss when reading silently.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, arguable thesis statement.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has one clear focus.
Add specific evidence (legal cases, statistics, examples) to support claims.
Write a proper conclusion.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and punctuation.

You have real passion and good instincts about counterargument—now it's time to add structure, evidence, and polish. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and still-relevant question: whether a stay-at-home wife's contributions should be valued equally with her husband's income, and whether this should translate into equal ownership of shared property. You clearly have strong convictions and some good instincts (the television example, your own family's experience, the point about legal documentation of ownership). However, the essay would benefit significantly from tighter organization, a clearer thesis, more consistent argumentation, and careful proofreading. Below are specific areas to focus on in revision.

Thesis and Focus

Your opening line is provocative but unclear in purpose—are you being sarcastic, or setting up a claim you'll refute? State your actual argument early and directly. Something like: "A wife who chooses to stay home makes contributions equal in value to her husband's income, and this equality should be reflected in shared ownership of marital property" would give readers a clear roadmap. As it stands, the essay circles around several related but distinct claims (emotional equality, financial/ownership equality, social judgment from outsiders) without clearly signaling how they connect to one overarching argument.

Organization

The essay would be stronger with a more deliberate structure. Right now, paragraphs move associatively rather than logically—the TV show example leads into a discussion of premarital expectations, which is revisited later in a separate paragraph, creating redundancy (the "husband should have known what he was getting into" point appears at least twice). Consider organizing around three clear pillars:

Why unpaid domestic labor has real value (use your minimum-wage/housekeeper comparison and your family example here—these are your strongest pieces of evidence)
Why this value should translate into equal legal/financial ownership (use your mortgage/contract point here—this is a genuinely original and practical argument)
Why outside social judgment of stay-at-home wives is misguided (your grocery store anecdote)

Grouping ideas this way will prevent repetition and help readers follow your logic.

Development of Evidence

Some of your best material is underdeveloped. The point about needing to pay a housekeeper minimum wage to replace a wife's labor is a compelling economic argument—but you introduce it and then move on quickly. Expand on it: What would a fair "salary" for a stay-at-home spouse actually look like? How does this comparison support your thesis about equal ownership?

Similarly, your personal family example is vivid, but you should draw out the connection explicitly: What is this example proving? Presumably that even when the wife also works, she still performs a "second job" of household labor—this should be tied back to your ownership argument (perhaps working wives deserve even more credit, or this proves domestic labor is inherently undervalued regardless of employment status).

The television script example is confusing because the dialogue is missing ( placeholders appear where quotations should be). Please fill these in—right now, readers cannot follow the scene you're describing, which weakens your primary illustrative example.

Logical Consistency

There are a few contradictions to resolve. Early in the essay, you argue the wife should have equal ownership regardless of the husband's wishes; later, you suggest the arrangement depends on premarital agreement ("if he didn't like the idea... perhaps the marriage should not have been entered into"). This makes it sound like ownership rights are contingent on the husband's prior consent, which seems to undercut your equality argument. Clarify whether you believe equal ownership is a right regardless of prior agreement, or whether it's something couples should negotiate beforehand.

Tone and Audience Awareness

Some phrasing is casual for academic argument ("get some rest," "watching a little television," "goose and gander" idiom). Consider tightening language for a more formal register. Also, be cautious with anecdotal evidence about "gossiping women" at the grocery store—while relatable, this risks sounding like a personal grievance rather than evidence supporting a broader social argument. You might strengthen this section by referencing a statistic, sociological observation, or even hypothetical scenario instead of (or alongside) the anecdote.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are frequent errors that need correction in revision:
Comma splices ("She then went on... He then struck back by saying that the house was also his because oddly enough he paid for it too, She...")
Inconsistent/incorrect punctuation with quotation marks (commas placed outside quotes, or replacing quotes: `t his`, `',theirs'`)
Subject-verb agreement and word choice issues ("fixing on an automobile," "loose the house" instead of "lose")
Sentence fragments ("Unless the husband has such a great job...")

A careful proofreading pass—reading the essay aloud—will catch many of these.

Revision Priorities

Write a clear, direct thesis statement.
Reorganize paragraphs around 3 main supporting arguments.
Fill in the missing dialogue in your television example.
Resolve the contradiction about premarital consent vs. inherent equal rights.
Proofread carefully for punctuation and sentence-boundary errors.

You have real conviction and some strong, original ideas here (especially the legal/ownership argument)—with tighter structure and cleaner prose, this essay could be quite persuasive.

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and complex topic, and you've clearly done some research to support your points. However, there is a significant disconnect between your stated purpose and what you actually deliver. This is the most important issue to address in revision, along with several concerns about evidence, structure, and logical consistency.

Major Issues to Address

Purpose vs. Execution

Rather, it's purpose is to inform you on all the facts about Marijuana."* This is a strong, clear thesis for an informative/expository essay. However, your conclusion undermines this entirely: *"you will see there can only be one logical answer"* strongly implies there is only one correct conclusion—legalization. This is argumentative, not informative.

Revision advice: Decide which essay you are actually writing. If informative, remove leading language and present balanced perspectives without steering the reader toward a single "correct" answer. If argumentative, revise your thesis to openly state your position (eg, "Marijuana should be legalized because...") and structure your evidence to support that claim persuasively.

"NO ONE has ever died from marijuana" — This is a common claim but is difficult to substantiate and easily challenged (eg, overdose statistics may not capture indirect deaths, impaired driving fatalities, etc). Avoid absolute claims like this unless you can cite a rigorous source.
DEA quote (1988) — This administrative law judge's ruling is real but dated and contextually specific; explain who made this statement and why it's significant, rather than presenting it as an official agency stance.
"Psychology and You" is cited informally—provide full bibliographic information (page numbers, edition, publication year) for all sources.
Hemp/paper claim — Verify this statistic and cite it properly; it's a widely repeated claim that needs a specific source.

Structure and Organization
Your essay follows a reasonable logical progression (history → physical effects → mental effects → comparison to alcohol → economic/political arguments), which is a strength. However, the transitions are abrupt, and the essay would benefit from:

Clearer topic sentences for each paragraph that connect back to your thesis.

Comparison to Alcohol Prohibition
This is a compelling rhetorical move, but it's underdeveloped. You assert similarity without explaining *specific* parallels (eg, black market violence, government revenue loss, unenforceability). Expand this comparison with concrete historical evidence to make it persuasive rather than merely suggestive.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Your draft contains several typos and errors that should be corrected in revision:
"govermnent" → government
"Pgychology" → Psychology
"eanount" → amount
"sufferers" (not "suffers")
"high"" — check quotation mark formatting throughout
Run-on sentences, eg, "The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States" is missing a verb ("is").

I recommend a careful proofreading pass, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch these errors.

Citation and Evidence

You'll strengthen your credibility significantly by:
Using a consistent citation format (MLA/APA) for all sources.
Updating statistics—many of your figures (arrests, death tolls) are outdated. Current data will make your argument more relevant and harder to dismiss.
Adding *counterarguments*—even in an informative essay, acknowledging opposing views (eg, studies suggesting marijuana can be harmful for developing brains, gateway drug debates, etc) increases credibility.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Reconcile your purpose statement with your conclusion—pick a lane (informative or persuasive) and stay in it.

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic you clearly care about, and your passion comes through in the essay's final paragraphs. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a tightly argued piece. To strengthen this draft, focus on three main areas: thesis clarity, structural organization, and precision of language/evidence.

Thesis and Argument

Your essay needs a clear, arguable thesis statement early on—ideally at the end of your introduction. Right now, the opening paragraphs describe historical context but don't tell the reader what *argument* you're making about double standards today. Are you arguing that:
Historical gender roles still shape modern expectations for women?
Media perpetuates outdated double standards despite legal equality?
Double standards persist in specific, identifiable ways (media, childhood socialization, career expectations)?

Pick one focused claim and let every paragraph work to support it. Currently, the essay moves through history, biblical references, childhood dating rules, fairy tales, media/beauty standards, and a bill of rights—each could be its own essay. Choose 2-3 strongest threads and develop them fully rather than touching many topics briefly.

Structure and Organization

Consider this revision structure:
Introduction with clear thesis
Brief historical context (1 paragraph, not several) — trim the Adam/Eve section and general history; you don't need extensive backstory to make your contemporary argument
Body paragraphs, each addressing ONE specific double standard with evidence (eg, media representation, childhood socialization/fairy tales, dating double standards)
Conclusion that ties back to thesis

Evidence and Citations

Your citation "(Bloom, 24)" needs a corresponding Works Cited/References entry, and you should introduce Bloom with context (Who is this author? What is the source?) before quoting.
The Adam/Eve reference is presented as fact ("The Bible says... Therefore, since the man was first, he is the leader"). Frame this instead as *one interpretation used historically to justify patriarchy*, not as an accepted truth. This strengthens your credibility as a writer analyzing a viewpoint rather than endorsing it.
Strengthen your media/fairy-tale argument with specific examples—you mention Cinderella and Sleeping Beauty well, but could add a modern media example (a specific show, ad campaign, or statistic) for more persuasive power.

Clarity and Word Choice

There are several distracting typos and word-choice errors that likely occurred during autocorrect/dictation (eg, "warm" appears repeatedly where you likely mean "women"; "horn" for "home"; "had such a hard tire" for "had such a hard time"). Please proofread carefully—these errors undermine your credibility as a writer, even though your ideas are sound. Reading your essay aloud can help catch these.

Also watch for:
Comma splices and run-on sentences ("Women were getting restless, they no longer wanted...")
Informal phrases that weaken academic tone ("fine and dandy," "uppity," "fickle")—consider whether these serve your argument or should be replaced with more analytical language, even when describing historical attitudes ironically.

Tone

Your ironic tone in early paragraphs (mocking the "uppity" women framing) is effective *if* it's clearly a rhetorical strategy. Make sure readers understand you're voicing historical attitudes critically, not endorsing them—a brief signal ("as some men saw it" or "in the dismissive language of the era") can clarify this.

Strongest Moments

Your analysis of fairy tales (Cinderella, Sleeping Beauty) and media beauty standards is your most original and analytically sharp material. Consider making this the *centerpiece* of your essay rather than one paragraph among many. You could expand this into two full paragraphs: one on childhood socialization through stories, one on adult media/beauty standards, connecting both explicitly to your thesis.

Next Steps

Draft a one-sentence thesis and write it at the top of your revision as a guide.
Cut the biblical/historical background to one paragraph.
Expand your media and childhood socialization analysis—this is your strongest material.
Proofread line-by-line for typos, especially "warm/women" and comma splices.
Consolidate your multiple conclusions into one paragraph that clearly restates your argument and its significance.

You have real insight here—this revision is about sharpening focus and cleaning up the technical presentation so your ideas land with full force.

Feedback on Your Essay: Censorship and the First Amendment

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic, and it's clear you've done research to support your position. You've gathered compelling case studies and statistics that could form the backbone of a strong argument. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas to reach its full potential. Below is feedback organized by key areas of concern.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your essay opens with rhetorical questions rather than a clear thesis statement. While rhetorical questions can be effective hooks, readers need to know your specific claim early on. What exactly are you arguing? Is it that:
No entity should have authority to ban books, or
Current censorship practices violate constitutional rights, or
Censorship is ultimately ineffective at protecting children?

Your conclusion touches on all three ideas, but the essay doesn't develop any single line of argument fully. Consider drafting a clear thesis statement such as: "Despite claims that book banning protects students, censorship efforts by school boards and citizen groups violate constitutional rights and fail to shield children from the realities they seek to avoid." Then ensure each paragraph builds toward proving this specific claim.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

Your essay would benefit from a clearer organizational logic. Currently, you move through a brief history of censorship, then several case studies, then a conclusion—but the connections between these sections aren't always explicit. Consider:

Adding topic sentences that explicitly link each paragraph to your thesis. For example, before discussing the Salvail case, tell readers *why* this case matters to your argument.
Analyzing your evidence more deeply. You present facts (the PAW statistics, the court cases) but often don't explain their significance. What do the 376 attacks and 44 states tell us about the scope of the problem? How does the Salvail case's outcome support your argument, while the Cary case's outcome complicates it? You mention that teachers *lost* despite a First Amendment violation—this is a crucial point that deserves more analysis, as it seems to undermine your argument that authority is being wrongly exercised. Don't let this contradiction go unaddressed.
Strengthening transitions. Currently, paragraphs shift between historical context, legal cases, and library controversies somewhat abruptly.

Use of Evidence

Your case studies (Salvail, Cary, the Mesa Library incident) are excellent choices, but they need fuller development:
Provide dates for the court cases to help readers situate them.
Explain the reasoning behind court decisions in more detail. Why did the Nashua Board fail to justify removing *Ms.* magazine? What specific legal standard did they fail to meet?
Address the Cary case's implications directly. This case seems to reveal a limitation in your argument—if teachers waived rights through prior agreements, doesn't this complicate the claim that authorities have no right to censor? Engaging with this complexity would strengthen your credibility as a writer.

Logical Consistency

Be careful with claims that might weaken your argument. For instance, you criticize censors for trying to "stop the world from turning" through banning books, then argue children will encounter these themes elsewhere (television, parks, streets). While this point about inevitability is reasonable, consider whether it fully supports intellectual freedom as a *value*, or whether it merely argues censorship is *futile*. These are related but distinct claims—you might strengthen your essay by explicitly arguing for the value of literary works (as you begin to do with Angelou) rather than relying primarily on the "they'll see it anyway" argument, which readers might find less persuasive than a rights-based argument.

Style and Mechanics

There are numerous typos and errors that need correction before submission, including:
"fireedom" → freedom
"bum them" → burn them
"Attacks on the Freedom ofspeech" → check exact title and capitalization
"curriculwn" → curriculum
"Salvail v. Nashua Board ofeducation" → needs proper spacing/formatting for case citation
Inconsistent comma usage (eg, "In New Hampshire,, the magazine")
"an previous agreement" → a previous
Random symbols like "<?>" should be removed or replaced with an actual example

Please proofread carefully, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch errors your eye might skip over. Additionally, court case names should be italicized (*Salvail v. Nashua Board of Education*), and you should verify all quotations and statistics against your original sources.

Citations

I don't see any in-text citations or a works cited page. Since you're using specific statistics, quotations, and case details, you need to cite your sources according to your assigned citation style (MLA is standard for literature/humanities papers). This is essential for academic integrity and to allow readers to verify your claims.

Next Steps

Focus your revision on: (1) crafting a clear, arguable thesis; (2) analyzing your evidence rather than just presenting it; (3) addressing complications in your evidence (like the Cary case); and (4) thorough proofreading. With these revisions, this could become a compelling, well-supported argument.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Confederate Flag Controversy

Overall Impression

You've gathered a substantial amount of source material on this controversial topic, and your topic itself is compelling and relevant. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of quotations than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you need to establish a clear thesis, analyze your sources rather than simply presenting them, and revise for clarity, grammar, and citation accuracy.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, your position is unclear until the very last paragraph, and even there it's stated ambiguously ("If we let these people have their way it will only lead to bigger controversy down the road"—who is "these people," and what "way"?). An argumentative essay needs a clear, specific thesis stated early (ideally at the end of your introduction) that tells readers exactly what you will argue and why. 

Revision suggestion: Decide your actual position—Should the flag remain on public buildings? Is it a symbol of heritage, hate, or both depending on context? Once you know your claim, every paragraph should work to support it.

Over-reliance on Quotations

This is the essay's biggest weakness. Nearly every paragraph consists of one long quotation after another with little transition or analysis. Quotations should support *your* argument, not replace it. For each quote you use, ask: *Why am I including this? What does it prove? How does it connect to my thesis?*

For example, the paragraph with five different survey respondents (Thomson, Minor, Lukes, Lucas, Blair) simply lists opinions without any synthesis. What do these responses reveal collectively? Do they show regional divides? Generational differences? You need to interpret the pattern for your reader.

Similarly, the Alexander Stephens "Cornerstone Speech" quotation is extremely important historical evidence—but you present it with no analysis or moral framing. Given that this speech explicitly argues for Black inferiority as the foundation of the Confederacy, quoting it without commentary risks seeming to endorse or minimize its content. You must directly address what this speech reveals about the Confederacy's founding purpose and explain how it relates to your argument about the flag's meaning today.

Organization

The essay would benefit from clearer structural signposting. Consider organizing around key sub-arguments rather than around sources. For instance:

Paragraph 1: Introduction and thesis
Paragraph 2: Historical origins of the flag and Confederacy (including the Stephens speech—analyzed, not just quoted)
Paragraph 3: The "heritage" argument (Southern voices)
Paragraph 4: The "hate symbol" argument (opposing voices)
Paragraph 5: Public opinion data (polls) and what they reveal
Paragraph 6: Your evaluation/rebuttal
Conclusion: Restate and reinforce your thesis

Right now, your polling data appears twice in different forms without being connected, and the historical background (Stephens' speech) appears very late, after all the modern controversy—this reverses the logical order. Consider moving historical context earlier.

Handling Counterarguments

You include Senator Campbell's comparison of the flag to Confederate violence to "Old Glory" and westward expansion genocide. This is an interesting counterpoint, but you don't explain whether you find it persuasive or flawed. A strong argumentative essay doesn't just present multiple views—it evaluates them and explains why one is more convincing than another.

Citations

Your in-text citations (eg, "(Riley 30)") are inconsistent and missing a Works Cited page. Also check:
Names appear to be misspelled or garbled ("Carles Lunford," "Howell Felfling," "Tommie Phillips L. Cavera")—verify these against your sources.
Ensure consistent citation style (MLA, APA, etc) throughout, and include a full Works Cited/References list.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are numerous errors that need correction:
Spelling: "fory" (forty), "siad" (said), "todl" (told), "unacceptabe," "Africa-Americans"
Punctuation: Apostrophes are frequently misused or omitted ("ancestors lives," "It's" vs "Its")
Quotation marks: Several appear as stray symbols (�) — this is likely a formatting/encoding issue, but proofread carefully to fix it.
Sentence fragments: "There is always going to be controversy for since we live in this world" is unclear and grammatically awkward.

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these errors before submission.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently undercuts the complexity you've built throughout the essay. Phrases like "these people" and "let them have their way" sound dismissive and vague, and don't reflect the nuance found in your source material (eg, the Black South Carolinians who were split on the issue, or the newspaper editor who acknowledges both heritage and harm). Revise your conclusion to clearly and respectfully restate your argument, incorporating the complexity you've researched.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Write a clear, specific thesis statement.
Reduce direct quotations; add your own analysis after each one.
Directly engage with the Stephens speech—explain its significance.
Reorganize paragraphs logically (historical → modern arguments → your evaluation).
Fix citation format and add a Works Cited page.
Proofread thoroughly for spelling, punctuation, and clarity.
Rewrite the conclusion to reflect nuance and reinforce your thesis clearly.

You have rich material here—now it's time to make it *your* argument.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely, engaging topic, and your passion comes through clearly. You identify a real problem (frivolous litigation) and connect it to broader consequences (costs, court backlogs, workplace effects). However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: evidence and credibility, organization, and proofreading. Below are specific suggestions.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Right now, your argument relies heavily on anecdotes and generalizations ("I heard of a case last week," "Seems like everybody is suing everybody"). For academic writing, you need verifiable sources:

Cite specific cases with dates and sources. The inmate lawsuit examples are memorable, but without citations, readers may doubt their accuracy or think you're exaggerating. Find documented cases (court records, news articles) and cite them properly.
Provide statistics. You make quantitative claims ("millions of tax dollars," "three to five years for a civil case") without sourcing them. Where does this data come from? Include citations from court administration reports, insurance industry studies, or government data.
Address counterarguments. A stronger essay would acknowledge that many lawsuits *are* legitimate (workplace injuries, malpractice, civil rights violations are often real). By not addressing this, your argument risks seeming one-sided. Consider adding a paragraph that concedes some lawsuits are valid, which will actually strengthen your credibility when arguing that *frivolous* ones are the problem.

Improve Organization

Your essay currently reads as a list of grievances rather than a structured argument. Consider this framework:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly and specifically. Right now, your thesis is buried. Try something like: "Frivolous lawsuits impose significant costs on taxpayers, businesses, and the judicial system itself, and lawmakers must enact reforms to curb this abuse without restricting legitimate claims."
Body paragraphs: Organize by *category of harm* rather than jumping between topics. For example:
  - Paragraph 1: Direct costs to taxpayers (court infrastructure, staff, inmate lawsuits)
  - Paragraph 2: Costs to consumers/workers (insurance fraud, workers' comp, malpractice premiums)
  - Paragraph 3: Systemic effects (court backlogs, delayed criminal trials, jail overcrowding)
  - Paragraph 4: Counterargument and rebuttal
  - Conclusion: Your policy recommendations (currently your strongest paragraph—consider expanding it)
Transitions: Right now, paragraphs shift abruptly between prison lawsuits, insurance fraud, and workers' comp. Add transitional sentences that show how these topics connect to your central argument.

Refine Your Tone

Phrases like "ridiculous cases," "everybody is suing everybody," and "begging for people to call them" are colloquial and undermine your credibility in an academic context. Consider:

Replacing rhetorical questions ("Are all of these lawsuits necessary?") with declarative analytical statements.
Removing casual asides ("you and I," "now a days") in favor of more formal phrasing.
Being more precise: "frivolous" is a strong word—make sure you define what makes a lawsuit frivolous, and apply that definition consistently.

Proofread Carefully

There are numerous typos and OCR-style errors throughout (eg, "govermnent," "ft~tes," "bmtes," "wn t~g," "fiLI1," "corporation" where you likely mean "cooperation," "payoff" should be "pay off," "ne Hundreds" seems to be a stray fragment). Before your next draft:

Read the essay aloud slowly to catch errors your eyes skip over.
Use spell-check, but don't rely on it exclusively—it won't catch wrong-word errors like "corporation/cooperation."
Check for consistent verb tense (you shift between present and past tense in places).

Sharpen Your Conclusion/Recommendations

Your final paragraph contains your most concrete and actionable ideas—this is a strength. Consider moving some of this specificity earlier, or expanding this section into two paragraphs: one addressing *judicial reforms* (dismissal powers, cost-shifting) and another addressing *industry accountability* (insurance investigation standards, professional licensing consequences).

Next Steps

Draft a clear thesis statement.
Find 3-5 credible sources with actual data/citations.
Reorganize using the structure above.
Add a counterargument paragraph.
Do a careful proofreading pass.

You have a strong foundation of ideas—the revision process now should focus on substantiating and organizing them for an academic audience.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: The 1995 Government Shutdown

Strengths
You've chosen a compelling historical case study and clearly did research into the specifics of the 1995 shutdown—the dates, the numbers of affected workers, and the political players involved show genuine effort. Your instinct to frame this through Jefferson's warning about partisanship is a strong rhetorical strategy that gives your essay a thematic backbone. The essay also attempts something ambitious: connecting a historical episode to broader claims about how the U.S. government should function.

Areas for Revision

Strengthen and Sustain Your Thesis
Your opening quotation from Jefferson sets up an argument—that partisanship threatens government stability—but you don't return to this claim consistently. After the introduction, the essay becomes largely descriptive/narrative rather than argumentative. Revise so that each body paragraph explicitly ties back to your thesis: how does *this* detail (the fiscal year deadline, the vetoed bills, the layoffs) prove that partisanship specifically, rather than other factors, caused the shutdown? Right now the essay tells us *what happened*; it needs to more consistently show *why it proves your claim*.

Verify Your Sources
The Jefferson quotation needs a citation. As written, readers cannot verify where or when Jefferson said this, and the phrasing feels paraphrased rather than a direct quote—please locate the original source and quote it accurately, or clarify that you are summarizing his view. Similarly, your claim that the 1962 shutdown was "the first" government shutdown should be fact-checked; the history of funding gaps is more complex than a single "first" event, and getting this wrong undermines your credibility on the rest of the essay's factual claims. Any statistics (800,000 workers, specific base numbers) should have a cited source (news report, government record) so readers can trust the numbers.

Reduce Narrative, Increase Analysis
Large sections of the essay—particularly the paragraphs detailing the emergency spending bill dates and the back-and-forth of vetoes—read like a timeline rather than an argument. Consider condensing the purely factual/chronological material and expanding the analytical sections where you explain *why* this back-and-forth reflects a partisanship problem rather than, say, a normal and healthy legislative disagreement. You gesture toward this complexity in the paragraph about divided power having both advantages and disadvantages—that paragraph is actually one of your strongest, because it engages in genuine analysis. Write more like that throughout.

Revise "I feel" Statements
Academic argumentative writing generally avoids phrases like "I feel," which weaken your authority as an arguer. Instead of "I feel the American people have been unfairly made to pay," try "The American people have been unfairly made to pay" or "This shutdown unfairly forced ordinary Americans to bear the cost of a political stalemate." State your claims with confidence and back them with evidence and reasoning, rather than framing them as personal feelings.

Address Counterarguments More Fully
You briefly acknowledge that divided power "can be good," which is a promising move—but you dismiss this too quickly to return to your thesis. A stronger essay would seriously engage with the counterargument (why do we have separation of powers and checks and balances in the first place?) before explaining why, in this specific case, partisanship crossed the line from healthy debate into dysfunction.

Clarify Your Ending
Your final paragraph raises an interesting point—that the November 18 pact only delayed the problem—but the essay ends here without returning to Jefferson or restating your central argument. Add a concluding paragraph that explicitly answers: "Was Jefferson right?" Tie the shutdown's aftermath back to your opening thesis for a satisfying close.

Grammar and Mechanics
Please proofread carefully for the following recurring issues:
Apostrophes: "Congress' bill" should be "Congress's bill" (or check your style guide); "TheShutdown'" and "govermnent's" contain typos.
Quotation marks: Ensure quotation marks are used consistently and correctly around the Jefferson quote and any other cited material.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Several sentences ("This event is the shutdown of the United States government and it will not end until government officials. decide to work together...") contain stray periods or comma splices that disrupt readability.
Consistent tense: You shift between past and present tense; choose one primary tense for narrating historical events (past) and one for your argumentative claims (present).
Word choice: "figthing" should be "fighting"; "'m seven years" should be "in seven years."

Next Steps
For your revision, I recommend: (1) writing a clear, one-sentence thesis statement and placing it at the end of your introduction; (2) creating a paragraph outline where each paragraph's first sentence explicitly connects to that thesis; (3) fact-checking and citing your key claims; and (4) doing a full proofreading pass focused on the mechanical issues listed above. This is a promising draft with real potential—tightening the argumentative focus will make it much stronger.

Feedback on "Abortion" Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely difficult topic and identified several angles worth exploring—bodily autonomy, socioeconomic consequences, public health, and legal precedent. However, the essay needs significant revision in four key areas: thesis clarity, evidence/citation, logical reasoning, and mechanics. Below are specific suggestions organized by priority.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your thesis—"Abortion is the right of the people and it should remain so"—is more of an assertion than an argument. A strong thesis should preview *why* this is true and give the reader a roadmap of your reasoning. Consider something like: "Because abortion access protects survivors of assault, prevents unsafe medical practices, and upholds a fundamental principle of bodily autonomy, it should remain legal and unrestricted."

Right now, your paragraphs read as a list of loosely related reasons rather than a building argument. Each paragraph needs a clear topic sentence that connects back to your thesis, and you need transitions between paragraphs so the reader understands how one point leads to the next.

Evidence and Citations

This is the essay's biggest weakness. You cite several statistics and a legal case, but none are sourced, and one is factually incorrect:

"Roe versus Way" should be Roe v. Wade (1973). This is a significant error that undermines your credibility on a legal point central to your argument.
The "one in four women" statistic, the "$125,000 to raise a child" figure, and the claim about average family size by income bracket all need citations. Without sources, these numbers appear invented or unreliable, even if they're accurate.
Academic essays should cite sources using a consistent format (MLA or APA, depending on your instructor's requirements).

Action step: Go back and find the actual sources for every statistic. If you can't find a source, remove the claim or reframe it as a hypothesis rather than fact.

Logical Reasoning—Some Arguments Need Rethinking

A few of your points, while well-intentioned, contain reasoning that could be strengthened or reconsidered:

The "balance" argument (paragraph 4): Suggesting abortion should be used to maintain a numerical balance between socioeconomic classes is ethically troubling and echoes historical eugenics rhetoric, even if unintentionally. This argument will alienate readers rather than persuade them. Consider replacing it with an argument about how the *financial burden* of unplanned parenthood affects a family's ability to escape poverty—this is a related but far less fraught claim.

The slippery-slope argument (paragraph 6): "If they remove this right, who's to say they won't remove other rights" is a logical fallacy (slippery slope) unless you can show historical evidence that rights removal tends to cascade. This point needs support or should be cut.

The rape paragraph: This relies entirely on emotional appeal ("Would you be able to love him or her?"). Emotional appeals are only effective when paired with facts, expert testimony, or trauma research. Consider integrating a study or quote from a psychologist about trauma recovery in survivors forced to carry a pregnancy to term.

Missing Counterargument

A strong argumentative essay acknowledges the opposing view and refutes it directly. Right now, you dismiss opponents ("no one should be able to decide...") without engaging with their strongest points (eg, fetal personhood, religious/moral objections). Adding a paragraph that fairly represents the pro-life position and then rebuts it with evidence would significantly strengthen your credibility.

Conclusion

The essay currently ends on the infant mortality point without any concluding paragraph. You need a conclusion that synthesizes your strongest points and restates your thesis in a new way—not simply repeats it.

Mechanics and Proofreading

There are several errors that need correction:
"Hapeo povertyg" — unclear typo, likely garbled text; rewrite this sentence.
"Rowe versus Way" → "Roe v. Wade"
"eightteen" → "eighteen"
"sky racket alsop" → "skyrocket also"
"if the remove" → "if they remove"
"Would be able to love him or her?" → missing subject ("Would you be able to...")
Watch subject-verb agreement and pronoun consistency throughout ("force" vs. "forced").

Read your essay aloud before submitting—this catches many of these errors.

Priority Revision Checklist
Fix the Roe v. Wade error and add citations for all statistics.
Rewrite or remove the socioeconomic "balance" paragraph.
Add a counterargument paragraph with rebuttal.
Write a proper concluding paragraph.
Proofread carefully for grammar and typos.

You have the seeds of a compelling essay here—the coat-hanger/unsafe-abortion argument and the public-health angle are your strongest points. Focus your revision on building those out with solid evidence.
